KMS envelope encryption for Rust

KMS envelope encryption for Rust

Library provides envelope encryption with a key encryption key held by a KMS, and AEAD encryption without a KMS:

  • KMS providers for:
    • Google Cloud KMS;
    • AWS KMS;
    • Azure Key Vault and Managed HSM;
    • HashiCorp Vault Transit;
    • A local KMS with a key the application holds, for development, tests and single-host installs;
  • AEAD algorithms ChaCha20-Poly1305, AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCM and AES-256-GCM-SIV;
  • Tink’s KMS envelope format for every provider, so Tink reads the values of GCP and AWS keys and the library reads Tink’s, see Formats;
  • Crypto backends ring, aws-lc-rs (including its FIPS module) and the pure Rust RustCrypto crates. A value encrypted by one backend decrypts with any other;
  • Per-call envelopes, store-less DEK reuse, and an in-process cache of decrypted data encryption keys;
  • Deterministic encryption with AES-SIV, for values that are looked up by equality;
  • Typed fields, Encrypted<T, F>, stored with serde, tokio-postgres or sqlx;
  • Optional KMS-side random generators for GCP and AWS;
  • Field encryption with a keyring, in the kms-aead-keyring crate: key versions kept in Firestore, PostgreSQL, Cloud Storage or S3, central staged rotation, beacons, and values in Tink’s wire format;
  • Streaming encryption for files of any length, with reads of any byte range that fetch only the chunks that cover it;
  • Signatures with Ed25519 and ECDSA P-256 keys held by a KMS, verified without a KMS in Rust and in a browser, and MACs under keyring key versions;
  • Signed keyring records, so that whoever can write the keyring store cannot change which keys are used;
  • Asymmetric KEKs, for producers that encrypt with a public key and can never decrypt;
  • Tracing spans for every operation and every KMS call, with no plaintext, key material or AAD in them;
  • No unsafe code.

To start using the library, see Getting started.

Envelope encryption

A value is encrypted with an AEAD algorithm under a data encryption key (DEK). The DEK is then encrypted by a key encryption key (KEK) that never leaves the KMS, and the encrypted DEK is stored next to the cipher text. KMS documentation, and Azure Key Vault’s wrapKey and unwrapKey operations, call this wrapping the DEK. To read the value, the KMS decrypts the DEK, and the DEK decrypts the value locally.

So the KMS sees only DEKs, never the data, and a KMS call is needed only to encrypt or decrypt a DEK. Access to the data is controlled by access to the KEK: revoke the KMS permission, or destroy the key, and the stored values cannot be decrypted.

The additional authenticated data (AAD) is authenticated but not encrypted: a value decrypts only with the AAD it was encrypted with. Use it to tie a value to where it is stored, such as a table and a row id, so that a value copied to another row fails to decrypt.

Choosing a mode

The ways to use a KMS differ in how many DEKs exist, how often the KMS is called and what a stored value holds:

  • Per-call envelope, the default of KmsAeadEnvelope: a new DEK for every value, encrypted with one KMS call, and stored in the value itself. Every decryption decrypts its DEK with one more KMS call, unless the envelope has a DEK cache. Use it when values are written rarely, such as secrets and configuration, or when one DEK per value is a requirement. See Envelope encryption;
  • Store-less DEK reuse, a KmsAeadEnvelope with a DekReusePolicy: one DEK encrypts many values and is renewed by age or by count, so a write calls the KMS only to renew it. Values still carry their encrypted DEK, so nothing is stored apart from them, and reads decrypt through the DEK cache. Use it for high write rates where a KMS call per value costs too much. See DEK caching and reuse;
  • One fixed key, encrypt of AeadCipher or DeterministicCipher with one key that the KMS encrypts once and the DEK cache decrypts: values carry a 4-byte key id in Tink’s wire format instead of an encrypted DEK, and BigQuery and Tink read them with a keyset of that key. Use it for a column that BigQuery reads, with no key rotation. See One fixed key;
  • Keyring, the kms-aead-keyring crate: a keyring record per scope holds the encrypted DEK versions, and values carry a 4-byte key id in Tink’s wire format instead of an encrypted DEK. The record is the place for central rotation and retirement of keys, and for values that BigQuery and Tink read across rotations. See Keyring and field encryption.
Per-call envelopeStore-less DEK reuseOne fixed keyKeyring
Cratekms-aeadkms-aeadkms-aeadkms-aead-keyring

KMS calls on readone decryption per value, or per DEK cache missone decryption per DEK per cache TTLone decryption per cache TTLone decryption per key version per cache TTL

Crypto-shreddingKEK destructionKEK destructionKEK destructionKEK destruction, or dangerously_shred() and the deletion of the record’s backups

No mode needs another. Values of the envelope modes move to a keyring with a sweep, see Move envelope values to a keyring.

Streamed values come in the same modes: under a keyring, under an envelope, or with a DEK the application holds, see Streaming encryption.

Both envelope modes encrypt a value of a Rust type, such as an application newtype, into an Encrypted<T, Envelope> that carries its encrypted DEK, and serde, tokio-postgres and sqlx store it as bytes, so a column of secrets needs no store of its own. See Stored values.

AeadCipher and DeterministicCipher work without a KMS at all, with keys the application holds.
