Getting started

Cargo.toml:

[dependencies]
kms-aead = { version = "0.31", features = ["gcp-kms-encryption", "aws-lc"] }

The crate has no default features. A build makes three choices, and they do not constrain each other:

  1. the KMS providers, any number of them, or none for AEAD without a KMS;
  2. the TLS root store and the TLS crypto provider of each provider that has a choice;
  3. the crypto backends that run the AEAD algorithms, one or more.

Features

Every feature of both crates is in Cargo features.

Choosing a backend

There is no default backend: each backend feature adds a type to kms_aead::backends, Ring, AwsLc or RustCrypto, and every type that encrypts takes one as its backend type parameter. Which one fits a build, FIPS and the C toolchain included, is in Choose a provider and a backend.

With an envelope

The local KMS needs no cloud account, so it is the quickest way to try the library. An ephemeral KEK lives as long as the process:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

let aad = b"orders/1042";
let secret = SecretValue::from("4111 1111 1111 1111");

let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> = envelope.encrypt(aad, &secret).await?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt(aad, &encrypted).await?;

assert_eq!(decrypted, secret);
Ok(())
}

To move to a cloud KMS, replace the provider, see KMS providers. The rest of the code stays the same.

With a keyring

Field encryption under a keyring is the kms-aead-keyring crate, see Keyring and field encryption. Its features are in Cargo features.

Cargo.toml:

[dependencies]
kms-aead = { version = "0.31", features = ["gcp-kms-encryption", "ring"] }
kms-aead-keyring = { version = "0.31", features = ["ring", "admin", "firestore-tls-roots"] }
secret-vault-value = "1"
tokio = { version = "1", features = ["macros", "rt-multi-thread"] }

A keyring on the memory store with a local KEK, for an application type Email. It needs no cloud credentials, and needs the admin feature for create, rotate and promote:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldAad, FieldName, FieldSpec, KeyId, Keyring, KeyringAdmin,
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, Plaintext, Purpose,
    Encryptor,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

const TENANT: KeyringId = KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42");
const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
);

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(provider, TENANT, KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
// The application's handle on the same keyring
let keyring: &Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = admin.keyring();

// The AAD ties the value to user 42's email: `user/42/email`
let aad: FieldAad = EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from("42")?);
let alice = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
// One encryptor per write of a record, for every encrypted field it holds
let encryptor: Encryptor<'_, MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = keyring.encryptor().await;
let value: Encrypted<Email, Randomized> = encryptor.encrypt(&aad, &alice).await?;
assert_eq!(value.key_id(), KeyId::new(1));

// A staged rotation keeps the previous version decrypting. One process has nothing to wait for,
// so it promotes at once
let staged: KeyId = admin.rotate(Purpose::Randomized).await?;
admin.promote_now(staged).await?;
assert_eq!(keyring.decrypt(&aad, &value).await?, alice);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

The registry, its handles and the encryptor are in The registry and its handles.

Full example available here, run it with cargo run -p kms-aead-keyring --example local_kms --features ring,admin.

MSRV

The minimum supported Rust version is 1.99, for every feature.

Encrypt fields with a KMS

A field or a secret in the application’s own table, encrypted under a DEK that a cloud KMS encrypts, with no keyring store. Every value carries its encrypted DEK, so nothing is stored apart from the values. For central rotation, deterministic fields across rotations or BigQuery reads across rotations, see Choosing a mode and the keyring.

Which mode

Values areModeSee
written rarely, such as secrets and configurationper-call envelope: a new DEK for every value, one KMS call to encrypt itEnvelope encryption
written at a high ratestore-less DEK reuse: one DEK for many values, renewed by age, count and bytesStore-less DEK reuse
grouped, such as a file in chunks or a tenant’s rows, with one DEK the application keepsthe *_with_dek and *_with_encrypted_dek calls, with the encrypted DEK stored apartWorking with DEKs yourself
encrypted with a key the application holds, with no KMSAeadCipherAEAD without a KMS

The path

  1. A provider for the KMS, such as GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref), with the rights of KMS rights: decrypt for a reader, encrypt too for a writer, see KMS providers;
  2. KmsAeadEnvelope::new(provider), or with_options with a DekReusePolicy for store-less DEK reuse and a DekCache for repeated reads, see DEK caching and reuse;
  3. a newtype per field, such as Email, that implements Plaintext, and normalises the value if it is ever looked up, see Plaintext;
  4. the AAD that ties the value to its place, <table>/<row id>/<column>, so a value copied into another row fails to decrypt, see AAD;
  5. envelope.encrypt(aad, &value) into an Encrypted<Email, Envelope>, stored as a serde field, a BYTEA column through tokio-postgres or sqlx, or hex in a TEXT column, see Storing;
  6. envelope.decrypt(aad, &encrypted) with the same AAD, rebuilt from the row.

A first example, with the local KMS, is in With an envelope.

Lookups, readers and rotation

  • Finding rows by an encrypted field needs a deterministic field or a beacon, see Find rows by an encrypted value;
  • Tink reads envelope values on Cloud KMS and AWS KMS keys, and BigQuery does not, see Read values in BigQuery or Tink;
  • the KEK rotates in the KMS, and the stored DEKs are encrypted again before the old version is disabled, see Rotate keys and re-encrypt values;
  • values move to a keyring later with a sweep, see Move envelope values to a keyring.

A keyring per tenant

A multi-tenant application gives each tenant a keyring of its own: its own key versions, rotated, flagged and destroyed apart from the others, in one keyring store such as Firestore or PostgreSQL. The keyring id names the tenant, see The record for the ids it takes.

One KEK per tenant or one shared KEK

A KEK per tenantOne shared KEK
A DEK copied into another tenant’s recordfails at the KMS, which decrypts with the tenant’s own KEKdecrypts, since the KMS decrypts it for any principal allowed to decrypt with the KEK
KMS rightsper tenant, so a principal can be limited to some tenantsone grant covers every tenant
Crypto-shredding a tenantdestroying its KEK, backups of the record and exported keysets includeddangerously_shred(), and then deleting the record’s backups, the store’s other copies and the exported keysets, or a KEK rotation for every other keyring
KMS keys to manageone per tenantone

The first row is the store-writer threat of Keyring deployment, and the third the two routes of Crypto-shredding. A deployment that needs either guarantee uses a KEK per tenant.

Creating the keyrings

An application opens only keyrings that exist, so each tenant’s keyring is created first:

  • by the administrative job, as part of onboarding a tenant, with create or open_or_create;
  • or by the application on the tenant’s first request, with open_or_create. Such an application builds with the admin feature and runs with the administrative rights, so it is trusted as the administration is, see Applications and administration and Keyring limits.

The path through the API

  1. One KeyringRegistry per process and store, with the store and the backend, see The registry and its handles;
  2. registry.open(provider, keyring_id) for each tenant the process serves. A second open of an open keyring reads nothing from the store and shares the first one’s record and watch. The call takes the provider by value: GcpKmsProvider and HcVaultTransitProvider are Clone, and the other providers are built once per keyring;
  3. a FieldSpec constant per encrypted field, the same for every tenant, since each keyring has DEKs of its own, see Encrypted fields and AAD;
  4. on every write, keyring.encryptor() and its encrypt, and the encryption marker it gives, see The encryption marker; on every read, keyring.decrypt;
  5. registry.shutdown(None) before the process exits, see The registry and its handles.

Rotations and sweeps run per keyring: the administrative job loops over the tenants, see Rotate keys and re-encrypt values.

What many keyrings cost

A keyring stays open until the registry shuts down, so a process holds every keyring it opened:

  • store reads: one at the first open, and one revalidation per keyring per record_ttl, or per change signal, see How the record is refreshed;
  • connections: on Firestore, one listen stream per store, with a target on each keyring’s document; on PostgreSQL, at most two connections per store, however many keyrings, see Keyring stores;
  • the DEK cache: one per registry, shared by its keyrings, with an entry per version in use per keyring. It holds DekCacheOptions::DEFAULT_MAX_ENTRIES, 1024, by default, and an entry evicted for capacity is decrypted through the KMS again on its next use, so size it above the tenants a process serves times the versions each uses, see Sizing;
  • KMS calls: one decryption per version per DEK cache TTL in every process, and two per version in each reencrypt_deks. A KEK rotation re-encrypts every keyring under the KEK, see The KMS decrypt burst;
  • metrics: the plaintext size histogram has a series per keyring and version, see Metrics for its cardinality limit.

Find rows by an encrypted value

A randomised value is different every time it is encrypted, so a data store cannot find a row by it. Two ways make a field searchable by equality: a deterministic field, whose stored value is equal for equal plaintexts, and a beacon, a truncated HMAC stored beside a field that stays randomised. Both leak which rows hold the same value, see What it leaks and What a beacon leaks. Neither suits a field with few distinct values, such as a status or a country.

Beacon or deterministic field

BeaconDeterministic field
Stored valueRandomised, so its AAD can name the row, and a value copied into another row fails to decryptEqual for equal values, so its AAD names the field and never the row, and a value copied into another row decrypts there
MatchThe truncated HMAC, with deliberate collisionsAlways the exact value
KeyA beacon key, rotated separately from the data keysThe field’s own key
CostAn extra column, and a decryption and filter of the candidates on every lookupNothing beyond the field
BigQuerySQL cannot calculate a beacon; it matches beacons the application calculatedSQL decrypts the field and searches it by equality
FIPSHMAC-SHA256 on the configured backendAES-SIV, which is not FIPS-approved

With the default 16 bits, there are 65,536 possible beacons. A column of 1,000,000 distinct emails gives about 1,000,000 / 65,536, or 15, emails per beacon, so a lookup of one email returns about 15 rows. The application decrypts them and keeps the one equal to the email it looks for. How the bits trade rows to filter against what the column leaks is in Bits.

With kms-aead alone

The application keeps the key itself, as a key id and the key encrypted by the KMS, such as in its settings, and rotates it by hand:

  • a deterministic field: DeterministicCipher with a key the KMS encrypts once, see Deterministic encryption;
  • a beacon: KmsAeadEnvelope::beacon or BeaconMac, see Beacons. A rotation keeps both keys and queries with a beacon under each until a re-index, see Rotating a beacon key.

With a keyring

Each purpose has its own key versions, rotated centrally, and a lookup matches every version that still decrypts:

  • a deterministic field: an encryptor’s encrypt_deterministic on every write, and Keyring::deterministic_probes for a lookup, under the deterministic feature, see Deterministic fields;
  • a beacon: an encryptor’s encrypt and beacon in the same write, and Keyring::beacon_probes for a lookup, see Beacons.

Each rotation of a deterministic or beacon version adds a probe to every lookup until a sweep, see Rotation cadence.

A lookup

  1. Normalise the value in its newtype, such as a lowercased email, so that the write and the lookup encode the same bytes, see Normalisation;
  2. compute the probes of the value looked for, one per key that still decrypts: with kms-aead alone one, or two during a rotation by hand until the re-index; with a keyring one per version;
  3. query by them: WHERE email_beacon = ANY($1) on PostgreSQL with the probes bound as a BYTEA[], an in filter on Firestore, which takes up to 30 values, see Queries across rotations;
  4. for a beacon, decrypt every row found and keep the ones whose plaintext equals the value: an equal beacon never proves an equal value.

A deterministic field in BigQuery is queried in SQL, with one DETERMINISTIC_ENCRYPT per version, see Queries.

Read values in BigQuery or Tink

Values the library writes are in Tink’s formats, so Tink in another language and BigQuery’s SQL read them, given the keys. Three routes lead there, and they differ in what the reader needs and in whether the key rotates:

Envelope valuesOne fixed keyKeyring
Written byKmsAeadEnvelope::encrypt, per call or store-lessAeadCipher::encrypt or DeterministicCipher::encrypt, with a key the KMS encrypts oncean encryptor’s encrypt or encrypt_deterministic
A value carriesits encrypted DEKa 4-byte key ida 4-byte key id
Tink reads withKmsEnvelopeAead, on a Cloud KMS or AWS KMS keya keyset of the key, as TinkKeyset builds itexport_tink_keyset, on a Cloud KMS key, an AWS KMS key named by its ARN, or a Vault Transit key for Tink for Go
BigQuery reads withnothing: SQL has no KMS envelopeKEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN over the keyset encrypted by a Cloud KMS keyKEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN over export_bigquery_keyset, on a Cloud KMS key
Algorithms BigQuery readsnoneAES-256-GCM and AES-SIVAES-256-GCM and AES-SIV
Rotationthe KEK in the KMSnone: a second key needs a second key id and a keyset of bothcentral, with a new export after each rotation
SeeTink’s KMS envelopeOne fixed keyBigQuery and Tink interop

The AAD

The reader decrypts a value only with the AAD it was encrypted with, so the AAD is one the reader can build:

  • envelope values and one fixed key: the application’s own bytes, which a Tink reader passes as they are, and SQL builds from the row when they are text, see AAD;
  • a keyring: the field’s default AAD, <kind>/<id>/<field> for a randomised field, which SQL rebuilds from the row as UTF-8 text, see AAD in SQL. A custom AAD fails on a field declared for BigQuery.

With a keyring

  1. Declare each field the reader decrypts with FieldSpec::with_interop(Interop::BigQuery) or Interop::Tink: the keyring then refuses to encrypt it under an algorithm or a KEK the reader does not read, see Fields BigQuery reads and Fields Tink reads;

  2. the administrative job exports the keyset, with export_bigquery_keyset under the bigquery-export feature or export_tink_keyset under admin, and writes its bytes where the query or the reader takes them;

  3. the job exports again:

    • the Readable keysets after every rotate, before the promotion, and after every retire, every mark_compromised and every KEK rotation;
    • the Single keyset of a new deterministic version after its promotion, since a pending version has none. Until then, an equality lookup in SQL computes no probe under the new version and misses the rows encrypted under it, with no error.

    See Rotation cadence, Retiring the old version and Encrypted keysets.

Full example of a Tink export available here, and of a BigQuery sweep in SQL here.

An exported keyset holds the keys of the versions it selects, so treat it as a copy of the record’s readable versions, see Keyring limits. BigQuery cannot calculate a beacon: a beacon lookup in BigQuery matches beacons the application calculated, see BigQuery.

Encrypt large files and serve ranges

Uploads of any size are encrypted as they arrive, with one chunk of 16 KiB in memory, and a reader fetches only the chunks that cover the bytes it needs: one entry of a zip archive, or the range a video player asks for. The object store holds the encrypted object and serves Range requests as it does for any object. Feature stream, see Streaming encryption.

Keyring or envelope

KeyringEnvelope
Keythe keyring’s active version, named by a 4-byte key id in the object’s headera DEK of the object’s own, or a reused one under a DekReusePolicy
Stored beside the objectthe encryption marker, see The encryption markerthe encrypted DEK, in the object’s metadata or in the row that names the object
KMS callsone decryption per key version per DEK cache TTLone encryption per new DEK, one decryption per object per DEK cache TTL
Rotationthe keyring’sthe KEK’s, by re-encrypting the stored DEKs

Both give the same StreamEncryptor and StreamDecryptor. There is no size cap: the encryptor does not need the length in advance.

Upload

An EncryptingWriter wraps the sink that writes the object. Copy the request body into it and call shutdown, which writes the final chunk; a writer dropped before its shutdown leaves an object that no reader decrypts whole. The object’s AAD names where it belongs, such as upload/<id>/file. A handler that holds the body as a Stream of Bytes uses an EncryptingStream instead, see Encrypting and decrypting.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry,
    KeyringRegistryOptions,
};
use kms_aead_keyring::stream::{ChunkReadPlan, EncryptingWriter, PrefixedStreamHeader, StreamFormat};
use tokio::io::AsyncWriteExt;
/// An object store that serves `Range` requests, held in memory.
struct Store(Vec<u8>);
impl Store {
    fn get(&self, range: std::ops::Range<u64>) -> &[u8] {
        &self.0[range.start as usize..range.end as usize]
    }
    fn size(&self) -> u64 {
        self.0.len() as u64
    }
}
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("uploads".to_string())?)?;
let admin = registry
    .open_or_create(provider, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"), KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
let keyring = admin.keyring();
let mut body = &vec![7_u8; 1_200_000][..];
let mut sink = Vec::new();

const UPLOAD_FILE: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("upload"),
    FieldName::from_static("file"),
);
let aad = UPLOAD_FILE.aad(&EntityId::try_from("upload-7")?);

let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await.stream_encryptor(&aad).await?;
let mut writer = EncryptingWriter::new(encryptor, &mut sink);
tokio::io::copy(&mut body, &mut writer).await?;
writer.shutdown().await?;
let stored_len = writer.stored_len_written();
let store = Store(sink);
assert_eq!(store.size(), stored_len);

// Serving: plan from the size the store reports first, so that a length the rules refuse
// reaches neither the store nor the KMS. One zip entry, or the range of a video request
let last: ChunkReadPlan = StreamFormat::Prefixed.plan_final_chunk_read(store.size())?;
let plan = StreamFormat::Prefixed.plan_chunk_read(store.size(), 1_000_000..1_064_512)?;

// The header once per request, then the decryptor
let header = PrefixedStreamHeader::try_from(store.get(0..StreamFormat::Prefixed.header_len() as u64))?;
let decryptor = keyring.stream_decryptor(&aad, &header).await?;

// The final chunk authenticates the length the store reports before any range is served
decryptor.decrypt_chunk_read::<Vec<u8>>(&last, store.get(last.ciphertext()))?;
let entry: Vec<u8> = decryptor.decrypt_chunk_read(&plan, store.get(plan.ciphertext()))?;
assert_eq!(entry.len(), 64_512);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

A download that serves the whole object takes it from its first byte to its last with decrypting_stream or decrypting_reader of the keyring or the envelope, and needs no stored length, see Encrypting and decrypting.

The DEK beside the object

An envelope takes the place of the keyring. stream_encryptor_bare returns the encrypted DEK with the encryptor, and stream_decryptor_bare takes it back; the application stores it beside the object and hands it to the reader. The rest is as above:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::stream::{EncryptingWriter, StreamFormat};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use tokio::io::AsyncWriteExt;
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("uploads".to_string())?)?;
let mut body = &vec![7_u8; 1_200_000][..];
let mut object = Vec::new();
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<_, Ring>::new(provider)?;
let aad = b"upload/7/file";

let (encryptor, encrypted_dek) = envelope.stream_encryptor_bare(aad).await?;
let mut writer = EncryptingWriter::new(encryptor, &mut object);
tokio::io::copy(&mut body, &mut writer).await?;
writer.shutdown().await?;
let stored_len = writer.stored_len_written();

let header = &object[..StreamFormat::Bare.header_len()];
let decryptor = envelope.stream_decryptor_bare(aad, header, &encrypted_dek).await?;
let last = StreamFormat::Bare.plan_final_chunk_read(stored_len)?;
assert_eq!(last.plaintext().end, 1_200_000);
Ok(())
}

The envelope’s stream_encryptor, without the _bare suffix, writes the encrypted DEK inside the object instead, so that nothing is stored beside it, and decrypting_stream or decrypting_reader reads the whole object back, see Streaming encryption. A store-less envelope, one with a DekReusePolicy, encrypts many objects under one DEK in either form.

What to watch for

  • The stored length a range is planned with is the object store’s word until the final chunk has been decrypted. A store that cuts an object inside a chunk and reports the shorter size passes every range that stays clear of the cut. Read the final chunk through plan_final_chunk_read once per object before the first range. A zip reader that counts from the end of the archive needs it first;
  • make one decryptor per request, in an Arc: its methods take &self. Do not cache it across requests: a decryptor, like an encryptor, keeps decrypting after a shred, a destroy or a compromised flag of its version until it is dropped, see Streaming encryption;
  • DecryptingReader, DecryptingStream and decrypt_stream yield the plaintext of a chunk as soon as the chunk is authentic, so write the response to a place you can discard. A failure of the source or the sink is STREAM_IO, and the retry is the whole stream again. They take the end of their source as the end of the object;
  • the object carries no signature. A party without the DEK that must check it gets a Signature<T> over a document that lists the object’s SHA-256 digest, see Publish content anyone can verify;
  • a sweep re-encrypts a streamed object whole: decrypt it under the version it names and encrypt it again under the active one, with no helper.

The limits are in Streaming encryption.

Full examples available here for a keyring, here for reading a range from an envelope, and here for a request body as a Stream.

Encrypt streaming HTTP bodies

A handler streams a large export, one record at a time, and the body leaves the server encrypted. The client decrypts it as it arrives and decodes the records one by one. Neither side holds the export in memory, and the encrypted DEK travels inside the body. Feature stream, see Streaming encryption.

The encryption sits on the bytes of the body, between the format and the socket:

  • the server turns its items into bytes with a streaming format, such as JSON lines (one JSON document per line) of axum-streams, and passes the bytes through an EncryptingStream, which yields the header and then one encrypted chunk at a time;
  • the client passes the bytes of the response to decrypting_stream of the envelope, and hands the plaintext to the decoder of reqwest-streams;
  • the format knows nothing about encryption, and the envelope knows nothing about items. The body is one Cobblestone stream in the envelope form, so the KMS is called as for any streamed value: one encryption per new DEK, one decryption per response per DEK cache TTL, see DEK caching and reuse.

The server

The response of StreamBodyAs is taken apart: its body is the encoded bytes, and its Content-Type names the format. The handler replaces the content type with an opaque one, since the body is ciphertext, and keeps the format’s in a header of its own. The AAD names the export, so that a body of another export does not decrypt under it:

use std::sync::Arc;

use axum::body::Body;
use axum::extract::State;
use axum::http::header::CONTENT_TYPE;
use axum::http::{HeaderValue, Response, StatusCode};
use axum::response::IntoResponse;
use axum_streams::{JsonNewLineStreamFormat, StreamBodyAs};
use futures::stream;
use kms_aead::stream::EncryptingStream;
use futures::StreamExt;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::LocalKmsProvider;
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use serde::Serialize;

type Envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>;
#[derive(Serialize)]
struct Order {
    id: u64,
}

const EXPORT_AAD: &[u8] = b"exports/orders/2026-10";

async fn export_orders(
    State(envelope): State<Arc<Envelope>>,
) -> Result<Response<Body>, (StatusCode, String)> {
    let orders = stream::iter((0..50_000).map(|id| Order { id })).map(Ok::<_, axum::Error>);
    let (mut parts, encoded) =
        StreamBodyAs::new(JsonNewLineStreamFormat::new(), orders).into_response().into_parts();
    let inner_content_type = parts.headers.remove(CONTENT_TYPE);

    let encryptor = envelope
        .stream_encryptor(EXPORT_AAD)
        .await
        .map_err(|error| (StatusCode::INTERNAL_SERVER_ERROR, error.code().to_string()))?;
    let ciphertext = EncryptingStream::new(encryptor, encoded.into_data_stream());

    let mut response = Response::new(Body::from_stream(ciphertext));
    response
        .headers_mut()
        .insert(CONTENT_TYPE, HeaderValue::from_static("application/octet-stream"));
    if let Some(inner_content_type) = inner_content_type {
        response.headers_mut().insert("x-inner-content-type", inner_content_type);
    }
    Ok(response)
}

The route is get(export_orders), with the envelope in an Arc as the router’s state. EncryptingStream takes its source as a Stream of Result<Bytes, _> whose error converts into a boxed error, and Body::from_stream takes the encrypted chunks the same way, so no error is mapped by hand. An error in the items ends the body with an error, never with a clean end.

The client

reqwest-streams decodes a reqwest::Response, so the decrypted bytes are wrapped in one that carries the format’s content type, which the decoder reads. The source of decrypting_stream is Unpin, so the byte stream of the response goes through Box::pin. The decoder takes the longest record it accepts, which bounds what a server can make the client buffer:

use axum::http::header::CONTENT_TYPE;
use axum::http::Response;
use futures::TryStreamExt;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::LocalKmsProvider;
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use reqwest_streams::JsonStreamResponse;
use serde::Deserialize;

#[derive(Deserialize)]
struct Order {
    amount_cents: u64,
}
const EXPORT_AAD: &[u8] = b"exports/orders/2026-10";
const MAX_ORDER_LEN: usize = 64 * 1024;

async fn download_orders(
    envelope: &KmsAeadEnvelope<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>,
    url: &str,
) -> Result<u64, Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
    let response = reqwest::get(url).await?.error_for_status()?;
    let inner_content_type = response
        .headers()
        .get("x-inner-content-type")
        .cloned()
        .ok_or("the response names no inner content type")?;

    let plaintext = envelope
        .decrypting_stream(EXPORT_AAD, Box::pin(response.bytes_stream()))
        .await?
        .map_err(std::io::Error::other);
    let decrypted = Response::builder()
        .header(CONTENT_TYPE, inner_content_type)
        .body(reqwest::Body::wrap_stream(plaintext))?;

    let mut orders = reqwest::Response::from(decrypted).json_nl_stream::<Order>(MAX_ORDER_LEN);
    let mut total_cents = 0;
    while let Some(order) = orders.try_next().await? {
        total_cents += order.amount_cents;
    }
    Ok(total_cents)
}

The example runs both sides in one process, on a loopback port, with the local KMS and no credentials. It decodes 50,000 orders from a body of about 3.8 MB and compares each one with the order that was sent. Run it with cargo run --example axum_encrypted_stream --features stream,ring.

What to watch for

  • Cobblestone releases a chunk only when 16 KiB of plaintext has gathered or the stream ends, and EncryptingStream has no flush. This fits exports, reports and bulk downloads. A feed that sends a small record every few seconds delivers nothing until 16 KiB of records have gathered, so server-sent events and notifications do not fit;
  • the client’s decoder receives records in groups of one chunk, and only once the chunk is authentic. A body that is cut, modified or extended ends in an error item, STREAM_LENGTH or DECRYPT, after the records of the earlier chunks were already decoded. The retry is the whole download again, so write what the client decodes to a place it can discard;
  • the status and the headers are sent before the first record. A failure while the items stream ends the body with an error and cannot change the status;
  • the content type convention belongs to the two sides. The name of the header is not a standard, and the client should check its value against the formats it decodes;
  • ciphertext does not compress, so a compression layer on the route gains nothing;
  • the AAD must be the same on both sides. A value that names the export, the tenant or the user keeps a body from decrypting under another route, see Encrypted fields and AAD.

Full example available here.

Publish content anyone can verify

A rate sheet, a data export or a release goes out with a signature, and whoever downloads it checks that it came from the publisher and was not changed on the way, with no KMS and no secret. The private key stays in a KMS. Verifiers need the public key, which the publisher serves as a JWK Set, a JSON document that lists public keys. See Signatures and MACs.

Sign a document

A bank publishes its exchange rates as a JSON document. The type that holds the document implements SignableType, which names its payload type, and is signed as JsonEncoded::new(rates), which stands for the compact JSON of the value. The payload type is signed with the bytes, so a signature over the rates never verifies as another kind of document. Canonicalise the JSON before it is signed if anything serialises it again. To publish large files, sign one document that lists their SHA-256 digests instead of each file, and compare the digest of each downloaded file with its entry once the document verifies.

The signing key is a KMS key version that signs, such as a Cloud KMS EC_SIGN_P256_SHA256 version. Every signing provider is built from a key reference that names one version, see “Signing” on the page of Google Cloud KMS, AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault and Vault Transit:

use std::collections::BTreeMap;
use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsSigningKeyRef, GcpKmsSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signer, SigningAlgorithm, VerifyingKeySet};
use kms_aead::{JsonEncoded, KmsAeadResult, SignedPayloadType, SignableType};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct ExchangeRates {
    base: String,
    date: String,
    rates: BTreeMap<String, f64>,
}

impl SignableType for ExchangeRates {
    const PAYLOAD_TYPE: SignedPayloadType =
        SignedPayloadType::from_static("application/vnd.example.exchange-rates+json");
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsSigningKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "rates".to_string(),
    "rates-signing".to_string(),
    NonZeroU32::MIN,
    SigningAlgorithm::EcdsaP256Sha256,
);
let signer = Signer::<_, Ring>::new(GcpKmsSigningProvider::new(&key_ref).await?).await?;

// Once per key: the JWK Set to serve at a stable URL, such as /.well-known/rate-keys.json
let jwks: String = VerifyingKeySet::new([signer.verifying_key().clone()])?.to_jwks()?;

// Per publication: the signature to publish beside the document
let rates = JsonEncoded::new(ExchangeRates {
    base: "EUR".to_string(),
    date: "2026-10-26".to_string(),
    rates: BTreeMap::from([("GBP".to_string(), 0.8412), ("USD".to_string(), 1.0873)]),
});
let signature: Vec<u8> = signer.sign(&rates).await?.into_value();
let _ = (jwks, signature);
Ok(())
}

Verify in Rust

A verifier is built from the JWK Set alone and needs no KMS client, so it runs in a CLI, a CI job or another service on any crypto backend:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::signing::{Signature, Verifier, VerifyingKeySet};
use kms_aead::{JsonEncoded, KmsAeadResult, SignedPayloadType, SignableType};
use std::collections::BTreeMap;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalSigningKeyName, LocalSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signer, SigningAlgorithm};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct ExchangeRates {
    base: String,
    date: String,
    rates: BTreeMap<String, f64>,
}
impl SignableType for ExchangeRates {
    const PAYLOAD_TYPE: SignedPayloadType =
        SignedPayloadType::from_static("application/vnd.example.exchange-rates+json");
}
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalSigningProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(
    LocalSigningKeyName::try_from("rates".to_string())?,
    SigningAlgorithm::EcdsaP256Sha256,
)?;
let signer = Signer::<_, Ring>::new(provider).await?;
let downloaded_jwks = VerifyingKeySet::new([signer.verifying_key().clone()])?.to_jwks()?;
let rates = JsonEncoded::new(ExchangeRates {
    base: "EUR".to_string(),
    date: "2026-10-26".to_string(),
    rates: BTreeMap::from([("GBP".to_string(), 0.8412), ("USD".to_string(), 1.0873)]),
});
let downloaded_signature = signer.sign(&rates).await?.into_value();
let verifier = Verifier::<Ring>::new(VerifyingKeySet::from_jwks(&downloaded_jwks)?)?;
let signature = Signature::<JsonEncoded<ExchangeRates>>::try_from(downloaded_signature)?;
verifier.verify(&rates, &signature)?;
Ok(())
}

A verification that fails returns SIGNATURE_INVALID, and a signature with a key id the set lacks SIGNING_KEY_UNKNOWN.

Verify in the browser

A page or a service worker verifies the same signature with WebCrypto: import the keys of the JWK Set, hash the payload and call verify. The code is in In a browser, with the browsers that support Ed25519 and P-256, and the test vectors that run against it in CI. Use P-256 where every engine must verify.

Rotate signing keys

A signing key rotates by publishing before signing:

  1. create the new key version in the KMS;
  2. add its public key to the served JWK Set beside the current one;
  3. switch the signers to the new version once verifiers have fetched the set;
  4. keep the old key in the set for as long as its signatures are verified.

A signature carries the id of its key, the first four bytes of the key’s JWK thumbprint, so verifiers pick the key with no coordination. Removing a key from the set revokes every signature under it, which is the response to a compromised key. See Key ids and rotation.

What to watch for

  • The JWK Set is the trust root. Serve it over HTTPS from a domain the publisher controls, and let a verifier that needs more pin it in its own configuration. The library does not sign the set;
  • a signature has no expiry and the set has no revocation list. Put a version or a date in the document, such as the date of the rates, so that a verifier can refuse an old document that is still validly signed;
  • ECDSA signatures are malleable: never use signature bytes as an identifier;
  • the signature does not hide the document, see Large files for the content.

Full example available here.

Check integrity between your own services

One service puts a job on a queue, an entry in a cache or a row in a table, and another reads it. The reader needs to know that nobody who can write the queue, the cache or the table changed the bytes. A MAC, a keyed hash, answers that with a key only the application’s services hold, and costs an HMAC in process. The MAC does not hide the bytes, see Envelope encryption for that. See Signatures and MACs.

Keyring MACs

A keyring holds MAC key versions beside its other versions, encrypted by the KEK and rotated, flagged and retired as the others are. Create the keyring with a MAC algorithm, HMAC-SHA256 or HMAC-SHA512. A service that writes calls mac_attached on its Encryptor, and a service that reads calls verify_with_mac. The type that is authenticated implements SignableType, whose payload type separates one kind of message from another: a MAC over a job never verifies as a cache entry. The calls take it as JsonEncoded::new(job), which stands for the compact JSON of the value.

use kms_aead_keyring::{JsonEncoded, SignableType, SignedPayloadType, WithMac};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, MacAlgorithm,
};

#[derive(Debug, PartialEq, Eq, Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct Job {
    task: String,
    image_id: u64,
}

impl SignableType for Job {
    const PAYLOAD_TYPE: SignedPayloadType = SignedPayloadType::from_static("example.com/jobs/v1");
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("jobs".to_string())?)?;
let admin = registry
    .open_or_create(
        provider,
        KeyringId::from_static("backend"),
        KeyringAlgorithms::new().with_mac(MacAlgorithm::HmacSha256),
    )
    .await?;
let keyring = admin.keyring();
// The service that enqueues: the payload and its tag in one value
let message: Vec<u8> = keyring
    .encryptor()
    .await
    .mac_attached(&JsonEncoded::new(Job { task: "resize".to_string(), image_id: 7 }))
    .await?
    .into_value();

// The service that works: the tag is checked before the payload is decoded
let with_mac = WithMac::<JsonEncoded<Job>>::try_from(message)?;
let job = keyring.verify_with_mac(&with_mac).await?.0;
assert_eq!(job, Job { task: "resize".to_string(), image_id: 7 });
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

A value whose bytes changed fails with MAC_INVALID. Where the payload already has a place of its own, such as the columns of a row, Encryptor::mac computes a detached Mac<T> to store beside it, and Keyring::verify_mac checks it against the payload as read.

Both services build a Keyring for the same keyring id, with decrypt on the keyring’s KEK and read access to its record, see Applications and administration apart. Every service that holds the key can compute a valid tag, so a MAC protects the bytes from whoever writes the store, and says nothing about which service wrote them.

Rotation

A MAC version rotates and retires like the others, see Rotate keys and re-encrypt values. A sweep re-encrypts Encrypted fields and does not touch MACs, and a value under a retired version fails with KEY_VERSION_RETIRED. Authenticate the payloads that still matter again under the active version before retiring the old one. Messages that are consumed within minutes, such as a queue’s, need no such pass: wait for them to drain.

A signature instead

Use a signature when the party that checks should not be able to forge, or is not one of your services:

MACSignature
Who can checkholders of the keyring’s key: your servicesanyone with the public key: a partner, a browser, an offline tool
Who can forgeevery checkerthe holder of the private key only
Costan HMAC in processone KMS call to sign, none to verify

A webhook your customers verify, a release, and a record whose writer must be told apart from its readers are signatures. See Publish content anyone can verify, and Protect the keyring record for the keyring’s own record.

What to watch for

  • Nothing outside the payload type and the payload is authenticated. A message that must not be replayed carries a timestamp or a nonce, and a message for one recipient names it;
  • a process on 0.30 refuses a record that holds a MAC version, so move every process to 0.31 before the keyring gets its first MAC version.

Full example available here.

Log JSON payloads without leaking data

A webhook handler or a queue consumer receives JSON from outside, and some of it does not parse: a sender cut the body short, a proxy answered with an HTML page, a field changed its type. The log line that explains the failure needs the payload, and the payload holds emails, phone numbers and amounts that a log must not keep. RedactedJson writes an outline of the bytes instead: the shape of the JSON, with every value hidden unless the application opts into showing it. It does no encryption and needs no KMS, so it works for any bytes and any logger. kms-aead builds without features for it, which is the default.

Outline instead of the payload

RedactedJson::new::<P>(bytes) takes the bytes and a policy P, a type that says what the outline shows. ShapeOnly shows nothing but the shape. Display and Debug both write the outline and never the bytes, so the value goes to format!, to tracing as a field, to log or to a logger of the application’s own.

use kms_aead::redaction::{RedactedJson, ShapeOnly};

fn reject(body: &[u8]) {
    if let Err(error) = serde_json::from_slice::<serde_json::Value>(body) {
        eprintln!(
            "WARN webhook payload rejected ({:?}): {}",
            error.classify(),
            RedactedJson::new::<ShapeOnly>(body)
        );
    }
}
let body = br#"{"event":"order.paid","customer":{"email":"ann@example.com"},"paid":true,"total_cents":117"#;
reject(body);
assert_eq!(
    RedactedJson::new::<ShapeOnly>(body).to_string(),
    "{<key>: <string>, <key>: {<key>: <string>}, <key>: <bool>, <key>: <number>, <error: EOF while parsing an object>"
);

A body that stops inside total_cents logs this line:

WARN webhook payload rejected (Eof): {<key>: <string>, <key>: {<key>: <string>}, <key>: <bool>, <key>: <number>, <error: EOF while parsing an object>

The log shows the type of every value, <key> for every key, the nesting, and where serde_json stopped reading. error.classify() gives the category of the failure, Eof, Syntax, Data or Io. Log that, and not the message of serde_json::Error, which for a value of the wrong type can quote the input.

Choose what the outline shows

A policy is a type of the application’s own that implements JsonRedaction and sets the constants it needs. Each constant is off by default:

ConstantEffect on the outline
FIELD_NAMESshows the keys of objects, except the keys of DATA_KEYED_MAPS;
DATA_KEYED_MAPSlists the objects whose keys are data, such as a map from an email to a balance, so their keys stay hidden; balances[] names the values of the map balances;
SIZESshows the length of every hidden value and key, the length of the JSON and the line and column of the failure;
REVEALED_FIELDSshows the values of the listed fields, and the names of the fields on the way to each.
 
use kms_aead::redaction::{FieldPaths, JsonRedaction, RedactedJson};

struct WebhookLog;

impl JsonRedaction for WebhookLog {
    const FIELD_NAMES: bool = true;
    const REVEALED_FIELDS: FieldPaths = FieldPaths::from_static(&["event", "items[].sku"]);
}
let body = br#"{"event":"order.paid","customer":{"email":"ann@example.com"},"items":[{"sku":"desk","qty":2},{"sku":"lamp","qty":1}],"total_cents":117"#;
let outline = RedactedJson::new::<WebhookLog>(body);
assert_eq!(
    outline.to_string(),
    r#"{"event": "order.paid", "customer": {"email": <string>}, "items": [{"sku": "desk", "qty": <number>}, {"sku": "lamp", "qty": <number>}], "total_cents": <number>, <error: EOF while parsing an object>"#
);

The outline of the same kind of body:

{"event": "order.paid", "customer": {"email": <string>}, "items": [{"sku": "desk", "qty": <number>}, {"sku": "lamp", "qty": <number>}], "total_cents": <number>, <error: EOF while parsing an object>

A path is the names of the fields joined by ., with [] after a field for every item of the array it holds, such as customer.plan or items[].sku, and [].id for the items of an array at the top level. A path takes in the whole value at its end. A field whose name has a ., [ or ] cannot be named. A malformed path is a compile error in the build that uses the policy.

Revealed values go to the log whenever the JSON fails to parse. Reveal codes and identifiers, never personal data.

SIZES has a cost: a length gives away a hidden boolean, since true and false differ in length, and it narrows a short string down. Leave it off for any payload where that matters.

Input that is not JSON

Broken, truncated and non-JSON input is outlined as far as it goes, and the outline never panics. An HTML error page from a proxy and a base64 string print as their class only, and a syntax error in the middle ends the outline there, with serde_json’s fixed message:

not JSON: text
not JSON: base64-like text
{"event": "order.paid", "a": <error: expected value>

The classes of bytes that do not start as JSON are empty, whitespace only, hex digits, base64-like text, text and binary data.

Limits

  • The outline is one line of printable ASCII, 4 KiB at most, whatever the input;
  • a revealed string is escaped, with ", \, <, > and every character outside printable ASCII written as JSON escapes, and cut after 64 bytes;
  • the items of an array with the shape of the item before them print as a count, such as <2 more of the same shape>, and an array prints 8 items of different shapes at most;
  • serde_json reads 128 levels of nesting at most.

JsonEncoded<T, P> applies the same policies to the JSON that fails to decode after an encrypted value is opened, see JSON that does not decode. The event it logs is in Observability.

Writers that can never read

A partner posts documents, a device reports readings, an upload client sends files. Each encrypts what it sends and has no way to decrypt it again, whatever its credentials leak. An asymmetric KEK is a KMS key pair: the public half encrypts the DEKs and is not a secret, the private half stays in the KMS and decrypts them for the readers. See Asymmetric KEKs.

The three sides

SideHoldsDoes
Producerthe public key and its fingerprint, in its configurationencrypts values; no KMS client, no KMS right
Readera KMS provider, with decrypt on the keydecrypts values, as for any KEK
Administrationthe public key’s source, read right on the keyhands the public key to the producers
  1. Create an RSA key for asymmetric decryption in the KMS, 3072 bits, with the algorithm RSA-OAEP SHA-256. Cloud KMS, AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault and Vault Transit offer it;
  2. grant the readers the decrypt right and the administration the right to read the public key, see the rights per KMS;
  3. the administration fetches the public key on its provider, writes to_json() and the key’s fingerprint to the producers’ configuration;
  4. each producer builds a PublicKeyKekProvider from them and an envelope over it.

The producer

The fingerprint comes from a channel other than the one that delivered the key. pinned refuses a key with another fingerprint with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_MISMATCH, so a key taken from a wrong place does not become a key that encrypts for whoever supplied it:

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::PublicKeyKekProvider;
use kms_aead::{AsymmetricKekPublicKey, Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope, AsymmetricKekFingerprint};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider, LocalKekId};
use kms_aead::AsymmetricKekAlgorithm;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let reader_provider = LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider::<AwsLc>::ephemeral(
    LocalKekId::try_from("submissions".to_string())?,
    AsymmetricKekAlgorithm::RsaOaepSha256,
)?;
let public_key = reader_provider.public_key(None)?;
let public_key_json = public_key.to_json()?;
let fingerprint_from_config = public_key.fingerprint().to_string();
let public_key = AsymmetricKekPublicKey::try_from_json(&public_key_json)?;
let fingerprint = AsymmetricKekFingerprint::try_from(fingerprint_from_config)?;
let producer = KmsAeadEnvelope::<_, AwsLc>::new(PublicKeyKekProvider::<AwsLc>::pinned(
    public_key,
    fingerprint,
)?)?;

let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> = producer
    .encrypt(b"submissions/1042", &SecretValue::from("account 1234"))
    .await?;

// The reader's side: the provider it uses today
let reader =
    KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider<AwsLc>, AwsLc>::new(reader_provider)?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = reader.decrypt(b"submissions/1042", &encrypted).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("account 1234"));
Ok(())
}

Encrypt one test value and decrypt it with a reader before a new producer writes anything that matters. The provider logs the fingerprint at INFO when it is built, which an operator compares with the administration’s.

What the compiler guarantees

PublicKeyKekProvider encrypts and does not decrypt. Every call that decrypts a value or a DEK, decrypt, beacon, the stream decryptors and the *_with_encrypted_dek calls, requires a decrypting provider, so over a producer none of them compiles. A producer streams as well: an envelope over it encrypts a large file with stream_encryptor and decrypts none, see Large files.

The guarantee covers the producer’s calls into the library. A producer process holds the plaintext DEKs of its cached writes, and its own code can read what it wrote in memory. A service that writes and reads holds a KMS provider and is a reader.

With a keyring

The administration opens its registry with an AsymmetricKekProvider, which encrypts each new version’s DEK with the public key, and the applications open theirs with the plain KMS provider. Whoever holds the public key can also encrypt a DEK of their own and file it in a record they can write, and every application then decrypts it. Run every keyring under an asymmetric KEK with signed records, and RecordSignaturePolicy::Required on every application, before the first DEK a public key encrypts, see Protect the keyring record.

What to watch for

  • The producer encrypts randomised values only. A beacon or a deterministic value needs a key’s plaintext, which only a reader holds;
  • Ring has no RSA encryption: a producer on it fails at construction with UNSUPPORTED_ALGORITHM. Use AwsLc or RustCrypto;
  • the encryption is local, so the KMS audit log no longer shows who encrypted a DEK. The decrypt log stays;
  • anyone holding the public key makes a reader pay for an asymmetric KMS decryption by sending a made-up value. Rate-limit what readers decrypt;
  • a rotation of the KEK is a new public key for every producer;
  • RSA is not quantum-safe, so data that must stay confidential for decades stays under a symmetric KEK.

Full example available here.

Protect the keyring record

A keyring record decides which key versions read and which encrypt. Without a signature, its integrity rests on the access control of the keyring store: whoever can write the record can re-activate a compromised version, clear a flag or point a version at another DEK. Under an asymmetric KEK, which encrypts for anyone holding its public key, a writer can also plant a DEK of its own. A signed record closes these: the administration signs every record it writes, and every application refuses a record that no key it trusts has signed. See Signing the keyring record.

Roll out

An existing deployment moves in this order, and each step is safe to deploy before the next:

  1. Administrative binaries on 0.31. A 0.30 administrative write removes the signature;
  2. the signing key’s public half to every registry, as VerifyIfSigned(keys). Records without a signature still read, and a signed one verifies from its first write;
  3. a signing administration, and sign_record on every keyring;
  4. Required on every application and on the administration.

The keys come from the application’s configuration, a JWK Set or the public key of the signing KMS key. They never come from the store or the record:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider, LocalSigningKeyName, LocalSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signer, SigningAlgorithm, VerifyingKeySet};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, RecordSignaturePolicy,
};

const TENANT: KeyringId = KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42");

fn local_kek() -> Result<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    Ok(LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenants".to_string())?)?)
}
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let store = MemoryKeyringStore::new();
let before = KeyringRegistry::<_, Ring>::new(store.clone(), KeyringRegistryOptions::new())?;
before.create(local_kek()?, TENANT, KeyringAlgorithms::new()).await?;
// A KMS signing provider in production, see "Publish content anyone can verify"
let signer = Signer::<_, Ring>::new(LocalSigningProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(
    LocalSigningKeyName::try_from("records".to_string())?,
    SigningAlgorithm::Ed25519,
)?)
.await?;
let keys = VerifyingKeySet::new([signer.verifying_key().clone()])?;

// Step 2: the administration's registry trusts the key and still reads unsigned records
let administration = KeyringRegistry::<_, Ring>::new(
    store.clone(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new()
        .with_record_signatures(RecordSignaturePolicy::VerifyIfSigned(keys.clone())),
)?;

// Step 3: sign the record as it is, with no other change
let admin = administration
    .admin(local_kek()?, TENANT)
    .await?
    .with_record_signer(signer)?;
admin.sign_record().await?;

// Step 4: an application that refuses a record no trusted key has signed
let application = KeyringRegistry::<_, Ring>::new(
    store,
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new().with_record_signatures(RecordSignaturePolicy::Required(keys)),
)?;
application.open(local_kek()?, TENANT).await?;
before.shutdown(None).await;
administration.shutdown(None).await;
application.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

A new keyring is signed from its first write with create_signed or open_or_create_signed. The administration re-signs the record on every write it makes: rotate, promote, shred and the rest need the signer’s KMS right, cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToSign on Cloud KMS.

Rotate the signing key

Add the new public key to every registry’s set first, then run sign_record on every keyring with the new signer. Remove the old key from the sets once every keyring is signed by the new one. The rotation of the key itself is that of Publish content anyone can verify.

What a signature closes

  • a store writer without the signing key cannot change a record that applications verify: a cleared compromised flag, a re-activated version or a DEK planted under an asymmetric KEK fails with RECORD_SIGNATURE_INVALID, before any field of the record is used and before any KMS call;
  • under Required, an unsigned record fails with RECORD_UNSIGNED, so a keyring that was never signed, or whose signature a writer stripped, is not read.

The sticky refusal and what only Required closes

Under VerifyIfSigned, a running process that holds a signed record refuses a later unsigned one with RECORD_SIGNATURE_REMOVED, keeps the record it holds and logs an ERROR. The refusal lasts as long as the process, and a process that starts after a writer stripped the signature has no record to compare with and reads whatever the store holds. Only Required protects those processes, and the keyring whose record was never signed. Run Required on every application once step 3 is done.

A signature proves that a signer wrote the record, and not that it is the latest. A store writer can restore an older signed record. Running processes refuse a record that moves a version backwards with RECORD_REGRESSED, and a process that has just started has nothing to compare with. Keep backups and the store’s version history to compare against, see Keyring deployment.

Stop signing

Switch every reader to VerifyIfSigned, write the record unsigned with dangerously_unsign_record on an administration without a signer, then restart the readers: the running ones refuse the unsigned record until they do.

What to watch for

  • Whoever may sign records and write the store may write any record. Give the signing right to the administration only, and read its audit log;
  • a keyring under an asymmetric KEK runs with Required on every application before the first DEK a public key encrypts, see Writers that can never read;
  • an administration without a signer is refused over a signed record with RECORD_SIGNER_MISSING.

Full example available here.

Rotate keys and re-encrypt values

Two keys rotate, on their own schedules:

  • a DEK encrypts values. A new DEK changes nothing already written, so the values under the old one are re-encrypted by a sweep before the old DEK is retired;
  • the KEK encrypts DEKs in the KMS. A new KEK version changes no value: the DEKs are encrypted again under it before the old version is disabled.

Both run without downtime, since the old key keeps decrypting until one of the last steps retires or disables it, and a check runs before that step. This page walks through each rotation and links to the procedure of each step. How often to rotate is in Rotation cadence.

DEK rotation

On a keyring, a DEK rotation is a new key version of a purpose, staged before it encrypts anything. The administrative job runs every call on its KeyringAdmin, and the application runs the sweep, see Applications and administration. In order:

  1. stage and promote the new version: rotate, export the keysets again, wait until every process has the pending version, promote. The steps and the waits are in Rotation cadence, and why the wait is needed in Staged rotation;
  2. sweep the records whose encryption marker is below the keyring’s current one. They are the records that can hold a value under an older version, and records with no value of the rotated purpose, whose re-encryption writes the marker alone, see Sweeps. The query and the write per database:
    • Firestore;
    • PostgreSQL;
    • BigQuery, in SQL;
    • beacons and deterministic fields: Rotating beacon versions and Queries across rotations;
  3. retire, then destroy the old version, once every process has refreshed since the promotion and no record is left under it, see Retiring the old version.

The rotate example runs the whole cycle on the memory store and the local KMS: cargo run -p kms-aead-keyring --example rotate --features ring,admin.

Why nothing stops

  • A pending version decrypts in every process that has seen it and encrypts nothing, so by the promotion every process that reads a value under it already knows it;
  • a process that has not taken the promotion yet encrypts under the previous version, which stays DecryptOnly, so its values decrypt everywhere;
  • the sweep reads a value under the old version and writes it under the new one, conditional on the record as it read it, so a write of the application in between is never overwritten;
  • retire is the first step that refuses a read, and reinstate undoes it.

What can go wrong

  • A process left behind by a promotion meets a value under the new version and reloads its record, see Staged rotation;
  • a re-encryption that fails is counted and logged, and some failures repeat in every run, see Running a sweep;
  • a retire too early refuses a value the sweep missed, see Retiring the old version;
  • a stale keyset misses the values under the new version in SQL, see Read values in BigQuery or Tink;
  • a destroy is final, apart from the copies of the DEK that backups of the record and earlier keyset exports keep, see Crypto-shredding.

What to alert on

  • “The keyring record could not be revalidated”, from the rotation until the old version is retired: a process that logs it is one a promotion can leave behind, and one that can still encrypt under the old version;
  • error.type KEY_VERSION_UNKNOWN on kms_aead.operation.duration, a read that met a version its process could not load, and KEY_VERSION_RETIRED once the old version is retired;
  • SweepReport::failed above zero, and the sweep’s WARN per record;
  • the values and bytes per version, which say when the next rotation is due, see Alerting on volume.

The metrics need the opentelemetry feature, see Metrics.

KEK rotation

A KEK rotation is a new version of the key in the KMS. In order:

  1. rotate the KEK in the KMS, as its provider page describes under “Key versions and rotation”: Google Cloud KMS, AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault, Vault Transit or the local KMS;
  2. re-encrypt, check, disable, check again and destroy, as in Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation. A keyring’s part, reencrypt_deks and kek_versions_in_use, is in Rotating the KEK, with a sample.

Why nothing stops

No value changes, and the old KEK version decrypts every DEK until it is disabled. The disable waits until no process holds an encrypted DEK from before the re-encryption, so by then every process reads through DEKs under the new version. A DEK the re-encryption missed fails in the application from the disable on, once its DEK cache entry expires, and the check after the disable finds it. Enabling the old version again restores every DEK under it, at once under Cloud KMS, see Google Cloud KMS.

The KMS decrypt burst

reencrypt_deks gives every DEK new encrypted bytes, and the DEK cache keys its entries by those bytes. Each process that takes the refreshed record evicts the old entries, so its next call under each version decrypts that version’s DEK through the KMS again: one KMS decryption per process, per keyring and per version in use, within the bound of How late a change is seen after each reencrypt_deks. With a keyring per tenant, re-encrypt the keyrings in batches below the KMS’s request quota, with room for that burst, see Pacing. A sweep or an application over the quota fails with the KMS’s error, such as ResourceExhausted on Google Cloud KMS or ThrottlingException on AWS KMS, both a NetworkError.

What can go wrong

  • An early re-encryption or check under Cloud KMS, inside the propagation of the new primary version or of the disable, see Google Cloud KMS;
  • a pinned Azure version re-encrypts under the old version, see Azure Key Vault;
  • an early disable fails the reads of a process that still holds the old encrypted DEKs, see The check;
  • Vault Transit’s min_decryption_version disables every version below it at once, see Rotating the KEK;
  • AWS KMS key material cannot be disabled when generated, and leaves the key unusable when imported material is deleted or expires, see AWS KMS;
  • a destroyed old version makes every backup taken before the re-encryption unreadable.

What to alert on

  • “The keyring record could not be revalidated”, from the re-encryption until the disable: a process that logs it keeps the old encrypted DEKs, and its reads fail after the disable once its DEK cache entries expire;
  • error.type on kms_aead.kms.duration with kms_aead.operation decrypt_dek, after the disable: a DEK the re-encryption missed, or a process that still holds old bytes;
  • the rate of kms_aead.kms.duration with decrypt_dek, and ResourceExhausted or ThrottlingException in its error.type, during the burst;
  • an error_code on the Keyring re-encrypt DEKs span of the administrative job.

With kms-aead alone

DEKs

Without a keyring there is no version to stage, and the DEK rotates with the mode:

  • per-call envelope: every value has a DEK of its own, so there is no DEK to rotate;
  • store-less DEK reuse: the envelope renews its DEK by age, count and bytes, as its DekReusePolicy sets, and the values keep their encrypted DEK, so the renewal is the rotation and needs no sweep, see Store-less DEK reuse;
  • one fixed key, a deterministic key or a beacon key the application keeps: a second key under a second key id, both kept for reads, and a re-index of the values or beacons under the old one, see One fixed key and Rotating a beacon key.

A DEK that leaked keeps decrypting every value encrypted under it, whatever the KEK. To retire it:

  1. stop encrypting under it: a store-less writer renews its DEK after invalidate of that DEK, or clear, on its envelope’s DEK cache, in every process, see The DEK lives in the cache;
  2. read each value under it with decrypt and encrypt it again with encrypt, which takes a new DEK, and write it back only where the stored bytes are still the ones read;
  3. treat backups and exports that hold the old values as exposed, see What rotation does not protect.

The KEK

The KEK rotates in the KMS, and before the old version is disabled, the DEKs stored in the values and apart from them are encrypted again under its new version with reencrypt_encrypted_dek, once per distinct encrypted DEK, and the application’s own encrypted keysets through the provider. No value is decrypted. The procedure, when the sweep starts, its pacing, the checks and a rollback are in Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation, and the alerts are those of the keyring’s KEK rotation above, on kms_aead.kms.duration.

A leaked key or a shredded tenant

The emergency runbook has the steps for a keyring. This page is the entry point, with the cases kms-aead alone adds.

With a keyring

What happenedRunbook
A plaintext DEK leaked, or may haveA plaintext DEK leaked
A principal got KMS rights it should not haveA KMS IAM over-grant
An encrypted DEK or a keyring record leakedAn encrypted DEK or the keyring record leaked
A refused record moving backwards, logged at ERROR, or a version the job did not createA version the administrative job did not create
A keyring record was deleted or corruptedThe keyring record was deleted or corrupted
The KMS is downA KMS outage
A tenant’s values must become unreadableCrypto-shredding, and dangerously_shred() under a shared KEK

A leaked DEK of envelope values

A DEK that leaked decrypts every value encrypted under it, and a KEK rotation does not change that. With a per-call envelope it is the DEK of one value, and with store-less DEK reuse of every value the writer encrypted until it renewed the DEK. The values are re-encrypted under new DEKs, as in With kms-aead alone, and the DEK is invalidated in every DEK cache that holds it.

A DEK the application keeps itself, such as the key of One fixed key or a beacon key, is replaced by a key under a new key id, and the values or beacons under the old one are encrypted or computed again.

Backups, point-in-time recovery and data exports still hold the values the leaked DEK decrypts: treat them as exposed.

Crypto-shredding

A scope’s values become unreadable once nothing can decrypt their DEKs. With kms-aead alone, the DEKs are encrypted by the KEK, so shredding is the destruction of the KEK’s versions that encrypted them, backups included. Per KMS:

  • Google Cloud KMS: destroy the key versions. Cloud KMS schedules the destruction, 30 days later by default, and until then a version can be restored, see Google Cloud KMS;
  • AWS KMS: delete the key. Rotated key material is never destroyed on its own, see AWS KMS;
  • Azure Key Vault: delete the key, which deletes it with all its versions: Key Vault never deletes one version alone, see Azure Key Vault. Soft delete and purge protection delay the shredding, see Crypto-shredding;
  • Vault Transit: min_available_version and a trim of the key delete the versions below it, see Vault Transit, and a deletion of the key, once its configuration sets deletion_allowed, deletes every version;
  • local KMS: destroy every copy of the KEK version, backups included, see Local KMS.

Since a deletion of the key takes every version, shredding one scope under AWS KMS or Azure Key Vault needs a key per scope. With a keyring, a shared KEK has a second route, dangerously_shred(): it destroys every version of the keyring and deletes its record from the store, with the earlier versions the store keeps. The keys stay in every copy the store keeps beyond its API, such as a backup, and in every keyset exported from the keyring, see Crypto-shredding. A deployment that needs shredding guarantees uses a KEK per scope, see A keyring per tenant.

Shredding a tenant with dangerously_shred()

Under a shared KEK, KeyringAdmin::dangerously_shred() ends the tenant’s keyring in one call: it destroys every version and deletes the record from the store. The call can be repeated after a failure, and returns Ok on a keyring that is gone already. The keyring id is free for a new keyring afterwards.

  • Every process that holds the keyring takes the deletion at its next revalidation, within seconds with push. Its handles fail with NoActiveVersion for a write and KeyVersionDestroyed for a read, and the plaintext DEK leaves its memory with that revalidation;
  • a copy of the record from before the shred is refused by a process that took the deletion, with RecordRegressed and an ERROR, because it revives versions the process holds destroyed. A store writer who restores an old record gains nothing against that process;
  • after a deliberate restore, such as one that undoes an accidental deletion, restart every process that took the deletion. A process that starts after the restore takes the record;
  • dangerously_shred() leaves the store’s backups, its history and every exported keyset where they are, and the KMS untouched. They still decrypt while a KEK version that encrypted their DEKs exists, see What survives a shred.

Full example available here.

Applications and administration apart

A keyring deployment has two sides, which build, run and are granted differently. The concepts and the rights tables are in Applications and administration; this page is the checklist of each side, and what to alert on in production.

The application

  • Builds without the admin feature, with cargo build -p <application>, since Cargo unifies the features of every package one command builds;
  • runs as a principal with KMS decrypt on the KEK and read access to the keyring store, listen included, see KMS rights and Store rights;
  • where records are signed, opens its registry with RecordSignaturePolicy::Required and the public key of the record signer, from its configuration and never from the store, see Protect the keyring record;
  • calls KeyringRegistry::open, and on the Keyring it returns encryptor, decrypt and the probes, and runs the sweeps;
  • awaits registry.shutdown(None) before it exits.

The administration

  • Builds with the admin feature, and bigquery-export where it exports BigQuery keysets;
  • runs in a job or in infrastructure automation, never in a request handler, as the only principal with KMS encrypt on the KEK and write access to the keyring store;
  • holds the record signer where records are signed: a Signer on a KMS signing key, with the right to sign and no other principal holding it, passed to create_signed, open_or_create_signed or with_record_signer, see Protect the keyring record;
  • calls create or open_or_create for each new keyring, and on the KeyringAdmin the lifecycle calls, reencrypt_deks and the keyset exports, see Key versions and lifecycle;
  • logs every call it makes, since a version its log does not show is the start of A version the administrative job did not create;
  • opens its registry with the longest record_ttl in the fleet, which the promotion guard waits.

An application that creates keyrings lazily, such as one per tenant on its first request, is both sides at once, see A keyring per tenant.

What to alert on

The events are logged through tracing, see Keyring events and Events, and the metrics need the opentelemetry feature, see Metrics.

SignalWhat it meansNext step
