Without a keyring, the rows of kms-aead apply: error.type on kms_aead.operation.duration and kms_aead.kms.duration, the KMS audit log, and the WARN of old layouts.

Choose a provider and a backend

A build makes two choices that do not constrain each other: the KMS that holds the KEK, and the crypto backend that runs the AEAD algorithms. Both are Cargo features, see Cargo features.

KMS providers

The KMS decides who else reads the values, what a rotation takes and how a scope is crypto-shredded:

Google Cloud KMSAWS KMSAzure Key VaultVault TransitLocal KMS
Tink reads envelope valuesyesyesnonono
Tink reads keyring keysetsyesyes, with a key named by its ARNnoTink for Go onlyno
BigQuery readsyesnononono
KMS random generatoryes, at the HSM protection levelyesnonono
KEK version on each encrypted DEKyesno, Noneyesyesyes
Disabling an old KEK versionyes, eventually consistentno, for key material AWS KMS generatedyesyes, with min_decryption_versionyes, by dropping it from the list
Crypto-shredding a scopedestroying its KEK versionsdeleting the keydeleting the key, with all its versionsdeleting its key versionsdestroying every copy of the KEK

Notes on the table:

  • Tink reads envelope values with KmsEnvelopeAead and its own KMS client, see Tink’s KMS envelope, and keyring keysets with KeysetHandle.read, see Exporting a keyset to Tink;
  • BigQuery’s KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN decrypts with Cloud KMS only, see Read values in BigQuery or Tink;
  • the KEK version decides how a KEK rotation is checked before the old version is disabled, see Rotate keys and re-encrypt values. Each provider page has its rotation, under “Key versions and rotation”: Google Cloud KMS, AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault, Vault Transit and Local KMS;
  • with an RSA key on Azure Key Vault the encryption is a public-key operation, so whoever holds the public key encrypts a DEK of their own into a keyring record. Use an AES key from Managed HSM, with algorithm set to AzureKmsAlgorithm::Aes256KeyWrap, see Azure Key Vault and Keyring limits;
  • the local KMS has no IAM, no audit log and no HSM, see Local KMS;
  • crypto-shredding per KMS is in A leaked key or a shredded tenant; a keyring under a shared KEK is shredded with dangerously_shred(), see Shredding a keyring.

The TLS root store and the TLS crypto provider are a feature choice for Google Cloud KMS, Azure and Vault Transit, with the rules in Feature rules.

Crypto backends

ringaws-lcaws-lc-fipsrustcrypto
AES-256-GCM-SIVnoyesyesyes
RSA-OAEP encryption, for a producer under an asymmetric KEKnoyesyesyes
FIPS modulenonoyesno
Needs a C toolchainyesyesyes, with CMake and Gono
A second crypto library beside a cloud provideryesnonoyes
Key schedules zeroisednoyes, by AWS-LC’s allocatoryes, by AWS-LC’s allocatoryes

Every backend has ChaCha20-Poly1305, AES-128-GCM and AES-256-GCM, writes the same bytes, and decrypts the values of any other, so a move from one backend to another needs no migration of the data, see Cross-backend compatibility.

  • Producers under an asymmetric KEK: a producer that encrypts DEKs with a public key fails at construction with UNSUPPORTED_ALGORITHM on ring, which has no RSA encryption. Readers need nothing of RSA on any backend, see Writers that can never read;
  • No second crypto library: the default features of the four cloud providers already build aws-lc-rs for TLS, so aws-lc adds nothing to a provider build;
  • FIPS: aws-lc-fips runs the backend on the AWS-LC FIPS module and moves every user of aws-lc-rs in the build to it, the rustls provider included. AES-256-GCM is the algorithm for a FIPS deployment. AES-SIV, the deterministic algorithm, is not FIPS-approved and runs on RustCrypto whatever the backend, so deterministic fields stay outside the validated boundary, see aws-lc-rs and FIPS;
  • No C toolchain: rustcrypto is pure Rust, with getrandom as the CSPRNG;
  • Zeroisation: what each backend leaves in freed memory is in Crypto backends.

A build may enable several backends at once, and code generic over the backend bounds it with B: AeadBackend and nothing else, see Code generic over the backend.

Develop and test without a cloud

The local KMS, the memory store and local servers for the other stores run the same code paths as production, with no cloud account. What each one keeps, and for how long, decides where it fits.

PieceForKeeps its keys
LocalKmsProvider::ephemeraltestsfor the life of the provider
LocalKmsProvider::new with LocalKek::from_env or from_filea development databaseas long as the variable or the file
MemoryKeyringStoretestsfor the life of the process
a Firestore emulator, a local PostgreSQL or an S3-compatible servera development keyring storeas long as the server’s data

The KMS

  • Tests: LocalKmsProvider::ephemeral has one random KEK that lives as long as the provider. Every DEK it encrypts, and so every value, is unreadable once the process exits, see Tests and development;
  • a development environment whose data outlives a run keeps a KEK in a variable or a file, with LocalKek::from_env or LocalKek::from_file, see the same section. A value written with the local KMS never decrypts under a cloud KMS, so a development database starts again empty on the move to one.

The provider is in every build, with no feature, and logs its KEK id and where each version came from at INFO when it is created.

The keyring store

  • Tests: MemoryKeyringStore, in kms_aead_keyring::testing, loses its records, and with them every DEK, when the process exits. A test that creates keyrings in it enables admin in [dev-dependencies] only, see Applications and administration;

  • Firestore: the emulator of the Google Cloud CLI, gcloud emulators firestore start, and FIRESTORE_EMULATOR_HOST set to its address. FirestoreDb::new connects to the emulator while the variable is set, so the store needs no change. The sweep_firestore example runs on it:

    docker run --rm -d --name kms-aead-firestore -p 8687:8687 \
  gcr.io/google.com/cloudsdktool/google-cloud-cli:emulators \
  gcloud emulators firestore start --host-port=0.0.0.0:8687
FIRESTORE_EMULATOR_HOST=127.0.0.1:8687 PROJECT_ID=kms-aead-emulator \
  cargo run -p kms-aead-keyring --example sweep_firestore --features ring,admin,firestore-tls-roots

  • PostgreSQL: a local server, the table of PostgreSQL, and a connection with NoTls. The sweep_postgres example reads its connection string from KMS_AEAD_KEYRING_POSTGRES_URL;

  • S3: an S3-compatible server such as MinIO, once it passes the store conformance suite, see S3-compatible servers.

Cloud Storage has no local server in the store’s tests.

A first run

The local_kms example creates a keyring on the memory store with a local KEK, rotates it and reads a value written before the rotation: cargo run -p kms-aead-keyring --example local_kms --features ring,admin. The same with kms-aead alone is in With an envelope.

Limits

  • The local KMS has no IAM and no audit log, and the KEK in a variable stays in the process environment, see Local KMS;
  • values encrypted under a local KEK are not exported to BigQuery, which decrypts with Cloud KMS only.

Move envelope values to a keyring

Values that kms-aead’s KmsAeadEnvelope wrote, per call or store-less, move to keyring values in five steps:

  1. the model gains encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker, and every record without one reads as EncryptionMarker::ENVELOPE: through #[serde(default)], or a column default of its bytes, '\x0100000000';
  2. the field keeps its stored bytes, whose layout the marker names: envelope values under ENVELOPE, epoch 0, and keyring values under any other marker, since the epochs of a keyring start at 1;
  3. reads go through the marker: Keyring::decrypt_envelope_for_migration decrypts an envelope value, with the AAD and the algorithm the application used for it, and Keyring::decrypt a keyring value;
  4. the sweep’s query, encryption_marker < $1 with the current marker, finds every envelope record too, and the re-encryption encrypts it as keyring values through an encryptor and writes the marker of the encryptor’s record;
  5. once no record holds ENVELOPE, the read path drops its envelope branch.

A range query never matches a record whose marker is missing: Firestore skips a document without the field, and < in SQL is never true on NULL. A PostgreSQL column added with a default holds it in every existing row:

ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN encryption_marker BYTEA NOT NULL DEFAULT '\x0100000000';
CREATE INDEX users_encryption_marker ON users (encryption_marker);

A BigQuery column holds NULL in the rows already there until an UPDATE writes ENVELOPE into them:

ALTER TABLE users ADD COLUMN encryption_marker BYTES;
ALTER TABLE users ALTER COLUMN encryption_marker SET DEFAULT b'\x01\x00\x00\x00\x00';
UPDATE users SET encryption_marker = b'\x01\x00\x00\x00\x00' WHERE encryption_marker IS NULL;

The UPDATE in SQL of Sweeps on BigQuery does not decrypt an envelope value, so its WHERE leaves the envelope rows out with AND encryption_marker > b'\x01\x00\x00\x00\x00', and they go through the Rust sweep.

Firestore documents written before the marker get ENVELOPE written into them first, or the migration’s first sweep scans the whole collection and tests doc.encryption_marker < current on each, since #[serde(default)] reads a missing marker as ENVELOPE.

The sweep’s re-encryption branches on the marker. An envelope value is decrypted with the algorithm the application used for it and encrypted as a keyring value; a keyring value goes through Encryptor::reencrypt, and ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted) turns its Option into the outcome. An envelope value names no key version, so its outcome has from_compromised: false:

let (email, outcome) = match StoredEmail::try_from(row)? {
    StoredEmail::Envelope(value) => {
        let email: Email = keyring
            .decrypt_envelope_for_migration(&envelope_aad(user), &value, ENVELOPE_ALGORITHM)
            .await?;
        let outcome = ReencryptOutcome::Reencrypted { from_compromised: false };
        (encryptor.encrypt(&aad, &email).await?, outcome)
    }
    StoredEmail::Keyring { value, .. } => {
        let reencrypted = encryptor.reencrypt(&aad, &value).await?;
        let outcome = ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted);
        (reencrypted.map_or(value, |reencrypted| reencrypted.value), outcome)
    }
};

The keyring’s provider decrypts the envelope values, so they must have been encrypted by the keyring’s KEK. decrypt_envelope_for_migration reads Tink’s KMS envelope, the layout kms-aead 0.30 writes. Values written by kms-aead 0.29 or earlier are upgraded in place first, with KmsAeadEnvelope::upgrade_legacy_value(aad, value) under kms-aead’s legacy-formats feature, see Upgrading stored values. The layout of a value is always the marker’s, never inferred from its bytes.

decrypt_envelope_for_migration decrypts whatever encrypted DEK an envelope value carries, under the keyring’s KEK. It knows no key version, and so no compromised flag and no destroyed state: keep its branch in the read path for the migration only.

Full example available here, run it with cargo run -p kms-aead-keyring --example migrate --features ring,admin.

Migrating from 0.29

0.30 replaces the ring-only API with a choice of crypto backends, adds store-less DEK reuse, the DEK cache, deterministic encryption and the local KMS, and makes ring optional. It writes Tink’s KMS envelope format for every provider, so values written by 0.29 and earlier read only with the deprecated legacy-formats feature, which also rewrites them in the new format, see Old values. The default algorithm of the envelope is AES-256-GCM, see Default algorithm.

0.30 also adds what 0.29 had no counterpart for, so nothing below maps to it:

  • typed fields: Encrypted<T, F> takes any type that implements Plaintext, such as an application newtype, where the tables below name SecretValue, see Stored values;
  • beacons, for lookups of randomised fields, see Beacons;
  • the kms-aead-keyring crate, with key versions kept in a store, staged rotation and sweeps, see Keyring and field encryption. Envelope values move to it with a sweep, see Move envelope values to a keyring.

Features

ring is no longer turned on by the provider features. A 0.29 build that relied on it fails with “kms-aead needs a crypto backend for its KMS providers”, so add a backend feature. aws-lc adds no second crypto library to a cloud provider build, see Choose a provider and a backend.

The crate declares rust-version = "1.99".

HashiCorp Vault Transit types have the Hc prefix:

GcpKmsProviderOptions, AwsKmsProviderOptions and AzureKmsProviderOptions are #[non_exhaustive], so a struct literal of one fails to compile. Build them with new() and the with_* setters.

Traits replaced by inherent methods

AeadEncryption and KmsAeadEnvelopeEncryption are gone. Their methods are inherent methods of AeadCipher and KmsAeadEnvelope, so delete the trait imports. The AAD is any &(impl AsRef<[u8]> + ?Sized), like 0.29’s Aad: AsRef<[u8]>, so &aad still compiles, and the calls that make no KMS call are not async:

0.290.30
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelopeEncryption; envelope.encrypt_value(&aad, &value).await?envelope.encrypt(&aad, &value).await?
envelope.encrypt_value_with_dek(&aad, &value, &dek).await?envelope.encrypt_with_dek(&aad, &value, &dek)?
envelope.decrypt_value(&aad, &cipher_text).await?envelope.decrypt(&aad, &value).await?
envelope.decrypt_value_with_dek(&aad, &cipher_text, &dek).await?envelope.decrypt_with_dek(&aad, &bare, &dek)?
encrypt_value_with_encrypted_dek, decrypt_value_with_encrypted_dekencrypt_with_encrypted_dek, decrypt_with_encrypted_dek
KmsAeadEnvelopeEncryption::<Aad>::generate_new_dek(&envelope)envelope.generate_encrypted_dek()
use kms_aead::AeadEncryption; cipher.encrypt_value(&aad, &value, &key).await?cipher.encrypt_bare(&aad, &value, &key)?
cipher.decrypt_value(&aad, &cipher_text, &key).await?cipher.decrypt_bare(&aad, &bare, &key)?

Every cipher text is an Encrypted<T, F>, see Formats. It has its own value() and into_value(), and so has EncryptedDataEncryptionKey. DataEncryptionKey no longer implements ValueStruct, and has its own value().

Your own provider

An application’s own implementation of the provider trait:

  • implements KmsAeadEncryptionProvider instead of KmsAeadRingEncryptionProvider, without #[async_trait]. Its methods stay async fn, and their futures must be Send;
  • adds fn kek(&self) -> &KekRef, with KekProvider::Other, KmsProvider::Other from 0.31, and a key name that identifies the key;
  • deletes generate_encryption_key when it returned the backend’s key. The replacement, generate_key_material(&self, len), has a default implementation that lets the backend generate the key, and is implemented only for a KMS generator;
  • returns EncryptedDataEncryptionKey::new(bytes) from encrypt_data_encryption_key, with the KEK version the KMS reported added by with_kek_version. A KMS that needs the key version to decrypt gets it from those bytes, as the Azure provider’s do.

The plaintext the provider encrypts is the DEK serialised as a Tink key, see Formats. The library never inspects the provider’s own bytes.

The trait has no dyn form, so a Box<dyn KmsAeadRingEncryptionProvider> becomes a type parameter P: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, or an enum of the providers the application uses. The trait requires Send and Sync, so P: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider needs no + Send + Sync.

0.31 moves decrypt_data_encryption_key to a trait of its own, KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, see Migrating from 0.30.

Errors

KmsAeadError is #[non_exhaustive], so an exhaustive match on it needs a _ arm. It has two new variants, UnsupportedAlgorithm and LegacyFormat. code() returns the code of any variant, and source() returns the root cause.

KmsAeadSystemError, KmsAeadNetworkError, KmsAeadEncryptionError and the type of their public field, KmsAeadErrorPublicGenericDetails, are #[non_exhaustive] too, so a struct literal of one fails to compile, and a pattern on one needs ... Build the errors with create(code, message), or with new(public, message) and, where it has one, with_root_cause, and the details with KmsAeadErrorPublicGenericDetails::new(code).

The From conversions of SDK errors into KmsAeadError are gone: gcloud_sdk::error::Error, gcloud_sdk::tonic::Status, the AWS SdkError, the hyper client error, azure_core::Error and SystemTimeError. Map an SDK error in the application.

The variant of a Google Cloud KMS or AWS KMS error follows its code. In 0.29 every gRPC status was a NetworkError and every AWS error an EncryptionError. A PermissionDenied or NotFound from Google Cloud KMS is an EncryptionError now, and a timeout, a connection failure or a ThrottlingException from AWS KMS a NetworkError. The codes stay the same. See Retries.

ConstantTimeEq is no longer implemented on the public types. == still compares in constant time. The Debug of Encrypted prints only the length, and == is gone from randomised values: only an Encrypted<T, Deterministic> has it.

Error codes that changed:

0.290.30Where
INVALID_CIPHERTEXTINVALID_CIPHER_TEXT_FORMATEncrypted::try_from of a Bare value shorter than a nonce and a tag
ENCRYPTIONRANDOM_GENERATORa CSPRNG failure, for a nonce or a key
DECRYPT_KEYENCRYPT_KEYthe GCP encryption, when its request cannot be built
DECRYPT_KEYRANDOM_GENERATORthe GCP KMS generator, when its request cannot be built
SYSTEM_TIME_ERRORremoved
UNKNOWN_ENCRYPTED_KEY_LAYOUTremovedstored encrypted DEKs are the provider’s bytes
AZURE_KEY_VERSION_MISSINGAZURE_ENCRYPTED_DEK_FORMATthe Azure decryption of bytes outside its layout; the old code stays for a 0.29 value without a key version, with legacy-formats
AZURE_WRAP_ALGORITHMremovedAzureKmsAlgorithm has no variant for Rsa1_5 or an unknown algorithm

The other 0.29 codes keep their spelling. The new codes are listed in Error handling.

AWS KMS decryption names the key

The AWS provider sends the configured key as KeyId on every decryption. In 0.29 a decryption left it out, so AWS KMS decrypted under whichever key the encrypted DEK named, for any key the principal may decrypt with. A 0.30 process refuses a DEK encrypted under another key with IncorrectKeyException. A deployment that relied on decrypting under a second key configures a provider per key.

An alias name such as alias/orders becomes an alias ARN, where 0.29 built a key ARN of it. Configure the key ARN rather than an alias, see AWS KMS.

Default algorithm

The default algorithm of KmsAeadEnvelope is AES-256-GCM, which every Tink implementation reads in a KMS envelope. The default of 0.29 was ChaCha20-Poly1305.

A value does not record its algorithm. Values encrypted under the default of 0.29 decrypt only with ChaCha20-Poly1305 configured, and an envelope on the new default fails them with DECRYPT. A 0.29 envelope that named no algorithm becomes:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsKeyRef, GcpKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, KmsAeadEnvelope, KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions};

async fn example(key_ref: GcpKmsKeyRef) -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
// 0.29: KmsAeadRingEnvelopeEncryption::new(provider).await?
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<GcpKmsProvider, Ring>::with_options(
    GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref).await?,
    KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions::new().with_algorithm(AeadAlgorithm::ChaCha20Poly1305),
)?;
let _ = envelope;
Ok(())
}

Configure it on every envelope that reads those values, on the one that upgrades them, see Old values, and on every envelope that writes new values beside them: a reader cannot tell the two algorithms apart. AES-256-GCM suits a column that holds no 0.29 values.

Old values

0.30 writes Tink’s KMS envelope, and encrypts every DEK but Azure’s as a serialised Tink key. 0.29 wrote envelope layout v1 and the encrypted DEK layout v1 [0xFF][0x01]…, and 0.28.1 and earlier a length-prefixed envelope, all with the raw DEK encrypted. 0.30 reads them only with the deprecated legacy-formats feature, and fails with LEGACY_FORMAT without it, see Formats.

The envelopes that read and upgrade those values keep the algorithm they were encrypted with, see Default algorithm.

To move stored values to the new format:

  1. upgrade every process that reads the values to 0.30 with legacy-formats. 0.29 and earlier cannot read Tink’s format, so no process writes it before every reader has the upgrade;
  2. rewrite the stored values with upgrade_legacy_value and upgrade_legacy_encrypted_dek of KmsAeadEnvelope, one or two KMS calls per value, see Upgrading stored values. upgrade_legacy_value takes the value’s AAD, and fails with DECRYPT on a value that does not decrypt under the envelope’s algorithm and that AAD;
  3. turn the feature off.

Encrypted::<T, Envelope>::separate reads Tink’s format only, with or without the feature, so code that separated stored 0.29 values runs after the sweep.

Migrating from 0.30

0.31 adds streaming encryption, signatures and MACs, record signing for keyrings, asymmetric KEKs and the deletion of a keyring’s record. Eight changes break a build against 0.30: one public field changes its type, two enums gain a variant and become #[non_exhaustive], the three LocalKek loaders take a format, the serde and serde-json features are removed, the provider trait’s decryption moves to a trait of its own, KeyringStore gains a required method, the firestore feature is split into three, and the keyring’s write-handle and sweep names, with KekProvider and KekVersion, are renamed without aliases. One behaviour changes without breaking a build: a running process takes a keyring record gone from the store as shredded. The readers of a deployment move to 0.31 before its producers. Everything else is additive.

The unsupported algorithm error

KmsAeadUnsupportedAlgorithmError::algorithm is a BackendAlgorithm in 0.31, an AeadAlgorithm in 0.30, so that the one error names whichever algorithm a backend lacks: an AEAD algorithm, as AES-256-GCM-SIV on ring, or an asymmetric KEK algorithm, as RSA-OAEP (RSA encryption with OAEP padding) on ring. The variant, KmsAeadError::UnsupportedAlgorithm, and its code, UNSUPPORTED_ALGORITHM, do not change.

0.300.31
error.algorithm == AeadAlgorithm::Aes256GcmSiverror.algorithm == BackendAlgorithm::Aead(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256GcmSiv)
let algorithm: AeadAlgorithm = error.algorithm;let BackendAlgorithm::Aead(algorithm) = error.algorithm else { … };
error.algorithm.key_len()match error.algorithm { BackendAlgorithm::Aead(algorithm) => algorithm.key_len(), _ => … }

BackendAlgorithm is #[non_exhaustive], so a match on it needs a _ arm, and it implements From<AeadAlgorithm> and From<AsymmetricKekAlgorithm>. Its Display is the inner algorithm’s name, so a log line that printed error.algorithm prints the same text.

Purpose and KeyAlgorithm

Purpose and KeyAlgorithm of kms-aead-keyring gain the Mac variant for the MAC versions of a keyring, a MAC being a keyed tag that proves the bytes it covers are unchanged, see MACs in a keyring. Both are #[non_exhaustive] from 0.31, so an exhaustive match on either needs a _ arm: a breaking change for every such match, made once so that a later purpose is additive.

0.300.31
match purpose { Purpose::Randomized => …, Purpose::Deterministic => …, Purpose::Beacon => … }the same arms and _ => …, or Purpose::Mac => …
match algorithm { KeyAlgorithm::Randomized(_) => …, KeyAlgorithm::Deterministic(_) => …, KeyAlgorithm::Beacon(_) => … }the same arms and _ => …, or KeyAlgorithm::Mac(_) => …

Loading a local KEK

LocalKek::from_secret, from_file and from_env take a SymmetricKeyFormat as their last argument: pass SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw where 0.30 took raw bytes (from_secret) and SymmetricKeyFormat::Base64 where it read base64 (from_file, from_env). The methods keep their names, so the old signatures cannot stay as deprecated wrappers, and every call site fails to compile until it names a format.

0.300.31
LocalKek::from_secret(version, material)LocalKek::from_secret(version, material, SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)
LocalKek::from_file(version, &path)LocalKek::from_file(version, &path, SymmetricKeyFormat::Base64)
LocalKek::from_env(version, "APP_DEV_KEK")LocalKek::from_env(version, "APP_DEV_KEK", SymmetricKeyFormat::Base64)

SymmetricKeyFormat is in kms_aead::formats and is #[non_exhaustive]. Raw is the key bytes themselves, Base64 the standard base64 of the key bytes, trailing whitespace allowed, and Jwk an oct JSON Web Key. The key is 32 bytes in every format. The loaders of the signing and asymmetric KEK keys take the same kind of enum, see Key formats. from_file reads a file of up to 32 KiB, where 0.30 read up to 256 bytes.

The serde features

The serde and serde-json features of kms-aead and of kms-aead-keyring are removed: serde support, JsonEncoded<T> and JWK are in every build of both. Delete the two features from the dependency on each crate in your Cargo.toml; Cargo refuses an unknown feature.

The Firestore features

The firestore feature of 0.30 is firestore-tls-roots in 0.31. In 0.31 firestore is the Firestore keyring store on firestore-rs without its default features, so it has no TLS root store and no crypto provider, as gcs has none. A build that names firestore therefore enables a firestore-rs or gcloud-sdk root store feature of its own, or one of the two features that do:

  • firestore-tls-roots: the system’s native roots, firestore-rs’s tls-roots, which brings native-tls and so OpenSSL on Linux;
  • firestore-tls-webpki: rustls, with the bundled WebPKI roots for the gRPC connection to Firestore, and no native TLS;

each with firestore-rs’s auth-default-crypto. The Cloud Storage features are split the same way: gcs, gcs-tls-roots and gcs-tls-webpki.

0.300.31
features = ["firestore"]features = ["firestore-tls-roots"], the same build as 0.30
no equivalentfeatures = ["firestore-tls-webpki"], for a build without OpenSSL

The rustcrypto feature adds rsa

The rustcrypto feature adds the rsa crate for RSA-OAEP, so every application that enables it carries rsa in its lockfile. The feature also adds p256 and ed25519-dalek, for signatures. kms-aead depends on serde, serde_bytes, serde_json, sha2, base64ct, der, spki, pkcs8, sec1 and pem-rfc7468 in every build, for serde support, the key framing, JSON Web Keys and the thumbprints of public keys, and kms-aead-keyring on sha2, for the canonical digest of a record.

The aws features read aws login sessions

The features that enable AWS KMS turn on aws-config’s credentials-login, so that the default credentials chain takes the session of aws login, as the AWS CLI does.

Readers before producers

The encrypted DEK a public key encrypts is a layout of 0.31, which starts with 0x02, and no earlier release reads it, see DEKs encrypted with a public key. A deployment moves every reader, every application and administrative job that decrypts, to 0.31 before the first producer encrypts with a public key, as every reader moved to 0.30 before the first value in Tink’s envelope format, see Upgrading stored values. Nothing already stored changes: the encrypted DEKs the KMS encrypted keep their bytes and keep decrypting.

A keyring record gains a signature member, which 0.30 reads and ignores, and which a 0.30 administrative write removes, since 0.30 rewrites the record from its own fields: move every administrative binary to 0.31 before the first record is signed. A keyring’s first MAC version is created only once every process reads the keyring on 0.31, see MACs in a keyring.

Run a keyring under an asymmetric KEK with RecordSignaturePolicy::Required on every application: anyone holding the public key can file a DEK of their own in its record, and under VerifyIfSigned a store writer who holds it plants a version for every process that starts afterwards, see Keyrings under an asymmetric KEK. An existing Azure Key Vault or Vault Transit RSA deployment moves its encryptions to the producer in the order of Migrating an Azure or Transit RSA deployment.

A store-less writer whose renewed DEK leaves the DEK cache before it encrypts now encrypts with the DEK it generated, in place of decrypting its encrypted DEK again, so a store-less encrypt makes no KMS decryption under any provider, see The DEK lives in the cache.

Names

0.31 uses one vocabulary: encrypt and decrypt for every confidentiality operation, and “open” only for opening a keyring. The keyring’s write handle, its re-encryption of a value under the active version, and the sweep’s encryption marker, the 5 bytes a record stores beside its encrypted fields to name the key versions they are under, take the new words. KekProvider and KekVersion take names that say they belong to any key of a KMS. No old name stays as an alias: a build that uses one fails until it uses the new one.

Area0.300.31
KMS nameskms_aead::KekProviderkms_aead::KmsProvider
kms_aead::providers::KekVersionkms_aead::providers::KmsKeyVersion
Keyring writesSealerEncryptor

Sealer::reseal_deterministicEncryptor::reencrypt_deterministic

sweep::runsweep::reencrypt_records
sweep::run_batchedsweep::reencrypt_records_batched

sweep::ResealOutcome, ResealOutcome::Resealedsweep::ReencryptOutcome, ReencryptOutcome::Reencrypted
SweepReport::resealed, SweepReport::resealed_from_compromisedSweepReport::reencrypted, SweepReport::reencrypted_from_compromised

ErrorsKeyringError::SealerOutdated, code SEALER_OUTDATEDKeyringError::EncryptorOutdated, code ENCRYPTOR_OUTDATED

RegressionRule::SealEpochRevertedRegressionRule::ReencryptEpochReverted
RegressionRule::SealRangeRevertedRegressionRule::ReencryptRangeReverted

Nothing stored changes. A keyring record keeps its seal_epoch and seal_epochs keys, and a encryption marker keeps its 5 bytes, 0x01 and the epoch, so records and markers that 0.30 wrote read in 0.31, and the reverse. The book’s sweep examples name the marker column encryption_marker where 0.30’s named it seal; an existing column keeps its name, since the library never reads a column by name. KmsProvider and KmsKeyVersion keep every value, method and Display string: the provider and kek_version span fields keep their values, and a keyring record’s kek.provider reads as before. KekRef and LocalKekVersion keep their names, since they name a key encryption key.

Telemetry

These renames change what a dashboard, a log query or an alert matches:

Signal0.300.31
Keyring sweep span field/kms_aead_keyring/resealed/kms_aead_keyring/reencrypted
Keyring sweep span field/kms_aead_keyring/resealed_from_compromised/kms_aead_keyring/reencrypted_from_compromised
kms_aead.operation of a keyring callresealreencrypt
error.type and the error_code span fieldSEALER_OUTDATED, INVALID_SEAL_MARKERENCRYPTOR_OUTDATED, INVALID_ENCRYPTION_MARKER

The error and log messages use the new words too, so a query on the text of a message, such as the WARN of a sweep that could not re-encrypt a record, needs the new text.

Your own provider

The provider trait of 0.30 is two traits in 0.31, so that a provider that only encrypts, such as the PublicKeyKekProvider of a producer, has no decryption, and an envelope over it has no call that decrypts:

  • KmsAeadEncryptionProvider keeps kek, encrypt_data_encryption_key and generate_key_material, and gains encrypts_with_public_key, with a default of false;
  • KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, whose supertrait is KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, holds decrypt_data_encryption_key, with the signature of 0.30.

A provider of 0.30 fails to compile until its decrypt_data_encryption_key moves into an impl of the new trait. A wrapper implements each trait where the provider it wraps does:

use std::sync::atomic::{AtomicU64, Ordering};

use kms_aead::{
    DataEncryptionKey, EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, KekRef, KmsAeadDecryptionProvider,
    KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, KmsAeadResult,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

/// The provider `P`, with a count of its decryptions.
struct Counted<P> {
    inner: P,
    decryptions: AtomicU64,
}

impl<P: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider> KmsAeadEncryptionProvider for Counted<P> {
    fn kek(&self) -> &KekRef {
        self.inner.kek()
    }

    async fn encrypt_data_encryption_key(
        &self,
        dek: &DataEncryptionKey,
    ) -> KmsAeadResult<EncryptedDataEncryptionKey> {
        self.inner.encrypt_data_encryption_key(dek).await
    }

    async fn generate_key_material(&self, len: usize) -> KmsAeadResult<Option<SecretValue>> {
        self.inner.generate_key_material(len).await
    }

    fn encrypts_with_public_key(&self) -> bool {
        self.inner.encrypts_with_public_key()
    }
}

// The method of 0.30's impl, in an impl of its own.
impl<P: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider> KmsAeadDecryptionProvider for Counted<P> {
    async fn decrypt_data_encryption_key(
        &self,
        encrypted_dek: &EncryptedDataEncryptionKey,
    ) -> KmsAeadResult<DataEncryptionKey> {
        self.decryptions.fetch_add(1, Ordering::Relaxed);
        self.inner.decrypt_data_encryption_key(encrypted_dek).await
    }
}

Code generic over a provider names the trait its calls need. KmsAeadDecryptionProvider covers both, so code that encrypts and decrypts names it alone:

0.300.31
P: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, for an envelope’s decrypt, decrypt_with_dek, *_with_encrypted_dek, beacon, decrypt_deterministic_dek, reencrypt_dek, reencrypt_encrypted_dek or legacy upgradesP: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider
P: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, for DekCache::with_dekP: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider
P: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, for a Keyring, a KeyringAdmin, an Encryptor or a KeyringRegistry call that returns oneP: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider
P: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, for encrypt, encrypt_with_dek and the generate_encrypted_* calls onlyunchanged
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, to call decrypt_data_encryption_key on a provideruse kms_aead::KmsAeadDecryptionProvider

A provider that encrypts DEKs with the public half of a key pair returns true from encrypts_with_public_key, so that a keyring refuses the BigQuery and Tink exports its KEK cannot serve, see Producers and readers.

Your own keyring store

KeyringStore gains a fifth method, delete, which has no default: a build that implements the trait for an application’s own store fails until the store implements it.

fn delete(
    &self,
    id: &KeyringId,
    expected: &Self::Revision,
) -> impl Future<Output = KeyringResult<Deletion>> + Send;

It deletes the record when its stored revision is expected, with every earlier version of it the store keeps, such as the noncurrent versions of a versioned bucket:

  • Deletion::Deleted once the store holds no record of the id, also when it held none;
  • Deletion::Conflict when the stored revision is another. The live record stays, though the store may have removed its earlier versions first;
  • errors are returned as load returns them, as KeyringError::Store with StoreOperation::Delete, whose text in the error is delete. A delete that fails may have removed part of the earlier versions already, and the caller repeats it with the same expected;
  • a store with push signals the deletion as a change;
  • the store never gives an id the same revision twice, across a delete too. A revision that restarts at 1 for a record created again would let a process that missed the deletion keep the deleted record.

StoreOperation gains the Delete variant. It is #[non_exhaustive], so a match on it has a _ arm already. What each library store deletes, and what it leaves to the operator, is in What a delete removes.

A keyring record gone from the store

In 0.30 a running process kept a record that went missing from the store in use, with a WARN, and only new processes failed at open. In 0.31 the process takes it as a shredded keyring:

  • it destroys every version of its cached record in memory, and writes nothing. That evicts the keyring’s keys from the DEK cache;
  • it logs a WARN, “The keyring store no longer holds the keyring record; every version is destroyed in this process”, or an INFO when the record it held had every version destroyed already, as a shred leaves it, sets the changed field of the Keyring load span to deleted, and stops revalidating and watching the keyring;
  • its handles then fail before any KMS call: encrypting with NoActiveVersion, reading with KeyVersionDestroyed, reload with RecordMissing;
  • the registry’s next open of the id reads the store again: RecordMissing, or a record created since. A handle opened before stays on the destroyed record.

So an accidental deletion of a record stops a running process at its next revalidation, within reload_min_interval of the signal with push and once the cached record is older than record_ttl without. A record restored from a copy is refused by that process with RecordRegressed, because it revives versions the process holds destroyed, so the process restarts after a restore. The text of the WARN ended in “the cached record stays in use” in 0.30, so change a log alert that matches that ending. The runbook is The keyring record was deleted or corrupted.

Under record signing, the deleted record stays the baseline of the id: the next record must be signed if the deleted one was, and a keyring created again opens, see Signing the keyring record.

New in 0.31

The other changes are additive. KmsAeadError is already #[non_exhaustive], so its new SignatureError variant needs no change in a match that has a _ arm.

  • streaming encryption of values of any length, behind the stream feature, see Streaming encryption;
  • signatures and MACs, see Signatures and MACs;
  • asymmetric KEKs, see Asymmetric KEKs;
  • SymmetricKeyFormat::Jwk, which loads a local KEK from an oct JWK, see Local KMS;
  • signed keyring records, see Signing the keyring record;
  • KeyringAdmin::dangerously_shred(), which destroys every version of a keyring in one write and deletes its record from the store with the earlier versions the store keeps, see Shredding a keyring.

Stored values

Three types carry what the library writes to a data store and reads back:

  • Encrypted<T, F>: an encrypted value that decrypts to a T, in the layout of the format F;
  • Beacon<T>: a blind index of a T, stored beside its encrypted value for lookups;
  • Plaintext: the trait of every T, which fixes the bytes a value is encrypted as.

The T is a type of the application, such as an Email newtype, so a column names what it holds: Encrypted<Email, Envelope> and Beacon<Email>. Neither type holds a T, only the bytes to store.

Encrypted<T, F>

Every call that encrypts returns an Encrypted<T, F>. The format F comes from kms_aead::formats and fixes the layout of the bytes:

FormatLayoutWritten byRead bykey_id()Equality
EnvelopeTink’s KMS envelope, the encrypted DEK insideKmsAeadEnvelope::encryptKmsAeadEnvelope::decryptnonone
Bare[nonce: 12][ciphertext][tag: 16]KmsAeadEnvelope::encrypt_with_dek, AeadCipher::encrypt_bareKmsAeadEnvelope::decrypt_with_dek, AeadCipher::decrypt_barenonone
Randomized[0x01][key id: u32 BE][nonce: 12][ciphertext][tag: 16]AeadCipher::encrypt, Encryptor::encryptAeadCipher::decrypt, Keyring::decryptyesnone
Deterministic[0x01][key id: u32 BE][synthetic IV: 16][ciphertext]DeterministicCipher::encrypt, Encryptor::encrypt_deterministicDeterministicCipher::decrypt, Keyring::decrypt_deterministicyes== and Hash, in constant time
  • key_id() returns the KeyId that the TINK prefix names, the key to decrypt the value with. The prefix is not authenticated: a changed key id fails to decrypt;
  • an Envelope value carries its encrypted DEK instead of a key id, and a Bare value names no key: the application keeps track of the key that decrypts it;
  • the randomised formats encrypt equal plaintexts into different values, so they have no ==. Compare value() to tell whether two values are the same stored bytes;
  • only Deterministic values are equal for equal plaintexts under one key and one AAD, which is what a lookup by the encrypted value needs, see Deterministic encryption;
  • Debug shows the length only.

See Formats of Encrypted for the shortest value of each layout and the layouts of 0.29 and earlier.

Bytes in and out

value() and into_value() return the bytes to store, and to_hex_string() returns them as lowercase hex. The only ways back are Encrypted::try_from for bytes and Encrypted::try_from_hex_string for hex in either case. Both check the bytes against the layout of F before any KMS call, so a truncated or foreign value fails when it is read, with INVALID_CIPHER_TEXT_FORMAT, UNKNOWN_CIPHER_TEXT_LAYOUT, INVALID_HEX, or LEGACY_FORMAT for a layout of 0.29 or earlier without the legacy-formats feature. The check reads the framing only: a changed value fails to decrypt with DECRYPT.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher, Encrypted, KeyId};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;
let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Randomized> =
    cipher.encrypt(KeyId::new(1), b"users/1042/token", &SecretValue::from("token"), &dek)?;

// The key the prefix names
assert_eq!(encrypted.key_id(), KeyId::new(1));

// Hex for a text column, bytes for a binary one
let stored_hex: String = encrypted.to_hex_string();
let stored: Vec<u8> = encrypted.into_value();

let from_bytes = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Randomized>::try_from(stored)?;
let from_hex = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Randomized>::try_from_hex_string(&stored_hex)?;
assert_eq!(from_bytes.value(), from_hex.value());

// A prefix with nothing after it is no randomised value
assert!(Encrypted::<SecretValue, Randomized>::try_from(vec![0x01, 0, 0, 0, 1]).is_err());
Ok(())
}

Storing

Encrypted is stored as bytes, and read back through TryFrom, so a malformed stored value fails in the decode:

  • serde, in every build: Serialize and Deserialize, a bytes value in Firestore and an array of numbers in serde_json;
  • postgres: ToSql and FromSql of postgres-types, for tokio-postgres, as BYTEA, and a slice as BYTEA[] for = ANY($1);
  • sqlx: sqlx’s Type, Encode, Decode and PgHasArrayType for PostgreSQL, as BYTEA. It depends on sqlx-core and sqlx-postgres only, so the application chooses sqlx’s runtime and TLS stack.

A field of a serde record, here in JSON:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};
use kms_aead::{KmsAeadResult, Plaintext};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);
impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> { self.0.to_plaintext() }
    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct User {
    id: i64,
    email: Encrypted<Email, Envelope>,
}

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

let email = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
let user = User {
    id: 1042,
    email: envelope.encrypt(b"users/1042/email", &email).await?,
};

let json: String = serde_json::to_string(&user)?;
let read_back: User = serde_json::from_str(&json)?;
let decrypted: Email = envelope.decrypt(b"users/1042/email", &read_back.email).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, email);
Ok(())
}

A BYTEA column, with sqlx:

use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::Encrypted;
use sqlx::PgPool;

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);
async fn store(pool: &PgPool, id: i64, email: &Encrypted<Email, Envelope>) -> sqlx::Result<()> {
    sqlx::query("INSERT INTO users (id, email) VALUES ($1, $2)")
        .bind(id)
        .bind(email)
        .execute(pool)
        .await?;
    Ok(())
}

async fn load(pool: &PgPool, id: i64) -> sqlx::Result<Encrypted<Email, Envelope>> {
    sqlx::query_scalar("SELECT email FROM users WHERE id = $1")
        .bind(id)
        .fetch_one(pool)
        .await
}

A TEXT column, as hex, with tokio-postgres:

use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::Encrypted;
use tokio_postgres::Client;

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);
async fn store(
    client: &Client,
    id: i64,
    email: &Encrypted<Email, Envelope>,
) -> Result<(), tokio_postgres::Error> {
    let email_hex: String = email.to_hex_string();
    client
        .execute("UPDATE users SET email_hex = $1 WHERE id = $2", &[&email_hex, &id])
        .await?;
    Ok(())
}

async fn load(
    client: &Client,
    id: i64,
) -> Result<Encrypted<Email, Envelope>, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let row = client
        .query_one("SELECT email_hex FROM users WHERE id = $1", &[&id])
        .await?;
    let email_hex: String = row.try_get("email_hex")?;
    Ok(Encrypted::<Email, Envelope>::try_from_hex_string(&email_hex)?)
}

Hex takes twice the space of the bytes. Use it where the data store has no binary type, or where the value travels as text.

Beacon<T>

A beacon, or blind index, is a truncated HMAC-SHA256 of a T’s plaintext under a beacon key. It is stored beside the field’s encrypted value, which stays randomised, and a data store matches it by equality without the plaintext or a decryption:

[0x01][key id: u32 BE][HMAC-SHA256: the first ceil(bits / 8) bytes]
  • 0x01 is the version of the beacon layout;
  • key_id() returns the KeyId of the beacon key that calculated it, so that a column holds beacons of two keys during a re-index;
  • BeaconBits sets how many bits of the HMAC a beacon keeps, 1 to 256, BeaconBits::DEFAULT, 16, unless set. BeaconBits::new returns None outside that range;
  • value() and into_value() return the bytes to store, and Beacon::try_from reads them back, failing with INVALID_BEACON_FORMAT on bytes of another layout;
  • == and Hash compare the bytes, == in constant time, and Debug shows the key id only.
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::{Beacon, BeaconAlgorithm, BeaconBits, BeaconMac, KeyId};
use kms_aead::{KmsAeadResult, Plaintext};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);
impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> { self.0.to_plaintext() }
    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let mac = BeaconMac::<Ring>::new(BeaconAlgorithm::HmacSha256)?;
let beacon_key = mac.generate_dek()?;

let email = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
let beacon: Beacon<Email> =
    mac.beacon(KeyId::new(1), b"users/email", BeaconBits::DEFAULT, &email, &beacon_key)?;

// [0x01][key id: 4 bytes][16 bits of HMAC: 2 bytes]
assert_eq!(beacon.key_id(), KeyId::new(1));
assert_eq!(beacon.value().len(), 7);

let read_back = Beacon::<Email>::try_from(beacon.value().to_vec())?;
assert_eq!(read_back, beacon);
Ok(())
}

The bits trade lookups against leakage, see Bits.

Beacon<T> is stored as Encrypted is, as bytes, with serde and the postgres and sqlx features. A lookup matches the column by equality, so it gets an ordinary index:

CREATE TABLE users (
  id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
  email BYTEA NOT NULL,
  email_beacon BYTEA NOT NULL
);
CREATE INDEX users_email_beacon ON users (email_beacon);

use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::{Beacon, Encrypted};
use tokio_postgres::Client;

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);
// The candidates for one email, which the application decrypts and filters
async fn candidates(
    client: &Client,
    beacon: &Beacon<Email>,
) -> Result<Vec<Encrypted<Email, Envelope>>, tokio_postgres::Error> {
    let rows = client
        .query("SELECT email FROM users WHERE email_beacon = $1", &[beacon])
        .await?;
    rows.iter().map(|row| row.try_get("email")).collect()
}

See Beacons for calculating beacons with a KMS-encrypted beacon key, the context, normalisation and key rotation, and Beacons in kms-aead-keyring for beacons under key versions.

Plaintext

Plaintext maps a type to the bytes it is encrypted as, and back. Every T of Encrypted<T, F> and Beacon<T> implements it. An application type implements it by mapping to one of the built-in types, such as a newtype over String that is encrypted as the same UTF-8 bytes:

use kms_aead::{KmsAeadResult, Plaintext};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let email = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
assert_eq!(Email::from_plaintext(email.to_plaintext()?)?, email);
Ok(())
}

A newtype over SecretValue is written the same way:

use kms_aead::{KmsAeadResult, Plaintext};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(SecretValue);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        Ok(self.0.clone())
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        Ok(Email(plaintext))
    }
}

fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let email = Email(SecretValue::from("alice@example.com"));
let decrypted: Email = Email::from_plaintext(email.to_plaintext()?)?;
assert_eq!(decrypted.0.as_sensitive_str(), "alice@example.com");
Ok(())
}

It differs from the String newtype as follows:

  • the decrypted value is zeroised on drop, and Debug prints *** instead of it;
  • text is encrypted as the same bytes as through String, so AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING still reads it;
  • there is no UTF-8 check on read, so bytes that are not UTF-8 decrypt without an error;
  • the text is reached through SecretValue’s accessors rather than as a &str: as_sensitive_str(), which panics on bytes that are not UTF-8, or sensitive_value_to_str(), which returns an error.

SecretValue suits values that are secrets or personal data, and String values that are used as text throughout.

The built-in types encode as follows. The encodings are fixed, so that other readers of the same values, such as BigQuery’s AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING, decode them:

TypeEncoding
StringUTF-8
Vec<u8>, SecretValuethe bytes themselves
booltrue or false
i32, i64, u32, u64decimal ASCII, which SQL reads with CAST
JsonEncoded<T>the compact JSON of any serde type
  • decrypt fails with INVALID_PLAINTEXT when the plaintext is not an encoding of T, and the error never quotes the bytes;
  • the decrypted value belongs to the application, which protects it. Only SecretValue as T is zeroised on drop; String and Vec<u8> take over the decrypted buffer without a copy;
  • a beacon is the HMAC of the encoded bytes, so normalisation, such as lowercasing an email, is the newtype’s job, see Beacons.

JsonEncoded<T> encrypts a structured value of any serde type as its compact JSON, which BigQuery reads with AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING and its JSON functions:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, JsonEncoded, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq, Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct Address {
    street: String,
    city: String,
}

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

let address = JsonEncoded::new(Address {
    street: "Mannerheimintie 1".to_string(),
    city: "Helsinki".to_string(),
});
let encrypted: Encrypted<JsonEncoded<Address>, Envelope> =
    envelope.encrypt(b"users/1042/address", &address).await?;
let decrypted: JsonEncoded<Address> = envelope.decrypt(b"users/1042/address", &encrypted).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, address);
Ok(())
}

JSON that does not decode

A plaintext that is not the JSON of its type, such as a record written by an older version of the application, fails with INVALID_PLAINTEXT and logs a WARN event with an outline of the JSON. By default the outline shows the shape: the type of every value, the nesting, and where serde_json stopped reading, with no value and no length, since a length gives away a hidden boolean. Finding the field that changed takes more, and a type opts into it with a policy, the second type parameter of JsonEncoded: a type of the application’s own that implements JsonRedaction and sets the constants it needs. The rustdoc says what each shows and how FieldPaths names a field.

use kms_aead::redaction::{FieldPaths, JsonRedaction};
use kms_aead::JsonEncoded;
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct Customer {
    id: String,
    email: String,
    plan: String,
    renewal_date: String,
}

struct CustomerLog;

impl JsonRedaction for CustomerLog {
    const FIELD_NAMES: bool = true;
    const SIZES: bool = true;
    const REVEALED_FIELDS: FieldPaths = FieldPaths::from_static(&["id", "plan"]);
}

type CustomerJson = JsonEncoded<Customer, CustomerLog>;

let customer = CustomerJson::from(Customer {
    id: "cus_4821".to_string(),
    email: "ada.lovelace@example.com".to_string(),
    plan: "team".to_string(),
    renewal_date: "2027-03-01".to_string(),
});

JsonEncoded::new builds a value under the default policy, and from a value of a type with a policy of its own. A record written when plan was a number logs this outline under the default policy:

{<key>: <string>, <key>: <string>, <key>: <number>, <key>: <string>}

and this under CustomerLog:

89 bytes: {"id": "cus_4821", "email": <string, 24 chars>, "plan": 2, "renewal_date": <string, 10 chars>}

The outline names the field and the value that differs, and shows the length of the email and nothing else of it. A plaintext is encrypted because it is secret, so a policy reveals codes and identifiers only. A revealed value goes to the logs whenever the JSON does not decode: never reveal personal data. kms_aead::json=off in an EnvFilter turns the event off. The event’s fields are in Observability. RedactedJson outlines any JSON the same way, with no encryption, see Log JSON payloads without leaking data.

Full example available here.

Envelope encryption

KmsAeadEnvelope encrypts values under data encryption keys (DEKs) on the crypto backend it is built with, and encrypts the DEKs with the KEK of its provider. A call that makes no KMS call is not async, and every call opens one DEBUG span, see Observability.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, KmsAeadEnvelope, KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
// AES-256-GCM by default
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

// Or another algorithm
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::with_options(
    provider,
    KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions::new().with_algorithm(AeadAlgorithm::ChaCha20Poly1305),
)?;
let _ = envelope;
Ok(())
}

The value does not record the algorithm, so the writer and the reader of a value must be configured with the same one. The backend can differ, see Crypto backends. The default, AES-256-GCM, is read by every Tink implementation too, see Formats. Values that 0.29 and earlier encrypted under their default decrypt only with ChaCha20-Poly1305 configured, see Migrating from 0.29.

Over a provider that only encrypts, such as the PublicKeyKekProvider of a producer, the envelope has the calls that encrypt: encrypt, encrypt_with_dek and the generate_encrypted_* calls. The calls that decrypt a DEK or a value, from decrypt to the re-encryptions, need a provider that implements KmsAeadDecryptionProvider too, as every KMS provider does, and do not compile over one that does not, see KMS providers.

Encrypting values

encrypt encrypts a value under a new DEK, encrypts the DEK with one KMS call, and returns both in one Encrypted<T, Envelope>, where T is the type of the value. decrypt decrypts the DEK with one KMS call, decrypts the value and returns a T. A value with no type of its own is a SecretValue:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

let aad = b"orders/1042";
let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> = envelope
    .encrypt(aad, &SecretValue::from("4111 1111 1111 1111"))
    .await?;

// The bytes to store, and the value read back from them
let stored: Vec<u8> = encrypted.into_value();
let read_back = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::try_from(stored)?;

let decrypted: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt(aad, &read_back).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("4111 1111 1111 1111"));
Ok(())
}

The bytes in and out of an Encrypted are in Stored values.

The value is in Tink’s KMS envelope format for every provider, so Tink’s KmsEnvelopeAead reads it with the same KMS key and algorithm, see Formats.

A new DEK comes from the backend’s CSPRNG, or from the KMS generator when the provider is configured to use one, see KMS random generators.

Typed fields

encrypt and decrypt take a value of any type that implements Plaintext, such as an application’s Email newtype, and the result is an Encrypted<T, Envelope> that names the type. The calls run in the envelope’s mode, a DEK per value or store-less DEK reuse, and decrypt through the envelope’s DEK cache, whatever the type. Plaintext, the bytes of an Encrypted and how serde, tokio-postgres and sqlx store it are in Stored values.

One fixed key

AeadCipher::encrypt encrypts a field under a key the application holds, in Tink’s layout of a key whose output prefix is TINK, [0x01][key id: u32 BE] before the AEAD value: an Encrypted<T, Randomized>. DeterministicCipher::encrypt does the same for an Encrypted<T, Deterministic>. The key id is a KeyId, the application’s constant, and Tink and BigQuery read the values with a keyset of the one key under that id:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::cache::{DekCache, DekCacheOptions};
use kms_aead::formats::{Randomized, TinkKeyType, TinkKeyset};
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsKeyRef, GcpKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{
    AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher, DataEncryptionKey, Encrypted, EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, KeyId,
    KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, KmsAeadResult, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

// The id of the one key, in every value and in the keyset
const EMAIL_KEY: KeyId = KeyId::new(1);

async fn example() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);
let provider = GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref).await?;
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;
let cache = DekCache::new(DekCacheOptions::new())?;

// Once, by an administrative job: the key encrypted by Cloud KMS, stored in the settings
let (_, encrypted_dek): (DataEncryptionKey, EncryptedDataEncryptionKey) =
    cipher.generate_encrypted_dek(&provider).await?;

// On every write and read: the key decrypted through the cache, no KMS call while it is cached
let email = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
let (encrypted, _) = cache
    .with_dek(&provider, &encrypted_dek, |dek| {
        cipher.encrypt(EMAIL_KEY, b"users/1042/email", &email, dek)
    })
    .await?;
let encrypted: Encrypted<Email, Randomized> = encrypted?;

let (decrypted, _) = cache
    .with_dek(&provider, &encrypted_dek, |dek| {
        cipher.decrypt(b"users/1042/email", &encrypted, dek)
    })
    .await?;
let decrypted: Email = decrypted?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, email);

// Once, for BigQuery: the keyset of the one key, encrypted by the same Cloud KMS key
let mut keyset = TinkKeyset::new(EMAIL_KEY);
let (added, _) = cache
    .with_dek(&provider, &encrypted_dek, |dek| {
        keyset.add_key(EMAIL_KEY, TinkKeyType::from(cipher.algorithm()), dek)
    })
    .await?;
added?;
let first_level_keyset: EncryptedDataEncryptionKey = provider
    .encrypt_data_encryption_key(&DataEncryptionKey::from(keyset.to_bytes()))
    .await?;
let _ = first_level_keyset;
Ok(())
}

BigQuery reads the column with KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN over the encrypted keyset, passed as a BYTES query parameter, beside the key’s Tink URI as a STRING one, gcp-kms://projects/my-project/locations/europe-north1/keyRings/my-key-ring/cryptoKeys/my-key:

SELECT AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING(
  KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @first_level_keyset),
  u.email,
  CONCAT('users/', CAST(u.id AS STRING), '/email'))
FROM users u;
  • BigQuery reads AES-256-GCM values with AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING and AEAD.DECRYPT_BYTES, and the AES-SIV values of DeterministicCipher with DETERMINISTIC_DECRYPT_STRING and DETERMINISTIC_DECRYPT_BYTES. KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN decrypts with Cloud KMS only;
  • AeadCipher::decrypt reads the values Tink and BigQuery write under the same key and id. The caller picks the key by key_id(), which the prefix names without authenticating it;
  • a rotation needs a second key id, values of both ids in one column and a keyset of both keys. kms-aead-keyring keeps those versions.

Beacons

A beacon, or blind index, finds rows by a field’s value without decrypting the column, see Beacons.

AAD

The AAD is authenticated but not encrypted: a value decrypts only with the AAD it was encrypted with, and a wrong AAD fails with DECRYPT. Use it to bind a value to its place, such as <table>/<row id>/<column>, so that a value copied into another row or column fails to decrypt.

The AAD is not stored in the value, so the reader rebuilds it from the same data. It is never logged or put in an error, but it is not secret either: keep secrets in the value itself.

The keyring takes a structured AAD instead of free bytes, built from the field and the row, see Why the AAD is structured.

Working with DEKs yourself

The *_with_dek and *_with_encrypted_dek calls let the application keep one DEK for many values and store the encrypted DEK apart from them, such as one DEK per tenant or per file. The values are Encrypted<T, Bare>, with no encrypted DEK in them.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Bare;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

// One KMS call: a new DEK and the same DEK encrypted
let (dek, encrypted_dek) = envelope.generate_encrypted_dek().await?;

// No KMS call: encrypt and decrypt with the plaintext DEK
let first: Encrypted<SecretValue, Bare> =
    envelope.encrypt_with_dek(b"files/1/chunk/0", &SecretValue::from("first"), &dek)?;
let second: Encrypted<SecretValue, Bare> =
    envelope.encrypt_with_dek(b"files/1/chunk/1", &SecretValue::from("second"), &dek)?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt_with_dek(b"files/1/chunk/0", &first, &dek)?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("first"));

// Later, with only the encrypted DEK: the envelope decrypts it with the KMS
let decrypted: SecretValue = envelope
    .decrypt_with_encrypted_dek(b"files/1/chunk/1", &second, &encrypted_dek)
    .await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("second"));
Ok(())
}

The calls are:

  • generate_encrypted_dek: a new DEK and the same DEK encrypted, one KMS call;
  • encrypt_with_dek and decrypt_with_dek: with a plaintext DEK, no KMS call;
  • encrypt_with_encrypted_dek and decrypt_with_encrypted_dek: with an encrypted DEK, decrypted through the envelope’s DEK cache, so one KMS call per call without a cache.

Be aware not to encrypt more than about 2^32 values under one DEK: every value takes a random 96-bit nonce, see Security considerations.

Storing encrypted DEKs

EncryptedDataEncryptionKey holds the bytes the provider returned, which also name the KMS key version where the KMS needs one to decrypt after a rotation. Store value() or to_hex_string(), and read them back with EncryptedDataEncryptionKey::from or try_from_hex_string():

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{KmsKeyVersion, LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, KmsAeadEnvelope};

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;
let (_dek, encrypted_dek) = envelope.generate_encrypted_dek().await?;

let stored: Vec<u8> = encrypted_dek.value().to_vec();
let read_back_encrypted_dek = EncryptedDataEncryptionKey::from(stored);

let stored_hex: String = encrypted_dek.to_hex_string();
let read_back_hex_encrypted_dek = EncryptedDataEncryptionKey::try_from_hex_string(&stored_hex)?;

assert_eq!(read_back_encrypted_dek, read_back_hex_encrypted_dek);

// The KEK version beside it, for the check before an old KEK version is disabled
let stored_kek_version: Option<String> = encrypted_dek.kek_version().map(KmsKeyVersion::to_string);
let read_back_kek_version: Option<KmsKeyVersion> =
    stored_kek_version.map(KmsKeyVersion::try_from).transpose()?;
let read_back_encrypted_dek = match read_back_kek_version {
    Some(kek_version) => read_back_encrypted_dek.with_kek_version(kek_version),
    None => read_back_encrypted_dek,
};
assert_eq!(read_back_encrypted_dek.kek_version(), encrypted_dek.kek_version());
Ok(())
}

An encrypted DEK that the provider has just returned carries in kek_version() the version of the KEK that encrypted it, as the KMS reported it. It is None under AWS KMS, whose Encrypt response names no key material, and wherever the KMS reported no version. Decryption never needs it, and neither value() nor to_hex_string() holds it, so an encrypted DEK read back from them has none. Stored beside the encrypted DEK, it shows without a KMS call which DEKs are still under an old KEK version, see The check. with_kek_version puts it back on the encrypted DEK read from the bytes.

Equality and hashing take the bytes alone, so an encrypted DEK read back without its KEK version is the same key, to the DEK cache and in a map, as the one the provider returned with it.

The encrypted DEK is the one Tink stores in its KMS envelope, so Encrypted::join of a bare value and its encrypted DEK is an Encrypted<T, Envelope> that Tink reads. See Formats for each provider’s bytes.

Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation

A KEK rotation makes the KMS encrypt new DEKs under a new key version, and every DEK stored before it keeps decrypting with the old version only. reencrypt_encrypted_dek and reencrypt_dek move a stored DEK to the current version, so the old version can be retired while the application keeps reading and writing:

  1. rotate the KEK in the KMS, as the page of each provider describes. Under Cloud KMS, wait until the new primary version has propagated, see Google Cloud KMS. Then wait until every store-less writer has renewed its DEK. Until then a writer keeps encrypting under its current DEK, encrypted under the old version, so the sweep of step 2 misses the values it writes, the check of step 3 does not see them, and they first fail at the check of step 6, after the disable. A writer renews its DEK when its DekReusePolicy says so, and at the latest when the DEK leaves its cache, at the cache’s DekCacheOptions::ttl, DekCacheOptions::DEFAULT_TTL of 15 minutes by default, see The DEK lives in the cache;
  2. re-encrypt every stored DEK: the DEKs of the stored values, the DEKs stored apart from their values, and the record of each keyring under the KEK with KeyringAdmin::reencrypt_deks. Then export the keyrings’ keysets again and re-encrypt the application’s own encrypted keysets, see Encrypted keysets, and replace the old keyset bytes wherever they are kept, see Exporting a keyset to BigQuery;
  3. check that the kek_versions_in_use of every keyring record lists the new version alone, and so does the KEK version an application stores beside its own DEKs. A None never passes this check, see A KEK version the KMS did not report;
  4. wait until no process holds an encrypted DEK from before step 2: the longest record_ttl in the fleet after the last reencrypt_deks, with no process logging “The keyring record could not be revalidated” meanwhile, and every process has read its DEKs stored apart from their values again, see The check;
  5. disable the old version, and wait until the disable has propagated in the KMS: under Cloud KMS typically up to a minute, and several hours in exceptional cases;
  6. check that the KMS decrypts every stored DEK, every keyring’s DEKs with reencrypt_deks, and every encrypted keyset, see The check. A DEK or a keyset it refuses is one the sweep missed: enable the old version again, sweep again, and go back to step 3;
  7. keep the old version disabled for as long as the backups of the stored values and of the keyring records are kept. Immediately before destroying it, run the check of step 6 again, and destroy it only when it passes.

A backup taken before the sweep holds the DEKs under the old version, so destroying the old version makes every such backup unreadable for good. Keyring records are the same, see Key lifecycle. What a rotation, a disabled version and a destroyed version are differs per KMS, see the page of each provider.

The sweep

reencrypt_encrypted_dek makes two KMS calls: the KMS decrypts the DEK with the version that encrypted it and encrypts it again with its current one. The AEAD bytes of a value stay as they are, so the sweep takes no AAD, decrypts nothing, and the value decrypts as before. Each distinct encrypted DEK is re-encrypted once and joined with each of its values, so the values a store-less envelope wrote under one DEK still share one afterwards:

use std::collections::HashMap;

use kms_aead::backends::AeadBackend;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::{
    Encrypted, EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, KmsAeadEnvelope,
    KmsAeadResult,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

/// The bytes to write back for each stored value in `rows`, beside the bytes read, with its DEK
/// under the KEK's current version. Each distinct encrypted DEK is re-encrypted once.
async fn reencrypt_values<P, B>(
    envelope: &KmsAeadEnvelope<P, B>,
    rows: &[Vec<u8>],
) -> KmsAeadResult<Vec<(Vec<u8>, Vec<u8>)>>
where
    P: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider,
    B: AeadBackend,
{
    let mut reencrypted: HashMap<EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, EncryptedDataEncryptionKey> =
        HashMap::new();
    let mut updates = Vec::with_capacity(rows.len());
    for row in rows {
        let stored = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::try_from(row.clone())?;
        let (bare, encrypted_dek) = stored.separate()?;
        let new_encrypted_dek = match reencrypted.get(&encrypted_dek) {
            Some(new_encrypted_dek) => new_encrypted_dek.clone(),
            None => {
                let new_encrypted_dek = envelope.reencrypt_encrypted_dek(&encrypted_dek).await?;
                reencrypted.insert(encrypted_dek, new_encrypted_dek.clone());
                new_encrypted_dek
            }
        };
        updates.push((row.clone(), Encrypted::join(&bare, &new_encrypted_dek)?.into_value()));
    }
    Ok(updates)
}
use std::num::NonZeroU32;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKek, LocalKekId, LocalKekVersion, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{DekReusePolicy, KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions};
#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let kek_id = LocalKekId::try_from("orders".to_string())?;
let [v1, v2] = [1, 2].map(|version| LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::new(version).unwrap()));
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::with_options(
    LocalKmsProvider::new(
        kek_id.clone(),
        vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v1, SecretValue::new(vec![0x01; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
    )?,
    KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions::new().with_dek_reuse(DekReusePolicy::new()),
)?;
let mut rows = Vec::new();
for card in ["4111 1111 1111 1111", "5500 0000 0000 0004"] {
    rows.push(envelope.encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from(card)).await?.into_value());
}
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(LocalKmsProvider::new(
    kek_id.clone(),
    vec![
        LocalKek::from_secret(v1, SecretValue::new(vec![0x01; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
        LocalKek::from_secret(v2, SecretValue::new(vec![0x02; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
    ],
)?)?;
for (read, new) in reencrypt_values(&envelope, &rows).await? {
    if let Some(row) = rows.iter_mut().find(|row| **row == read) {
        *row = new;
    }
}
let encrypted_deks = rows
    .iter()
    .map(|row| Ok(Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::try_from(row.clone())?.separate()?.1))
    .collect::<KmsAeadResult<Vec<EncryptedDataEncryptionKey>>>()?;
assert_eq!(encrypted_deks[0], encrypted_deks[1]);
let disabled = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(LocalKmsProvider::new(
    kek_id,
    vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v2, SecretValue::new(vec![0x02; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
)?)?;
for row in &rows {
    let stored = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::try_from(row.clone())?;
    disabled.decrypt(b"orders/1042", &stored).await?;
}
Ok(())
}

Write each value back only where the stored bytes are still the ones read, with a compare-and-set such as UPDATE orders SET card = $new WHERE id = $id AND card = $read, or the store’s own conditional write. An unconditional write puts back the value the sweep read over one the application wrote in the meantime, which is then lost. A row the application rewrote keeps its new value, and the checks of steps 3 and 6 find it if its DEK is still under the old version.

reencrypt_dek does the same for one value. It gives each value an encrypted DEK of its own, so values that shared a DEK would then cost one KMS encryption each in the sweep and one KMS decryption each on a cache miss afterwards. The same reencrypt_encrypted_dek re-encrypts the DEKs stored apart from their values, such as those of the *_with_encrypted_dek calls, of beacons and of deterministic fields. A value or a DEK in a layout of 0.29 or earlier fails with LEGACY_FORMAT: upgrade_legacy_value and upgrade_legacy_encrypted_dek rewrite it in Tink’s format, with its DEK under the current version, see Upgrading stored values.

The DEK cache plays no part: the sweep decrypts through the provider and leaves the cache as it is, so it evicts no entry that serves reads. Every call encrypts again, also for a DEK already under the current version. The sweep needs KMS encrypt and decrypt on the KEK.

The kek_version() of the encrypted DEK that reencrypt_encrypted_dek returns names the KEK version the KMS encrypted it with. Under Cloud KMS, a sweep that ran before the new primary version had propagated shows the old version there, and the DEKs it names are re-encrypted again.

Encrypted keysets

An encrypted keyset is a KMS ciphertext as well, under the KEK version that encrypted it, and reencrypt_encrypted_dek refuses one with INVALID_KEY_FORMAT, since what the KMS decrypts from it is a serialised keyset. A sweep of the DEKs leaves every keyset under the old version, so a query or a reader that decrypts one fails once the old version is disabled:

  • a keyring’s export_bigquery_keyset and export_tink_keyset: export each keyset again, with the same selection, after reencrypt_deks and, under Cloud KMS, after the new primary version has propagated. The new export holds the same keys, encrypted under the KEK’s current version. Write its bytes wherever the old ones are kept: the query parameter of each KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN, a column or a setting that holds the keyset, and the file or the setting a Tink reader decrypts. See BigQuery;
  • the application’s own encrypted keyset, such as first_level_keyset in One fixed key: the provider decrypts it and encrypts it again, and the new bytes replace the old ones wherever they are kept:
use kms_aead::{EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, KmsAeadResult};

/// `first_level_keyset` encrypted again under the KEK version the provider encrypts with now.
async fn reencrypt_keyset<P: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider>(
    provider: &P,
    first_level_keyset: &EncryptedDataEncryptionKey,
) -> KmsAeadResult<EncryptedDataEncryptionKey> {
    let keyset = provider.decrypt_data_encryption_key(first_level_keyset).await?;
    provider.encrypt_data_encryption_key(&keyset).await
}
use std::num::NonZeroU32;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKek, LocalKekId, LocalKekVersion, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{DataEncryptionKey, KmsAeadEncryptionProvider};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let kek_id = LocalKekId::try_from("orders".to_string())?;
let [v1, v2] = [1, 2].map(|version| LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::new(version).unwrap()));
let keyset = DataEncryptionKey::from(SecretValue::new(vec![0x0a; 16]));
let first_level_keyset = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    kek_id.clone(),
    vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v1, SecretValue::new(vec![0x01; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
)?
.encrypt_data_encryption_key(&keyset)
.await?;
let rotated = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    kek_id.clone(),
    vec![
        LocalKek::from_secret(v1, SecretValue::new(vec![0x01; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
        LocalKek::from_secret(v2, SecretValue::new(vec![0x02; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
    ],
)?;
let reencrypted = reencrypt_keyset(&rotated, &first_level_keyset).await?;
let disabled = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    kek_id,
    vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v2, SecretValue::new(vec![0x02; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
)?;
assert_eq!(disabled.decrypt_data_encryption_key(&reencrypted).await?, keyset);
Ok(())
}

The check decrypts the bytes of a BigQuery keyset and of the application’s own keyset through the provider, as it does an encrypted DEK, with EncryptedDataEncryptionKey::from of the bytes. A Tink keyset is a google.crypto.tink.EncryptedKeyset around the KMS ciphertext, and its check is a decryption with the Tink reader that uses it.
