Pacing

The sweep makes two KMS calls per distinct encrypted DEK, and the check one, on top of the calls the application makes. Under a KMS with a request quota, as the cloud KMSs have, pace the sweep below it: a fixed number of DEKs per second, or a pause after each batch, with room left for the application. A sweep over the quota fails with the KMS’s error, such as ResourceExhausted on GCP, and so do the application’s own calls meanwhile.

The check

Once the old version is disabled, the KMS refuses every DEK still under it, so the check decrypts each distinct encrypted DEK through the provider, never through the DEK cache, which would serve a missed DEK from memory. It needs no AAD and decrypts no value, and it runs online, under every provider. Any error counts as a missed DEK:

use std::collections::HashSet;

use kms_aead::backends::AeadBackend;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::{
    Encrypted, EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, KmsAeadEnvelope,
    KmsAeadResult,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

/// The distinct encrypted DEKs of the values in `rows` that the provider fails to decrypt.
async fn missed_deks<P, B>(
    envelope: &KmsAeadEnvelope<P, B>,
    rows: &[Vec<u8>],
) -> KmsAeadResult<Vec<EncryptedDataEncryptionKey>>
where
    P: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider,
    B: AeadBackend,
{
    let mut encrypted_deks: HashSet<EncryptedDataEncryptionKey> = HashSet::new();
    for row in rows {
        let stored = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::try_from(row.clone())?;
        let (_, encrypted_dek) = stored.separate()?;
        encrypted_deks.insert(encrypted_dek);
    }
    let mut missed = Vec::new();
    for encrypted_dek in encrypted_deks {
        if envelope
            .provider()
            .decrypt_data_encryption_key(&encrypted_dek)
            .await
            .is_err()
        {
            missed.push(encrypted_dek);
        }
    }
    Ok(missed)
}
use std::num::NonZeroU32;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKek, LocalKekId, LocalKekVersion, LocalKmsProvider};
#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let kek_id = LocalKekId::try_from("orders".to_string())?;
let [v1, v2] = [1, 2].map(|version| LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::new(version).unwrap()));
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(LocalKmsProvider::new(
    kek_id.clone(),
    vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v1, SecretValue::new(vec![0x01; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
)?)?;
let stored = envelope.encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("4111 1111 1111 1111")).await?;
let rows = vec![stored.into_value()];
let disabled = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(LocalKmsProvider::new(
    kek_id,
    vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v2, SecretValue::new(vec![0x02; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
)?)?;
assert_eq!(missed_deks(&disabled, &rows).await?.len(), 1);
Ok(())
}

A keyring’s record names the KEK version of each of its DEKs, as the KMS reported it, so a first check makes no KMS call and runs before the old version is disabled: kek_versions_in_use of the record that Keyring::reload returns must list the new version alone. Under Cloud KMS, a reencrypt_deks that ran before the new primary version had propagated leaves the old version listed. A None there is a version the KMS did not report, so the first check fails on it. Once the old version is disabled, KeyringAdmin::reencrypt_deks runs again: it decrypts every DEK in the record through the provider, and fails on one still under the old version. See Rotating the KEK. An application that stores the kek_version of its own DEKs beside them checks those the same way.

Under the local KMS the check needs no disable. A provider that lists the new version alone, as the sample above builds it, refuses every DEK under the old version, so the check runs before the deploy that drops the old version from the list, and a missed DEK costs no outage and no redeploy.

Besides the store-less writers that step 1 of Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation waits for, two things keep using the old version after a rotation, and each sets when to disable it:

  • every DEK cache serves the DEKs it holds, without a KMS call, until their entries expire. A value the sweep missed fails to decrypt in the application only once its entry has expired, see DEK caching. The check does not wait for that, since it never reads a cache;
  • a process keeps the encrypted DEKs it read before the sweep. A keyring’s cached record holds the ones it had before reencrypt_deks until it is refreshed, at a change signal of the store or once it is older than record_ttl, 5 minutes by default, and Cloud Storage and S3 send no signal, see Record cache. A process whose revalidations fail keeps its record for as long as they fail, and logs “The keyring record could not be revalidated; the cached record stays in use” at WARN on each failure. An encrypted DEK the application read from its settings at startup, as in One fixed key, stays as it was read. The DEK cache keys its entries by those bytes, so once an entry expires, the KMS decrypts the old bytes again and fails under a disabled old version, with no reload. This is the wait of step 4 in Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation.

A KMS whose changes take time to propagate, such as Cloud KMS, can keep encrypting under the old version for a while after the rotation and keep decrypting under it for a while after it is disabled. Wait before the sweep and again before trusting the check, see the page of each provider.

Rolling back a rotation

A swept value’s DEK is under the new version only, so a rollback keeps the new version enabled: disabling it makes every swept value and every value written since the rotation unreadable, until it is enabled again. Three providers make the old version encrypt again:

  • Cloud KMS: make the old version the primary version again;
  • Azure Key Vault: pin the old version in AzureKmsKeyRef;
  • the local KMS: point with_encrypting_version at the old version.

Rolling back to the old version needs no new sweep. To retire the new version afterwards, run the procedure above with the old version as the current one.

The other two always encrypt under the latest key. Vault Transit encrypts under the latest version of the key, since the provider names no version in its request, and no older version becomes the latest again: a rotation is undone by rotating once more and sweeping to that third version. AWS KMS keeps every earlier key material decrypting and none can be disabled, so a rotation needs no rollback there.

DEK caching and reuse

Without a cache, every read of a value decrypts its DEK with one KMS call, and every encrypt encrypts a new DEK with another. The library has two ways to make fewer KMS calls:

  • DekCache, a cache of decrypted DEKs, which serves repeated reads of values under the same DEK without the KMS;
  • store-less DEK reuse, with a DekReusePolicy, where one DEK encrypts many values, so a write calls the KMS only when the DEK is renewed.

DEK cache

kms_aead::cache::DekCache holds plaintext DEKs a provider decrypted, keyed by the provider’s KEK and the encrypted DEK’s bytes. A per-call envelope has no cache unless the options give it one:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::cache::{DekCache, DekCacheOptions};
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{KmsAeadEnvelope, KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions};
use std::num::NonZeroUsize;
use std::time::Duration;

fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let cache = DekCache::new(
    DekCacheOptions::new()
        .with_ttl(Duration::from_secs(5 * 60))
        .with_max_entries(NonZeroUsize::new(10_000).expect("not zero")),
)?;

let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::with_options(
    provider,
    KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions::new().with_dek_cache(cache.clone()),
)?;
let _ = envelope;
Ok(())
}

decrypt, beacon and the *_with_encrypted_dek calls decrypt through the envelope’s cache, and a store-less encrypt keeps its DEK there. Clone shares the same cache, so one cache may serve several envelopes and several KEKs: an entry serves only a lookup that names the same KEK, and a key decrypted under one KEK never reaches a caller with the provider of another. A lookup, through DekCache::with_dek or an envelope call that decrypts, needs a KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, so the envelope of a producer over a PublicKeyKekProvider puts its DEK in a cache it shares and reads back only its own store-less DEK from it, to encrypt with it.

The cache:

  • serves an entry for its TTL after it was inserted, however often it is used, 15 minutes by default;
  • holds at most max_entries entries, 1024 by default, and evicts the oldest entry past it;
  • makes one provider call for concurrent misses of the same key, and every caller receives its result, the key or the error;
  • keeps no entry for a failed decryption, so during a KMS outage every call fails as fast as the KMS does;
  • zeroises a key when its entry leaves the cache: once it is evicted, invalidated, cleared, or purged after it expired.

DekCache::disabled() holds nothing, and every lookup calls the provider. It is what a per-call envelope has by default.

Sizing

Size max_entries above the number of distinct DEKs read per TTL. With per-call envelopes every value has its own DEK, so a cache helps only values read more than once within the TTL. With store-less writers the number of DEKs is the number of writers times the renewals per TTL, which is small.

A longer TTL saves more KMS calls, and keeps DEKs in memory for longer, see Key lifetime.

Invalidation

A disabled KMS key or a revoked IAM grant reaches the cached keys only when their entries expire. To drop one at once, invalidate it with the envelope’s own KEK:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::cache::{DekCache, DekCacheOptions};
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{KmsAeadEnvelope, KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEncryptionProvider;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::with_options(provider,
    KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions::new().with_dek_cache(DekCache::new(DekCacheOptions::new())?))?;
let (_dek, encrypted_dek) = envelope.generate_encrypted_dek().await?;

// One encrypted DEK
envelope
    .dek_cache()
    .invalidate(envelope.provider().kek(), &encrypted_dek);

// Every DEK
envelope.dek_cache().clear();
Ok(())
}

Pass envelope.provider().kek() to invalidate, with the KmsAeadEncryptionProvider trait in scope: a KekRef built by hand that differs from it matches no entry.

Key lifetime

The cache runs no thread and no task of its own. Expired entries are dropped at the next call on the cache, at purge_expired, or when the last clone of the cache is dropped. With no calls, the bytes of an expired key stay in memory. An application whose process can sit idle while the cache holds keys, and that needs the TTL to bound their lifetime in memory, calls purge_expired on a timer of its own:

use kms_aead::cache::{DekCache, DekCacheOptions};
fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let cache = DekCache::new(DekCacheOptions::new())?;

let purged = cache.clone();
tokio::spawn(async move {
    let mut interval = tokio::time::interval(std::time::Duration::from_secs(60));
    loop {
        interval.tick().await;
        purged.purge_expired();
    }
});
Ok(())
}

A key reaches the caller only inside the closure of DekCache::with_dek, and stays intact until the closure returns, whatever leaves the cache meanwhile. The closure must not keep a copy of the key: a copy taken out of it outlives the cache’s control of its lifetime.

Store-less DEK reuse

A KmsAeadEnvelope with a DekReusePolicy encrypts many values under one DEK and renews it at the first encryption past max_age, past max_uses or past max_bytes, whichever comes first. A renewal makes one KMS encrypt call. Each value still carries its encrypted DEK in Tink’s KMS envelope, so nothing is stored apart from the values, and values under earlier DEKs stay readable.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{DekReusePolicy, Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope, KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
use std::num::NonZeroU64;
use std::time::Duration;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::with_options(
    provider,
    KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions::new().with_dek_reuse(
        DekReusePolicy::new()
            .with_max_age(Duration::from_secs(60 * 60))
            .with_max_uses(NonZeroU64::new(1_000_000).expect("not zero")),
    ),
)?;

// One KMS encryption for both values
let first: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> =
    envelope.encrypt(b"events/1", &SecretValue::from("first")).await?;
let second: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> =
    envelope.encrypt(b"events/2", &SecretValue::from("second")).await?;

// Read with any envelope over the same KEK and algorithm
let decrypted_first: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt(b"events/1", &first).await?;
let decrypted_second: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt(b"events/2", &second).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted_first, SecretValue::from("first"));
assert_eq!(decrypted_second, SecretValue::from("second"));
Ok(())
}

The defaults are a max_age of 24 hours and a max_uses of 2^24. max_age must be longer than zero and max_uses at most 2^32, the bound for random 96-bit nonces under one key, otherwise the envelope fails to build with INVALID_DEK_REUSE_POLICY.

Volume limit

max_bytes counts the plaintext bytes encrypted under the DEK. Its default depends on the algorithm:

AlgorithmDefault max_bytesMost allowed
AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCM2^38 bytes (256 GiB)2^52 bytes
AES-256-GCM-SIVno limit beyond max_usesany
ChaCha20-Poly1305no limit beyond max_usesany

The default stays below the 2^38.5 bytes RFC 8446 allows under one AES-GCM key, and the most allowed is the 2^48 blocks of RFC 8452. AES-GCM-SIV and ChaCha20-Poly1305 have no volume bound beyond max_uses. The citations are in Data volume.

A max_bytes set in the policy replaces the default, under any algorithm. Above the most allowed, the envelope fails to build with INVALID_DEK_REUSE_POLICY.

A value that would take the count past max_bytes renews the DEK before it is encrypted. A value longer than max_bytes alone is encrypted under a new DEK of its own, as the per-call envelope does, and the current DEK stays in use for the next values.

The bounds count blocks of plaintext and AAD, and one more block per value, and max_bytes counts plaintext bytes only. At the default max_uses and an AAD of 16 bytes or less, the difference is at most 3 × 2^24 blocks beside the 2^34 blocks of the default.

The DEK lives in the cache

The envelope keeps only the encrypted DEK. Its plaintext lives in the envelope’s DekCache, which a store-less envelope always has: DekCache::new(DekCacheOptions::new()) unless the options give it another, and INVALID_DEK_REUSE_POLICY beside DekCache::disabled().

The first encryption after the DEK has left the cache renews it. So the DEK is renewed:

  • at the cache’s TTL, whatever max_age;
  • when the cache evicts it for capacity;
  • after invalidate with envelope.provider().kek(), or after clear.

A store-less encrypt never decrypts a DEK. A writer that renewed the DEK and finds it gone from the cache before it encrypts, evicted by a concurrent call or past a short TTL, encrypts with the DEK its renewal generated and puts it back in the cache, so a store-less writer over a PublicKeyKekProvider, which has no decryption, works as any other. An invalidate or clear that lands while a writer renews stops that writer from caching the new DEK: it encrypts with the DEK it generated, and the next writer renews.

In a cache shared with readers, size max_entries above the number of distinct DEKs read per TTL, or give the writer a cache of its own, otherwise reads evict the writer’s DEK and it renews more often than the policy says.

A writer that falls quiet leaves its DEK in memory until the cache’s next call or purge_expired, see Key lifetime. The values encrypted while the cache holds the DEK are readable by whoever reads the process’s memory in that window.

Deterministic encryption

Feature deterministic.

Randomised encryption gives a different cipher text every time, even for the same value. A deterministic algorithm gives equal cipher texts for equal values under one key and one AAD, so an encrypted column can be compared, joined or looked up by equality without decrypting it.

The library provides AES-SIV (RFC 5297) with a 64-byte key, DeterministicAlgorithm::Aes256Siv. Its name for FromStr and Display is aes-256-siv. DeterministicCipher takes a backend, as AeadCipher does, and draws its keys from the backend’s CSPRNG. Every backend encrypts and decrypts with the RustCrypto aes-siv crate, so a value encrypted under one backend decrypts under any other.

AES-SIV is not a FIPS-approved mode. A FIPS deployment keeps deterministic fields outside its validated boundary, also on aws-lc-fips, where the keys come from the FIPS module’s DRBG.

Deterministic encryption fits exact-match lookups where the leak of equality is acceptable. When a field only needs lookups and its stored value should stay randomised, a beacon fits instead, see Find rows by an encrypted value.

What it leaks

Be aware what equal cipher texts tell a reader of the stored data, without any key:

  • Equality: which rows hold the same value;
  • Frequency: how often each value occurs. On a column with few distinct values, such as a country, a status or a yes/no answer, the most frequent cipher text is the most frequent value, and the values can be read back from public statistics.

And since there is no nonce, a leaked deterministic key lets its holder test candidate values against the whole column offline.

So deterministic encryption fits fields that are looked up by equality and have high entropy, such as emails and external ids. Do not use it:

  • for a column with few distinct values or a skewed distribution;
  • when equality itself is sensitive;
  • when the value is never looked up by equality: randomised encryption leaks nothing of the kind.

The AAD narrows what leaks: values are equal only under the same AAD, so a per-tenant AAD keeps equal values of two tenants apart.

An AAD that a lookup can rebuild names the field and never the row, so a deterministic value also decrypts in any other row of the same field. A deterministic field never decides identity or authorisation on its own; where identity matters, the check reads a randomised copy of the field, bound to the row by its AAD.

Usage

DeterministicCipher encrypts and decrypts with a DataEncryptionKey of DeterministicAlgorithm::key_len() bytes, 64 for AES-SIV, and refuses a key of another length with INVALID_KEY_LENGTH, such as the 16 or 32 bytes of a randomised algorithm’s key. The application keeps track of which key serves which algorithm, as kms-aead-keyring does with its key ids.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Deterministic;
use kms_aead::{DeterministicAlgorithm, DeterministicCipher, Encrypted, KeyId};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let cipher = DeterministicCipher::<Ring>::new(DeterministicAlgorithm::Aes256Siv)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;

let aad = b"customers/email";
let first: Encrypted<SecretValue, Deterministic> =
    cipher.encrypt(KeyId::new(1), aad, &SecretValue::from("user@example.com"), &dek)?;
let second: Encrypted<SecretValue, Deterministic> =
    cipher.encrypt(KeyId::new(1), aad, &SecretValue::from("user@example.com"), &dek)?;

// Equal values give equal cipher texts, to look up by
assert_eq!(first, second);
let decrypted: SecretValue = cipher.decrypt(aad, &first, &dek)?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("user@example.com"));
Ok(())
}

An Encrypted<T, Deterministic> is [0x01][key id: u32 BE][synthetic IV: 16][ciphertext]: Tink’s 5-byte TINK prefix that names the key by its KeyId, then the output of RFC 5297, so Tink and BigQuery read it with a keyset of that key, see One fixed key. Its equality compares in constant time, and its Debug prints only the length, since the bytes tell which values are equal.

Keys encrypted by a KMS

The envelope generates and decrypts deterministic keys with its provider:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Deterministic;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{DeterministicAlgorithm, DeterministicCipher, Encrypted, KeyId, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;
let algorithm = DeterministicAlgorithm::Aes256Siv;

// A new key, and the same key encrypted, to store
let (dek, encrypted_dek) = envelope.generate_encrypted_deterministic_dek(algorithm).await?;

// Later: decrypt the stored key
let decrypted_dek = envelope.decrypt_deterministic_dek(algorithm, &encrypted_dek).await?;

let cipher = DeterministicCipher::<Ring>::new(algorithm)?;
let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Deterministic> =
    cipher.encrypt(KeyId::new(1), b"aad", &SecretValue::from("value"), &dek)?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = cipher.decrypt(b"aad", &encrypted, &decrypted_dek)?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("value"));
Ok(())
}

decrypt_deterministic_dek calls the provider every time and does not go through the envelope’s DEK cache. To cache deterministic keys, use DekCache::with_dek, which holds the keys of both kinds of algorithm.

DeterministicCipher::generate_encrypted_dek does the same with any provider.

Beacons

A beacon, or blind index, finds rows by a field’s value without decrypting the column: an HMAC-SHA256 of the value under a beacon key, truncated, stored beside the encrypted field. A query calculates the beacon of the value it looks for and matches it by equality. Beacon<T> holds the bytes to store, and the beacon key is a 32-byte key encrypted by the KMS, as a DEK is:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{
    Beacon, BeaconAlgorithm, BeaconBits, BeaconMac, Encrypted, KeyId, KmsAeadEnvelope,
    KmsAeadResult, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

// The application's id of its beacon key, which every beacon names
const EMAIL_BEACON_KEY: KeyId = KeyId::new(1);

impl Email {
    // The beacon of an email matches regardless of case
    fn new(email: &str) -> Self {
        Email(email.to_lowercase())
    }
}

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

// Once, by an administrative job: the beacon key, stored beside the application's settings
let (_, encrypted_dek) = BeaconMac::<Ring>::new(BeaconAlgorithm::HmacSha256)?
    .generate_encrypted_dek(envelope.provider())
    .await?;

// On every write: the field and its beacon
let email = Email::new("Alice@Example.com");
let encrypted: Encrypted<Email, Envelope> =
    envelope.encrypt(b"users/1042/email", &email).await?;
let beacon: Beacon<Email> = envelope
    .beacon(EMAIL_BEACON_KEY, b"users/email", BeaconBits::DEFAULT, &email, &encrypted_dek)
    .await?;

// A lookup: the beacon of the value looked for, then a decryption of each matching row
let probe: Beacon<Email> = envelope
    .beacon(
        EMAIL_BEACON_KEY,
        b"users/email",
        BeaconBits::DEFAULT,
        &Email::new("alice@example.com"),
        &encrypted_dek,
    )
    .await?;
assert_eq!(probe, beacon);
let decrypted: Email = envelope.decrypt(b"users/1042/email", &encrypted).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, email);
Ok(())
}
  • the context, users/email here, names the field and never a row, so that equal values in one field give equal beacons. A beacon of one field never matches a beacon of another;
  • the key id, a KeyId, is the application’s number for the beacon key, written into every beacon so that a column can hold beacons of two keys at once;
  • the beacon key is decrypted through the envelope’s DEK cache, as a DEK is.

Beacon<T> is stored as Encrypted is, with serde and the postgres and sqlx features, and read back through TryFrom, which checks its layout, see Stored values. See Formats for its bytes.

A message authentication code (MAC) over a value’s bytes, which is not truncated and never serves a lookup, is in Beacons and MACs.

What a beacon leaks

A beacon column leaks to anyone who reads it, without any key:

  • Equality: which rows are likely to hold the same value;
  • Frequency: how often each value occurs, as far as the bits tell values apart.

And a leaked beacon key lets its holder test candidate values against the whole column offline.

So beacons fit fields that are looked up and have many distinct values, such as emails and external ids. Do not use them for low-cardinality fields, such as a status, a country or a yes/no answer, whose values the frequencies give away.

Bits

BeaconBits sets how many bits of the HMAC a beacon keeps, 1 to 256; BeaconBits::DEFAULT is 16. With n distinct values in a column and b bits, about n / 2^b values share each beacon:

  • fewer bits: more values share a beacon, so a lookup returns more rows, which the application decrypts and filters by the plaintext. A column of short beacons leaks less;
  • more bits: fewer rows to filter, down to one per value once 2^b is far above n, and the column then leaks which rows hold equal values as a deterministic column does.

An equal beacon never proves an equal value: a lookup always decrypts the rows it found and compares the plaintexts. The bits enter the HMAC, so beacons of one value under different bits never match: after a change of a column’s bits, its stored beacons match no lookup until a re-index computes them again. The numbers of the default for a column of 1,000,000 emails are in Find rows by an encrypted value.

Normalisation

A beacon is the HMAC of the bytes the Plaintext type encodes, so Alice@Example.com and alice@example.com give different beacons unless the type normalises the value. Normalising is the application’s job: the newtype normalises when it is made, as Email::new lowercases above, and every write and every lookup goes through it.

BigQuery

BigQuery’s SQL has no HMAC-SHA256 with a KMS-encrypted DEK, so a query in BigQuery cannot calculate a beacon. It matches beacons the application calculated and passed in as query parameters.

Rotating a beacon key

Rotating a beacon key in store-less mode is manual. Generate the new key and keep both, write beacons under the new one, query with a beacon under each key until a re-index has rewritten every stored beacon, then drop the old key. Beacon::key_id tells which key wrote a stored beacon. kms-aead-keyring does this with key versions, see Beacons.

Streaming encryption

Feature stream.

The library encrypts a value of any length as a streamed value: a header, then chunks of 16 KiB, each one authenticated on its own and bound to its position. A writer encrypts a value whose length it does not know in advance, holding one chunk in memory. A reader decrypts any byte range by fetching only the chunks that cover it.

Streaming fits files, uploads, backups and other objects in a store that serves Range requests. Fields of a record are Encrypted values, see Stored values.

The value comes in three forms, which differ in the bytes in front of the Cobblestone message: the salt, the key commitment and the chunks that every form shares, see Format.

  • the prefixed form starts with Tink’s output prefix, 0x01 and a key id, as every keyring value does. AeadCipher::stream_encryptor writes it, and it is the only form a keyring writes and reads;
  • the envelope form starts with the encrypted DEK, as Tink’s KMS envelope does, so the value decrypts with nothing stored beside it. KmsAeadEnvelope::stream_encryptor writes it;
  • the bare form is the Cobblestone message alone, for a caller that keeps the key id or the encrypted DEK itself. The _bare calls of AeadCipher and KmsAeadEnvelope write it.

Every form uses AES-256-GCM, so the DEK is 32 bytes. The derivation binds the AAD into the value, and the decryptor refuses a wrong key, a wrong AAD or a modified header with STREAM_KEY_COMMITMENT before it decrypts a chunk.

Encrypting and decrypting

A value that arrives as a Stream of Bytes, such as a request body or an object-store download, goes through two types that need no bridge to tokio’s I/O traits, and comes back out as a Stream. Each holds one chunk in memory, whatever the length of the value:

  • EncryptingStream takes a Stream of plaintext and yields the value: the header first, then one item per chunk, the final chunk when its source ends;
  • DecryptingStream takes the stored bytes after the header and yields the plaintext, one item per chunk, and only once the chunk is authentic. It reads its source to the end, which is the end of the value.

Both yield KmsAeadResult<Bytes>. A value that is cut, modified or extended ends in an error item, STREAM_LENGTH or DECRYPT, never in a clean end. The error is the last item. An error of the source is STREAM_IO. A source that is not Unpin goes through Box::pin.

The encryptor and the decryptor come from the cipher: AeadCipher::stream_encryptor and stream_decryptor make the prefixed form, stream_encryptor_bare and stream_decryptor_bare the bare one. A decryptor is made from the header and the DEK.

A value read from start to end needs no stored length. Every chunk but the last is 16,400 bytes as stored and the last one is shorter, so a chunk is decrypted as soon as 16,400 bytes of it have arrived, and the bytes left when the source ends are the final chunk. A value cut at a chunk boundary, or less than a tag (16 bytes) into a chunk, ends without that shorter chunk, STREAM_LENGTH. A value cut further into a chunk, or followed by more bytes, ends in a final chunk that is not authentic, DECRYPT. A ranged read does need the stored length: it fetches the chunks of a range without reading what comes before, so only the length tells it where the final chunk is and how long it is, see Reading a range.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher};
use std::sync::Arc;

use bytes::Bytes;
use futures::{stream, StreamExt, TryStreamExt};
use kms_aead::stream::{DecryptingStream, EncryptingStream, StreamFormat};

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;
let body = stream::iter([Bytes::from_static(b"a request "), Bytes::from_static(b"body")])
    .map(Ok::<_, std::io::Error>);
let encryptor = cipher.stream_encryptor_bare(b"upload/7/file", &dek)?;
let stored: Vec<Bytes> = EncryptingStream::new(encryptor, body).try_collect().await?;
let stored = stored.concat();

let header_len = StreamFormat::Bare.header_len();
let header = &stored[..header_len];
let decryptor = cipher.stream_decryptor_bare(b"upload/7/file", header, &dek)?;
let after_header = stream::iter([Bytes::copy_from_slice(&stored[header_len..])]).map(Ok::<_, std::io::Error>);
let response: Vec<Bytes> = DecryptingStream::new(Arc::new(decryptor), after_header).try_collect().await?;
assert_eq!(response.concat(), b"a request body");
Ok(())
}

A caller that holds the whole stored value from its first byte, such as a download, does not split the header off: KmsAeadEnvelope and Keyring have decrypting_stream and decrypting_reader, which read the header from the front of the source, make the decryptor and return the stream or the reader positioned at the first chunk, see With an envelope and With a keyring.

Full example available here.

The tokio adapters

Two wrapper types and two functions put the encryptor and the decryptor on tokio’s AsyncWrite and AsyncRead. Each holds one chunk in memory, whatever the length of the value:

  • EncryptingWriter encrypts what is written to it and writes the value to a sink. Call shutdown on it: only the shutdown writes the final chunk;
  • DecryptingReader reads a value from a source positioned after the header and yields its plaintext;
  • encrypt_stream and decrypt_stream run a whole source through a writer or a reader into a sink.

A std::io::Error that a wrapper returns for a failure of the value, such as a chunk that is not authentic, carries the KmsAeadError as its inner error: error.get_ref() and downcast_ref::<KmsAeadError>() find it.

Reading a range

A ranged read has three steps:

  • plan_chunk_read on the value’s StreamFormat takes the stored length of the value, as the store reports it, and a plaintext range, and returns a ChunkReadPlan: the chunks that cover the range and the stored bytes to fetch. .. is the whole plaintext. It needs no key, so a stored length that the length rules refuse fails before any KMS call. StreamFormat::Prefixed and StreamFormat::Bare are the prefixed and bare forms, and EnvelopeStreamHeader::form is the form of an envelope value;
  • the caller fetches plan.ciphertext() from the store, a byte range counted from the start of the value;
  • decrypt_chunk_read on a decryptor takes the plan and the fetched bytes, and returns the plaintext of exactly the range asked for, as a Plaintext type: Vec<u8> or SecretValue.

One zip entry of 63 KB at offset 1,000,000 of a value of 5,000,000 bytes covers four chunks, and the read fetches 65,600 bytes. An entry that starts in the last byte of a chunk covers five chunks.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::stream::StreamFormat;
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher};
use kms_aead::stream::encrypt_stream;
#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;
let plaintext = vec![7_u8; 5_000_000];
let source = &plaintext[..];

// Encrypting: the bare form, with a DEK the caller holds, from any `AsyncRead` such as a file
let encryptor = cipher.stream_encryptor_bare(b"backups/2026-10", &dek)?;
let mut stored = Vec::new();
encrypt_stream(encryptor, source, &mut stored).await?;

// Decrypting: the stored length goes into the plan, which needs no key, and the header comes from
// the store
let plan = StreamFormat::Bare.plan_chunk_read(stored.len() as u64, 1_000_000..1_064_512)?;
assert_eq!(plan.chunks(), 61..65);
assert_eq!(plan.ciphertext(), 1_000_456..1_066_056);

let fetched = &stored[plan.ciphertext().start as usize..plan.ciphertext().end as usize];
let decryptor = cipher.stream_decryptor_bare(
    b"backups/2026-10",
    &stored[..StreamFormat::Bare.header_len()],
    &dek,
)?;
let entry: Vec<u8> = decryptor.decrypt_chunk_read(&plan, fetched)?;
assert_eq!(entry, plaintext[1_000_000..1_064_512]);
Ok(())
}

Make one decryptor per request, in an Arc: its methods take &self, and the ranges, threads and DecryptingReader of that request share it. The header is read once per request and no range fetches it again. Do not cache the decryptor across requests: it decrypts until it is dropped, see Limits.

With an envelope

Streamed values come in the same modes as other values, an envelope or a keyring, see Choosing a mode. An envelope chooses and encrypts the DEK as encrypt does, and writes one of two forms:

  • stream_encryptor writes the envelope form, one object that holds everything a reader needs. A reader learns the header’s length from its first 4 bytes, the length w of the encrypted DEK, and every chunk sits 4 + w bytes after its place in the bare form. A KEK rotation rewrites every object;
  • stream_encryptor_bare writes the bare form and returns the encrypted DEK, which the application stores beside the object, such as in its metadata or its database row. The chunks sit at fixed offsets, and a KEK rotation re-encrypts the stored DEKs with reencrypt_encrypted_dek and leaves the objects as they are.
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use kms_aead::stream::{encrypt_stream, EnvelopeStreamHeader};
#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("backups".to_string())?)?;
let plaintext = vec![7_u8; 5_000_000];
let source = &plaintext[..];
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<_, Ring>::new(provider)?;

// Encrypting: the envelope form, with the encrypted DEK inside the value, from any `AsyncRead`
let encryptor = envelope.stream_encryptor(b"backups/2026-10").await?;
let mut stored = Vec::new();
encrypt_stream(encryptor, source, &mut stored).await?;

// Decrypting: the header's length from its first 4 bytes, then the header
let header_len = EnvelopeStreamHeader::header_len(&stored[..4])?;
let header = EnvelopeStreamHeader::try_from(&stored[..header_len])?;
let plan = header.form().plan_chunk_read(stored.len() as u64, 1_000_000..1_064_512)?;
let fetched = &stored[plan.ciphertext().start as usize..plan.ciphertext().end as usize];
let decryptor = envelope.stream_decryptor(b"backups/2026-10", &header).await?;
let entry: Vec<u8> = decryptor.decrypt_chunk_read(&plan, fetched)?;
assert_eq!(entry, plaintext[1_000_000..1_064_512]);
Ok(())
}

A caller that reads the value from its first byte fetches no header separately: decrypting_stream takes the stored value as a Stream of Bytes, and decrypting_reader as an AsyncRead. Each reads the first 4 bytes, w, and checks it before it reads on or calls the KMS, then reads the header and returns the adapter at the first chunk.

use bytes::Bytes;
use futures::{stream, StreamExt, TryStreamExt};
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::stream::EncryptingStream;
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("backups".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<_, Ring>::new(provider)?;
let upload = stream::iter([Bytes::from_static(b"a request body")]).map(Ok::<_, std::io::Error>);
let encryptor = envelope.stream_encryptor(b"backups/2026-10").await?;
let stored: Vec<Bytes> = EncryptingStream::new(encryptor, upload).try_collect().await?;
let download = stream::iter(stored).map(Ok::<_, std::io::Error>);
// The download streams the object from its first byte to its last
let response = envelope.decrypting_stream(b"backups/2026-10", download).await?;
let plaintext: Vec<Bytes> = response.try_collect().await?;
assert_eq!(plaintext.concat(), b"a request body");
Ok(())
}

stream_decryptor_bare decrypts the bare form from its 56-byte header and the stored encrypted DEK, see Large files. Full example of reading a range from both forms available here.

With a keyring

The stream feature of kms-aead-keyring adds Encryptor::stream_encryptor, Keyring::stream_decryptor and, for a value served whole, Keyring::decrypting_stream and decrypting_reader, which read the 61-byte header from the front of the source. The first two return the same StreamEncryptor and StreamDecryptor, in the prefixed form, under the active version for a write and under the version the header names for a read. The keyring applies its lifecycle states and its compromised_reads policy as decrypt does, and a FieldAad that carries an Interop is refused, since neither Tink nor BigQuery reads a streamed value.

The lifecycle states and the policy apply when the decryptor is made. A decryptor holds the key derived for its value, never the DEK, and keeps decrypting after the version is retired, destroyed or flagged compromised, and after a shred of the keyring, until it is dropped. Make it per request, as above, so that a shred takes effect for the next request. A request that serves only the whole value, with no ranges, calls decrypting_stream or decrypting_reader, which make their own decryptor.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry,
    KeyringRegistryOptions,
};
use std::sync::Arc;

use kms_aead_keyring::stream::{
    ChunkReadPlan, DecryptingReader, EncryptingWriter, PrefixedStreamHeader, StreamDecryptor,
    StreamEncryptor, StreamFormat,
};
use tokio::io::AsyncWriteExt;
/// An object of an object store that serves `Range` requests, held in memory.
struct Object(Vec<u8>);
impl Object {
    async fn read(&self, range: std::ops::Range<u64>) -> std::io::Result<Vec<u8>> {
        Ok(self.0[range.start as usize..range.end as usize].to_vec())
    }
    async fn read_from(&self, offset: u64) -> std::io::Result<&[u8]> {
        Ok(&self.0[offset as usize..])
    }
    fn size(&self) -> u64 {
        self.0.len() as u64
    }
}
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("uploads".to_string())?)?;
let admin = registry
    .open_or_create(provider, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"), KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
let keyring = admin.keyring();
let upload_id = EntityId::try_from("upload-7")?;
let upload = vec![7_u8; 1_200_000];
let mut body = &upload[..];
let mut object_writer = Vec::new();

const UPLOAD_FILE: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("upload"),
    FieldName::from_static("file"),
);

// Encrypting an upload: the length is not known in advance and nothing of the body is held whole
let aad = UPLOAD_FILE.aad(&upload_id);
let encryptor: StreamEncryptor<Ring> = keyring.encryptor().await.stream_encryptor(&aad).await?;
let mut writer = EncryptingWriter::new(encryptor, &mut object_writer);
tokio::io::copy(&mut body, &mut writer).await?;
writer.shutdown().await?;
let stored_len: u64 = writer.stored_len_written();
let object = Object(object_writer);
assert_eq!(object.size(), stored_len);

// Serving one zip entry: the plans first, from the store's size, so that a size the length rules
// refuse reaches neither the store nor the KMS
let last: ChunkReadPlan = StreamFormat::Prefixed.plan_final_chunk_read(object.size())?;
let plan: ChunkReadPlan = StreamFormat::Prefixed.plan_chunk_read(object.size(), 1_000_000..1_064_512)?;

// Then the header once and one decryptor per request, which reads the final chunk, so that the
// length is authentic before any range is served
let head: Vec<u8> = object.read(0..StreamFormat::Prefixed.header_len() as u64).await?;
let header = PrefixedStreamHeader::try_from(head.as_slice())?;
let decryptor: Arc<StreamDecryptor<Ring>> = Arc::new(keyring.stream_decryptor(&aad, &header).await?);
decryptor.decrypt_chunk_read::<Vec<u8>>(&last, &object.read(last.ciphertext()).await?)?;
let bytes: Vec<u8> = object.read(plan.ciphertext()).await?;
let entry: Vec<u8> = decryptor.decrypt_chunk_read(&plan, &bytes)?;

// The whole value as a response body, over the same decryptor
let body = DecryptingReader::new(
    decryptor.clone(),
    object.read_from(StreamFormat::Prefixed.header_len() as u64).await?,
);
assert_eq!(entry, upload[1_000_000..1_064_512]);
drop(body);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

A write stores the encryption marker of the encryptor’s record beside the object’s own record, as it does for fields, so that a sweep finds the object by the marker, see Sweeps.

Full example available here.

Driving the I/O yourself

StreamEncryptor and StreamDecryptor take bytes and return bytes, and read and write nothing themselves, so they run under any runtime; the adapters above are built on them. An application that reads its source with another runtime, or in a loop of its own, calls them directly.

An encryptor takes the plaintext in pieces of any size: the value is header(), the bytes every push returns, then the bytes finish returns. A decryptor decrypts the chunks of a plan from plan_chunk_read, as in Reading a range, and .. plans the whole value.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::stream::StreamFormat;
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher};
fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;
let mut encryptor = cipher.stream_encryptor_bare(b"backups/2026-10", &dek)?;
let mut stored = encryptor.header().to_vec();
for piece in [b"first piece, ".as_slice(), b"second piece".as_slice()] {
    stored.extend(encryptor.push(piece)?);
}
stored.extend(encryptor.finish()?);

let header = &stored[..StreamFormat::Bare.header_len()];
let decryptor = cipher.stream_decryptor_bare(b"backups/2026-10", header, &dek)?;
let plan = StreamFormat::Bare.plan_chunk_read(stored.len() as u64, ..)?;
let plaintext: Vec<u8> = decryptor.decrypt_chunk_read(&plan, &stored[plan.ciphertext().start as usize..])?;
assert_eq!(plaintext, b"first piece, second piece");
Ok(())
}

Full example available here.

Limits

Be aware of these when serving streamed values:

  • the stored length a ranged read plans with is the store’s word until the final chunk has been decrypted. A store that cuts an object inside a chunk and reports the shorter size passes the length rules and every range that stays clear of the cut chunk. A range counted from the end of the value needs the final chunk first. Read the final chunk through plan_final_chunk_read once per object before the first range, or keep the length and a hash of the plaintext in a record you trust and compare the end of the final chunk’s plaintext() with it;
  • DecryptingReader, DecryptingStream and decrypt_stream yield the plaintext of a chunk as soon as the chunk is authentic, so a value cut or modified further on has already given its earlier bytes out when the error comes. Write to a place you can discard. A reader stopped before its clean end has authenticated the chunks it yielded and nothing of the length;
  • the same three read their source to its end and decrypt the final chunk only then. Bytes after the value make its final chunk fail with DECRYPT, so a value inside a longer source goes through a source cut to its length, such as tokio’s AsyncReadExt::take, and a source that stays open after the value holds its final chunk back;
  • an EncryptingWriter dropped before its shutdown leaves a value that no reader decrypts whole;
  • a decryptor keeps the key derived for its value until it is dropped, and decrypts with it after a shred, a destroy or a compromised flag of its version. A decryptor cached across requests outlives them, so build one per request;
  • an encryptor, and an EncryptingWriter or EncryptingStream over it, keeps the key derived for its value until it is dropped: a compromised flag or a destroy of its version does not stop an upload in progress;
  • STREAM_IO names a failure of the source or the sink of encrypt_stream or decrypt_stream, or of the source of an EncryptingStream or a DecryptingStream. The retry is the whole stream again from the start, with a new encryptor or a fresh read of the source;
  • a streamed value is not an Encrypted<T, F>: it has no plaintext type and carries no signature or MAC. Origin or integrity that a party without the DEK can check is a detached Signature<T> or Mac<T> over a digest of the plaintext, see Signatures and MACs;
  • one DEK is an AES-256-GCM key for randomised values and the key of HMAC-SHA-512 for streamed values. A deployment that wants one key per primitive keeps a keyring for streamed values apart from the keyring of its fields;
  • a sweep re-encrypts a streamed value whole: decrypt it under the version it names and encrypt it again under the active one. This release has no helper for that.

The errors a streamed value adds are in Error handling.

Format

A streamed value is one message of C2SP chunked encryption, Cobblestone-256: keys derived with HKDF-SHA-512 and chunks of AES-256-GCM. C2SP is the Community Cryptography Specification Project, a home for small specifications that come with test vectors. The prefixed form puts Tink’s output prefix, 5 bytes, in front and the envelope form 4 + w bytes of the encrypted DEK, so an implementation of Cobblestone-256, such as the cryptography.cobblestone module of pyca/cryptography, decrypts a prefixed value from offset 5 and an envelope-form value from offset 4 + w, with the DEK. The Go package filippo.io/cobblestone implements Cobblestone-128 only, on AES-128-GCM, and reads none of these values. The bytes, the length rules and the seeking rules are in Formats.

The library runs the 35 vectors of Project Wycheproof for the specification, in every form, on every backend, see c2sp_chunked_encryption_aes_256_gcm_test.json.

Signatures and MACs

The library signs an application’s bytes with an asymmetric key held by a KMS, or by the local KMS, and verifies the signature without the KMS: in Rust on any crypto backend, and in a browser with WebCrypto. Beside the signature it offers a MAC, a keyed hash, under a symmetric key version of a keyring, for integrity inside one trust domain. Signatures are Ed25519 and ECDSA on P-256 with SHA-256; MACs are HMAC-SHA256 and HMAC-SHA512. Neither hides the bytes, see Envelope encryption for that.

Signature or MAC

SignatureMAC
Keyan asymmetric key: the private half signs, the public half verifiesone symmetric key: it computes and verifies
Who verifiesanyone holding the public key, with no KMS and no secret: another service, a browser, an offline toola holder of the key: a process with KMS decrypt on the keyring’s KEK and read access to its record
Who can forgethe holder of the private key onlyevery verifier
Costone KMS call per signaturean HMAC in process

Use a signature for a public archive, a document a browser checks, a bundle another organisation verifies, or a record whose writer must be told apart from its readers, such as the keyring record. Use a MAC for bytes that one application’s processes hand each other: a cache entry, a job, a token, a row whose integrity must not depend on the store. Be aware a MAC never proves origin to anyone who could not have computed it.

Signing and verifying

A signed value has a payload type and the bytes that stand for it. The payload type is signed with the bytes, so a signature over one kind of document never verifies as another kind. The library signs the SHA-256 of the payload, so a KMS sees the same few bytes whatever the size of the document. Canonicalise a document that may be serialised again before it is signed.

A serde type implements SignableType to name its payload type, and the call passes it as JsonEncoded::new(value), whose payload is the compact JSON of the value. Signer::sign and Verifier::verify_signed take and return the JsonEncoded, and the outer type, Signature or Signed, says what the bytes are for. A type with another encoding implements Signable itself, see Another encoding.

A Signer signs through a signing provider. Its verifying key goes into a VerifyingKeySet, the public keys a verifier trusts, which to_jwks writes as a JWK Set (RFC 7517, a JSON document that lists public keys) and from_jwks reads back:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalSigningKeyName, LocalSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signature, Signer, SigningAlgorithm, Verifier, VerifyingKeySet};
use kms_aead::{JsonEncoded, KmsAeadResult, SignedPayloadType, SignableType};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct Invoice {
    number: String,
    customer: String,
    currency: String,
    total_cents: u64,
}

impl SignableType for Invoice {
    const PAYLOAD_TYPE: SignedPayloadType =
        SignedPayloadType::from_static("application/vnd.example.invoice+json");
}

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
// The local provider stands in for a KMS key, see the providers below
let provider = LocalSigningProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(
    LocalSigningKeyName::try_from("invoices".to_string())?,
    SigningAlgorithm::Ed25519,
)?;
let signer = Signer::<_, Ring>::new(provider).await?;

// Once, by the signer's owner: the public key as a JWK Set, for verifiers and browsers
let jwks: String = VerifyingKeySet::new([signer.verifying_key().clone()])?.to_jwks()?;

let invoice = JsonEncoded::new(Invoice {
    number: "INV-1042".to_string(),
    customer: "Northwind Traders".to_string(),
    currency: "EUR".to_string(),
    total_cents: 12_500,
});
let stored: Vec<u8> = signer.sign(&invoice).await?.into_value();

// Anywhere: no KMS, no secret
let verifier = Verifier::<Ring>::new(VerifyingKeySet::from_jwks(&jwks)?)?;
let signature = Signature::<JsonEncoded<Invoice>>::try_from(stored)?;
verifier.verify(&invoice, &signature)?;

let forged = JsonEncoded::new(Invoice { total_cents: 1_250, ..invoice.0 });
assert_eq!(verifier.verify(&forged, &signature).unwrap_err().code(), "SIGNATURE_INVALID");
Ok(())
}

A verifier in another language checks the exact bytes that were signed. Publish the payload bytes with the signature, as to_payload returned them, rather than rebuilding them from fields: another serialiser may order the members or format numbers differently.

The signing providers sit beside the encryption providers, each in the feature of its KMS and named by a key reference that names one key version, never “the latest”: GcpKmsSigningProvider (Ed25519 on software keys, P-256 on software and HSM keys), AwsKmsSigningProvider (name the key by id or ARN, never by an alias), AzureKmsSigningProvider (P-256 only, since Key Vault signs no Ed25519), HcVaultTransitSigningProvider and LocalSigningProvider, in every build. Be aware the private key of a LocalSigningProvider is in process memory, with no IAM, no audit log and no HSM.

Full example available here.

Attached and detached

Signer::sign returns a detached Signature<T>, a small value to store beside the bytes, and sign_attached a Signed<T> that carries the payload. Verifier::verify checks a detached signature against the payload as read; Verifier::verify_signed checks an attached value and only then decodes and returns its payload. MACs have the same pair: a detached Mac<T>, checked with verify_mac on a keyring, and an attached WithMac<T>, which mac_attached computes and verify_with_mac checks before it returns the payload. The four types are stored as Encrypted is, with serde, postgres and sqlx, and none implements PartialEq. The bytes are in Formats.

Another encoding

A type whose payload is not JSON implements Signable itself: to_payload returns the bytes that are signed, and from_payload decodes the bytes of an attached value after they verified. Both fail with INVALID_PAYLOAD. This statement is signed as its UTF-8 text:

use kms_aead::errors::KmsAeadEncryptionError;
use kms_aead::{KmsAeadResult, Signable, SignableType, SignedPayloadType};

struct Statement(String);

impl SignableType for Statement {
    const PAYLOAD_TYPE: SignedPayloadType =
        SignedPayloadType::from_static("text/vnd.example.statement");
}

impl Signable for Statement {
    fn to_payload(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<Vec<u8>> {
        Ok(self.0.clone().into_bytes())
    }

    fn from_payload(payload: Vec<u8>) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_utf8(payload).map(Statement).map_err(|_| {
            KmsAeadEncryptionError::create("INVALID_PAYLOAD", "The payload is not UTF-8 text")
        })
    }
}

Payloads that do not decode

verify_signed and verify_with_mac decode a payload after it verified. A JsonEncoded payload that is not the JSON of its type fails with INVALID_PAYLOAD, which never quotes the payload, and logs a WARN event with an outline of the JSON, which the type’s policy sets as it does for an encrypted value, see JSON that does not decode.

Key ids and rotation

Every value starts with a key id that selects the key the verifier tries. The id of a signing key is the first four bytes of its JWK thumbprint (RFC 7638), so a signer and every verifier agree on it without a registry. A publisher with a numbering of its own sets it with Signer::with_key_id. A key set holds one key per id, and a verifier refuses an id its set lacks with SIGNING_KEY_UNKNOWN. The set is the trust root: a key in it verifies, and a key removed from it verifies nothing, whatever the KMS still holds. Keep one set per publisher.

A signing key rotates by publishing before signing:

  1. create the new key version in the KMS, or the new key on AWS KMS, or a new local version;
  2. add the new signer’s verifying_key() to the published key set beside the current key;
  3. switch the signers to the new version once the verifiers have refreshed the set;
  4. keep the old key in the set for as long as its signatures are verified. Removing it revokes every signature under it, which is the response to a compromised key.

Key formats

Every loader of a key takes the format of its bytes as an enum from kms_aead::formats, and no loader guesses it:

  • PrivateKeyFormat: Pkcs8Der or Pkcs8Pem, for LocalSigningKey and LocalAsymmetricKek;
  • PublicKeyFormat: Raw, SpkiDer, SpkiPem or Jwk, for VerifyingKey. An asymmetric KEK’s public key takes all but Raw;
  • SymmetricKeyFormat: Raw, Base64 or Jwk, for LocalKek.

openssl genpkey -algorithm ed25519 writes the PEM that Pkcs8Pem reads. from_file and from_env read a key of up to 32 KiB from a file or a variable:

use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::formats::{PrivateKeyFormat, PublicKeyFormat};
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalSigningKey, LocalSigningKeyVersion};
use kms_aead::signing::{SigningAlgorithm, VerifyingKey};
use kms_aead::{KeyId, KmsAeadResult};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let pem = "-----BEGIN PRIVATE KEY-----
MC4CAQAwBQYDK2VwBCIEIJ1hsZ3v/VpguoRK9JLsLMREScVpezJpGXA7rAMcrn9g
-----END PRIVATE KEY-----
";
let key = LocalSigningKey::from_secret(
    LocalSigningKeyVersion::from(NonZeroU32::MIN),
    SecretValue::from(pem),
    PrivateKeyFormat::Pkcs8Pem,
)?;

// The public key out in one format and back in
let spki = key.verifying_key().encode(PublicKeyFormat::SpkiPem)?;
let verifying =
    VerifyingKey::from_bytes(SigningAlgorithm::Ed25519, &spki, PublicKeyFormat::SpkiPem)?;
assert_eq!(verifying.key_id(), KeyId::new(0x90fa_cafe));
Ok(())
}

In a browser

A browser verifies a detached signature with WebCrypto: import the keys of the JWK Set, read the key id from the signature, hash the payload, build the pre-image and call verify. WebCrypto takes the signature as r || s for ECDSA and as the 64 bytes of RFC 8032 for Ed25519, which SubtleCrypto.verify supports in Chrome 137, Firefox 129 and Safari 17. P-256 works in every engine with WebCrypto.

// jwks: the parsed JWK Set; payloadType: the type's payload type string;
// payload and signature: Uint8Array. Returns the JWK that verified, or null.
async function verifyDetached(jwks, payloadType, payload, signature) {
  if (signature.length < 6 || signature[0] !== 0x01) {
    throw new Error("not a detached signature");
  }
  const keyId = new DataView(signature.buffer, signature.byteOffset, 5).getUint32(1);
  const raw = signature.subarray(5);
  const digest = new Uint8Array(await crypto.subtle.digest("SHA-256", payload));
  const preImage = pae(payloadType, digest);
  const jwk = await keyWithId(jwks, keyId);
  if (jwk === null || raw.length !== 64) return null;
  const [importParams, verifyParams] = algorithmOf(jwk);
  const key = await crypto.subtle.importKey("jwk", jwk, importParams, false, ["verify"]);
  const verified = await crypto.subtle.verify(verifyParams, key, raw, preImage);
  return verified ? jwk : null;
}

// The one key of the set with this id, or null: a miss is a refusal, never a search.
async function keyWithId(jwks, keyId) {
  for (const jwk of jwks.keys) {
    if ((await keyIdOf(jwk)) === keyId) return jwk;
  }
  return null;
}

// "DSSEv1" SP LEN(type) SP type SP LEN(body) SP body, lengths in ASCII decimal.
function pae(payloadType, body) {
  const ascii = new TextEncoder();
  const type = ascii.encode(payloadType);
  const head = ascii.encode(`DSSEv1 ${type.length} `);
  const middle = ascii.encode(` ${body.length} `);
  const out = new Uint8Array(head.length + type.length + middle.length + body.length);
  out.set(head, 0);
  out.set(type, head.length);
  out.set(middle, head.length + type.length);
  out.set(body, head.length + type.length + middle.length);
  return out;
}

function algorithmOf(jwk) {
  if (jwk.kty === "OKP" && jwk.crv === "Ed25519") {
    return [{ name: "Ed25519" }, { name: "Ed25519" }];
  }
  if (jwk.kty === "EC" && jwk.crv === "P-256") {
    return [{ name: "ECDSA", namedCurve: "P-256" }, { name: "ECDSA", hash: "SHA-256" }];
  }
  throw new Error("unsupported key");
}

// The id the publisher set, else the first four bytes of the RFC 7638 thumbprint: the members in
// lexicographic order, no whitespace. JSON.stringify keeps the insertion order, and base64url
// values need no escaping.
async function keyIdOf(jwk) {
  if (Number.isInteger(jwk.kms_aead_key_id)) return jwk.kms_aead_key_id;
  const members =
    jwk.kty === "OKP"
      ? { crv: jwk.crv, kty: jwk.kty, x: jwk.x }
      : { crv: jwk.crv, kty: jwk.kty, x: jwk.x, y: jwk.y };
  const input = new TextEncoder().encode(JSON.stringify(members));
  const digest = await crypto.subtle.digest("SHA-256", input);
  return new DataView(digest).getUint32(0);
}

An attached value is split first: w is the big-endian u16 at bytes 5 and 6, the signature the w bytes after it, and the payload the rest; verify before using the payload. The test vectors of Formats run against this code in CI.

MACs in a keyring

A keyring holds MAC key versions beside its other versions, under the purpose Mac: HMAC-SHA256 keys of 32 bytes or HMAC-SHA512 keys of 64 bytes, encrypted by the KEK as every DEK is, with the states, staged rotation and compromised flag of the others, see Key versions and lifecycle. Encryptor::mac computes a Mac<T> under the active MAC version and Encryptor::mac_attached a WithMac<T>. Keyring::verify_mac and verify_with_mac verify under the version a value names, with the keyring’s compromised-version policy as decrypt applies it. One MAC version serves every payload type of an application.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{JsonEncoded, SignedPayloadType, SignableType};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, MacAlgorithm,
};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Debug, PartialEq, Eq, Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct Job {
    task: String,
    image_id: u64,
}

impl SignableType for Job {
    const PAYLOAD_TYPE: SignedPayloadType = SignedPayloadType::from_static("example.com/jobs/v1");
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("jobs".to_string())?)?;
let admin = registry
    .open_or_create(
        provider,
        KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"),
        KeyringAlgorithms::new().with_mac(MacAlgorithm::HmacSha256),
    )
    .await?;
let keyring = admin.keyring();

let job = JsonEncoded::new(Job { task: "resize".to_string(), image_id: 7 });
let mac = keyring.encryptor().await.mac(&job).await?;
keyring.verify_mac(&job, &mac).await?;

let with_mac = keyring.encryptor().await.mac_attached(&job).await?;
let verified = keyring.verify_with_mac(&with_mac).await?.0;
assert_eq!(verified, job.0);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

A value under a retired version fails with KEY_VERSION_RETIRED, so compute a new MAC over the payload under the active version before retiring the old one; sweeps re-encrypt Encrypted fields and not MACs. Move every process to 0.31 before the first MAC version is created, since 0.30 refuses a record with a MAC version. Authenticator computes and verifies MACs with a key the caller holds.

Full example available here.

Beacons and MACs

Both are HMACs under a keyring version, and they serve different ends, see Beacons:

BeaconMAC
Purposefind rows by a field’s value without decrypting itprove that bytes were not changed since a key holder computed the tag
Outputthe first bits bits of HMAC-SHA256, truncated on purpose so that values collidethe full tag, 32 or 64 bytes, never truncated
TypeBeacon<T>, T: PlaintextMac<T> and WithMac<T>, T: Signable
Equalityequal beacons mean likely equal values, by designequal tags mean an equal payload under the key, which verify says

Signing the keyring record

A keyring record decides which versions are read and which encrypts, and its integrity rests on the store’s access control, see Keyring deployment. A store writer can re-activate a compromised version or point a version at another DEK, and under an asymmetric KEK, which encrypts for anyone holding its public key, plant a DEK of its own, see Asymmetric KEKs. A signed record closes both: the administration signs every record it writes, and an application refuses a record that no key it trusts has signed.

  • the administration holds a KeyringAdmin with a record signer, a Signer on any signing provider. KeyringRegistry::create_signed and open_or_create_signed sign the record from its first write, and with_record_signer adds a signer to an admin;
  • a signing admin re-signs the record on every write and refuses an unsigned record with RECORD_UNSIGNED; only sign_record signs an unsigned record. A NoRecordSigner admin refuses to overwrite a signed record with RECORD_SIGNER_MISSING;
  • every registry verifies the signature of every record it reads, before any field is used and before any KMS call. KeyringRegistryOptions::record_signatures decides what an unsigned record means: VerifyIfSigned(keys), the default, reads it, and Required(keys) refuses it with RECORD_UNSIGNED. A signature that does not verify fails with RECORD_SIGNATURE_INVALID under both.

The verifying keys come from the application’s configuration, a JWK Set or the public key of the KMS key, never from the store or the record:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider, LocalSigningKeyName, LocalSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signer, SigningAlgorithm, VerifyingKeySet};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, RecordSignaturePolicy,
};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let signer = Signer::<_, Ring>::new(LocalSigningProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(
    LocalSigningKeyName::try_from("records".to_string())?,
    SigningAlgorithm::Ed25519,
)?)
.await?;
let keys = VerifyingKeySet::new([signer.verifying_key().clone()])?;

let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new().with_record_signatures(RecordSignaturePolicy::Required(keys)),
)?;
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("records".to_string())?)?;
let admin = registry
    .open_or_create_signed(
        provider,
        signer,
        KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"),
        KeyringAlgorithms::new(),
    )
    .await?;
assert!(admin.keyring().record().signature().is_some());
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

Full example available here. The signed bytes are in Formats.

Move an existing deployment in this order:

  1. move every administrative binary to 0.31, since a 0.30 administrative write removes the signature;
  2. give every registry the signer’s public key as VerifyIfSigned(keys): unsigned records still read, and a signed one verifies from its first write;
  3. give the administration a signing admin and run sign_record on every keyring;
  4. switch the applications and the administration to Required;
  5. rotate the signing key by adding the new public key to every registry’s set first, then sign_record on every keyring with the new signer.

Under VerifyIfSigned, a running process that holds a signed record refuses a later unsigned one with RECORD_SIGNATURE_REMOVED, keeps the record it holds and logs an ERROR; a restarted process reads whatever the store holds. A store writer who strips the signature therefore downgrades only the processes that start after the write. Be aware that Required is the control for those, and the only one for a keyring whose record was never signed. To stop signing a keyring on purpose, switch every reader to VerifyIfSigned, write the record unsigned with dangerously_unsign_record on a NoRecordSigner admin, then restart the readers.

Limits

  • whoever may sign records may write any record, and a signature proves the record was written by a signer, not that it is the latest: a store writer can restore an older signed record, which running processes refuse when it moves a version backwards;
  • a signature is valid for as long as its key is in the verifier’s set. There is no expiry and no revocation list, so put a timestamp or a recipient in the payload: nothing outside the payload type and the payload is authenticated;
  • ECDSA signatures are malleable. Never use signature bytes as an identifier or compare them for equality; Ed25519 signatures are unique for a key and a message;
  • a MAC is forged by every holder of its key, which is every process with KMS decrypt on the keyring’s KEK and read access to its record;
  • the library does not sign the public key set. How it reaches a verifier is the deployment’s control;
  • RSA signatures, secp256k1, P-384, P-521, ML-DSA, JWS and COSE are not supported.

The errors of signing are in Error handling.

Crypto backends

The AEAD algorithms run on a crypto library that a feature compiles in. Each backend feature adds a type to kms_aead::backends, and AeadCipher, DeterministicCipher, KmsAeadEnvelope and LocalKmsProvider take that type as their backend type parameter B. A build may enable any number of backends, and there is no default one.

FeatureTypeLibrary
ringRingring
aws-lcAwsLcaws-lc-rs
aws-lc-fipsAwsLc, on the FIPS moduleaws-lc-rs
rustcryptoRustCryptothe RustCrypto AEADs

There is no default because Cargo unifies features: a default chosen by features would change for every crate in a build when one crate enables another backend.

Code generic over the backend

The backend types implement AeadBackend, a sealed trait in kms_aead::backends. Its items are the constant NAME, the backend’s name in spans and logs: ring, aws-lc or rustcrypto, and generate_random_bytes. AeadBackend names the crypto engine for every primitive the library runs on it: the AEAD algorithms, the beacon HMAC, the RSA operations of asymmetric KEKs and the signature verification. Code that works with any backend bounds its type parameter with AeadBackend and nothing else:

use kms_aead::backends::{AeadBackend, Ring};
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{KmsAeadEnvelope, KmsAeadResult};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

struct Orders<B> {
    envelope: KmsAeadEnvelope<LocalKmsProvider<B>, B>,
}

impl<B: AeadBackend> Orders<B> {
    fn new() -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        let provider = LocalKmsProvider::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("orders".to_string())?)?;
        Ok(Self {
            envelope: KmsAeadEnvelope::new(provider)?,
        })
    }

    async fn round_trip(&self, card: &SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        let encrypted = self.envelope.encrypt(b"orders/1042", card).await?;
        self.envelope.decrypt(b"orders/1042", &encrypted).await
    }
}

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let orders = Orders::<Ring>::new()?;
let card = SecretValue::from("4111 1111 1111 1111");
assert_eq!(orders.round_trip(&card).await?, card);
Ok(())
}

B::generate_random_bytes(len) returns len bytes from the CSPRNG of the backend B, for secrets the application generates itself.

Algorithms

AlgorithmKeyRingAwsLcRustCrypto
ChaCha20Poly1305 (RFC 8439)32 bytesyesyesyes
Aes128Gcm16 bytesyesyesyes
Aes256Gcm, the envelope’s default32 bytesyesyesyes
Aes256GcmSiv (RFC 8452)32 bytesnoyesyes
RsaOaepSha256 encryption under an asymmetric KEK2048, 3072 or 4096 bitsnoyesyes
local RSA private halfnoyesyes

Every AEAD algorithm writes [nonce: 12][ciphertext][tag: 16], with a random nonce from the backend’s CSPRNG. The two RSA rows are the RSA-OAEP (RSA encryption with OAEP padding) of Asymmetric KEKs: the encryption of a DEK with the public key, and the private half of a LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider. A backend that does not implement the algorithm fails at construction, never later, with KmsAeadError::UnsupportedAlgorithm and the code UNSUPPORTED_ALGORITHM:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::{errors::KmsAeadError, AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher};

let result = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256GcmSiv);
assert!(matches!(result, Err(KmsAeadError::UnsupportedAlgorithm(_))));

Streamed values, with the stream feature, run AES-256-GCM for their chunks and HMAC-SHA-512 for the key derivation, which every backend has: hmac::HMAC_SHA512 on ring and aws-lc-rs, and Hmac<Sha512> of the hmac and sha2 crates on RustCrypto. A value encrypted on one backend decrypts on every other, see Streaming encryption.

AeadAlgorithm reads and writes its names with FromStr and Display: chacha20-poly1305, aes-128-gcm, aes-256-gcm and aes-256-gcm-siv.

AEAD without a KMS

AeadCipher encrypts and decrypts values with a key the application holds. encrypt_bare writes the AEAD output alone, an Encrypted<T, Bare>:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Bare;
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher, Encrypted};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::ChaCha20Poly1305)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;

let aad = b"orders/1042";
let cipher_text: Encrypted<SecretValue, Bare> =
    cipher.encrypt_bare(aad, &SecretValue::from("secret"), &dek)?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = cipher.decrypt_bare(aad, &cipher_text, &dek)?;

assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("secret"));
Ok(())
}

Full example available here.

encrypt writes an Encrypted<T, Randomized>: the same output after Tink’s TINK prefix, which names the key by a KeyId, see One fixed key.

generate_dek makes a DEK without a KMS. generate_encrypted_dek makes a DEK and encrypts it with a provider in one call, for an application that stores the encrypted DEK itself. The other calls of AeadCipher make no KMS call and open no span.

Cross-backend compatibility

Every backend writes the same bytes for the same algorithm, so a value encrypted by one backend decrypts with any other that implements its algorithm. A writer on Ring and a reader on AwsLc work together, and moving an application from one backend to another needs no migration of its data:

use kms_aead::backends::{Ring, RustCrypto};
use kms_aead::formats::Bare;
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher, Encrypted};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let writer = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;
let reader = AeadCipher::<RustCrypto>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;

let dek = writer.generate_dek()?;
let cipher_text: Encrypted<SecretValue, Bare> =
    writer.encrypt_bare(b"aad", &SecretValue::from("secret"), &dek)?;

let decrypted: SecretValue = reader.decrypt_bare(b"aad", &cipher_text, &dek)?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("secret"));
Ok(())
}

ring

ChaCha20-Poly1305, AES-128-GCM and AES-256-GCM. It has no AES-256-GCM-SIV and no RSA encryption, so an encryption with a public key and a local RSA private half fail at construction with UNSUPPORTED_ALGORITHM. ring does not zeroise its expanded key schedules, so they stay in freed memory after each call.

aws-lc-rs and FIPS

aws-lc builds aws-lc-rs with every algorithm. It is the backend that adds no second crypto library to a build with a cloud provider, since the providers already build aws-lc-rs for TLS.

aws-lc-fips runs the same backend on the AWS-LC FIPS module, and builds only the FIPS module, so it does not need aws-lc. It needs CMake and Go to build. Be aware it moves every user of aws-lc-rs in the build to the FIPS module, the rustls aws-lc-rs provider included. A build with both features compiles both C libraries, and aws-lc-rs runs on the FIPS one. AES-256-GCM is the algorithm for FIPS deployments. AES-SIV, the deterministic algorithm, is not FIPS-approved, see Deterministic encryption.

aws-lc-rs copies the key into an AEAD context that AWS-LC allocates with its own allocator, which overwrites every allocation with zeros before freeing it. The expanded key schedule does not stay in freed memory after a call, as long as the application leaves AWS-LC’s allocator in place. The record keys AES-256-GCM-SIV derives for each call live on the stack, and AWS-LC does not clear them.

RustCrypto

Every algorithm, in pure Rust, with getrandom as the CSPRNG. It needs no C compiler. The feature turns on the crates’ zeroize features, so their key objects, and the Poly1305 and POLYVAL keys each call derives, are zeroised when dropped.

RSA-OAEP runs on the rsa crate. A LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider on RustCrypto decrypts DEKs from trusted sources only, see Asymmetric KEKs.

KMS providers

A provider encrypts and decrypts data encryption keys with one key encryption key (KEK). Each one is a type in kms_aead::providers, enabled by its feature:

KMSTypeFeature
Google Cloud KMSGcpKmsProvidergcp-kms-encryption
AWS KMSAwsKmsProvideraws-kms-encryption
Azure Key Vault and Managed HSMAzureKmsProviderazure-kms-encryption
HashiCorp Vault TransitHcVaultTransitProviderhashicorp-vault-transit-kms-encryption
A KEK the process holdsLocalKmsProvidernone, in every build
An RSA KEK the process holdsLocalAsymmetricKmsProvidernone, in every build

Every KMS provider above implements the two provider traits, KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, which encrypts DEKs, and KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, which decrypts them, and KmsAeadEnvelope takes any of them:

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
