use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsKeyRef, GcpKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;

async fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let kms_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);
let provider = GcpKmsProvider::new(&kms_ref).await?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<GcpKmsProvider, AwsLc>::new(provider)?;
let _ = envelope;
Ok(())
}

Each provider names its configured KEK on every decryption, so a DEK encrypted under another KEK fails at the KMS and never decrypts a value. provider.kek() returns that KEK as a KekRef: the KMS and the key name. It is also the provider and key field of the KMS spans and the key of the DEK cache. Nothing else is checked: an encrypted DEK carries no context that the KMS authenticates, see Security considerations.

Asymmetric KEKs

A KEK can be an RSA key pair: its public half encrypts a DEK with RSA-OAEP (RSA encryption with OAEP padding), in the process of whoever holds the public key, and its private half decrypts it. Each provider above reads the 0x02 encrypted DEK of such an encryption beside its own bytes, and fetches the public key for the administration with a public_key call. No side needs the KMS encrypt right: the encryption is a local operation, and the KMS only decrypts. The layout, the choices and the limits are in Asymmetric KEKs.

Two further providers are in every build and hold the public key:

  • PublicKeyKekProvider encrypts, for a producer that must never decrypt. It implements KmsAeadEncryptionProvider alone, so an envelope over it has no call that decrypts a DEK or a value, and no keyring opens over it;
  • AsymmetricKekProvider encrypts with the public key and decrypts through the KMS provider of the same KEK, for the keyring administration.

The call that fetches the public key, and the rights of a reader and of the administration:

KMSPublic keyReaderAdministration, in addition
Google Cloud KMSGcpKmsProvider::public_key(&version)cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToDecrypt, as in roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecryptercloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.viewPublicKey, as in roles/cloudkms.publicKeyViewer. roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecrypter does not carry it
AWS KMSAwsKmsProvider::public_key()kms:Decrypt on the keykms:GetPublicKey on the key
Azure Key VaultAzureKmsProvider::public_key(None) for the latest version, or Some(&version)keys/unwrap/actionkeys/get. “Key Vault Crypto Service Encryption User” carries read, wrap and unwrap and no decrypt
Vault TransitHcVaultTransitProvider::public_key(None) or Some(&version)update on transit/decrypt/<key>read on transit/keys/<key> or transit/export/public-key/<key>
Local KMSLocalAsymmetricKmsProvider::public_key(None) for its encrypting versionnonenone

encrypts_with_public_key() is true for PublicKeyKekProvider, AsymmetricKekProvider and LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider, so that a keyring refuses the BigQuery and Tink exports its KEK cannot serve.

KMS random generators

By default a DEK comes from the CSPRNG of the crypto backend. The GCP and AWS providers can take it from the KMS instead, at the cost of one more KMS request per new DEK:

  • GCP: GcpKmsProviderOptions::new().with_use_kms_random_gen(true), Cloud KMS GenerateRandomBytes at the HSM protection level;
  • AWS: AwsKmsProviderOptions::new().with_use_kms_random_gen(true), AWS KMS GenerateRandom.

Azure, Vault Transit and the local KMS always use the backend’s CSPRNG. Nonces always come from the backend’s CSPRNG.

Your own provider

An application can implement the provider traits for a KMS of its own, or as a wrapper around another provider: KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, with kek, encrypt_data_encryption_key and the defaulted generate_key_material and encrypts_with_public_key, and KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, with decrypt_data_encryption_key. A provider that only encrypts implements the first alone, and the calls that decrypt do not compile over it. A wrapper implements each trait where the provider it wraps does, as the example in Migrating from 0.30 shows. The implementation:

  • is Send and Sync, and writes encrypt_data_encryption_key, decrypt_data_encryption_key and generate_key_material as async fn, whose futures must be Send. The traits have no dyn form, so code that holds a provider is generic over it;
  • returns from kek() a KekRef with KmsProvider::Other and a key name that identifies the key across every KMS instance it may address. A wrapper returns the inner provider’s kek();
  • names that KEK to the KMS on every decryption;
  • adds to the encrypted DEK that encrypt_data_encryption_key returns, with with_kek_version, the KEK version the KMS’s response names, never the one it expects, and none when the response names none;
  • returns true from encrypts_with_public_key() when it encrypts DEKs with the public half of a key pair, so that a keyring refuses the BigQuery and Tink exports its KEK cannot serve. The method has a default of false;
  • keeps key material, plaintext or encrypted, out of its errors’ Display and Debug.

generate_key_material has a default implementation that returns Ok(None), so the backend generates the key.

Google Cloud KMS

Feature gcp-kms-encryption, or gcp-kms-encryption-webpki for the bundled WebPKI roots.

GcpKmsKeyRef names a CryptoKey by its project, location, key ring and key. The provider encrypts under the primary version of the key, and each encrypted DEK records the version that encrypted it, so DEKs encrypted before a rotation still decrypt afterwards.

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsKeyRef, GcpKmsProvider, GcpKmsProviderOptions};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

async fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let kms_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);

let provider = GcpKmsProvider::new(&kms_ref).await?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<GcpKmsProvider, AwsLc>::new(provider)?;

// Or with DEKs from the Cloud KMS generator at the HSM protection level
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<GcpKmsProvider, AwsLc>::new(
    GcpKmsProvider::with_options(
        &kms_ref,
        GcpKmsProviderOptions::new().with_use_kms_random_gen(true),
    )
    .await?,
)?;

let cipher_text = envelope
    .encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("secret"))
    .await?;
let _ = cipher_text;
Ok(())
}

Full example available here.

Authentication

GcpKmsProvider::new and with_options build a Cloud KMS client with the application default credentials. They look for credentials in the following places, preferring the first location found:

  • A JSON file whose path is specified by the GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS environment variable;
  • A JSON file in a location known to the gcloud command-line tool using gcloud auth application-default login;
  • On Google Compute Engine, Cloud Run, GKE, etc., the metadata server.

For local development don’t confuse gcloud auth login with gcloud auth application-default login, since the first one authorises only the gcloud tool to access the Cloud Platform.

GcpKmsProvider::with_client takes a client the application built itself, for other credentials.

The principal needs cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToEncrypt and useToDecrypt on the key, as in the roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyEncrypterDecrypter role, and cloudkms.locations.generateRandomBytes on the location when the KMS generator is on.

Key versions and rotation

A rotation adds a version to the CryptoKey and makes it the primary version, by hand or on the key’s rotation schedule. New DEKs are encrypted under the primary version from then on, and Cloud KMS decrypts every DEK with the version that encrypted it, for as long as that version is enabled.

To retire the old version, disable it once the sweep has reached every value, see Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation: decryptions under it fail, and enabling it again restores them. Destroying it schedules its destruction, 30 days later by default, and until then the version can be restored.

Changing the primary version and disabling a version are eventually consistent in Cloud KMS, see Cloud KMS resource consistency. Until a new primary version has propagated, Encrypt may still use the previous one, and a disabled version “typically remains usable for encrypting and decrypting data for up to 1 minute after it is disabled”, for several hours in exceptional cases. So:

  • start the sweep only once the new primary version has propagated. The kek_version() of the encrypted DEK an encryption returns, and the kek_version field of the KMS encrypt DEK span, name the version Cloud KMS encrypted with, the N of cryptoKeyVersions/N, see Observability. A keyring whose reencrypt_deks ran before the new primary version had propagated still lists the old version among its kek_versions_in_use: run reencrypt_deks again, see Rotating the KEK;
  • run the check only once the disable has propagated, and run it again before destroying the version. A check inside that window passes on DEKs that are still under the old version.

Enabling a version is strongly consistent, see Cloud KMS resource consistency: an enabled version is available at once, so enabling the old version again after a failed check takes effect immediately.

Asymmetric keys

A CryptoKey with the purpose ASYMMETRIC_DECRYPT and an algorithm RSA_DECRYPT_OAEP_2048_SHA256, RSA_DECRYPT_OAEP_3072_SHA256 or RSA_DECRYPT_OAEP_4096_SHA256 is an asymmetric KEK, see Asymmetric KEKs. 3072 bits is the size Cloud KMS marks as recommended. Asymmetric keys have no primary version and no automatic rotation, so a version is always named:

  • GcpKmsProvider::public_key(&version) sends GetPublicKey for the CryptoKeyVersion and returns the public key with the version the response names as its KEK version, for the producers. It fails with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_PUBLIC_KEY for a version whose algorithm is not RSA_DECRYPT_OAEP_2048_SHA256, RSA_DECRYPT_OAEP_3072_SHA256 or RSA_DECRYPT_OAEP_4096_SHA256;
  • a decryption of a 0x02 encrypted DEK sends AsymmetricDecrypt on <key>/cryptoKeyVersions/<version>, with the RSA block as ciphertext. The encrypted DEK names the version, as a symmetric ciphertext does. The request carries no CRC32C checksum fields: a corrupted DEK fails the value’s AEAD;
  • the principal of a reader needs cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToDecrypt, as in roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecrypter, and the administration also cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.viewPublicKey, as in roles/cloudkms.publicKeyViewer. Nobody needs useToEncrypt.

An asymmetric decryption costs more of the quota than a symmetric one, see Keyrings under an asymmetric KEK.

Signing

GcpKmsSigningProvider signs with one CryptoKeyVersion of a key with the purpose ASYMMETRIC_SIGN, see Signatures and MACs. GcpKmsSigningKeyRef names the key as GcpKmsKeyRef does, and adds the version, the N of cryptoKeyVersions/N, and the algorithm: Ed25519 for an EC_SIGN_ED25519 version and EcdsaP256Sha256 for EC_SIGN_P256_SHA256. Asymmetric keys have no primary version, so a signer always names one.

use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::formats::PublicKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsSigningKeyRef, GcpKmsSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signer, SigningAlgorithm};

async fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsSigningKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-signing-key".to_string(),
    NonZeroU32::MIN,
    SigningAlgorithm::EcdsaP256Sha256,
);

let provider = GcpKmsSigningProvider::new(&key_ref).await?;
let signer = Signer::<_, AwsLc>::new(provider).await?;

// The public key to publish for the verifiers
let jwk = signer.verifying_key().encode(PublicKeyFormat::Jwk)?;
let _ = jwk;
Ok(())
}

The provider authenticates as GcpKmsProvider does, and GcpKmsSigningProvider::with_client takes a client the application built itself. Creating the provider sends no request; Signer::new sends the first one. Cloud KMS lists EC_SIGN_ED25519 under the software protection level only, so a key at the HSM protection level signs with P-256.

  • sign sends AsymmetricSign with the pre-image as data for Ed25519, and the SHA-256 of the pre-image as digest.sha256 for P-256. Each carries its CRC32C checksum, dataCrc32c or digestCrc32c;
  • a response fails with SIGNATURE_ENCODING when verifiedDataCrc32c or verifiedDigestCrc32c is false, when signatureCrc32c is missing or does not match the signature, or when the signature is not 64 bytes (Ed25519) or strict DER (P-256), which is converted to r || s. A name other than the configured version fails with SIGNING_KEY_MISMATCH;
  • verifying_key sends GetPublicKey and parses pem. A key of another algorithm than the configured one fails with SIGNING_ALGORITHM_MISMATCH, and a pem that does not parse with INVALID_PUBLIC_KEY;
  • the principal needs cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToSign and cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.viewPublicKey, as in roles/cloudkms.signerVerifier. roles/cloudkms.signer alone lacks viewPublicKey.

A rotation is a new CryptoKeyVersion that a new signer names, see Key ids and rotation.

Errors

The code of a failed KMS call is the gRPC status code, such as PermissionDenied or NotFound. Which codes are a NetworkError and which an EncryptionError is in Retries. GcpKmsSigningProvider returns the same codes, with a SignatureError where the encryption provider returns an EncryptionError.

AWS KMS

Feature aws-kms-encryption.

AwsKmsKeyRef names the key by the AWS account and the key id, which is anything AWS KMS accepts: a key id, a key ARN, an alias name such as alias/orders, or an alias ARN.

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::providers::{AwsKmsKeyRef, AwsKmsProvider, AwsKmsProviderOptions};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

async fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let kms_ref = AwsKmsKeyRef::new(
    "123456789012".to_string(),
    "arn:aws:kms:eu-west-1:123456789012:key/1234abcd-12ab-34cd-56ef-1234567890ab".to_string(),
);

let provider = AwsKmsProvider::new(&kms_ref).await?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<AwsKmsProvider, AwsLc>::new(provider)?;

// Or with DEKs from AWS KMS GenerateRandom
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<AwsKmsProvider, AwsLc>::new(
    AwsKmsProvider::with_options(
        &kms_ref,
        AwsKmsProviderOptions::new().with_use_kms_random_gen(true),
    )
    .await?,
)?;

let cipher_text = envelope
    .encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("secret"))
    .await?;
let _ = cipher_text;
Ok(())
}

Full example available here.

Authentication and region

AwsKmsProvider::new loads the AWS configuration from the environment, as the AWS SDK does: environment variables, the shared config and credentials files, the instance or container role, and the session of aws login. It sends no request, so missing credentials or an unreachable endpoint fail the first encryption or decryption.

Without aws_region in the key reference, the provider takes the region of the AWS configuration it loads. Set one with AwsKmsKeyRef::new(..).with_aws_region(region).

The principal needs kms:Encrypt and kms:Decrypt on the key, and kms:GenerateRandom when the KMS generator is on.

Key ARN and aliases

A decryption names the configured key to AWS KMS, so a DEK encrypted under another key fails with IncorrectKeyException, even when the principal may decrypt with that key.

Configure the key ARN rather than an alias: a DEK encrypted under an alias that later moves to another key fails to decrypt with IncorrectKeyException.

The provider turns the key id into an ARN with the region and the account: an alias name into an alias ARN, a key id into a key ARN, and an ARN stays as it is. That ARN always starts with arn:aws:, so outside the aws partition, such as in GovCloud or China, give the full ARN.

Key versions and rotation

AWS KMS rotates the key material of a key, on its rotation schedule or on demand, and keeps every earlier key material for as long as the key exists. A DEK encrypted before a rotation decrypts afterwards. An encryption’s response names no key material, so the kek_version() of the encrypted DEK it returns is None.

Key material that AWS KMS generated cannot be disabled or deleted on its own. The sweep of Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation is optional for it: it moves each DEK to the current key material, and with nothing to disable, the check and the destruction do not apply.

Imported key material is deleted in two ways: by DeleteImportedKeyMaterial, for the material a KeyMaterialId names or the current one without it, and at its expiry. ExpirationModel defaults to KEY_MATERIAL_EXPIRES, and at its ValidTo, at most 365 days after the import, AWS KMS deletes the material without any call. Either way the key moves to PendingImport, and AWS KMS refuses every operation with it, whichever material a DEK is under, until the same material is imported again. Import with KEY_MATERIAL_DOES_NOT_EXPIRE, or import again before ValidTo, and keep a copy of every material to import again.

A sweep therefore retires no imported key material from a key in use, and with every kek_version None, neither a keyring record nor a stored kek_version shows which DEKs are under which material. After a deletion or an expiry, import the material again and decrypt every stored DEK: each keyring’s with reencrypt_deks, the application’s own as in The check. An error means the key is still in PendingImport: ListKeyRotations lists each material with its import state, and the one still missing is imported again.

Asymmetric keys

A KMS key of the spec RSA_2048, RSA_3072 or RSA_4096 with the usage ENCRYPT_DECRYPT is an asymmetric KEK, see Asymmetric KEKs:

  • AwsKmsProvider::public_key() sends GetPublicKey for the configured key and returns the DER SubjectPublicKeyInfo, for the producers. The key must be of usage ENCRYPT_DECRYPT and list RSAES_OAEP_SHA_256 among its EncryptionAlgorithms, and the call fails with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_PUBLIC_KEY otherwise, and when the response holds no public key;
  • a decryption of a 0x02 encrypted DEK sends Decrypt with the configured key as KeyId, EncryptionAlgorithm RSAES_OAEP_SHA_256 and the RSA block as CiphertextBlob. AWS KMS takes no encryption context with the RSA algorithms. The encrypted DEK records no key version, as under a symmetric key;
  • AWS KMS does not rotate an asymmetric key: a rotation is a new key, by hand, and so a new keyring;
  • the principal of a reader needs kms:Decrypt on the key, and the administration also kms:GetPublicKey. Nobody needs kms:Encrypt.

An asymmetric key is configured by its ARN, as a symmetric key is. AWS prices and limits the RSA requests apart from the symmetric ones, see Keyrings under an asymmetric KEK.

Signing

AwsKmsSigningProvider signs with an asymmetric KMS key of the usage SIGN_VERIFY, see Signatures and MACs. AwsKmsSigningKeyRef names the key as AwsKmsKeyRef does, and adds the algorithm: Ed25519 for an ECC_NIST_EDWARDS25519 key and EcdsaP256Sha256 for ECC_NIST_P256.

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::formats::PublicKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{AwsKmsSigningKeyRef, AwsKmsSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signer, SigningAlgorithm};

async fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let key_ref = AwsKmsSigningKeyRef::new(
    "123456789012".to_string(),
    "arn:aws:kms:eu-west-1:123456789012:key/1234abcd-12ab-34cd-56ef-1234567890ab".to_string(),
    SigningAlgorithm::EcdsaP256Sha256,
);

let provider = AwsKmsSigningProvider::new(&key_ref).await?;
let signer = Signer::<_, AwsLc>::new(provider).await?;

// The public key to publish for the verifiers
let jwk = signer.verifying_key().encode(PublicKeyFormat::Jwk)?;
let _ = jwk;
Ok(())
}

The provider loads the AWS configuration and takes the region as AwsKmsProvider does, and AwsKmsSigningProvider::with_client takes a client the application built itself. Creating the provider sends no request; Signer::new sends the first one.

An asymmetric key has no versions, and a rotation is a new key. Configure the key id or the key ARN, never an alias: an alias that moves to another key would sign with a key that the key id of the signature does not name. An alias name, alias/…, or an alias ARN fails at creation with AWS_SIGNING_KEY_ALIAS, a SystemError.

  • sign sends Sign with MessageType RAW and ED25519_SHA_512 for Ed25519, and with DIGEST and ECDSA_SHA_256 for P-256, where the message is the SHA-256 of the pre-image. The pre-image is at most 302 bytes, within the 4,096-byte limit of Message;
  • a response whose KeyId is not the configured key fails with SIGNING_KEY_MISMATCH, and one whose SigningAlgorithm is not the requested one with SIGNING_ALGORITHM_MISMATCH. A P-256 signature is DER, converted to r || s; one that is not strict DER, or an Ed25519 signature that is not 64 bytes, fails with SIGNATURE_ENCODING;
  • verifying_key sends GetPublicKey and parses the DER PublicKey. A KeySpec other than the configured one fails with SIGNING_ALGORITHM_MISMATCH;
  • the principal needs kms:Sign and kms:GetPublicKey on the key.

Errors

An error AWS KMS returned keeps its AWS error code, such as IncorrectKeyException or NotFoundException, and the message AWS KMS returned. An EncryptionError keeps the SDK error as its root cause. A response without the encrypted DEK, the plaintext DEK or the random bytes it was asked for fails with AWS_ERROR, an EncryptionError. Which errors are a NetworkError and which an EncryptionError is in Retries. AwsKmsSigningProvider returns the same codes, with a SignatureError where the encryption provider returns an EncryptionError.

Azure Key Vault

Feature azure-kms-encryption, or one of the TLS variants in Cargo features.

AzureKmsKeyRef names the key by the vault URL and the key name. Without a key version, the latest version encrypts new DEKs. Each encrypted DEK records the version that encrypted it, and the decryption uses that version, so DEKs encrypted before a rotation still decrypt afterwards.

The provider takes any Azure credential, for example one from azure_identity:

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::providers::{AzureKmsKeyRef, AzureKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

async fn example() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let kms_ref = AzureKmsKeyRef::new(
    "https://my-vault.vault.azure.net".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);

// Azure CLI or Azure Developer CLI credentials for local runs;
// use `azure_identity::ManagedIdentityCredential` in Azure deployments
let credential = azure_identity::DeveloperToolsCredential::new(None)?;

let provider = AzureKmsProvider::new(&kms_ref, credential)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<AzureKmsProvider, AwsLc>::new(provider)?;

let cipher_text = envelope
    .encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("secret"))
    .await?;
let _ = cipher_text;
Ok(())
}

Full example available here.

The provider sends no request until the first encryption or decryption. The principal needs the wrapKey and unwrapKey key operations, Key Vault’s names for encrypting and decrypting a DEK, as in the “Key Vault Crypto User” role.

Options

AzureKmsProviderOptions has:

  • algorithm: an AzureKmsAlgorithm, Key Vault’s alg of the wrapKey and unwrapKey operations, which must suit the key type: RsaOaepSha256 (RSA-OAEP-256) for RSA keys, Aes256KeyWrap (A256KW) for Managed HSM AES keys. The default, DEFAULT_ALGORITHM, is RsaOaepSha256 for every key, so a Managed HSM AES key needs algorithm set to Aes256KeyWrap and the provider built with AzureKmsProvider::with_options. RsaOaepSha1 (RSA-OAEP) is for an RSA key whose encrypted DEKs were written with it. The other AES key wraps, Aes128KeyWrap (A128KW), Aes192KeyWrap (A192KW), Pkcs11AesKeyWrap (CKM_AES_KEY_WRAP) and Pkcs11AesKeyWrapWithPadding (CKM_AES_KEY_WRAP_PAD), are for Managed HSM AES keys too. RSA1_5 is not offered. Encrypted DEKs do not record the algorithm, so it stays fixed for the life of the key;
  • client_options: settings passed to the Azure SDK, such as a custom HTTP transport or retry policy.

A Managed HSM AES key, with Aes256KeyWrap:

use kms_aead::providers::{
    AzureKmsAlgorithm, AzureKmsKeyRef, AzureKmsProvider, AzureKmsProviderOptions,
};

fn example() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let kms_ref = AzureKmsKeyRef::new(
    "https://my-hsm.managedhsm.azure.net".to_string(),
    "my-aes-key".to_string(),
);
let credential = azure_identity::DeveloperToolsCredential::new(None)?;

let provider = AzureKmsProvider::with_options(
    &kms_ref,
    AzureKmsProviderOptions::new().with_algorithm(AzureKmsAlgorithm::Aes256KeyWrap),
    credential,
)?;
let _ = provider;
Ok(())
}

Encrypted DEKs

Key Vault decrypts only with the key version that encrypted, so the provider’s encrypted DEK carries it before Key Vault’s encrypted DEK, in the library’s own layout, see Formats. No Tink client exists for Azure, so Tink does not read the values of this provider, and the plaintext Key Vault encrypts is the raw DEK of 16, 32 or 64 bytes, as in 0.29, rather than the DEK as Tink serialises it. Every algorithm takes it, Aes256KeyWrap too, which takes only multiples of 8 bytes. Store the encrypted DEK as it is, see Envelope encryption.

Key versions and rotation

A rotation adds a version to the key, by hand or by the key’s rotation policy. Without a key version in AzureKmsKeyRef, the latest version encrypts new DEKs; with one, that version does, so move it to the new version before a sweep. A sweep with a provider pinned to the old version re-encrypts under the old version. The kek_version() of the encrypted DEK an encryption returns is the version Key Vault encrypted with, read from the kid of its response.

Once the sweep has reached every value, see Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation, disable the old version: Key Vault refuses to decrypt under it, and enabling it again restores it. Key Vault deletes a key with all its versions and never one version alone, so the old version stays disabled rather than destroyed.

Asymmetric keys

An RSA key of 2048, 3072 or 4096 bits, RSA or RSA-HSM, is an asymmetric KEK, see Asymmetric KEKs. The provider has always encrypted with it through wrapKey; a 0x02 encrypted DEK moves the encryption into the producer, and the provider still decrypts through unwrapKey:

  • AzureKmsProvider::public_key(None) sends GetKey for the latest version, or public_key(Some(&version)) for one, and returns the JSON Web Key’s n and e as the public key of the version the kid of the response names. It fails with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_PUBLIC_KEY for a key whose kty is not RSA or RSA-HSM, whose key_ops lack unwrapKey, or whose kid names no version or another key;
  • a decryption of a 0x02 encrypted DEK sends unwrapKey on the key and the version the encrypted DEK names, with alg RSA-OAEP-256. This path ignores AzureKmsProviderOptions::algorithm, which stays the algorithm of the 0x01 layout;
  • the principal of a reader needs keys/unwrap/action, and the administration also keys/get. “Key Vault Crypto Service Encryption User” carries read, wrap and unwrap and no decrypt. Nobody needs keys/wrap/action.

An existing deployment on an RSA key is under an asymmetric KEK already and needs no re-encryption to move its encryptions to the producers, see Migrating an Azure or Transit RSA deployment. A Managed HSM AES key stays on wrapKey and unwrapKey with Aes256KeyWrap: it is a symmetric KEK and has no public half.

Signing

AzureKmsSigningProvider signs with one version of an EC P-256 key, EC or EC-HSM, with ES256, see Signatures and MACs. Key Vault signs no Ed25519, so the provider’s algorithm is EcdsaP256Sha256. AzureKmsSigningKeyRef names the vault, the key and the version, which a signer requires: it never follows the latest version.

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::formats::PublicKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{AzureKmsSigningKeyRef, AzureKmsSigningProvider, KmsKeyVersion};
use kms_aead::signing::Signer;

async fn example() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let key_ref = AzureKmsSigningKeyRef::new(
    "https://my-vault.vault.azure.net".to_string(),
    "my-signing-key".to_string(),
    KmsKeyVersion::try_from("4387e9f3d6e14c459867679a90fd0f79".to_string())?,
);
let credential = azure_identity::DeveloperToolsCredential::new(None)?;

let provider = AzureKmsSigningProvider::new(&key_ref, credential)?;
let signer = Signer::<_, AwsLc>::new(provider).await?;

// The public key to publish for the verifiers
let jwk = signer.verifying_key().encode(PublicKeyFormat::Jwk)?;
let _ = jwk;
Ok(())
}

AzureKmsSigningProvider::with_client_options takes the settings of the Azure SDK, such as a custom HTTP transport or retry policy. Creating the provider sends no request and fails with AZURE_KEY_NAME or AZURE_VAULT_URL as AzureKmsProvider does; Signer::new sends the first request.

  • sign calls the sign operation with ES256, the SHA-256 of the pre-image as value and the version in the request path. The result is r || s as RFC 7518 defines it, 64 bytes; another length fails with SIGNATURE_ENCODING;
  • a response whose kid is not the configured vault, key and version, or has none, fails with SIGNING_KEY_MISMATCH;
  • verifying_key calls get_key with the version and reads the crv, x and y of the JSON Web Key. A key that is not EC or EC-HSM on P-256 fails with SIGNING_ALGORITHM_MISMATCH;
  • the principal needs the sign and get key operations: the data actions Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults/keys/sign/action and Microsoft.KeyVault/vaults/keys/read, which “Key Vault Crypto User” grants.

A rotation is a new key version that a new signer names, see Key ids and rotation.

Errors

A failed request carries Key Vault’s error code, such as Forbidden or KeyNotFound, or AZURE_STATUS_<status> when the response has none. Which statuses are a NetworkError and which an EncryptionError is in Retries. AzureKmsSigningProvider returns the same codes, with a SignatureError where the encryption provider returns an EncryptionError. A decryption of an encrypted DEK in another layout fails with AZURE_ENCRYPTED_DEK_FORMAT, before any request.

HashiCorp Vault Transit

Feature hashicorp-vault-transit-kms-encryption, or one root store and one crypto provider variant, see Cargo features.

The provider encrypts DEKs with a key of the Vault Transit secrets engine. HcVaultTransitKeyRef names the key, and the mount is transit unless set with with_mount.

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::providers::{HcVaultTransitKeyRef, HcVaultTransitProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

async fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
// VAULT_ADDR, VAULT_TOKEN, VAULT_NAMESPACE, VAULT_CACERT and VAULT_CLIENT_TIMEOUT are read by
// the provider
let key_ref = HcVaultTransitKeyRef::new("orders".to_string());

let provider = HcVaultTransitProvider::new(&key_ref)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<HcVaultTransitProvider, AwsLc>::new(provider)?;

let cipher_text = envelope
    .encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("secret"))
    .await?;
let _ = cipher_text;
Ok(())
}

Full example available here.

Options

HcVaultTransitProvider::new reads its options from the variables the Vault CLI reads, with HcVaultTransitProviderOptions::from_env: VAULT_ADDR (required), VAULT_TOKEN, VAULT_NAMESPACE, VAULT_CACERT and VAULT_CLIENT_TIMEOUT. An empty variable counts as unset. VAULT_CLIENT_TIMEOUT takes the formats the Vault CLI takes: whole seconds (30), whole days (2d) or a Go duration (30s, 1h30m). Zero, which the Vault CLI reads as no time limit, is refused, as is a value in any other format. HcVaultTransitProvider::with_options takes them from the application:

use kms_aead::providers::{
    HcVaultTransitKeyRef, HcVaultTransitProvider, HcVaultTransitProviderOptions,
    HcVaultTransitToken,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
use std::time::Duration;

fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let options = HcVaultTransitProviderOptions::new("https://vault.example.com:8200".to_string())
    .with_token(HcVaultTransitToken::try_from(SecretValue::from("hvs.example"))?)
    .with_namespace("team-a".to_string())
    .with_timeout(Duration::from_secs(10));

let provider = HcVaultTransitProvider::with_options(
    &HcVaultTransitKeyRef::new("orders".to_string()).with_mount("transit-eu".to_string()),
    options,
)?;
let _ = provider;
Ok(())
}

The options are:

  • address: the Vault server, such as https://vault.example.com:8200;
  • token: sent with every request. Without one the requests carry no token, which is what a Vault Agent or Vault Proxy with use_auto_auth_token expects;
  • namespace: the Vault Enterprise namespace of the mount;
  • ca_certificate: a PEM file with CA certificates for a server whose certificate comes from a private CA. The file adds to the root store and does not replace it, unlike VAULT_CACERT in the Vault CLI, so a server with a certificate from any public CA in the root store is trusted as well;
  • timeout: the time limit for one request, from opening the connection to the last byte of the response, 60 seconds by default.

The provider sends no request when it is created, so a wrong token or an unreachable server fail the first encryption or decryption. An http address to a host other than localhost or a loopback address logs a WARN event at creation, since the token and the DEKs then cross the network unencrypted.

The token needs update on <mount>/encrypt/<key> and <mount>/decrypt/<key>.

Key versions and rotation

The encrypted DEK is the Transit ciphertext, such as vault:v1:.... It names the key version, so it still decrypts after the key is rotated. The KMS spans record that version as kek_version, and so does the kek_version() of the encrypted DEK an encryption returns: the N of vault:vN:, as min_decryption_version counts it.

The decrypt names the configured key in its URL, so Vault refuses a ciphertext of another key.

A rotation is <mount>/keys/<key>/rotate, and the new version encrypts every DEK after it. Once the sweep has reached every value, see Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation, disable the old versions by setting min_decryption_version in the key’s configuration to the new version: Vault then refuses to decrypt under older versions, with VAULT_STATUS_400, and lowering it again restores them. Once nothing needs them, the backups included, min_available_version and a trim of the key delete the older versions for good.

Asymmetric keys

A Transit key of the type rsa-2048, rsa-3072 or rsa-4096 is an asymmetric KEK, see Asymmetric KEKs:

  • HcVaultTransitProvider::public_key(None) reads <mount>/keys/<key> for the latest version, and public_key(Some(&version)) for one, and returns the PEM public key of that version, for the producers. It fails with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_PUBLIC_KEY for a key of another type or a version the key does not list;
  • a decryption of a 0x02 encrypted DEK sends <mount>/decrypt/<key> with the ciphertext vault:v<version>: and the standard base64 of the RSA block, padding_scheme oaep and hash_algorithm sha2-256. Both are always set and never left to the server’s defaults;
  • the token of a reader needs update on <mount>/decrypt/<key>, and the administration also read on <mount>/keys/<key> or on <mount>/export/public-key/<key>. Nobody needs update on <mount>/encrypt/<key>.

Signing

HcVaultTransitSigningProvider signs with one version of a Transit key of the type ed25519 or ecdsa-p256, see Signatures and MACs. HcVaultTransitSigningKeyRef names the key and the mount, transit unless set with with_mount, and adds the version and the algorithm: Ed25519 for an ed25519 key and EcdsaP256Sha256 for ecdsa-p256. The version is required and never zero, so a signer never signs with the latest version.

use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::formats::PublicKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{HcVaultTransitSigningKeyRef, HcVaultTransitSigningProvider};
use kms_aead::signing::{Signer, SigningAlgorithm};

async fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
// VAULT_ADDR, VAULT_TOKEN, VAULT_NAMESPACE, VAULT_CACERT and VAULT_CLIENT_TIMEOUT are read by
// the provider
let key_ref = HcVaultTransitSigningKeyRef::new(
    "invoices".to_string(),
    NonZeroU32::MIN,
    SigningAlgorithm::Ed25519,
);

let provider = HcVaultTransitSigningProvider::new(&key_ref)?;
let signer = Signer::<_, AwsLc>::new(provider).await?;

// The public key to publish for the verifiers
let jwk = signer.verifying_key().encode(PublicKeyFormat::Jwk)?;
let _ = jwk;
Ok(())
}

HcVaultTransitSigningProvider::with_options takes the options of HcVaultTransitProviderOptions. Creating the provider sends no request; Signer::new sends the first one.

  • sign posts to <mount>/sign/<key> with input, key_version, hash_algorithm sha2-256 and marshaling_algorithm jws. The input is the base64 of the pre-image for Ed25519, and for P-256 the base64 of its SHA-256 with prehashed set;
  • Vault answers vault:vN:<base64url> with the 64-byte Ed25519 signature, or r || s for P-256. An N other than the configured version fails with SIGNING_KEY_MISMATCH, and a signature that is not base64url without padding or not 64 bytes with SIGNATURE_ENCODING;
  • verifying_key reads <mount>/keys/<key> and takes the public_key of the configured version: base64 of the raw key for Ed25519, PEM for P-256. A key of another type fails with SIGNING_ALGORITHM_MISMATCH, and a version the key does not list with INVALID_PUBLIC_KEY;
  • the token needs update on <mount>/sign/<key> and read on <mount>/keys/<key>.

A rotation is <mount>/keys/<key>/rotate, and a new signer names the new version, see Key ids and rotation.

Errors

A response with an error status is VAULT_STATUS_<status>. VAULT_HTTP and VAULT_TIMEOUT are for a request that got no complete response, and VAULT_RESPONSE, VAULT_RESPONSE_TOO_LARGE and VAULT_CIPHERTEXT for a response or an encrypted DEK that cannot be read. Which codes are a NetworkError and which an EncryptionError is in Retries. HcVaultTransitSigningProvider returns the same codes, with a SignatureError where the encryption provider returns an EncryptionError. Invalid options fail at creation with VAULT_INVALID_OPTIONS, or VAULT_TLS when the TLS configuration cannot be built. A request body that cannot be serialised fails with VAULT_REQUEST, a SystemError, before any request is sent.

Local KMS

LocalKmsProvider encrypts DEKs with a key encryption key the process holds, with AES-256-GCM on the backend it is built with. It is in every build, with no feature, for:

  • development and tests without a cloud KMS;
  • single-host installs where the operator protects the KEK.

kms-aead builds one only when the application calls one of its constructors; no provider falls back to it. Each constructor logs one INFO event naming the KEK id, its versions and where each version came from (ephemeral, file, env or secret), never the key.

KEK ids and versions

A LocalKekId names the KEK: 1 to 255 bytes of ASCII alphanumerics, -, _ and .. Every encrypted DEK records it, and a decryption refuses a DEK encrypted under another id. The id is also the key of the DEK cache, so one id must name one set of KEK material: two providers with the same id and different keys share cache entries.

A provider holds one or more LocalKek versions of 32 bytes each. The highest version encrypts new DEKs unless with_encrypting_version names another, and every version it holds decrypts. Each encrypted DEK records its version, so adding a new version keeps the DEKs encrypted under older ones readable.

A KEK comes from one of three loaders, each taking the SymmetricKeyFormat the material is in: Raw, the 32 bytes themselves, Base64, their standard base64 with trailing whitespace allowed, or Jwk, an oct JSON Web Key. The format is always named and never guessed from the bytes:

  • LocalKek::from_secret(version, material, format): bytes the application already holds, such as a key an operator unseals with their own tool (systemd-creds, a TPM, sops, age);
  • LocalKek::from_file(version, path, format): the material in a file. The file must be a regular file of at most 32 KiB. No permission or ownership check runs: restricting who can read or replace the file is the operator’s job;
  • LocalKek::from_env(version, variable, format): the material in an environment variable. Raw takes the variable’s bytes as the key, which an environment holds only when none of them is 0x00. Be aware the variable stays in the process environment for the life of the process: the same user can read it through /proc/<pid>/environ, and every child process inherits it.

Jwk reads the JSON object of RFC 7518 section 6.4, where k is the 32 key bytes in base64url without padding:

{"kty": "oct", "k": "QkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkI"}

kty must be oct. alg is optional and must be A256GCM or A256KW when present; kid, use, key_ops and any other member are ignored. A k in padded or standard base64, or one that does not decode to 32 bytes, is refused. There is no PEM form of a symmetric key, since no standard gives it a label.

use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKek, LocalKekVersion};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let jwk = r#"{"kty": "oct", "k": "QkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkJCQkI"}"#;
let kek = LocalKek::from_secret(
    LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::MIN),
    SecretValue::from(jwk),
    SymmetricKeyFormat::Jwk,
)?;
let _ = kek;
Ok(())
}

0.30 took the raw bytes in from_secret and base64 in from_file and from_env, see Migrating from 0.30. Errors name the file or the variable, never its content.

Tests and development

An ephemeral provider has one random version-1 KEK that lives as long as the provider, so the DEKs it encrypts cannot be decrypted once the process exits. It suits tests:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadEnvelope;
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tests".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

let encrypted = envelope
    .encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("secret"))
    .await?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt(b"orders/1042", &encrypted).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("secret"));
Ok(())
}

For a development environment whose data outlives a run, keep a KEK in a variable or a file. Generate one with:

openssl rand -base64 32

use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKek, LocalKekId, LocalKekVersion, LocalKmsProvider};

fn example() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let version = LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::MIN);
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    LocalKekId::try_from("dev".to_string())?,
    vec![LocalKek::from_env(version, "APP_DEV_KEK", SymmetricKeyFormat::Base64)?],
)?;
let _ = provider;
Ok(())
}

A VM or a single host

On a host, keep the KEK out of the environment. With systemd, encrypt it with systemd-creds, bound to the host’s TPM where it has one, and let systemd pass it to the service as a credential:

openssl rand -base64 32 | systemd-creds encrypt --name=kek-v1 - /etc/credstore.encrypted/kek-v1

[Service]
LoadCredentialEncrypted=kek-v1:/etc/credstore.encrypted/kek-v1

systemd decrypts it into $CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY, readable only by the service:

use std::num::NonZeroU32;
use std::path::PathBuf;

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKek, LocalKekId, LocalKekVersion, LocalKmsProvider};

fn example() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let credentials = PathBuf::from(std::env::var("CREDENTIALS_DIRECTORY")?);
let version = LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::MIN);

let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<AwsLc>::new(
    LocalKekId::try_from("orders-host-1".to_string())?,
    vec![LocalKek::from_file(version, &credentials.join("kek-v1"), SymmetricKeyFormat::Base64)?],
)?;
let _ = provider;
Ok(())
}

To rotate, add kek-v2 the same way and list both versions, as the next section describes.

Key versions and rotation

Every version in the provider’s list decrypts, and the highest one encrypts unless with_encrypting_version names another. The kek_version() of the encrypted DEK an encryption returns is the version that encrypted, in decimal. A process that does not list a version fails on a DEK under it with LOCAL_KEK_UNKNOWN_VERSION, so a rotation without downtime takes two deploys:

  1. list the new version in every process, with with_encrypting_version keeping the current one encrypting;
  2. once every process lists the new version, drop with_encrypting_version, or point it at the new version, so the new version encrypts.
use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKek, LocalKekId, LocalKekVersion, LocalKmsProvider};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn example(
    kek_v1: SecretValue,
    kek_v2: SecretValue,
) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let v1 = LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::try_from(1)?);
let v2 = LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::try_from(2)?);

// The first deploy: version 2 decrypts, version 1 still encrypts
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    LocalKekId::try_from("orders".to_string())?,
    vec![
        LocalKek::from_secret(v1, kek_v1, SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
        LocalKek::from_secret(v2, kek_v2, SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
    ],
)?
.with_encrypting_version(v1)?;
let _ = provider;
Ok(())
}

A version that is not in the list fails with_encrypting_version with LOCAL_KEK_VERSIONS.

Then sweep, see Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation, and check with a provider that lists the new version alone, before the deploy that drops the old version, so that a DEK the sweep missed fails the check and no read in the application. Dropping the old version from the list disables it, and listing it again restores it. Destroying it means destroying every copy of its material, backups included.

An asymmetric KEK

LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider is the local KMS with an RSA key pair per version, for the same uses as LocalKmsProvider, see Asymmetric KEKs. A LocalAsymmetricKek is one version: an RSA private key of 2048, 3072 or 4096 bits in PKCS#8, zeroised on drop. 2048 bits is deprecated and stays for the keys that exist. The loaders have the names of LocalKek’s and take a PrivateKeyFormat from kms_aead::formats: Pkcs8Der, the DER bytes, or Pkcs8Pem, a PRIVATE KEY PEM block, as openssl genpkey writes it. The format is never guessed from the bytes, and a file is at most 32 KiB. from_env reads the PEM text for Pkcs8Pem, and for Pkcs8Der the standard base64 of the DER, since a variable holds text.

let version = LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::MIN);
let provider = LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider::<AwsLc>::new(
    LocalKekId::try_from("dev".to_string())?,
    AsymmetricKekAlgorithm::RsaOaepSha256,
    vec![LocalAsymmetricKek::from_file(version, &path, PrivateKeyFormat::Pkcs8Pem)?],
)?;

// The public half of the encrypting version, for the producers
let public_key = provider.public_key(None)?;
  • the KEK id names one KEK, symmetric or asymmetric, never both, and the version list, the encrypting version and the rotation work as for the symmetric KEK;
  • an encrypted DEK is the 0x02 layout with the KEK id and the decimal version. A decryption fails with ASYMMETRIC_ENCRYPTED_DEK_FORMAT, LOCAL_KEK_ID_MISMATCH, LOCAL_KEK_UNKNOWN_VERSION or DECRYPT;
  • LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider::ephemeral has one generated version-1 key of 2048 bits, for tests;
  • it needs a backend with RSA: aws-lc-rs or RustCrypto. On ring it fails at construction with UNSUPPORTED_ALGORITHM. On RustCrypto the private half decrypts DEKs from trusted sources only, see Asymmetric KEKs.

Limits

  • No IAM, no audit log and no HSM. Protecting the KEK, at rest and in backups, is the operator’s job;
  • Every DEK encryption is one AES-256-GCM encryption with a random 96-bit nonce, so a KEK version is safe for about 2^32 encryptions. With the per-call envelope every value costs one encryption: a host encrypting 1,000 values per second spends 2^32 encryptions in 50 days. Use DEK reuse so that one encryption serves many values, and add a new KEK version before the budget is spent;
  • Crypto-shredding means destroying every copy of the KEK, backups included;
  • DEKs encrypted by a local KEK cannot be exported to BigQuery, which decrypts with Cloud KMS.

Errors

An encryption fails with RANDOM_GENERATOR when the backend’s CSPRNG fails. A decryption fails with LOCAL_ENCRYPTED_DEK_FORMAT when the encrypted DEK is not in the local layout, LOCAL_KEK_ID_MISMATCH when it was encrypted under another KEK id, LOCAL_KEK_UNKNOWN_VERSION when the provider does not hold its version, and DECRYPT when it is not authentic under that version.

Creating a provider fails with INVALID_LOCAL_KEK_ID for an invalid id, LOCAL_KEK_VERSIONS when the versions are empty or list one version twice, and LOCAL_KEK_SOURCE or LOCAL_KEK_LENGTH when a KEK cannot be read or is not 32 bytes. An asymmetric provider fails with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_PRIVATE_KEY for private key bytes that are not in the named format or not an RSA key of 2048, 3072 or 4096 bits.

Asymmetric KEKs

An asymmetric KEK is a KMS key pair whose public half encrypts data encryption keys and whose private half decrypts them. A producer holding the public key encrypts DEKs in its own process, with no KMS right and no KMS call, and encrypts values under them. A reader decrypts the DEKs through the KMS, which checks its permission and logs the call.

Use one for producers that may encrypt and must never decrypt: a service that writes records it does not read back, a batch job that fills a table, a process outside the KMS’s reach. The library encrypts DEKs with RSA-OAEP (RSA encryption with Optimal Asymmetric Encryption Padding, RFC 8017), SHA-256 and an empty label, AsymmetricKekAlgorithm::RsaOaepSha256, on RSA keys of 3072 bits for a new key; 2048 and 4096 are accepted, and 2048 is deprecated. Cloud KMS, AWS KMS, Azure Key Vault, Vault Transit and the local KMS decrypt it.

Which way the keys go

With an asymmetric KEK the public key encrypts and the private key decrypts, as in every public-key encryption. The other direction is a signature, see Signatures and MACs:

SignatureAsymmetric KEKSymmetric KEK
Public keyverifies; anyone holding it checks the bytesencrypts DEKs; producers hold it, with no KMS rightnone
Private keysigns, inside the KMSdecrypts DEKs, inside the KMS, for the readersthe one key, inside the KMS
Who may producethe holder of the private key onlyanyone holding the public keyprincipals with the KMS encrypt right
Givesorigin and integrityconfidentialityconfidentiality

Any holder of the public key can produce a value that the readers decrypt, so an asymmetric KEK gives no origin. Data that must be both confidential and attributable is encrypted and then signed.

Producers and readers

A deployment has three sides:

SideHoldsKMS rightCalls
Producerthe public key, pinned in its configurationnoneKmsAeadEnvelope::encrypt over a PublicKeyKekProvider
Application, or any readera KMS providerasymmetric decrypt on the keyKmsAeadEnvelope::decrypt, or a Keyring
Administrationthe public key and a KMS provider, in an AsymmetricKekProviderasymmetric decrypt, and read of the public keythe KeyringAdmin calls and the re-encryptions

The readers use the providers they use today. They read the new layout by its first byte, and a provider decrypts the DEKs the KMS encrypted beside the DEKs a public key encrypted. The producer is an envelope writer and encrypts randomised values only: a beacon and a deterministic value both need a key’s plaintext, which only a reader decrypts. A keyring never has a producer, since encrypting under a keyring version needs the version’s plaintext DEK. What an asymmetric KEK changes in a keyring is who encrypts new versions’ DEKs, the administration with the public key.

use kms_aead::backends::AwsLc;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider, LocalKekId, PublicKeyKekProvider};
use kms_aead::{AsymmetricKekAlgorithm, AsymmetricKekPublicKey, Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
// The reader's provider: a cloud KMS provider in production
let reader_provider = LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider::<AwsLc>::ephemeral(
    LocalKekId::try_from("orders".to_string())?,
    AsymmetricKekAlgorithm::RsaOaepSha256,
)?;

// The administration hands the public key to the producers as a JSON document
let document = reader_provider.public_key(None)?.to_json()?;

// The producer: no KMS client, and no call that decrypts
let public_key = AsymmetricKekPublicKey::try_from_json(&document)?;
let producer = KmsAeadEnvelope::<PublicKeyKekProvider<AwsLc>, AwsLc>::new(
    PublicKeyKekProvider::new(public_key)?,
)?;
let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> = producer
    .encrypt(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("4111 1111 1111 1111"))
    .await?;

// The reader decrypts the DEK through its KMS
let reader =
    KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider<AwsLc>, AwsLc>::new(reader_provider)?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = reader.decrypt(b"orders/1042", &encrypted).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("4111 1111 1111 1111"));
Ok(())
}

Full example available here.

The KMS rights per provider:

KMSReaderAdministration, in addition
Google Cloud KMScloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToDecryptcloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.viewPublicKey, which roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecrypter does not carry
AWS KMSkms:Decrypt on the keykms:GetPublicKey on the key
Azure Key Vaultkeys/unwrap/actionkeys/get
Vault Transitupdate on transit/decrypt/<key>read on transit/keys/<key>

No side needs a KMS encrypt right. Be aware the KMS audit log no longer shows who encrypted a DEK: the encryption is a local operation. The decrypt log stays.

The public key

An AsymmetricKekPublicKey holds the key and what a reader needs to decrypt:

  • the KEK, a KekRef: the KMS and the key’s name there, as the reader’s provider names it;
  • the KEK version, where the KMS decrypts with a named one: the CryptoKeyVersion number on Cloud KMS, the 32-character version on Key Vault, the Transit version, the local version. AWS KMS has no versions, and AsymmetricKekPublicKey::new refuses a version there, or none where one is needed, with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_PUBLIC_KEY;
  • the algorithm;
  • the key, in a named PublicKeyFormat: SpkiDer as AWS KMS returns it, SpkiPem as openssl, Cloud KMS and Transit write it, or Jwk as Key Vault returns it. Raw has no meaning for an RSA key.

The administration fetches it with the right of the table above, on the provider it holds: GcpKmsProvider::public_key(&version), AwsKmsProvider::public_key(), AzureKmsProvider::public_key(None) for the latest version, HcVaultTransitProvider::public_key(None) and LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider::public_key(None). Each reads the version from the KMS’s response, so the key and the version belong together.

The public key reaches a producer as the rest of its configuration does. A producer that takes it from an unauthenticated place encrypts for whoever supplied it, and the reader’s KMS decrypts nothing. Every provider that holds the key logs its fingerprint, the SHA-256 of the SubjectPublicKeyInfo, at INFO, and PublicKeyKekProvider::pinned fails with ASYMMETRIC_KEK_MISMATCH when the key has another fingerprint than the operator expects. Encrypt one test value and decrypt it with a reader before a new producer writes anything that matters.

What each backend does

RingAwsLcRustCrypto
Encrypt with a public keyno: UNSUPPORTED_ALGORITHM at constructionyesyes
Decrypt through a cloud KMSyesyesyes
Local KMS private halfnoyesyes

ring has no RSA encryption, so a producer or a local private half on Ring fails at construction with KmsAeadError::UnsupportedAlgorithm. A reader on Ring needs nothing of RSA.

The rustcrypto feature adds the rsa crate for RSA-OAEP. Its private-key operation runs only in a LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider on RustCrypto. Encrypting with the public key is a public-key operation with no private key to leak, and a cloud reader runs no RSA. Every application that enables rustcrypto carries rsa in its lockfile. A local KMS on RustCrypto decrypts DEKs from trusted sources only, such as its own keyring record. A process that decrypts what others send runs its private half on AwsLc, or in a KMS.

The compile-time guarantee

PublicKeyKekProvider implements KmsAeadEncryptionProvider and not KmsAeadDecryptionProvider. Every call that decrypts a DEK or a value requires the second: decrypt, the *_with_encrypted_dek calls, beacon, reencrypt_dek, DekCache::with_dek and every keyring. Over a producer none of them compiles, so an encrypt-only deployment holds whatever its IAM policy says.

The guarantee covers the producer’s calls into the library. A DEK that generate_encrypted_dek returns is in the producer’s hands, and it decrypts its own writes with it through AeadCipher::decrypt_bare. Other code in the process is outside the guarantee: a type of the application’s own that implements KmsAeadDecryptionProvider under the producer’s KekRef is served from the producer’s DEK cache, and whoever reads the process’s memory reads the cached keys. A process that writes and reads holds a KMS provider, and is a reader.

Keyrings under an asymmetric KEK

The administration opens its registry with an AsymmetricKekProvider; the applications open theirs with the plain KMS provider. create, rotate, change_algorithm and mark_compromised encrypt the new version’s DEK with the public key, and reencrypt_deks decrypts every DEK through the KMS and encrypts it again with the public key. The record does not change.

Be aware that whoever holds the public key can encrypt a DEK of their own and file it in the record. A store writer who does so makes every application decrypt it, and the values encrypted under that version are the writer’s to read. The control is a signed record, see Signing the keyring record. Run every keyring under an asymmetric KEK with RecordSignaturePolicy::Required on every application, and turn it on before the first DEK a public key encrypts. Under any other mode a registry that loads a record holding a DEK encrypted with a public key logs a WARN and decrypts it, and so does a NoRecordSigner admin whose provider is asymmetric when it is built.

A keyring under an asymmetric KEK exports no BigQuery and no Tink keyset, since both readers decrypt the keyset with their KMS client’s symmetric decryption. export_bigquery_keyset, export_tink_keyset and an Encryptor on a field with an Interop fail with INTEROP.

An asymmetric decryption is a heavier KMS operation than a symmetric one: quotas and prices per request are higher for RSA on every KMS. A producer reuses DEKs, with a store-less envelope and a DekReusePolicy, and a keyring holds one DEK per version.

A KEK rotation moves new encryptions to a new version, and every stored DEK keeps decrypting under its own version. The producers hold the version they encrypt under in the public key, so a rotation is also a configuration change of every producer. AWS KMS has no versions: a rotation is a new key, and a keyring stays under the key it was created with.

Migrating an Azure or Transit RSA deployment

An envelope or a keyring on an Azure Key Vault RSA key or a Transit rsa-* key is under an asymmetric KEK already: Key Vault’s wrapKey operation, which encrypts a DEK, is the RSA-OAEP encryption its public key would make. Nothing stored has to be re-encrypted:

  1. deploy 0.31 to every reader first. 0.30 reads no DEK encrypted with a public key;
  2. for a keyring, turn record signing on before any DEK is encrypted with a public key: give the administration a record signer, run sign_record on every keyring, then set RecordSignaturePolicy::Required on every application;
  3. fetch the public key on the administration’s provider and build the AsymmetricKekProvider from it and the KMS provider. Every DEK the administration encrypts from then on is encrypted with the public key; reencrypt_deks moves the existing versions at the administration’s choice;
  4. hand the public key to the producers, which switch from a KMS provider to a PublicKeyKekProvider and drop their KMS rights;
  5. take keys/wrap/action, or update on transit/encrypt/<key>, out of the administration’s grant once no 0.30 administration remains.

A keyring on a Cloud KMS or AWS symmetric key cannot move to an asymmetric key: a keyring stays under the KEK it was created with. A new asymmetric key is a new keyring, filled by a sweep.

Threat model and limits

The threat model of Security considerations holds, with these changes:

  • a holder of the public key produces values that decrypt, and plants a DEK in a keyring record unless the applications require signed records;
  • encrypting with the public key leaves no KMS audit log. Who encrypted which DEK is recorded nowhere unless the producer logs it;
  • a producer with a wrong public key encrypts for someone else. Authenticate the channel the key arrives through, pin the fingerprint and test one value;
  • anyone with the public key makes the readers pay for decryptions: a made-up encrypted DEK costs the reader one asymmetric KMS decryption;
  • the header of the encrypted DEK is not authenticated. A change makes the decryption fail and makes no value decrypt;
  • RSA is not quantum-safe. An encrypted DEK kept in a record, a value or a backup decrypts for whoever later runs a cryptographically relevant quantum computer against the public key. Data whose confidentiality must outlast RSA stays under a symmetric KEK until a KMS decapsulates a post-quantum KEM in general availability, which none does today;
  • the producer’s DEK cache holds plaintext DEKs, as every store-less writer’s does;
  • the local KMS’s private half is in process memory, with no IAM, no audit log and no HSM.

The bytes of the encrypted DEK are in Formats, and the codes this chapter mentions are in Error handling.

Keyring and field encryption

The kms-aead-keyring crate encrypts fields of application records under a keyring: the record of one scope’s key versions, such as a tenant’s, kept in a keyring store. Each key version is a data encryption key (DEK) encrypted by a KEK in the KMS, with a lifecycle state.

Compared to an envelope, a keyring value carries a 4-byte key id instead of its encrypted DEK:

  • no KMS encryption on write, and one KMS decryption per key version per DEK cache TTL, on write and read;
  • 33 bytes of overhead per value, 21 for deterministic values, see Formats of Encrypted;
  • central staged rotation, retirement, destruction and a compromised flag, for every process that uses the keyring;
  • deterministic fields that a data store finds by equality, across rotations;
  • beacons, blind indexes that a data store matches by equality beside randomised fields;
  • values in Tink’s wire format, which Tink and BigQuery read too.

The price is one more piece of infrastructure, the keyring store, which holds one small JSON document per keyring. See Choosing a mode for the comparison with the envelope modes of kms-aead.

Applications and administration

See Applications and administration.

The registry and its handles

A first keyring, on the memory store with a local KEK, is in With a keyring.

KeyringRegistry::create creates the record, with one active version of each purpose its KeyringAlgorithms name, and fails with RecordExists when there is one already. open_or_create creates it on the first start and opens it on every later one, and admin only opens it. The three return a KeyringAdmin. KeyringRegistry::open returns a Keyring, and fails with RecordMissing when there is no record; it is the application’s call:

use firestore::FirestoreDb;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsKeyRef, GcpKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::store::FirestoreKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{Keyring, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<FirestoreKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    FirestoreKeyringStore::new(FirestoreDb::new("my-project").await?),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let keyring: Keyring<FirestoreKeyringStore, GcpKmsProvider, Ring> = registry
    .open(GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref).await?, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"))
    .await?;
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

The registry takes the store and the backend as the type parameters of KeyringRegistry<S, B>, and each call takes the provider, so that the handles are Keyring<S, P, B> and KeyringAdmin<S, P, B>.

A Keyring and a KeyringAdmin of one keyring in one registry share its cached record and its watch, so a write through the KeyringAdmin is seen by every Keyring at once. KeyringAdmin::keyring() returns the Keyring it holds, for id, record, reload, encryption and sweeps inside an administrative job. The example in With a keyring takes its Keyring there too, since the ephemeral local KEK has no second copy to open the keyring with: LocalKmsProvider is not Clone.

Keyring::encryptor takes the cached record, and the Encryptor encrypts under that record’s active versions; decrypt uses the version the value names. A write takes one encryptor and encrypts every encrypted field of the record through it, so that the encryption marker it stores beside them names the versions they are under, see The encryption marker. Neither calls the store while the cached record is fresh, nor the KMS while the version’s DEK is in the DEK cache, see Record cache.

A keyring stays open until the registry shuts down. Where the store has push, the registry follows the change signals of each open keyring with a task on the current tokio runtime. registry.shutdown(None) stops those tasks and the store’s listener and waits for them:

  • a revalidation read in flight finishes and is taken; waits, retry delays and connection attempts stop at once;
  • the open keyrings’ keys are evicted from the DEK cache, and no other key is;
  • the handles keep serving from their cached records, which their TTL revalidates without push, and later opens work too, with no watch.

Await it before the last clone of the registry is dropped: a registry dropped while its tasks run aborts them and logs a WARN. shutdown(Some(timeout)) aborts what still runs after timeout, with a WARN that counts the aborted tasks, and an abort leaves no half-written state.

The bytes of values, key ids and records are in Formats.

Applications and administration

A keyring has two sides, each with its own handle and its own rights:

  • the application decrypts and runs sweeps on a Keyring, which KeyringRegistry::open returns in every build, and encrypts, computes beacons and re-encrypts on the Encryptor the Keyring hands out for each write;
  • the administration creates keyrings, rotates, promotes, retires and destroys versions, flags compromised ones, re-encrypts DEKs, exports keysets and shreds keyrings, on a KeyringAdmin, which KeyringRegistry::create, open_or_create and admin return with the admin feature.

The application builds without the admin feature and runs under a principal with the rights of its row only:

SideHandleKMSKeyring store
ApplicationKeyringdecrypt: GCP roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecrypter, AWS kms:Decrypt, Azure Key Vault keys/unwrap/action, Vault Transit decryptread, and listen or watch: Firestore read, PostgreSQL SELECT and LISTEN, Cloud Storage object read, S3 GetObject
AdministrationKeyringAdminencrypt and decrypt, or under an asymmetric KEK decrypt and read of the public keyread, write and delete: Firestore write, PostgreSQL INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE, which also send the NOTIFY, Cloud Storage and S3 object write and delete

Sweeps run on the application’s side: re-encrypting a value encrypts it under a DEK the record already holds, and never asks the KMS to encrypt a DEK. Every call that encrypts a DEK, writes or deletes the record is a KeyringAdmin call. The exact roles per KMS are in KMS rights, and the permissions per store in Store rights.

The admin feature keeps the administrative calls out of the application’s build, and the rights keep them out of its reach: without the KMS encrypt right and write access to the store, those calls fail in any build.

An application opens only keyrings that exist: open on a keyring the store holds no record of fails with KeyringError::RecordMissing. So the administration creates each keyring before an application opens it:

  • from infrastructure: a job, a CLI or a deployment step, built with the admin feature and run with the administrative rights, calls create or open_or_create;
  • or from an application that provisions lazily, such as one keyring per tenant on the tenant’s first request. Such an application enables admin and is given the administrative rights deliberately, and is then trusted as the administration is, see Keyring limits.

An application’s own tests that create keyrings in MemoryKeyringStore enable admin for the tests only:

[dependencies]
kms-aead-keyring = { version = "0.31", features = ["ring", "firestore-tls-roots"] }

[dev-dependencies]
kms-aead-keyring = { version = "0.31", features = ["ring", "firestore-tls-roots", "admin"] }

Be aware Cargo unifies the features of the packages one command builds: a cargo build of a workspace with both the application and an administrative job gives the application admin too. Build and deploy the application with cargo build -p <application>.

KMS rights

A keyring record is signed only where the administration holds a record signer, see Signing the keyring record and Keyring deployment, so a deployment splits the two sides above into two principals:

  • Application: builds without the admin feature, and calls Keyring only: encryption, decryption, beacons and sweeps. It needs KMS decrypt on the KEK and read access to the keyring store. None of its calls asks the KMS to encrypt, so it needs nothing more;
  • Administration: builds with the admin feature, and calls the registry’s create, open_or_create and admin, and the KeyringAdmin they return: rotate, promote, change_algorithm, mark_compromised, retire, reinstate, destroy, dangerously_destroy_all_versions, shred, reencrypt_deks, export_bigquery_keyset and export_tink_keyset. It needs KMS encrypt and decrypt on the KEK, or under an asymmetric KEK decrypt and the read of the public key and no encrypt right, and where it signs records the right to sign with the record signer’s key. It is the only principal with write access to the keyring store. It runs in a job or in infrastructure automation, never in a request handler.
KMSApplicationAdministration
GCProles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecrypterroles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyEncrypterDecrypter
AWSkms:Decrypt on the keykms:Encrypt and kms:Decrypt on the key, plus kms:GenerateRandom with the KMS random generator
Azure Key Vaulta role with keys/unwrap/action on the keya role with keys/wrap/action and keys/unwrap/action on the key
Vault Transitupdate on transit/decrypt/<key>update on transit/encrypt/<key> and transit/decrypt/<key>

Under an asymmetric KEK the public key encrypts new DEKs in the administration’s own process, so no side has the KMS encrypt right: Cloud KMS useToEncrypt, AWS kms:Encrypt, Key Vault keys/wrap/action and Transit transit/encrypt leave the administration’s grant. The administration needs the read of the public key beside the applications’ decrypt:

KMSApplicationAdministration
GCPcloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.useToDecrypt, as in roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecrypterdecrypt, and cloudkms.cryptoKeyVersions.viewPublicKey, as in roles/cloudkms.publicKeyViewer. roles/cloudkms.cryptoKeyDecrypter does not carry it
AWSkms:Decrypt on the keykms:Decrypt and kms:GetPublicKey on the key
Azure Key Vaultkeys/unwrap/actionkeys/unwrap/action and keys/get. “Key Vault Crypto Service Encryption User” carries read, wrap and unwrap and no decrypt
Vault Transitupdate on transit/decrypt/<key>update on transit/decrypt/<key>, and read on transit/keys/<key> or transit/export/public-key/<key>

The KMS audit log no longer shows who encrypted a DEK, since the encryption is local, and anyone holding the public key can encrypt one: a keyring under an asymmetric KEK runs with RecordSignaturePolicy::Required on every application, see Keyrings under an asymmetric KEK. The public_key call of each provider is in KMS providers.

The permissions of each store are below.

Store rights

The store’s access control is what the record’s integrity rests on, unless the record is signed: only the administration writes the record, and the applications read it and listen to its changes, see above:

StoreApplicationAdministration
Firestoreread of the keyring documents, which the listener uses too, such as roles/datastore.viewerread, write and delete of the keyring documents, such as roles/datastore.user
PostgreSQLSELECT on the table, and LISTEN, which needs no grantSELECT, INSERT, UPDATE and DELETE on the table; each write and delete also sends the NOTIFY
Cloud Storagestorage.objects.getstorage.objects.get, storage.objects.create, storage.objects.delete, since a write replaces the object, and storage.objects.list, which dangerously_shred() needs to find the object’s generations
S3s3:GetObjects3:GetObject and s3:PutObject, and for dangerously_shred() also s3:ListBucketVersions, s3:DeleteObject and s3:DeleteObjectVersion

What to alert on

Each side logs events an operator acts on, such as a refused record, a failed revalidation or a re-encryption that failed, and records metrics with the opentelemetry feature. The list, with the next step for each, is in What to alert on.

Key versions and lifecycle

The record

A keyring record holds:

  • the keyring id, the scope it encrypts for: 1 to 128 ASCII alphanumerics, -, _ and ., except . and .. and ids that start and end with __. Such an id is a Firestore document id, a PostgreSQL key, a part of a GCS object name or an S3 object key, and a BigQuery string without escaping. It is an identifier, not a secret, and appears in spans and errors;
  • the KEK of the provider the keyring was created with;
  • the algorithms new versions use, one per purpose;
  • the re-encryption epoch, which every write that makes a version active moves on by one, and which the application stores beside its records as an EncryptionMarker, see Sweeps;
  • every key version ever created, in creation order, each with its encrypted DEK, its state and, once it was active, its re-encryption range: the markers of the records encrypted under it, see Records under a version.

Every store keeps it as the same JSON document, see Keyring records for the document and the rules every read checks. A record that breaks one fails with RecordInvalid, naming the rule and never the bytes.

The kek field is informational: it catches a keyring opened with the wrong provider, with KekMismatch, and is no security control, since a writer of the record sets it as freely as any other field. What refuses a DEK encrypted under another KEK is the KMS, since every decryption names the configured KEK.

Keyring::record() returns the cached record, to inspect versions and states, such as record.pending(Purpose::Randomized) for the version a rotation staged. An administrative job reads it as admin.keyring().record().

Key ids

Every value carries the key id of the version that encrypted it, and the id names the version’s purpose: randomised, deterministic or beacon. So a value of one purpose never decrypts under a version of another, and the purpose of a value is known from its bytes, see Keyring key ids. The keyring numbers the versions of each purpose from its first id: randomised versions are 1, 2, 3, …, deterministic ones 2147483649, 2147483650, … and beacon ones 1073741825, 1073741826, …. Ids are never reused: a destroyed version keeps its id in the record.

A key id is a kms_aead::KeyId, which the keyring re-exports. value.key_id() reads the key id of a stored value, and version.algorithm().purpose() the purpose of a version. An operation that names a reserved key id fails with ReservedKeyId before any store call.

States

                       promote                          retire           destroy
  rotate ──▶ Pending ──────────▶ Active ──▶ DecryptOnly ──────▶ Retired ─────────▶ Destroyed
                │                   ▲            ▲                 │                   ▲
                │       create ─────┘            └────reinstate────┘                   │
                └───────────────────────────────destroy────────────────────────────────┘
  • Pending: staged by a rotation. Decrypts, and never encrypts until it is promoted. At most one per purpose;
  • Active: encrypts new values and decrypts. At most one per purpose;
  • DecryptOnly: decrypts only. Every version a promotion replaced;
  • Retired: refused on read with KeyVersionRetired. The encrypted DEK stays in the record, so reinstate can undo it;
  • Destroyed: the encrypted DEK is removed from the record, and reads fail with KeyVersionDestroyed.

A promotion moves the previous active version to DecryptOnly, and so does mark_compromised on an active version. Any state right of Active may also be compromised. The flag is set by mark_compromised, with a time and an optional note, and never clears. Pending and Active are never compromised: a compromised active version moves to DecryptOnly in the same write that flags it.

KeyringRegistry::create starts each purpose with an active version. Every later version starts as pending, except the replacement mark_compromised adds for an active version, which is active at once.

The calls, on a KeyringAdmin, with the admin feature, see Applications and administration:

CallDoes
rotate(purpose)stages a pending version with the record’s algorithm for the purpose, and returns its key id
promote(key_id)makes the pending version active; the previous active one becomes DecryptOnly. Returns a Rotation with both ids. Refuses with PromotionTooEarly until record_ttl has passed since the version was staged
promote_now(key_id)as promote, without the wait
change_algorithm(algorithm)makes algorithm the one new versions of its purpose use, and stages a version of it as rotate does
mark_compromised(key_id, note)flags the version; for the active version also adds an active replacement in the same write
retire(key_id)DecryptOnly to Retired, once a sweep found no value left under it
reinstate(key_id)Retired back to DecryptOnly, for a value that turned up after all; a compromised flag stays
destroy(key_id)Retired or Pending to Destroyed
dangerously_destroy_all_versions()every version of every purpose to Destroyed, in any state, in one write: the keyring encrypts and decrypts nothing from then on, except through a stream encryptor or decryptor already made, see Shredding a keyring, and the record stays in the store
