dangerously_shred()destroys every version in one write, as the call above, then deletes the record from the store with the earlier versions the store keeps, see Shredding a keyring
reencrypt_deks()decrypts and encrypts again every DEK in the record, under the KEK’s current version; no value changes. Returns a DekReencryption per version, with the KEK version its DEK moved from and to

A call that does not apply to the version’s state fails with InvalidTransition, which names the version, its state and the Transition:

  • rotate and change_algorithm while the purpose has a pending version fail with Transition::Rotate, naming that version. Promote or destroy it first;
  • retire, mark_compromised and reinstate refuse a pending version, which never encrypted a value. destroy it instead.

Each call reads the record, applies the change and writes it with a compare-and-swap, reading again after a conflict up to KeyringOptions::cas_retries times, KeyringOptions::DEFAULT_CAS_RETRIES by default, before RecordConflict.

States move forward only: nothing returns to Pending, and Pending moves only to Active or Destroyed. A running process refuses a record that moves a version or the re-encryption epoch backwards, see Record cache.

Staged rotation

A rotation is two calls, so that no process meets a value under a version it does not know yet:

  1. rotate(purpose) stages the version. Every process decrypts under it once its record refreshes, and none encrypts under it;
  2. after a wait, promote(key_id) makes it active. Processes encrypt under it from their next refresh, and every process that reads those values already has the version.

Promote only after the longest time any process may run without a successful refresh of its record, counted from KeyVersion::staged_at(). For a healthy process that time is at most record_ttl, KeyringRegistryOptions::DEFAULT_RECORD_TTL by default. A process whose revalidations fail keeps its cached record for as long as they fail, and logs a WARN on each failure: “The keyring record could not be revalidated; the cached record stays in use”. A deployment whose instances can take hours to update or to reach the store waits as long. Alert on that WARN: an instance that logs it is one a promotion can leave behind.

promote holds the promotion back for the registry’s record_ttl from staged_at(): before that it fails with PromotionTooEarly, whose remaining is the time left, so the job can sleep that long and call it again. The guard covers healthy processes only. promote_now skips it, for a fleet known to have refreshed since the rotation, such as one restarted since, and for a compromise that cannot wait.

The guard waits the record_ttl of the registry the KeyringAdmin was opened from. Nothing in the record carries the TTL the other processes run with, so the administrative job’s registry needs the longest record_ttl in the fleet. The guard also compares this host’s clock with the staged_at() the staging host’s clock wrote, and a skew between the two clocks shortens or lengthens the wait by as much.

An instance that has not seen the version when it reads a value under it treats the key id as unknown and reloads its record once, at most once per reload_min_interval. When the store answers, the read succeeds after the reload; when it does not, the read fails with KeyVersionUnknown. The wait before promote is what keeps that path rare.

use std::sync::Arc;

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyId, KeyVersionState, KeyringAdmin, KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringError, KeyringId,
    KeyringRecord, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, Purpose, Rotation,
};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .open_or_create(provider, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"), KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;

// The administrative job stages the version
let staged: KeyId = admin.rotate(Purpose::Randomized).await?;
let record: Arc<KeyringRecord> = admin.keyring().record();
let pending = record
    .pending(Purpose::Randomized)
    .ok_or("no pending version")?;
assert_eq!(pending.state(), KeyVersionState::Pending);

// Inside the record TTL from `pending.staged_at()`, `promote` refuses and says how long is left
assert!(matches!(
    admin.promote(staged).await,
    Err(KeyringError::PromotionTooEarly(error))
        if error.remaining <= KeyringRegistryOptions::DEFAULT_RECORD_TTL
));

// The job waits that long and promotes it; `promote_now` stands in for the wait here
let rotation: Rotation = admin.promote_now(staged).await?;
assert_eq!(rotation.current, staged);
assert_eq!(rotation.previous, Some(KeyId::new(1)));
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

Compromised versions

mark_compromised on the active version replaces it with a version that is active at once, not pending. Encrypting under a compromised key has to stop at each process’s next refresh, and a wait before the replacement takes over would leave the leaked key encrypting new values for that long. The cost is the one staged rotation avoids: a process that has not seen the replacement yet reloads its record once, rate-limited, when it meets a value under it. A pending version of the purpose stays pending, to be promoted or destroyed as planned.

The replacement needs a new DEK encrypted. When the KMS cannot encrypt it, in an outage or without the encrypt right, the flag is written alone, a WARN is logged, and the call returns Ok(None): the purpose then has no active version, and encrypt fails with NoActiveVersion until a version is promoted. Once the KMS answers, rotate and promote_now restore one, or promote_now on the pending version if the purpose has one: promote would refuse with PromotionTooEarly for up to record_ttl from the staging. A compromised flag is never held back by the KMS.

destroy and dangerously_destroy_all_versions remove encrypted DEKs from this record only. A backup of the record still holds them, and a keyset exported while the version decrypted holds its key under the same KEK. The KMS decrypts both as long as the KEK version that encrypted them exists, see Crypto-shredding.

Shredding a keyring

dangerously_shred() is the call for a keyring whose values must become unreadable under a KEK that other keyrings share. It destroys every version in one write, as dangerously_destroy_all_versions does, signed as every lifecycle write is, then deletes the record from the store at the revision that write left. The runbook has the rules of the two steps, the retry of a shred that failed, and the copies a store keeps beyond its API, see Shredding a keyring.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringError, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let id = KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42");
let admin = registry
    .open_or_create(provider, id.clone(), KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
admin.dangerously_shred().await?;

// The id is free: the registry reads the store again and finds no record
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
assert!(matches!(
    registry.open(provider, id).await,
    Err(KeyringError::RecordMissing(_))
));
Ok(())
}

A keyring already gone makes KeyringRegistry::admin fail with RecordMissing, which a job that erases a tenant treats as done. The handles opened before the shred stay on a record with every version destroyed, and the registry’s create makes a new keyring under the id, with key ids from 1.

dangerously_shred() never calls the KMS and leaves every KMS key version as it was. Values encrypted under the keyring stay where they are and decrypt under nothing. Every process that holds the keyring takes the deletion at its next revalidation, see A record gone from the store. A stream decryptor made before the shred keeps decrypting until it is dropped, so a service builds one per request, see Shredding a keyring.

Reading a compromised version

KeyringOptions::compromised_reads decides what decrypt does with a value under a compromised version:

  • CompromisedReadPolicy::Block, KeyringOptions::DEFAULT_COMPROMISED_READS: fails with KeyVersionCompromised, decided on the cached record before any decryption;
  • CompromisedReadPolicy::Allow: decrypts, with one WARN event per value. Use it for the time of a sweep when availability matters more, and be aware the leaked DEK can forge values under that id meanwhile.

decrypt_with_trust decrypts the value whatever the policy, and returns a Decrypted with the value, the key id of the version that decrypted it and its KeyTrust, Trusted or Compromised. It is the call for a sweep. A compromised version’s DEK is decrypted by the provider for that call only and never returns to the DEK cache.

Keyring stores

A keyring store keeps one record per keyring id, with a compare-and-swap and a delete on each, and optionally signals changes to it. The library provides:

StoreTypeFeatureRevisionPush
Memorytesting::MemoryKeyringStorenonea write counterevery write
Firestorestore::FirestoreKeyringStorefirestorethe document’s update timea listener on the document
PostgreSQLstore::PostgresKeyringStorepostgresa revision columnNOTIFY
Cloud Storagestore::GcsKeyringStoregcsthe object’s generationnone
S3store::S3KeyringStores3the object’s ETagnone

Every store keeps the record as the same JSON document, and every read checks it. A store with push makes a change reach the other processes within seconds; without push, the record TTL bounds it, see Record cache.

The rights of the application and the administration on each store are in Store rights.

Back the store up, or keep its history, since values cannot be read without the encrypted DEKs in the record, see the runbook. A backup is also a copy of the record that KeyringAdmin::dangerously_shred() does not reach, see What a delete removes.

Memory

MemoryKeyringStore loses its records when the process exits, and with them the encrypted DEK of every version: a value encrypted under a keyring in it, and kept anywhere that outlives the process, never decrypts again. Use it for tests, or for values that never outlive the process.

It lives in kms_aead_keyring::testing for that reason, and is compiled in every build. Clone shares the same records. It keeps each record as its JSON document, as the other stores do, and signals a watch on every write. A delete removes the entry.

Firestore

The record of a keyring is the document kms_keyrings/<keyring id>, whose fields are those of the record’s JSON. with_collection takes another collection than FirestoreKeyringStore::DEFAULT_COLLECTION, as firestore-rs’s FirestoreCollectionId. A create fails when the document exists, and an update carries the update time it read as its precondition.

use firestore::FirestoreDb;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::store::FirestoreKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{Keyring, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let db = FirestoreDb::new("my-project").await?;
let store = FirestoreKeyringStore::new(db);

let registry = KeyringRegistry::<FirestoreKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    store,
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let keyring: Keyring<FirestoreKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .open(provider, KeyringId::try_from("tenant-42".to_string())?)
    .await?;
let _ = keyring;
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

FirestoreDb is the firestore crate’s own client: the application builds it with the project, database and credentials it chooses. The version of the firestore crate is part of this crate’s API under the Firestore features.

The firestore feature is the store on firestore-rs without its default features, so it has no TLS root store and no crypto provider, and needs a firestore-rs or gcloud-sdk root store feature from the application, or one of:

  • firestore-tls-roots: the system’s native roots, firestore-rs’s tls-roots, which brings native-tls and so OpenSSL on Linux;
  • firestore-tls-webpki: rustls, with the bundled WebPKI roots for the gRPC connection to Firestore, and no native TLS;

each with firestore-rs’s auth-default-crypto. A delete removes the document, with its update time as the precondition.

Each store holds one listen stream, with a target on the document of each keyring it watches. Firestore’s point-in-time recovery restores an earlier record. Be aware the firestore crate logs every listen event at trace level on its firestore target, documents included, so that level writes encrypted DEKs to the log. Keep it off where the log leaves the administration’s reach.

PostgreSQL

One row per keyring, in a table the operator creates. The library runs no migrations. The table, kms_keyrings unless with_table names another:

CREATE TABLE kms_keyrings (
    id         TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
    record     JSONB NOT NULL,
    revision   BIGINT NOT NULL,
    updated_at TIMESTAMPTZ NOT NULL DEFAULT now()
);
  • the rights of the application and the administration on the table are in Store rights;
  • the table name is unqualified, so the connection’s search_path selects the schema;
  • the revision of a row is the server’s clock in microseconds when the row is created, and the larger of that and the previous revision plus one at each write. A row deleted and created again therefore never repeats a revision of the id, which a running process that missed the deletion would take for an unchanged record;
  • every write sends NOTIFY <table>, '<keyring id>' when it commits, through pg_notify in the same statement, and so does a delete, which removes the row at its revision;
  • PostgreSQL lets any session notify any channel. A keyring revalidates at most once per reload_min_interval however many signals arrive, so a stray NOTIFY costs one read.

The store connects through tokio-postgres, with a tokio_postgres::Config and a TLS connector the application chooses. NoTls for a local database:

use kms_aead_keyring::store::{PostgresKeyringStore, PostgresTable};
use tokio_postgres::{Config, NoTls};

fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let config: Config = "postgres://app@localhost/app".parse()?;
let store: PostgresKeyringStore<NoTls> = PostgresKeyringStore::new(config, NoTls)
    .with_table(PostgresTable::try_from("tenant_keyrings".to_string())?);
let _ = store;
Ok(())
}

tokio-postgres-rustls’s MakeRustlsConnect for production, here with the platform’s root certificates:

use kms_aead_keyring::store::PostgresKeyringStore;
use rustls::{ClientConfig, RootCertStore};
use tokio_postgres::Config;
use tokio_postgres_rustls::MakeRustlsConnect;

fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let mut roots = RootCertStore::empty();
roots.add_parsable_certificates(rustls_native_certs::load_native_certs().certs);
let tls = MakeRustlsConnect::new(
    ClientConfig::builder()
        .with_root_certificates(roots)
        .with_no_client_auth(),
);
let config: Config = "postgres://app@db.internal/app?sslmode=require".parse()?;
let store: PostgresKeyringStore<MakeRustlsConnect> = PostgresKeyringStore::new(config, tls);
let _ = store;
Ok(())
}

postgres-native-tls’s MakeTlsConnector works the same way for the platform TLS library.

The store opens at most two connections, whatever the number of keyrings on it:

  • the query connection, on the first read or write. When it is lost, the call that finds it lost fails, and the next call connects again. Concurrent calls wait for one attempt;
  • the listener connection, on the first watch, shared by every keyring that watches. It closes when the last watch is dropped. When it is lost, every keyring hears of it, the store reconnects after a delay that doubles from 0.5 to 30 seconds, and the keyrings revalidate.

Clones of the store share both connections. Set connect_timeout on the Config to bound each attempt: calls wait for the attempt in progress.

The postgres feature also enables kms-aead’s postgres feature, which gives Encrypted tokio-postgres’s ToSql and FromSql as BYTEA, see Encrypted fields.

Cloud Storage

Each record is the object kms-keyrings/<keyring id>.json in a bucket, through the google.storage.v2 gRPC API. with_prefix sets another ObjectPrefix than GcsKeyringStore::DEFAULT_PREFIX; one that should read as a folder ends with /. A write is conditional on the generation the writer read, and a revalidation reads the object only when its generation changed.

  • the rights of the application and the administration on the bucket are in Store rights;
  • Cloud Storage has no change notification the store listens to, so a process sees a change once its record is older than record_ttl, 5 minutes by default;
  • turn on object versioning on the bucket: every record written is then kept as a noncurrent version, to restore from;
  • a delete removes every generation of the object, the noncurrent ones included, so versioning does not keep a shredded record. Soft delete keeps what a delete removes, see What a delete removes.
use gcloud_sdk::google::storage::v2::storage_client::StorageClient;
use gcloud_sdk::GoogleApi;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::{GcsBucketName, GcsKeyringStore};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let client =
    GoogleApi::from_function(StorageClient::new, "https://storage.googleapis.com", None).await?;
let store = GcsKeyringStore::new(client, GcsBucketName::try_from("my-keyrings".to_string())?)?;
let _ = store;
Ok(())
}

The client is gcloud-sdk’s, built with the credentials, endpoint and TLS stack the application chooses. The gcs feature has no TLS root store, so it needs one of gcloud-sdk’s root store features from the application, or one of:

  • gcs-tls-roots: the system’s native roots;
  • gcs-tls-webpki: the bundled WebPKI roots.

S3

Each record is the object kms-keyrings/<keyring id>.json in a bucket of Amazon S3 or of an S3-compatible server. with_prefix sets another ObjectPrefix than S3KeyringStore::DEFAULT_PREFIX; one that should read as a folder ends with /. A create is conditional on no object existing (If-None-Match: *), an update on the ETag the writer read (If-Match), and a revalidation reads the object only when its ETag changed (If-None-Match on the read).

  • the rights of the application and the administration on the objects are in Store rights;
  • S3 has no change notification the store listens to, so a process sees a change once its record is older than record_ttl, 5 minutes by default;
  • turn on versioning on the bucket: every record written is then kept as a noncurrent version, to restore from;
  • a delete removes every version and delete marker of the object, so versioning does not keep a shredded record, see What a delete removes.
use aws_config::BehaviorVersion;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::{S3BucketName, S3KeyringStore};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let config = aws_config::load_defaults(BehaviorVersion::latest()).await;
let store = S3KeyringStore::new(
    aws_sdk_s3::Client::new(&config),
    S3BucketName::try_from("my-keyrings".to_string())?,
);
let _ = store;
Ok(())
}

The client is aws-sdk-s3’s, built with the credentials, region, endpoint and HTTP client the application chooses. The s3 feature enables the SDK’s default HTTPS client, rustls on aws-lc-rs, as kms-aead’s AWS KMS provider does.

S3-compatible servers

A server is S3-compatible for this store when it passes the store conformance suite, kms-aead-keyring/tests/store_conformance.rs. Its checks include the ones a compare-and-swap needs from the server: it refuses a create over an existing object, an update at a stale ETag, and an update of an object that does not exist. A server that writes the object in the last case, as some MinIO releases do, recreates a deleted record on the next administrative write. To run the suite against a server, with the credentials of the environment:

KEYRING_S3_ENDPOINT=http://minio:9000 KEYRING_S3_BUCKET=my-keyrings \
  cargo test -p kms-aead-keyring --features s3 --test store_conformance s3:: -- --ignored

Set the server’s endpoint on the client, and force_path_style(true) when it serves buckets by path, as MinIO does by default. aws-sdk-s3 adds a CRC checksum to every upload by default, which some S3-compatible servers do not accept, and every write of the record then fails there. request_checksum_calculation(RequestChecksumCalculation::WhenRequired) sends a checksum only where the operation requires one, which the store’s PutObject does not:

use aws_config::BehaviorVersion;
use aws_sdk_s3::config::RequestChecksumCalculation;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::{S3BucketName, S3KeyringStore};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let config = aws_config::defaults(BehaviorVersion::latest())
    .endpoint_url("http://minio:9000")
    .load()
    .await;
let client = aws_sdk_s3::Client::from_conf(
    aws_sdk_s3::config::Builder::from(&config)
        .force_path_style(true)
        .request_checksum_calculation(RequestChecksumCalculation::WhenRequired)
        .build(),
);
let store = S3KeyringStore::new(client, S3BucketName::try_from("my-keyrings".to_string())?);
let _ = store;
Ok(())
}

What a delete removes

KeyringStore::delete is the call KeyringAdmin::dangerously_shred() makes once every version of the record is destroyed, see Shredding a keyring. It removes the record with the copies the store itself keeps. A copy the store keeps outside the reach of its API is the operator’s to delete:

StoreA delete removesThe operator’s
Memorythe entrynothing
Firestorethe document, with its update time as the preconditionthe document served to reads at an earlier time for one hour, and for seven days with point-in-time recovery on; backups and exports
PostgreSQLthe row at its revision, and sends the NOTIFY as a write doesthe row’s earlier versions until a vacuum reclaims them; backups, WAL archives and replicas
Cloud Storageevery generation of the object, noncurrent ones included: the noncurrent generations first, then the live object, conditional on the generation read, then that generationthe bucket’s soft delete keeps every deleted generation restorable for its retention, 7 days by default
S3every version and delete marker of the object: the noncurrent ones first, then the live object with If-Match on its ETag, then the live version, then the delete marker that leftreplication and backups

Point-in-time recovery is Firestore’s retention of every version of a document for up to seven days. Soft delete is Cloud Storage’s retention of a deleted object, which can be restored until the retention ends. A bucket of keyring records turns soft delete off, or counts the retention into the time a shred takes. The WAL is PostgreSQL’s write-ahead log of committed changes.

A lifecycle rule of the bucket that deletes live objects, such as a Cloud Storage Delete rule on age or an S3 expiration of current versions, shreds the keyrings it removes: every running process that holds one takes the deletion at its next revalidation. A rule on noncurrent versions only leaves the live records alone. The condition of the S3 delete holds only on a server that honours If-Match on DeleteObject, as S3 does for general purpose buckets. Community MinIO releases ignore it; PGSTY’s Silo fork honours it from RELEASE.2026-09-16, within one server pool.

A delete needs rights a write does not: the administration’s permissions per store are in Store rights.

Your own store

KeyringStore is a trait with five methods, load, revalidate, compare_and_swap, delete and watch, which returns None for a store without push, a Revision type and a KIND, such as StoreKind::Other("spanner"), for spans and errors. When implementing it:

  • write each method as an async fn whose future is Send;
  • implement delete(id, expected), which has no default:
    • it deletes the record when its stored revision is expected, with every earlier version of it the store keeps, and returns Deletion::Deleted once the store holds no record of the id, also when it held none, and Deletion::Conflict when the stored revision is another, leaving the record;
    • it returns errors as load does, as KeyringError::Store with StoreOperation::Delete. A delete that fails may have removed part of the earlier versions already, and the caller repeats it with the same expected; one that conflicts may have removed them too. The keyring deletes a record only in a shred, once every version of it is destroyed, so those versions hold only keys the shred destroys. A store that cannot delete them in one step with the live record removes them first, since a record gone from the store reads as shredded and no shred deletes it again;
    • it never gives an id the same revision twice, across a delete too: a revision that restarts at 1 for a record created again lets a process that missed the deletion keep the deleted record;
    • a store with push signals the deletion as a change;
  • read a stored document with KeyringRecord::from_json and write one with KeyringRecord::to_json, so the document is the one every store keeps and every read checks it;
  • return the client’s errors as KeyringError::Store, with the client’s error as the root cause. Never format the record or the request into an error and never log either, since both carry encrypted DEKs.

MemoryKeyringStore is the reference implementation, see its source, and the conformance suite holds the checks every store passes.

Record cache

Each Keyring keeps its record in the process, and the decrypted DEKs of its versions in kms-aead’s DEK cache. encrypt, decrypt and beacon read both, so the hot path calls neither the store nor the KMS.

Options

KeyringRegistryOptions configures the record cache of every keyring a registry opens, and its keyring field holds the KeyringOptions of the handles it returns. Each default is a public constant of the type that holds the option:

OptionDefaultWhat it does
record_ttlDEFAULT_RECORD_TTL, 5 minuteshow long the cached record serves without a store call
reload_min_intervalDEFAULT_RELOAD_MIN_INTERVAL, 5 secondsthe least time between two store reads that an unknown key id, a re-encryption under a pending version or a change signal causes
watchDEFAULT_WATCH, truewhether to follow the store’s change signals, where it has them
dek_cacheNone, a new cachethe cache of decrypted DEKs; a new one has kms-aead’s DekCacheOptions::DEFAULT_TTL and DekCacheOptions::DEFAULT_MAX_ENTRIES
keyring.cas_retriesKeyringOptions::DEFAULT_CAS_RETRIES, 5the conflicts a write of a KeyringAdmin retries before RecordConflict
keyring.compromised_readsKeyringOptions::DEFAULT_COMPROMISED_READS, Blockwhat decrypt does with a value under a compromised version, see Lifecycle
 
use std::time::Duration;

use kms_aead::cache::{DekCache, DekCacheOptions};
use kms_aead_keyring::{KeyringOptions, KeyringRegistryOptions};

fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let options: KeyringRegistryOptions = KeyringRegistryOptions::new()
    .with_record_ttl(Duration::from_secs(60))
    .with_dek_cache(DekCache::new(
        DekCacheOptions::new().with_ttl(Duration::from_secs(5 * 60)),
    )?);
assert_eq!(options.keyring.cas_retries, KeyringOptions::DEFAULT_CAS_RETRIES);
Ok(())
}

A DekCache may be shared with other keyrings and envelopes.

How the record is refreshed

  • Age: the first call that finds the record older than record_ttl revalidates it with a conditional read, while the other calls keep using it;
  • Change signals: on a store with push, a signal causes a revalidation, at most once per reload_min_interval. Signals inside the interval collapse into the next one;
  • Unknown key ids: a value with a key id the record does not have causes one reload, which concurrent misses share, at most once per reload_min_interval. Values with random key ids then cost at most one store read per interval. A re-encryption of a value under a version the record holds as pending reloads the same way, and shares the same interval: another process encrypted it, so it saw the version active;
  • Own writes: a KeyringAdmin call takes the record it wrote. Every Keyring of the same keyring in the registry shares that cached record, so the process that promotes a version encrypts under it at once.

Keyring::reload reads the record now, past the TTL and the rate limit, for operators and tests.

How late a change is seen

A change written by another process, such as a staged version, a promotion or a compromised flag, reaches a running process:

  • with push: within reload_min_interval of the signal, about 5 seconds by default;
  • without push, on Cloud Storage and S3, with watch off, or while the listener is disconnected: once the cached record is older than record_ttl, at most 5 minutes by default;
  • not until the store answers again, while its revalidations fail: it keeps its cached record, see below.

So record_ttl is the bound to set for how long a compromised version may keep being read somewhere in the fleet, and the least wait between staging a version and promoting it, see Staged rotation. Writers on the old record keep encrypting under the previous version until their record refreshes, and those values decrypt, since the previous version is DecryptOnly.

When the store fails or misbehaves

  • A failed revalidation keeps the cached record in use, logs a WARN, “The keyring record could not be revalidated; the cached record stays in use”, and is retried after reload_min_interval. A process that logs it is one that misses every change until the store answers again, so alert on it;
  • a record gone from the store is taken as a shredded keyring, see A record gone from the store;
  • a refreshed record is taken only if it moves every version forward from the cached one: no version missing, no changed algorithm or creation time, no compromised flag cleared, no state moved back against the lifecycle, such as a version back to Pending, no re-encryption epoch below the cached one, which would give records encrypted under different versions one marker, and no re-encryption range moved back or started or ended at or below the cached epoch, which would leave the records this process encrypted under a version outside its range. Otherwise the process keeps its record and logs an ERROR naming the first rule broken. reload and a write of a KeyringAdmin return RecordRegressed in that case.

The last rule is the detection of a rewritten or restored record in a running process. A process that starts after such a write has no record to compare with and takes it.

A record gone from the store

A revalidation (by age or change signal), a forced reload, the read before an administrative write and a shred can each find that the store holds no record of the keyring. The process takes it as a shredded keyring:

  • it destroys every version of its cached record in memory, and writes nothing. That evicts the keyring’s keys from the DEK cache, and no other keys;
  • it logs a WARN, “The keyring store no longer holds the keyring record; every version is destroyed in this process”, or an INFO when the record it held had every version destroyed already, as a shred leaves it, and sets the changed field of the Keyring load span to deleted. The process that ran the shred logs nothing and sets no field;
  • it stops revalidating and watching the keyring;
  • the keyring’s handles fail before any KMS call: encrypting with NoActiveVersion, reading with KeyVersionDestroyed, and reload with RecordMissing.

The registry’s next open of the id reads the store again. It fails with RecordMissing, or takes a record created since. Handles opened before stay on the destroyed record, and a handle on the destroyed record reads the store no more, also for an administrative call: shred repeated on it returns Ok, and every other call RecordMissing.

How late the process sees the deletion is the bound of how late a change is seen: within reload_min_interval of the signal with push, and once the cached record is older than record_ttl at the next call without. A process that makes no call keeps the plaintext DEK until the DEK cache drops it, see DEK eviction.

The deleted record stays the baseline of the id in the registry, for the checks of a held record: the next record under the id must be signed if the deleted one was, and must not revive a version the deleted record holds destroyed. A keyring created again has other versions and opens; a copy of the record from before the deletion fails with RecordRegressed, so a record restored after an accidental deletion is taken by a new process, not by the one that took the deletion. See Signing the keyring record. The deletion is what KeyringAdmin::dangerously_shred() ends with, see Shredding a keyring, and a deletion by anyone else has the same effect on the process.

DEK eviction

Taking a refreshed record evicts from the DEK cache every key it takes out of use: versions retired, destroyed or newly compromised, and the old bytes of a re-encrypted one. mark_compromised evicts the key from its own process before it returns. A record gone from the store evicts every key of the keyring, and no key of another keyring.

A stream decryptor holds the key derived for its value, never the DEK, until it is dropped. Taking a record that destroys its version, or flags it compromised, does not stop it, see Streaming encryption.

The DEK cache drops expired keys only when it is called. A process that can sit idle while it holds keys calls keyring.dek_cache().purge_expired() on a timer of its own, so the cache’s TTL bounds how long a key stays in memory, see Key lifetime.

Encrypted fields and AAD

Encrypted values

A keyring value is kms-aead’s Encrypted<T, Randomized>, re-exported by the keyring: the bytes to store, which decrypt to a T. T is any type that implements Plaintext, usually an application newtype such as Email, so a field names what it holds. Its layout is in Formats of Encrypted. The keyring encrypts and decrypts it with kms-aead’s AeadCipher::encrypt and decrypt under the key of the version, the calls an application with one fixed key makes itself.

  • value() and into_value() return the bytes;
  • key_id() returns the key id of the version that encrypted it;
  • Encrypted::try_from(bytes), serde and tokio-postgres check stored bytes against the layout when the value is made, and fail on bytes that are no keyring value before any decryption;
  • decrypt checks the key id first, and fails with ReservedKeyId on a reserved id and with KeyPurposeMismatch on a deterministic one, before any store or KMS call;
  • Debug shows the length only. A randomised value has no ==: two values of one plaintext differ.

The plaintext types and their encodings are in Stored values.

In application records

Encrypted is stored as bytes, with serde and the postgres and sqlx features, see Storing. A field of a serde record:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Keyring, KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId,
    KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
);

#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct User {
    id: String,
    email: Encrypted<Email, Randomized>,
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
use kms_aead_keyring::KeyringAdmin;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(provider, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"), KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
let keyring: &Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = admin.keyring();
let id = EntityId::try_from("42")?;
let alice = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
let user = User {
    id: "42".to_string(),
    email: keyring.encryptor().await.encrypt(&EMAIL.aad(&id), &alice).await?,
};
let stored: Vec<u8> = serde_json::to_vec(&user)?;

let read: User = serde_json::from_slice(&stored)?;
let email: Email = keyring.decrypt(&EMAIL.aad(&id), &read.email).await?;
assert_eq!(email, alice);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

PostgreSQL

A BYTEA column, with tokio-postgres:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::LocalKmsProvider;
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::PostgresKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{Encrypted, EntityId, FieldSpec, Keyring};
use tokio_postgres::{Client, NoTls};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::Plaintext;
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);
impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> { self.0.to_plaintext() }
    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

type TenantKeyring = Keyring<PostgresKeyringStore<NoTls>, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>;

async fn store_email(
    client: &Client,
    keyring: &TenantKeyring,
    field: &FieldSpec,
    id: &str,
    email: &Email,
) -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let value: Encrypted<Email, Randomized> = keyring
        .encryptor()
        .await
        .encrypt(&field.aad(&EntityId::try_from(id)?), email)
        .await?;
    client
        .execute("UPDATE users SET email = $1 WHERE id = $2", &[&value, &id])
        .await?;
    Ok(())
}

async fn email_of(
    client: &Client,
    keyring: &TenantKeyring,
    field: &FieldSpec,
    id: &str,
) -> Result<Email, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    let row = client
        .query_one("SELECT email FROM users WHERE id = $1", &[&id])
        .await?;
    let value: Encrypted<Email, Randomized> = row.try_get("email")?;
    Ok(keyring.decrypt(&field.aad(&EntityId::try_from(id)?), &value).await?)
}
fn main() {}

A column holding a value that is no keyring value fails in the decode, before any decryption. The keyring’s sqlx feature binds Encrypted and EncryptionMarker in sqlx the same way, as BYTEA on PostgreSQL, and turns on kms-aead’s sqlx feature, so the application needs no kms-aead dependency line for it, see Stored values.

AAD

The default AAD of a value is its place in the application’s model, <kind>/<id>/<field>, such as user/42/email:

  • EntityKind, such as user;
  • EntityId, the row’s id, which binds the value to its row;
  • FieldName, such as email.

Each part is 1 to 255 bytes of UTF-8 without /, NUL or another ASCII control character. An id that holds / is encoded by the application first, with percent-encoding for example.

A FieldSpec names the kind and the field once, as a constant, and FieldSpec::aad(&id) builds the AAD of one row. from_static checks a kind or field name at compile time when it initialises a constant.

So a value decrypts only in its own row and field. A value copied into another row fails with KeyringError::KmsAead with code DECRYPT. And since the AAD names the logical place, not the physical one, a value moves between tables and data stores without being encrypted again.

An EntityId can be personal data, so no span, event or error of the crate carries it.

Deterministic fields have an AAD without the entity id, see Deterministic fields, and beacons a context without it, see Beacons.

Why the AAD is structured

kms-aead’s AeadCipher and KmsAeadEnvelope take the AAD as any impl AsRef<[u8]>: the application chooses the bytes and rebuilds them itself on every read. The keyring’s encrypt and decrypt take a FieldAad instead, which only a FieldSpec builds:

  • field binding: the default AAD names the kind and the field, so a value copied into another field of the same row fails to decrypt;
  • row binding: it names the entity id, so a value copied into another row fails to decrypt. The application’s own id, such as a user id, is the EntityId;
  • deterministic fields: a deterministic AAD must not hold the row id, or equal plaintexts in different rows give different values and equality lookups never match. DeterministicAad is a separate type, with no entity id and no constructor from arbitrary bytes, see Deterministic fields;
  • BigQuery: BigQuery decrypts in SQL, so the AAD has to be one SQL can rebuild from the row. <kind>/<id>/<field> is this crate’s convention, which CONCAT('user/', u.id, '/email') rebuilds; BigQuery itself prescribes no format;
  • Tink: Tink takes any AAD bytes. The requirements of a Tink field are on the algorithm and the KEK, not on the AAD;
  • the interop requirement: a FieldAad carries the Interop of its FieldSpec, so encrypting checks the field’s requirements whichever way the AAD was built. Free bytes would name no field and skip that check.
AADBuilt byField bindingRow bindingBigQuery reads itInterop checked
Free byteskms-aead’s AeadCipher, KmsAeadEnvelopeif the bytes name the fieldif the bytes name the rowif SQL rebuilds the bytesno
Defaultspec.aad(&id)yesyesyesyes
With a contextspec.aad(&id).with_context(bytes)yesyesyes, with the same bytes appended in SQLyes
Customspec.custom_aad(bytes)if the bytes name the fieldif the bytes name the rowno, refusedyes
Deterministicspec.deterministic_aad()yesno, so that equal values matchyesyes

The application’s own context

There are three ways to put the application’s own data, such as a user id or a tenant, into the AAD:

  • spec.aad(&id) with the application’s id as the EntityId. The id follows the EntityId rules: 1 to 255 bytes of UTF-8 without /, NUL or another ASCII control character. It is treated as personal data and never logged;
  • FieldAad::with_context(bytes) appends / and any bytes, such as a tenant or a schema version. A value encrypted with a context decrypts only with the same one. The context may itself hold /, so keep the contexts of one field apart yourself. A BigQuery reader appends the same bytes in SQL, CONCAT('user/', u.id, '/email/tenant-7');
  • FieldSpec::custom_aad(bytes) takes any bytes, with no structure, in place of the default AAD. It gives up field binding and row binding, unless the bytes name the field and the row themselves, and BigQuery readability: on a field declared with Interop::BigQuery it fails with KeyringError::CustomAadUnsupported, since SQL cannot rebuild such bytes. On a Tink field or a field without interop it keeps the field’s algorithm and KEK checks.
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldAad, FieldName, FieldSpec, Interop, Keyring,
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringError, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
};

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
);

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
use kms_aead_keyring::KeyringAdmin;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(provider, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"), KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
let keyring: &Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = admin.keyring();
let user_id: u64 = 4711;
let id: EntityId = EntityId::try_from(user_id.to_string())?;
let email: String = "alice@example.com".to_string();

// user/4711/email
let default_aad: FieldAad = EMAIL.aad(&id);
// user/4711/email/tenant-7
let with_context: FieldAad = EMAIL.aad(&id).with_context(b"tenant-7");
// signup/4711, bound to neither the field nor the kind
let custom: FieldAad = EMAIL.custom_aad(format!("signup/{user_id}").as_bytes())?;

for aad in [default_aad, with_context, custom] {
    let value: Encrypted<String, Randomized> = keyring.encryptor().await.encrypt(&aad, &email).await?;
    assert_eq!(keyring.decrypt(&aad, &value).await?, email);
}

let bigquery_email: FieldSpec = EMAIL.with_interop(Interop::BigQuery);
assert!(matches!(
    bigquery_email.custom_aad(b"signup/4711"),
    Err(KeyringError::CustomAadUnsupported(Interop::BigQuery))
));
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

Deterministic fields

Feature deterministic, to create deterministic key versions. The deterministic types exist in every build, so values pass through a process without the feature.

Deterministic fields fit exact-match lookups where the leak of equality is acceptable. When a field only needs lookups and its stored value should stay randomised, a beacon fits instead.

What they leak

A deterministic column leaks equality and frequency to anyone who reads it, and a leaked deterministic DEK lets its holder test candidate values offline, see What it leaks in kms-aead.

The AAD of a deterministic field names its kind and field and no entity, see AAD, so a deterministic value also decrypts in any row of the same kind and field, and a deterministic field never decides identity or authorisation on its own.

Usage

A keyring with deterministic versions names their algorithm in its KeyringAlgorithms: AES-SIV, the one DeterministicAlgorithm has:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{DeterministicAlgorithm, KmsAeadResult};
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Deterministic;
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    DeterministicAad, Encrypted, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Keyring, KeyringAdmin,
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
);

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(
        provider,
        KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"),
        KeyringAlgorithms::new().with_deterministic(DeterministicAlgorithm::Aes256Siv),
    )
    .await?;
let keyring: &Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = admin.keyring();

let aad: DeterministicAad = EMAIL.deterministic_aad();
let alice = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
let first: Encrypted<Email, Deterministic> =
    keyring.encryptor().await.encrypt_deterministic(&aad, &alice).await?;
let second: Encrypted<Email, Deterministic> =
    keyring.encryptor().await.encrypt_deterministic(&aad, &alice).await?;

// Equal plaintexts give equal values under one version
assert_eq!(first, second);
assert_eq!(keyring.decrypt_deterministic(&aad, &first).await?, alice);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

A deterministic value is an Encrypted<T, Deterministic>, a type of its own, so it is never handed to decrypt by mistake. Unlike a randomised value it has == and Hash, which compare the bytes. AES-SIV runs on the RustCrypto aes-siv crate whatever the keyring’s AEAD backend.

decrypt_deterministic applies the compromised read policy, and decrypt_deterministic_with_trust reports the version’s trust, as their randomised counterparts do.

AAD

The AAD of a deterministic value is <kind>/<field>, such as user/email, built by FieldSpec::deterministic_aad(). It holds no entity id, otherwise equal plaintexts in different rows would give different values and an equality query would find nothing.

DeterministicAad::with_context(bytes) appends / and a context, such as a tenant id, to keep the values of two scopes apart. A context that differs per row defeats equality across rows, so it is a constant for the field. There is no constructor from arbitrary bytes: a deterministic value exists to be queried, and the query rebuilds its AAD.

Queries across rotations

A value encrypted under one deterministic version and the same plaintext under the next one differ. So an equality query matches against every version that still decrypts, with deterministic_probes:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{DeterministicAlgorithm, KmsAeadResult};
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Deterministic;
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    DeterministicAad, Encrypted, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, KeyId, Keyring, KeyringAdmin,
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, Plaintext, Purpose,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);
impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> { self.0.to_plaintext() }
    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}
const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(EntityKind::from_static("user"), FieldName::from_static("email"));
#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(
        provider,
        KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"),
        KeyringAlgorithms::new().with_deterministic(DeterministicAlgorithm::Aes256Siv),
    )
    .await?;
let keyring: &Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = admin.keyring();
let aad: DeterministicAad = EMAIL.deterministic_aad();
let alice = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
let before: Encrypted<Email, Deterministic> =
    keyring.encryptor().await.encrypt_deterministic(&aad, &alice).await?;

let staged: KeyId = admin.rotate(Purpose::Deterministic).await?;
admin.promote_now(staged).await?;

// One probe per version that still decrypts, newest first
let probes: Vec<Encrypted<Email, Deterministic>> = keyring.deterministic_probes(&aad, &alice).await?;
assert_eq!(probes.len(), 2);
assert!(probes.contains(&before));
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

deterministic_probes returns one probe per pending, active or decrypt-only deterministic version, compromised ones included, so a query finds the rows encrypted under any of them, and a sweep the rows encrypted under a compromised one. A staged version is probed before its promotion, so a process that has not seen the promotion yet finds the values of the processes that have. Retired and destroyed versions give no probe. The probes go into the query:

StoreQuery
PostgreSQLWHERE email_det = ANY($1), with the probes bound as a BYTEA[]
Firestorean in filter on the field, which takes up to 30 values
BigQueryIN over one DETERMINISTIC_ENCRYPT per version, each with that version’s Single keyset, see Queries

After a promotion the list has two probes until a sweep re-encrypts every row and the old version is retired. That is why deterministic versions are rotated rarely, and every rotation is followed by a full sweep, see Rotation cadence.

Be aware a query with several probes puts the values of one plaintext under each version side by side, so a reader of the query log links a value’s history across a rotation.

Beacons

A beacon, or blind index, finds rows by a field’s value without decrypting the column: a truncated HMAC-SHA256 of the value, stored beside the field’s randomised value, which a data store matches by equality. The keyring computes beacons under its beacon versions, of the purpose Purpose::Beacon, which rotate like any other.

kms-aead’s Beacons describes the primitive, Beacon<T> and BeaconBits, with keys the application keeps itself, and Formats its bytes.

A deterministic field also finds rows by equality, by its encrypted value, see Find rows by an encrypted value.

What a beacon leaks

A beacon column leaks likely equality and frequency to anyone who reads it, and a leaked beacon key lets its holder test candidate values offline, see What a beacon leaks in kms-aead.

A beacon is computed in a field’s context, <kind>/<field> such as user/email, with no entity id: equal values in different rows give equal beacons, which is what a lookup matches. A beacon of one field never matches a beacon of another.

Usage

A keyring with beacon versions names their algorithm in its KeyringAlgorithms: HMAC-SHA256, the one BeaconAlgorithm has. An encryptor’s beacon computes the beacon of a value under the active beacon version, in the same write as the field’s value, and Keyring::beacon_probes the beacons of a value under every beacon version a lookup matches:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Beacon, BeaconAlgorithm, BeaconBits, KmsAeadResult};
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Keyring, KeyringAdmin, KeyringAlgorithms,
    KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

impl Email {
    // Beacons of one address match regardless of case
    fn new(email: &str) -> Self {
        Email(email.to_lowercase())
    }
}

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
)
.with_beacon_bits(BeaconBits::DEFAULT);

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(
        provider,
        KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"),
        KeyringAlgorithms::new().with_beacon(BeaconAlgorithm::HmacSha256),
    )
    .await?;
let keyring: &Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = admin.keyring();

// On every write: the field and its beacon, through one encryptor
let aad = EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from("42")?);
let email = Email::new("Alice@Example.com");
let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await;
let encrypted: Encrypted<Email, Randomized> = encryptor.encrypt(&aad, &email).await?;
let beacon: Beacon<Email> = encryptor.beacon(&EMAIL, &email).await?;

// A lookup: the probes of the value looked for, then a decryption of each matching row
let probes: Vec<Beacon<Email>> = keyring
    .beacon_probes(&EMAIL, &Email::new("alice@example.com"))
    .await?;
assert!(probes.contains(&beacon));
assert_eq!(keyring.decrypt(&aad, &encrypted).await?, email);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

The probes go into the query as the probes of a deterministic field do:

StoreQuery
PostgreSQLWHERE email_beacon = ANY($1), with the probes bound as a BYTEA[]
Firestorean in filter on the field, which takes up to 30 values

Beacon is stored as Encrypted is, see Stored values.

Bits

FieldSpec::with_beacon_bits sets how many bits of the HMAC the field’s beacons keep, BeaconBits::DEFAULT, 16, unless set; what the bits trade is in Bits. The bits enter the HMAC, so after a change of a field’s bits, its stored beacons match no probe until a re-index computes them again. A beacon is the HMAC of the bytes the Plaintext type encodes, so the field’s newtype normalises the value, as Email::new lowercases above, see Normalisation.

BigQuery

The keyring never exports beacon keys to BigQuery: export_bigquery_keyset of the beacon purpose fails with ExportAlgorithmUnsupported, see Exporting a keyset to BigQuery. A BigQuery query matches beacons the application computed, see BigQuery in kms-aead.

Rotating beacon versions

A beacon version rotates as the other purposes do, see Staged rotation, and a column holds beacons of several versions while it is re-indexed:

  1. rotate(Purpose::Beacon) stages the version. beacon_probes returns a probe under it from the next refresh of each process, so lookups find the beacons computed under it before every process has seen the promotion;
  2. after the wait, promote it. Writes compute beacons under it from each process’s next refresh;
  3. lookups use beacon_probes throughout: one probe per pending, active or decrypt-only beacon version, compromised ones included, newest first;
  4. the promotion moved the keyring’s re-encryption epoch on, so every row written before it has a EncryptionMarker below the current one, and a re-index sweep visits them, see Sweeps. It computes each row’s beacon again under the active version of its encryptor and writes the encryptor’s marker; a row without a beacon gets the marker alone;
  5. once the sweep reports no failure and that query returns nothing, retire the old version, and its probe drops out of every lookup.

beacon.key_id() reads the key id of a stored beacon, so that the sweep recomputes only the beacons under an older version. Here a re-index over an in-memory table, run by sweep::reencrypt_records as any sweep is, see Running a sweep. A beacon under the active version keeps its bytes and only the value is re-encrypted; any other gives both again from the plaintext:

use std::collections::BTreeMap;
use std::num::NonZeroUsize;
use std::sync::Mutex;

use futures_util::stream;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::LocalKmsProvider;
use kms_aead::{Beacon, BeaconBits};
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::sweep::{self, ReencryptOutcome, EncryptionMarker, SweepReport};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Decrypted, Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, KeyTrust, Keyring,
    KeyringResult, Purpose,
};
use kms_aead::providers::LocalKekId;
use kms_aead::{BeaconAlgorithm, KmsAeadResult};
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyId, KeyringAdmin, KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
    Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

type UsersKeyring = Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>;

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
)
.with_beacon_bits(BeaconBits::DEFAULT);

#[derive(Debug, Clone, PartialEq)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

#[derive(Clone)]
struct UserRow {
    email: Encrypted<Email, Randomized>,
    email_beacon: Beacon<Email>,
    encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker,
}

/// The users table, by user id.
type Table = Mutex<BTreeMap<String, UserRow>>;

/// Computes the beacon of the row of `user` again under the active beacon version, and re-encrypts
/// its value under the active randomised version, with the marker of the encryptor's record.
async fn reindex(keyring: &UsersKeyring, table: &Table, user: &str) -> KeyringResult<ReencryptOutcome> {
    let Some(row) = table.lock().unwrap().get(user).cloned() else {
        return Ok(ReencryptOutcome::Skipped);
    };
    let read: Vec<u8> = row.email.value().to_vec();
    let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await;
    let aad = EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from(user)?);
    let (email, email_beacon, outcome) = if encryptor.is_current(row.email_beacon.key_id()).await? {
        let reencrypted = encryptor.reencrypt(&aad, &row.email).await?;
        let outcome = ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted);
        let email = reencrypted.map_or(row.email, |reencrypted| reencrypted.value);
        (email, row.email_beacon, outcome)
    } else {
        let decrypted: Decrypted<Email> = keyring.decrypt_with_trust(&aad, &row.email).await?;
        let from_compromised = decrypted.trust != KeyTrust::Trusted;
        let email = encryptor.encrypt(&aad, &decrypted.value).await?;
        let email_beacon = encryptor.beacon(&EMAIL, &decrypted.value).await?;
        (email, email_beacon, ReencryptOutcome::Reencrypted { from_compromised })
    };
    let encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker = encryptor.record().encryption_marker();
    // Conditional on the row as it was read: a row the application rewrote meanwhile keeps its
    // own value, which it encrypted under the active versions anyway.
    let mut table = table.lock().unwrap();
    match table.get_mut(user) {
        Some(stored) if stored.email.value() == read.as_slice() => {
            *stored = UserRow { email, email_beacon, encryption_marker };
            Ok(outcome)
        }
        _ => Ok(ReencryptOutcome::Skipped),
    }
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(
        provider,
        KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"),
        KeyringAlgorithms::new().with_beacon(BeaconAlgorithm::HmacSha256),
    )
    .await?;
let keyring: &UsersKeyring = admin.keyring();
let table = Table::default();
for (user, email) in [("41", "ada@example.com"), ("42", "alice@example.com")] {
    let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await;
    let email = Email(email.to_string());
    let row = UserRow {
        email: encryptor.encrypt(&EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from(user)?), &email).await?,
        email_beacon: encryptor.beacon(&EMAIL, &email).await?,
        encryption_marker: encryptor.record().encryption_marker(),
    };
    table.lock().unwrap().insert(user.to_string(), row);
}
let staged: KeyId = admin.rotate(Purpose::Beacon).await?;
admin.promote_now(staged).await?;
// Every row whose marker is below the keyring's, after the promotion of a beacon version
let current: EncryptionMarker = keyring.record().encryption_marker();
let candidates = table
    .lock()
    .unwrap()
    .iter()
    .filter(|(_, row)| row.encryption_marker < current)
    .map(|(user, _)| user.clone())
    .collect::<Vec<String>>();

let report: SweepReport = sweep::reencrypt_records(
    keyring.id(),
    stream::iter(candidates.into_iter().map(Ok)),
    NonZeroUsize::new(8).unwrap(),
    |candidate| {
        let table = &table;
        async move { reindex(keyring, table, &candidate).await }
    },
)
.await;
println!("{report:?}");
assert_eq!(report.reencrypted, 2);
assert_eq!(table.lock().unwrap()["42"].email_beacon.key_id(), staged);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

A deterministic field is re-encrypted the same way, with reencrypt_deterministic, under the deterministic feature.

Beacon versions are rotated by policy and on compromise only: each one adds a probe to every lookup until the re-index is done.

BigQuery and Tink interop

Keyring values are in Tink’s wire format, so Tink and BigQuery’s AEAD functions read the values the keyring writes, and the keyring reads theirs, given the same keys under the same key ids.

Wire format

Every value starts with Tink’s 5-byte TINK prefix, a version byte 0x01 and the key id, followed by what the version’s algorithm writes, see Formats of Encrypted. These are the layouts of Tink’s AEAD and deterministic AEAD primitives, and of BigQuery’s AEAD.ENCRYPT and DETERMINISTIC_ENCRYPT. A randomised version with another algorithm, such as ChaCha20-Poly1305, writes the same prefix before its own body, which only kms-aead reads.

The prefix is not authenticated. A value relabelled with another key id selects another version, whose key the body does not decrypt under.

AAD in SQL

Both default AADs are UTF-8 text, so SQL rebuilds them from the row with no byte escaping:

-- Randomised: <kind>/<id>/<field>
AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING(keyset, u.email_enc, CONCAT('user/', u.id, '/email'))

-- Deterministic: <kind>/<field>
DETERMINISTIC_DECRYPT_STRING(keyset, u.email_det, 'user/email')

keyset is KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset) over an export of the keyring’s versions, see Exporting a keyset to BigQuery. SQL cannot rebuild an AAD of the application’s own bytes, so FieldSpec::custom_aad fails on a field declared with Interop::BigQuery, see Why the AAD is structured.

The plaintext encodings are the ones SQL reads: String and JsonEncoded<T> with AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING, the integers with a CAST of it, and Vec<u8> with AEAD.DECRYPT_BYTES. An application type reads as the type it encodes as, such as Email as a String.

Fields BigQuery reads

BigQuery decrypts through KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN, which implements AES-256-GCM and AES-SIV only and decrypts with Cloud KMS only. A field BigQuery also decrypts is declared with FieldSpec::with_interop(Interop::BigQuery), and the keyring then refuses to encrypt it unless:

  • a randomised value is encrypted under an AES-256-GCM version, the default of a new keyring;
  • a deterministic value is encrypted under an AES-SIV version;
  • the KEK is in Google Cloud KMS.

The check runs on an encryptor’s encrypt and encrypt_deterministic, before encrypting, and fails with KeyringError::Interop naming the first requirement broken. Values already written always decrypt: only encrypting checks. So a change_algorithm away from AES-256-GCM, or a keyring on another KMS, fails the next write of the field instead of silently writing values BigQuery cannot read.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::errors::{InteropError, InteropRequirement};
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Interop, Keyring, KeyringAdmin,
    KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringError, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
    KeyringResult, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
)
.with_interop(Interop::BigQuery);

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
// A local KEK, which BigQuery cannot decrypt with
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(provider, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"), KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
let keyring: &Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = admin.keyring();

let aad = EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from("42")?);
let alice = Email("alice@example.com".to_string());
let refused: KeyringResult<Encrypted<Email, Randomized>> =
    keyring.encryptor().await.encrypt(&aad, &alice).await;
assert!(matches!(
    refused,
    Err(KeyringError::Interop(InteropError {
        requirement: InteropRequirement::Kek(_),
        ..
    }))
));
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

Fields Tink reads

A field that Tink also decrypts, with the keyset of export_tink_keyset, is declared with FieldSpec::with_interop(Interop::Tink). The keyring then refuses to encrypt it unless:

  • a randomised value is encrypted under an AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCM or AES-256-GCM-SIV version;
  • a deterministic value is encrypted under an AES-SIV version;
  • the KEK has a Tink key URI: a Cloud KMS key, an AWS KMS key named by its ARN, or a Vault Transit key.

ChaCha20-Poly1305 is left out, since Tink for Python and C++ do not read it in a keyset. The check and its errors are the ones of Interop::BigQuery, with Interop::Tink in the error.

Exporting a keyset to BigQuery

Feature bigquery-export. It builds on kms-aead’s GCP provider with no TLS root store, so the application also enables kms-aead’s gcp-kms-encryption, or another root store feature of kms-aead’s GCP provider.

BigQuery reads keyring values through KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN, which takes a Tink keyset encrypted by a Cloud KMS key. KeyringAdmin::export_bigquery_keyset builds that keyset from the keyring’s versions, on a keyring whose provider is GcpKmsProvider, under the bigquery-export feature, which implies admin. The bytes it returns are the Cloud KMS ciphertext of Tink’s serialised Keyset message, encrypted with no associated data, without the EncryptedKeyset message Tink’s own keyset readers take, see Exporting a keyset to Tink:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsKeyRef, GcpKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    encodings, EncryptedKeyset, KeyringAdmin, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
    KeysetSelection, Purpose,
};

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, GcpKmsProvider, Ring> = registry
    .admin(GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref).await?, KeyringId::try_from("tenant-42".to_string())?)
    .await?;

let keyset: EncryptedKeyset<encodings::BigQuery> = admin
    .export_bigquery_keyset(KeysetSelection::Readable(Purpose::Randomized))
    .await?;
// The query parameters of `KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset)`: a `STRING` and a `BYTES`
let kms_key: &str = keyset.key_uri();
let keyset_bytes: &[u8] = keyset.value();
let _ = (kms_key, keyset_bytes);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

The selection:

  • KeysetSelection::Readable(purpose): every version of the purpose that decrypts and is not compromised, the pending one included, with the active version as the primary. BigQuery decrypts a value under the key its prefix names, so one keyset reads a column written across rotations. It fails with NoActiveVersion when the purpose has none;
  • KeysetSelection::Single(key_id): the one version as the primary, also when it is compromised. It is the keyset of DETERMINISTIC_ENCRYPT, which encrypts under the primary, for the probe of one version, and of a read through a compromised version. A retired, destroyed or unknown version fails as a read under it does, and a pending one with KeyVersionPending, since BigQuery would encrypt under it.

Beacon versions are never exported: a selection of the beacon purpose fails with ExportAlgorithmUnsupported. BigQuery’s SQL cannot compute a beacon either, see BigQuery in kms-aead.

The type parameter of an EncryptedKeyset is its encoding, from the encodings module, which names the reader of its bytes. The EncryptedKeyset<encodings::BigQuery> this export returns:

  • value() and into_value(): the encrypted keyset, as a BYTES column or query parameter holds it;
  • key_uri(): Tink’s key URI of the Cloud KMS key, gcp-kms://projects/…/cryptoKeys/…, the first argument of KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN, as a STRING query parameter;
  • key_ids(): the key ids of the versions inside, in ascending order.

The export selects its versions from the record in the store, read at the time of the call, not from the record the process has cached. A version another process flagged compromised is therefore left out of a Readable keyset, even before this process’s cached record refreshes. The read is checked as a revalidation is: a stored record that moves a version backwards fails the export with RecordRegressed, and a missing one with RecordMissing.

A keyset is a copy of the record at the time of the export. A Readable keyset exported while a version is pending already holds it, so the order of a rotation is: rotate, export the Readable keysets again, wait, promote. Values encrypted under the new version then read in BigQuery from the first one. After mark_compromised, whose replacement is active at once, the replacement’s values do not read, and the compromised version stays in a keyset exported before it, until the keyset is exported again.

A KEK rotation changes no key inside a keyset and leaves the keyset under the KEK version that encrypted it at the export, so KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN fails once that version is disabled. Export every keyset again after reencrypt_deks, once the new primary version has propagated, and replace the old bytes in every query and column that holds them before the old version is disabled, see Encrypted keysets.

Queries

Every value reaches the query as a parameter: @kms_key is the export’s key_uri(), each keyset parameter its value(), and @email the address an equality query looks for:

-- Randomised values, with a Readable(Randomized) keyset
SELECT AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING(
  KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset), u.email_enc, CONCAT('user/', u.id, '/email'))
FROM users u;

-- Deterministic values, with a Readable(Deterministic) or Single keyset
SELECT DETERMINISTIC_DECRYPT_STRING(KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset), u.email_det, 'user/email')
FROM users u;

-- An equality query: one probe per deterministic version, each with its Single keyset
SELECT u.id
FROM users u
WHERE u.email_det IN (
  DETERMINISTIC_ENCRYPT(KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset_d2), @email, 'user/email'),
  DETERMINISTIC_ENCRYPT(KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset_d1), @email, 'user/email')
);

The probes in BigQuery are those of the active and decrypt-only versions. A pending version has no Single keyset, and no value is encrypted under it before its promotion.

The requirements:

  • the keyring’s KEK is a Cloud KMS key, and the query runs in the KMS key’s location;
  • the exported versions are AES-256-GCM or AES-SIV. Any other algorithm fails with ExportAlgorithmUnsupported, before any KMS call;
  • the field is declared with Interop::BigQuery, so that the keyring never encrypts it under a version the keyset could not hold, see Fields BigQuery reads;
  • the principal that queries has decrypt on the KMS key, and the one that exports has encrypt and decrypt on it. The export is a KeyringAdmin call, see KMS rights.

How it behaves

The crate’s live test runs the export and the queries against Cloud KMS and BigQuery:

  • a value whose key id is not in the keyset fails to decrypt, and reads as NULL under SAFE.AEAD.DECRYPT_STRING;
  • the keyset is encrypted with no associated data, since KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN decrypts with none. What the KMS decrypts from it is a serialised Tink keyset, which is no serialised Tink key, so copied into a record as a version’s encrypted DEK it decrypts nothing and fails with INVALID_KEY_FORMAT;
  • the export decrypts each selected version through the provider and never through the DEK cache, so the keys stay in the process for the call only. Its span records dek_cache as bypassed.

Cloud KMS encrypts at most 64 KiB of plaintext, which a keyset reaches at several hundred versions.

Exporting a keyset to Tink

export_tink_keyset exports the same versions as Tink’s encrypted keyset, which Tink for Java, Go and Python decrypt with KeysetHandle.read and their own KMS client. It is a KeyringAdmin call, needs the admin feature only, and runs on a keyring with any provider whose KEK has a Tink key URI. The bytes are a google.crypto.tink.EncryptedKeyset around the KMS ciphertext of the serialised keyset, see Tink keysets and the TINK prefix.

key_uri() is the URI of Tink’s KMS client:

  • gcp-kms://projects/…/cryptoKeys/… for a Cloud KMS key;
  • aws-kms://arn:aws:kms:… for an AWS KMS key named by its ARN;
  • hcvault://<host>/…/keys/<name> for a Vault Transit key, which Tink for Go reads. Tink for Java and Python have no Vault Transit client.

An Azure Key Vault key, a local or an application’s own KEK, and an AWS key named without its ARN have no Tink client. The export fails on them with KeyringError::Interop, Interop::Tink and InteropRequirement::Kek, before any store or KMS call.

After a KEK rotation, a Tink keyset is exported again and replaces the old one wherever a Tink reader decrypts it, as a BigQuery keyset does, see Encrypted keysets.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{GcpKmsKeyRef, GcpKmsProvider};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    encodings, EncryptedKeyset, KeyringAdmin, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
    KeysetSelection, Purpose,
};

#[tokio::main]
