async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, GcpKmsProvider, Ring> = registry
    .admin(GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref).await?, KeyringId::try_from("tenant-42".to_string())?)
    .await?;

let keyset: EncryptedKeyset<encodings::Tink> = admin
    .export_tink_keyset(KeysetSelection::Readable(Purpose::Randomized))
    .await?;
std::fs::write("tenant-42.keyset", keyset.value())?;
println!("{}", keyset.key_uri());
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

Tink for Python reads the keyset with the KMS client of that URI, and decrypts a randomised value with the value’s default AAD as UTF-8:

import tink
from tink import aead
from tink.integration import gcpkms

aead.register()
key_uri = 'gcp-kms://projects/my-project/locations/europe-north1/keyRings/my-key-ring/cryptoKeys/my-key'
with open('tenant-42.keyset', 'rb') as keyset_file:
  keyset_handle = tink.read_keyset_handle(
      tink.BinaryKeysetReader(keyset_file.read()),
      gcpkms.GcpKmsClient(key_uri).get_aead(key_uri),
  )
email_aead = keyset_handle.primitive(aead.Aead)


def decrypt_email(value: bytes, user_id: str) -> str:
  return email_aead.decrypt(value, f'user/{user_id}/email'.encode()).decode()

The selection, the fresh read of the record and the rights are those of the BigQuery export. The algorithms are those of Fields Tink reads: a selected ChaCha20-Poly1305 or beacon version fails with ExportAlgorithmUnsupported, before any KMS call.

Full example available here: it exports a keyset for each Cloud KMS or AWS KMS key whose variables are set, and Tink for Python decrypts a value encrypted before the rotation with it, through tests/tink_interop.py read-keysets.

Operations

Running a keyring in production: how often to rotate, how sweeps move values to the active versions, the migration from envelope values, and what to do when a key leaks. The tasks, step by step with their checks and alerts, are in Rotate keys and re-encrypt values and A leaked key or a shredded tenant.

Rotation cadence

A rotation is staged: rotate, export the BigQuery keysets again, wait for every process to see the pending version, then promote. How often to rotate follows from the nonce and volume bounds of each purpose, and deterministic and beacon versions rotate rarely, since each adds a probe to every lookup until a sweep. See Rotation cadence.

Rotating the KEK

The KEK rotates in the KMS on its own schedule, and reencrypt_deks moves the record’s DEKs to its current version without changing any value, before the old KEK version is disabled. See Rotating the KEK.

Sweeps

A sweep re-encrypts stored values under the active versions, after a rotation, after a compromise and to migrate envelope values: a job the application runs on a schedule, which finds the records behind by their encryption marker. Sweeps has the mechanism, and the sweeps by store have the queries and writes of Firestore, PostgreSQL and BigQuery.

Migrating from envelope values

Values that KmsAeadEnvelope wrote move to keyring values in five steps, with the encryption marker telling the two layouts apart and a sweep re-encrypting the envelope values. See Move envelope values to a keyring.

Emergency runbook

What to do after a leak of a plaintext DEK, an encrypted DEK or the record, a KMS IAM over-grant, a version the administrative job did not create, a deleted or corrupted record or a KMS outage, and how to crypto-shred a scope with dangerously_shred() or a KEK per scope. See Emergency runbook.

Rotation

Rotation cadence

A rotation is staged: rotate(purpose) adds a pending version, which every process decrypts under once its record refreshes, and promote(key_id) makes it active once every process has had time to see it, see Staged rotation. The previous version keeps decrypting. Nothing is lost while the change propagates: processes on the old record encrypt under the previous version until their record refreshes, and those values decrypt.

The administrative job runs a rotation in steps, on its KeyringAdmin, see Applications and administration:

  1. rotate(purpose);
  2. export the Readable BigQuery and Tink keysets again, which then hold the pending version;
  3. wait the longest time any process may run without a successful refresh of its record, counted from staged_at(), and check that no process logged “The keyring record could not be revalidated” meanwhile;
  4. promote(key_id). Until record_ttl has passed since staged_at() it fails with PromotionTooEarly, whose remaining is the time left. promote_now(key_id) skips that guard, for a fleet known to have refreshed since the rotation or a compromise that cannot wait.

The rotate example runs the whole cycle, and a KEK re-encryption after it, on the memory store and the local KMS: cargo run -p kms-aead-keyring --example rotate --features ring,admin.

How often:

  • a randomised version is good for about 2^32 values, see Nonces, and an AES-GCM version for about 2^38 bytes, see Data volume. The keyring stops at neither, since its DEKs are shared across processes: estimate the values and bytes a fleet encrypts, or measure them, see Alerting on volume. At 550 values per second a version reaches 2^32 in 90 days, so rotate every 30 to 90 days, sooner at higher rates;
  • a deterministic version has no nonce, so it is rotated by policy and on compromise only. Every deterministic rotation adds a probe to each equality query until a full sweep re-encrypts every row, see Queries across rotations, so rotate deterministic versions rarely;
  • a beacon version likewise, since every beacon rotation adds a probe to each lookup until a re-index, see Rotating beacon versions.

After a promotion, export the Single BigQuery keysets of the new deterministic version, since a pending version has none.

Rotation does not touch values already written. A sweep re-encrypts them: a job that runs on a schedule until its SweepReport shows nothing scanned. Retiring and destroying the old version are separate steps of the administrative job, see Retiring the old version.

Alerting on volume

With the opentelemetry feature, the keyring records the plaintext size of each value it encrypts under each version, see Metrics: the histogram’s count is the values, and its sum their bytes. Each process records its own, and the alert sums them across the fleet. Here at half of 2^32 values and of 2^38 bytes per version, in PromQL over the Prometheus export, with the range covering the version’s life:

sum by (kms_aead_keyring, kms_aead_key_id) (
  increase(kms_aead_plaintext_size_bytes_count{
    kms_aead_mode="keyring", kms_aead_operation="encrypt", kms_aead_purpose="randomized"
  }[90d])
) > 0.5 * 2^32

sum by (kms_aead_keyring, kms_aead_key_id) (
  increase(kms_aead_plaintext_size_bytes_sum{
    kms_aead_mode="keyring", kms_aead_operation="encrypt",
    kms_aead_algorithm=~"aes-(128|256)-gcm"
  }[90d])
) > 0.5 * 2^38

A histogram starts at zero in every new process, and increase() counts across those restarts. The [90d] range needs 90 days of retention: Prometheus keeps 15 days by default, so keep the metric in long-term storage, through remote write or in a backend with a longer retention. The bytes alert keeps to AES-GCM versions, the only ones with a volume bound. Rotate the version the alert names, as above.

Rotating the KEK

The KEK rotates in the KMS on its own schedule. After a rotation, reencrypt_deks() decrypts every DEK in the record and encrypts it again under the KEK’s current version. No value changes. A keyring that is not re-encrypted keeps working as long as the old KEK versions stay enabled in the KMS.

reencrypt_deks is a KeyringAdmin call and runs in the administrative job, like every call that encrypts a DEK.

The record keeps the KEK version of each encrypted DEK, as the KMS reported it when it encrypted the DEK: KeyVersion::kek_version. reencrypt_deks returns one DekReencryption per version, with the KEK version its DEK was under and the one the KMS encrypted it with now, and kek_versions_in_use of the record lists every KEK version the record’s DEKs are under. Before the old KEK version is disabled, the record that Keyring::reload returns must list the new version alone, which needs no KMS call. Once the old version is disabled, reencrypt_deks runs again: it decrypts every DEK through the KMS and fails on one the KMS no longer decrypts. Here with the local KMS, rotated from version 1 to version 2, where dropping version 1 from the provider stands for disabling it:

use std::num::NonZeroU32;

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::SymmetricKeyFormat;
use kms_aead::providers::{
    KmsKeyVersion, LocalKek, LocalKekId, LocalKekVersion, LocalKmsProvider,
};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    DekReencryption, KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

const TENANT: KeyringId = KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42");

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let kek_id = LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?;
let [v1, v2] = [1, 2].map(|version| LocalKekVersion::from(NonZeroU32::new(version).unwrap()));
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let before_rotation = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    kek_id.clone(),
    vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v1, SecretValue::new(vec![0x01; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
)?;
registry
    .create(before_rotation, TENANT, KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;

// The administrative job after the rotation, with the new version encrypting
let rotated = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    kek_id.clone(),
    vec![
        LocalKek::from_secret(v1, SecretValue::new(vec![0x01; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
        LocalKek::from_secret(v2, SecretValue::new(vec![0x02; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?,
    ],
)?;
let admin = registry.admin(rotated, TENANT).await?;

// One entry per version: the KEK version its DEK was under, and the one it is under now
let moved: Vec<DekReencryption> = admin.reencrypt_deks().await?;
let new_kek_version = KmsKeyVersion::from(v2);
assert!(moved
    .iter()
    .all(|reencryption| reencryption.kek_version.as_ref() == Some(&new_kek_version)));

// The check before the old version is disabled: every DEK is under the new version
let record = admin.keyring().reload().await?;
assert_eq!(record.kek_versions_in_use(), [Some(&new_kek_version)]);

// The check once the old version is disabled: every DEK still decrypts
let disabled = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::new(
    kek_id,
    vec![LocalKek::from_secret(v2, SecretValue::new(vec![0x02; 32]), SymmetricKeyFormat::Raw)?],
)?;
registry.admin(disabled, TENANT).await?.reencrypt_deks().await?;
Ok(())
}

Under Cloud KMS, a reencrypt_deks that ran before the new primary version had propagated reports the old version as the one the DEK is under now, and the record keeps listing it: run reencrypt_deks again once the rotation has propagated. Under Vault Transit, min_decryption_version disables every version below it at once, which is why the check asks for the new version alone.

The whole procedure, with the application’s own DEKs and keysets, is in Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation.

A KEK version the KMS did not report

A None among the versions in use stands for the DEKs whose KMS reported no version: every DEK under AWS KMS, and under a KMS with versions, a version that is unknown, as with an application’s own provider that returns none. A Cloud KMS or Vault Transit response without a key version is logged at WARN, see Events. reencrypt_deks keeps such a DEK at None, so the check before the disable never passes. The procedure in Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation then goes from step 2 to step 4, and the check after the disable, step 6, is the only one.

Between the disable and that check, a DEK still under the old version fails in the application too, once its DEK cache entry has expired. Under AWS KMS only imported key material can be removed, see AWS KMS.

Sweeps

A sweep re-encrypts stored values under the active versions: after a rotation, to retire the old version, after a compromise, and to migrate envelope values. It is a job the application runs, a CLI or a Kubernetes Job, never a task inside the library: only the application knows its tables, its queries and its write rules. The job runs on a schedule, and again until its SweepReport shows nothing scanned. A run that stops part-way or fails on some records needs no checkpoint: the next run’s marker query returns exactly the records it did not write. Retiring the old version is a separate step of the administrative job, see Retiring the old version.

The encryption marker

A keyring record counts a re-encryption epoch, which moves on by one with every administrative write that makes a version active:

CallRe-encryption epoch
create, and open_or_create when it creates1
promotemoves on by one
mark_compromised of the active version, with its replacementmoves on by one
mark_compromised that writes the flag alone, or of any other versionunchanged
rotate, change_algorithmunchanged: the version they add is pending
retire, reinstate, destroy, reencrypt_deks, dangerously_destroy_all_versionsunchanged

A write moves the epoch once, however many versions it makes active. KeyringRecord::encryption_marker() returns it as an EncryptionMarker:

[0x01][re-encryption epoch: u32 BE]

Markers compare as their epochs do, and so do their bytes, in Firestore, PostgreSQL and BigQuery alike. EncryptionMarker::ENVELOPE is epoch 0, 01 00 00 00 00, the marker of a record of envelope values, below the marker of every keyring.

Each record of the application keeps the marker in one field beside its encrypted fields. A write that touches any encrypted field of a record takes one Encryptor from keyring.encryptor(), encrypts all of the record’s encrypted fields again through it, computes its beacons again, and stores encryptor.record().encryption_marker() in the same write, as write_user does in Running a sweep. The encryptor keeps the record it took from the cache, so the marker names the epoch whose active versions the fields are under, also when the cache refreshes during the write.

A marker read from keyring.record() names whichever record the cache held when it was read. Read before the encrypting, it can name an older epoch than the versions the fields are under, which puts the record outside the range of those versions. Read after, it can name a newer one, and the record then drops out of every sweep.

Why the row holds an encryption marker

Decryption never reads the marker. Every keyring value carries its key id in its prefix, 0x01 followed by the key id, so a read finds the version that encrypted a value from the value alone.

The marker lets the database find rows, where the library cannot: encrypted bytes cannot be indexed or compared, and the library does not scan the application’s tables. A row carries one small column that a range query can use, and the column answers two questions:

  • which rows are still to be re-encrypted after a rotation: those with encryption_marker < current;
  • which rows can still hold a value under a version, before it is destroyed: those with a marker in the ReencryptRange of the version. A query that returns nothing makes the destroy safe.

The marker is an epoch and not a key version because the fields of a row are under different purposes, randomised, deterministic and beacon, and the versions of each purpose rotate independently. A version marker would need a column per purpose. The epoch is one counter that the keyring moves on whenever any version becomes active. An Encryptor is a snapshot of all active versions at one epoch, and each version records the epochs it was active in, so both questions are one range query on one column.

An application that never sweeps, because it keeps old versions alive or re-encrypts a row when it next writes it, may leave the column out. Bulk re-encryption and the safe destruction of old versions need it.

Finding records behind

The records a sweep still has to visit are those whose marker is below the current one:

  • encryption_marker < $1 with the marker of keyring.record().encryption_marker(), on one indexed field, in every store;
  • row.encryption_marker < current in Rust, on a record read back before its re-encryption.

The query also matches a record with nothing to re-encrypt: after a rotation of a purpose the record holds no value of, such as a beacon rotation for a record without beacons, its marker is below the current one all the same. The re-encryption then writes the encryptor’s marker alone, without decrypting anything or calling the KMS, and the record matches once.

Each process stamps the epoch of the record it caches. A process that has not refreshed its record since a promotion encrypts under the previous version and stamps the previous epoch, so its writes stay in the query and the next sweep visits them. So the final check that the query returns nothing runs once the longest time any process may run without a successful refresh of its record has passed since the promotion.

The marker comes from the cached record, so a job that starts right after a promotion in another process calls reload() first.

Records under a version

KeyVersion::reencrypt_range() returns the markers of the records that can hold a value under the version, as a ReencryptRange:

  • start(), the epoch of the write that made the version active;
  • end(), the epoch of the first write after it stopped being active that moved the epoch on, or None while there was none: the active version, and a version flagged compromised while it was active, until a version is next made active.

A version that was never active, pending or destroyed while pending, has no range, and neither has an unknown key id: record.version(key_id) is None. The queries:

StoreQuery
Firestoretwo range filters on encryption_marker, as below
PostgreSQLWHERE encryption_marker >= $1 AND ($2::BYTEA IS NULL OR encryption_marker < $2) with &range.start() and &range.end()
BigQueryWHERE encryption_marker >= @start AND (@end IS NULL OR encryption_marker < @end), with @end a NULL of type BYTES for an open range
Rustrange.contains(row.encryption_marker)

Firestore takes both bounds on the one field and serves them from its ascending index on encryption_marker, and an open range is one filter:

use firestore::{FirestoreDb, FirestoreResult};
use futures_util::stream::{self, BoxStream, StreamExt};
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::GcpKmsProvider;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::FirestoreKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::sweep::{EncryptionMarker, ReencryptRange};
use kms_aead_keyring::{KeyId, KeyVersion, Keyring};
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};

#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct UserDoc {
    #[serde(rename = "_firestore_id", skip_serializing)]
    id: String,
    #[serde(default)]
    encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker,
}

/// The users that can hold a value under `key_id`, as a stream: a range can hold millions.
async fn users_under<'a>(
    db: &'a FirestoreDb,
    keyring: &Keyring<FirestoreKeyringStore, GcpKmsProvider, Ring>,
    key_id: KeyId,
) -> Result<BoxStream<'a, FirestoreResult<UserDoc>>, Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
    // The store's record: a compromise or promotion in another process is in it
    let range: Option<ReencryptRange> = keyring
        .reload()
        .await?
        .version(key_id)
        .and_then(KeyVersion::reencrypt_range);
    let Some(range) = range else {
        // Never active: no record holds a value under it
        return Ok(stream::empty().boxed());
    };
    let users: BoxStream<'a, FirestoreResult<UserDoc>> = db
        .fluent()
        .select()
        .from("users")
        .filter(|q| {
            q.for_all([
                q.field("encryption_marker").greater_than_or_equal(range.start()),
                range.end().and_then(|end| q.field("encryption_marker").less_than(end)),
            ])
        })
        .obj()
        .stream_query_with_errors()
        .await?;
    Ok(users)
}

fn main() {}

The range serves two decisions:

  • after mark_compromised, the range of the compromised version returns every record to re-encrypt and decide on, see The trust of re-encrypted values. The writes of processes that have not seen the compromise yet are in it too: they encrypt under the version and stamp an epoch below the range’s end. Run the query again once the longest time any process may run without a successful refresh of its record has passed since the compromise, for the writes of the last of them. A version flagged compromised after it stopped being active moves no epoch, and keeps the range it had;
  • before retire, and again before destroy, the query of the version returning nothing shows that no record holds a value under it, see Retiring the old version. That check needs one record at most: limit(1) in Firestore, EXISTS in SQL.

One epoch counts the activations of every purpose of the keyring, so a range can match a record with no field of the version’s purpose, such as a record without beacons in the range of a beacon version. Its re-encryption finds every value current and writes the marker alone. No range holds EncryptionMarker::ENVELOPE, since every range starts at epoch 1 or later. Records of several keyrings in one table add the keyring’s id to the query, as the sweep’s query does, see Sweeps by store.

Re-encrypting a value

Encryptor::reencrypt(aad, value) returns None for a value under the active randomised version of the encryptor’s record, without decrypting it or calling the KMS. A value under an older version the encryptor’s record holds, a decrypt-only version with a higher key id than the active one included, is read with decrypt_with_trust and encrypted again under the active version. The Reencrypted it returns holds the new value and the trust of the version the old value was read under, so the compromised-value decision stays with the caller, see The trust of re-encrypted values. Reencrypted::outcome() is the record’s ReencryptOutcome::Reencrypted, with from_compromised set from that trust, and ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted) of the returned Option is Skipped for None. reencrypt_deterministic does the same for deterministic values, under the deterministic feature.

An encryptor never encrypts a value back under an older version. A value under a version that is pending in the encryptor’s record, or missing from it, was encrypted by a process with a newer record, and an encryptor taken before a mark_compromised would otherwise move it back under the compromised version. The re-encryption fails with KeyringError::EncryptorOutdated, after reloading the cached record when it lacks the version or holds it as pending, under the same rate limit as decrypt’s reload for a key id it lacks. The encryptor keeps its own record, so that its marker still names every version the write encrypts under. The write is safe to retry: it takes a new encryptor and runs again, and the new encryptor, taken from the reloaded record, finds the value current or encrypts it under its active version. Inside reload_min_interval of another such reload the cached record may not have moved yet, and the retry fails the same way until it does. When the store’s record itself holds the version as pending, as for a value from another keyring whose key id is pending here or after a restore from an older backup, every retry fails with ENCRYPTOR_OUTDATED, at no more than one store read per reload_min_interval. A key id the reloaded record lacks too fails with KeyVersionUnknown.

A beacon cannot be re-encrypted: it is a MAC of the plaintext. encryptor.is_current(beacon.key_id()) applies the same rule to it: true under the active beacon version of the encryptor’s record, and the beacon stays as it is; false under an older one, and the field is read with decrypt_with_trust, whose plaintext gives both the value under the active version, through the encryptor’s encrypt, and the beacon under the active beacon version, through its beacon(field, &plaintext); and EncryptorOutdated under a version newer than the encryptor’s record.

When nothing needs a re-encryption, the write still stores the encryptor’s marker, conditional on the record as the sweep read it, and the re-encryption returns ReencryptOutcome::Skipped.

Running a sweep

sweep::reencrypt_records drives the application’s re-encryption over a stream of candidates, with at most concurrency in flight, and counts what happened. sweep::reencrypt_records_batched cuts the same stream into batches, re-encrypted and written back one batch per call, as BatchedSweepOptions sets: 100 candidates a batch and 4 batches in flight by default, DEFAULT_BATCH_SIZE and DEFAULT_CONCURRENCY. The stream returns each candidate as a Result, so that the query that finds them can fail into the report rather than end the sweep in silence. A sweep is data plane: it takes a Keyring and needs the application’s rights only, since a re-encryption encrypts under a DEK the record already holds. The rotation before it and the retire after it are the administrative job’s, on a KeyringAdmin. Here over an in-memory table, with both in one process:

use std::collections::BTreeMap;
use std::num::NonZeroUsize;
use std::sync::Mutex;

use futures_util::stream;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::LocalKmsProvider;
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::sweep::{self, ReencryptOutcome, EncryptionMarker, SweepReport};
use kms_aead_keyring::testing::MemoryKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Keyring, KeyringResult,
};
use kms_aead::providers::LocalKekId;
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    KeyId, KeyringAdmin, KeyringAlgorithms, KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions,
    Plaintext, Purpose,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

type UsersKeyring = Keyring<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>;

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
);
const TENANT: KeyringId = KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42");

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

#[derive(Clone)]
struct UserRow {
    email: Encrypted<Email, Randomized>,
    encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker,
}

/// The users table, by user id.
type Table = Mutex<BTreeMap<String, UserRow>>;

/// Re-encrypts the row of `user` under the active version, with the marker of the encryptor's record.
async fn reencrypt(keyring: &UsersKeyring, table: &Table, user: &str) -> KeyringResult<ReencryptOutcome> {
    let Some(row) = table.lock().unwrap().get(user).cloned() else {
        return Ok(ReencryptOutcome::Skipped);
    };
    let read: Vec<u8> = row.email.value().to_vec();
    let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await;
    let aad = EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from(user)?);
    let reencrypted = encryptor.reencrypt(&aad, &row.email).await?;
    let outcome = ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted);
    // The value is current when the rotation was of a purpose this row holds no value of
    let email = reencrypted.map_or(row.email, |reencrypted| reencrypted.value);
    let encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker = encryptor.record().encryption_marker();
    // Conditional on the row as it was read: a row the application rewrote meanwhile keeps its
    // own value, which it encrypted under the active versions anyway.
    let mut table = table.lock().unwrap();
    match table.get_mut(user) {
        Some(stored) if stored.email.value() == read.as_slice() => {
            *stored = UserRow { email, encryption_marker };
            Ok(outcome)
        }
        _ => Ok(ReencryptOutcome::Skipped),
    }
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let provider =
    LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("tenant-kek".to_string())?)?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<MemoryKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    MemoryKeyringStore::new(),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let admin: KeyringAdmin<MemoryKeyringStore, LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring> = registry
    .create(provider, TENANT, KeyringAlgorithms::new())
    .await?;
let keyring: &UsersKeyring = admin.keyring();
let table = Table::default();
for (user, email) in [("41", "ada@example.com"), ("42", "alice@example.com")] {
    let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await;
    let email = encryptor
        .encrypt(&EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from(user)?), &Email(email.to_string()))
        .await?;
    let encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker = encryptor.record().encryption_marker();
    table.lock().unwrap().insert(user.to_string(), UserRow { email, encryption_marker });
}
let staged: KeyId = admin.rotate(Purpose::Randomized).await?;
admin.promote_now(staged).await?;
// Every row whose marker is below the keyring's
let current: EncryptionMarker = keyring.record().encryption_marker();
let candidates = table
    .lock()
    .unwrap()
    .iter()
    .filter(|(_, row)| row.encryption_marker < current)
    .map(|(user, _)| user.clone())
    .collect::<Vec<String>>();

let report: SweepReport = sweep::reencrypt_records(
    keyring.id(),
    stream::iter(candidates.into_iter().map(Ok)),
    NonZeroUsize::new(8).unwrap(),
    |candidate| {
        let table = &table;
        async move { reencrypt(keyring, table, &candidate).await }
    },
)
.await;
println!("{report:?}");
assert_eq!(report.reencrypted, 2);
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

The SweepReport counts every candidate once in scanned, and once in reencrypted, skipped or failed. A candidate the stream returns as an error is counted in scanned and failed, and logged at WARN with the error’s Display; a stream whose query cannot go on returns its error and ends. A re-encryption that fails does not stop the sweep either: it is counted in failed and logged at WARN with the candidate’s Display and the error’s Display. A re-encryption that fails with EncryptorOutdated is one of them: a process with a newer record rewrote the record after the query returned it. The record is counted as failed, and the next run’s query no longer returns it, because the process that encrypted it stamped a newer marker. A value under a version the store’s record itself holds as pending, such as a value from another keyring whose key id is pending here, fails in every run instead. A candidate is the application’s own type, such as a record id, a struct of key columns or the record itself, and its Display names the record and nothing else. A record id is often an entity id, so read the sweep’s log as an administrative log.

The stream returns Result<T, E> and the re-encryption Result<ReencryptOutcome, E>, with the application’s own error type, any type that implements Display. The keyring’s calls inside it return a KeyringError, and a From impl on the application’s error lets ? convert it, as the examples on Firestore, PostgreSQL and BigQuery do with an error of their data store; a re-encryption with no errors of its own returns KeyringResult<ReencryptOutcome>, as above. The error’s Display is the application’s text and goes to the log as it is, so it carries no value, secret or personal data. The report counts failures and keeps no error: an application that wants the errors themselves records them inside its re-encryption.

Retiring the old version

Once the sweep is done, the administrative job retires the version a promotion replaced, and later destroys it, on its KeyringAdmin:

  1. wait, from the promotion, the longest time any process may run without a successful refresh of its record, see Staged rotation, and check that no process logged “The keyring record could not be revalidated” meanwhile. Until then a process on the old record encrypts under the old version and stamps the old epoch, see Finding records behind;
  2. run the sweep until a run reports nothing scanned and nothing failed;
  3. run the old version’s range query. When it returns nothing, retire(key_id). Reads under the version fail with KeyVersionRetired from then on, logged at WARN;
  4. export the Readable BigQuery and Tink keysets again, which leave a retired version out, and write them where the old ones are kept, see BigQuery and Tink interop. A keyset exported before keeps reading values under the retired version in SQL and in Tink;
  5. keep the version retired for as long as the backups of the stored values taken before the sweep are kept. A destroyed version makes the values in them unreadable, as a destroyed KEK version does, see Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation, and only a backup of the record from before the destroy still decrypts them, see below. A value the sweep missed fails with KeyVersionRetired: reinstate(key_id), export the keysets again, sweep again, and go back to step 3;
  6. run the range query again. When it still returns nothing, delete every copy of the keysets whose key_ids() include the retired version, then destroy(key_id). The other keysets, such as the Single keyset of the active deterministic version and the Readable keysets of other purposes, keep reading their values in SQL and in Tink. No call brings a destroyed version back.

destroy removes the encrypted DEK from the record only. A backup of the record still holds it, and so does every copy of a keyset exported while the version decrypted, both decrypted by the KMS as long as the KEK version that encrypted them exists, see Crypto-shredding. dangerously_shred() destroys every version and deletes the record, with the same copies left behind.

Sweeps by store

At millions of records a sweep streams its candidates from the store in one query and never holds its result in memory, and it writes them back in batches. Every example, on Firestore, PostgreSQL and BigQuery, has the same five properties:

  • it resumes without a checkpoint: the query is the checkpoint, since a re-encrypted record no longer matches it, so a cancelled or failed run is started again over the same query;
  • a concurrent writer wins: the write is conditional on the record the sweep read, and a record that changed in between is Skipped. The application’s own write re-encrypts every field under the active versions anyway;
  • concurrency bounds the batches in flight, and with them the load on both the store and the KMS. The DEK cache keeps each version’s key, so a sweep makes one KMS decrypt per key version and cache TTL, never one per record. The keys of a compromised version are the exception: they are decrypted for every value and never cached;
  • each write stores the marker of the encryptor that re-encrypted the record, so the record lies in the range of every version it is under;
  • it runs on a schedule until a run reports nothing scanned. The retire, and later the destroy, of the old version are the administrative job’s, see Retiring the old version.

The examples keep one keyring’s records in a collection or table. Each keyring counts its own epochs, so records of several keyrings in one table keep their keyring’s id beside the marker, and the query compares within one keyring, tenant = $2 AND encryption_marker < $1, on an index over both fields: a composite index in Firestore.

The trust of re-encrypted values

A sweep reads every field with decrypt_with_trust, and reports in ReencryptOutcome::Reencrypted whether the KeyTrust of any of them is compromised. A value read under a compromised version is untrusted data: whoever holds the leaked DEK can have forged it. Re-encrypting it under a new version makes it read as trusted everywhere afterwards.

So after a compromise the application decides per record before it re-encrypts:

  • a quarantine field beside the marker, for a person to review;
  • a comparison against a backup taken before the compromise;
  • or acceptance, when forged values do no harm.

SweepReport::reencrypted_from_compromised counts the records that went through that decision, or fewer on the BigQuery MERGE path, whose batches with a conflict count as skipped.

Sweeps on Firestore

A sweep of a Firestore collection: the encryption marker as a field, the query for the documents behind, and the conditional write back. The mechanism is in Sweeps, and the example has the five properties of Sweeps by store.

The marker is a bytes field, encryption_marker, and the query is one range filter on it. Firestore indexes a field ascending and descending by default, and the query needs the ascending index only, so a field override keeps just that one. firestore-rs declares it with its index management, behind its admin feature:

use firestore::{FirestoreDb, FirestoreIndexSyncReport, FirestoreResult};

/// Keeps the ascending index of `encryption_marker` in the `users` collection group, and drops the descending
/// one.
async fn sync_users_indexes(db: &FirestoreDb) -> FirestoreResult<FirestoreIndexSyncReport> {
    db.fluent()
        .indexes()
        .collection_group("users")
        .field_overrides(|f| f.fields([f.field("encryption_marker").indexes([f.ascending()])]))
        .sync()
        .await
}

The declaration runs from an administrative job, such as a deploy step, since .sync() needs the Firestore Admin API and roles/datastore.indexAdmin; the application that sweeps does not run it. The index management chapter of firestore-rs covers .plan(), .sync() and running them from a deploy.

A project that manages its indexes outside Rust sets the same override with gcloud:

gcloud firestore indexes fields update encryption_marker --collection-group=users --index=order=ASCENDING

The query streams with stream_query_with_errors(), so that a document that does not read is a failed candidate, and the sweep never ends early on it. sweep::reencrypt_records_batched cuts the documents into batches of 500, each re-encrypted through one encryptor and written back in one BatchWrite. Each write of the batch carries its document’s update time as its precondition, and Firestore refuses it once anything changed the document since the query read it:

use std::fmt::{self, Display, Formatter};
use std::num::NonZeroUsize;
use std::time::Duration;

use firestore::errors::FirestoreError;
use firestore::gcloud_sdk::google::rpc::Code;
use firestore::{
    paths, FirestoreBatchWriteResponse, FirestoreDb, FirestoreDbOptions,
    FirestoreSimpleBatchWriteOptions, FirestoreSimpleBatchWriter, FirestoreTimestamp,
    FirestoreWritePrecondition,
};
use futures_util::TryStreamExt;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::GcpKmsProvider;
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::FirestoreKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::sweep::{self, BatchedSweepOptions, ReencryptOutcome, EncryptionMarker, SweepReport};
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Keyring, KeyringError, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
use serde::{Deserialize, Serialize};
use kms_aead::providers::GcpKmsKeyRef;
use kms_aead_keyring::{KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions};

type UsersKeyring = Keyring<FirestoreKeyringStore, GcpKmsProvider, Ring>;

const USERS: &str = "users";
const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
);
/// Firestore's limit of writes in one `BatchWrite`.
const BATCH_SIZE: NonZeroUsize = NonZeroUsize::new(500).unwrap();
const CONCURRENCY: NonZeroUsize = NonZeroUsize::new(16).unwrap();
/// How long the batch writer retries a failed `BatchWrite` before the batch fails with its error.
/// The writer retries with no limit of its own, so without this a sweep against a Firestore that
/// refuses or fails its requests never ends.
const BATCH_WRITE_RETRY_LIMIT: Duration = Duration::from_secs(5);
/// How long a request waits for an answer, so that one to a Firestore that stopped answering fails.
const REQUEST_TIMEOUT: Duration = Duration::from_secs(10);

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

#[derive(Deserialize)]
struct UserDoc {
    #[serde(rename = "_firestore_id")]
    id: String,
    #[serde(rename = "_firestore_updated")]
    updated: FirestoreTimestamp,
    email: Encrypted<Email, Randomized>,
}

/// The fields a re-encryption writes: the encrypted fields and the marker of the encryptor that encrypted
/// them.
#[derive(Serialize, Deserialize)]
struct UserFields {
    email: Encrypted<Email, Randomized>,
    encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker,
}

/// The document id, and nothing of its fields.
impl Display for UserDoc {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        write!(f, "{USERS}/{}", self.id)
    }
}

/// Why the sweep failed on a document or a batch: an error of the keyring or of the users
/// collection, or the status of a document's write in a batch, `None` for a status the batch did
/// not return.
enum SweepError {
    Keyring(KeyringError),
    Users(FirestoreError),
    Write(Option<Code>),
}

impl From<KeyringError> for SweepError {
    fn from(error: KeyringError) -> Self {
        SweepError::Keyring(error)
    }
}

impl From<FirestoreError> for SweepError {
    fn from(error: FirestoreError) -> Self {
        SweepError::Users(error)
    }
}

/// The text the sweep logs: codes, never Firestore's messages, since a deserialisation error can
/// quote the field it could not read.
impl Display for SweepError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        match self {
            SweepError::Keyring(error) => write!(f, "the keyring failed with {}", error.code()),
            SweepError::Users(FirestoreError::DatabaseError(error)) => {
                write!(f, "the users collection failed with {}", error.public.code)
            }
            SweepError::Users(FirestoreError::DeserializeError(_)) => {
                f.write_str("a document of users did not deserialise")
            }
            SweepError::Users(_) => f.write_str("the users collection failed"),
            SweepError::Write(Some(code)) => write!(f, "its write failed with {code:?}"),
            SweepError::Write(None) => f.write_str("its write returned no status"),
        }
    }
}

/// Re-encrypts every user whose marker is below the current one, in batches of [`BATCH_SIZE`] with
/// [`CONCURRENCY`] in flight, and returns the sweep's report.
async fn sweep_users(
    keyring: &UsersKeyring,
    writer: &FirestoreSimpleBatchWriter,
) -> Result<SweepReport, FirestoreError> {
    let current: EncryptionMarker = keyring.record().encryption_marker();
    let candidates = writer
        .db
        .fluent()
        .select()
        .fields(paths!(UserFields::{email, encryption_marker}))
        .from(USERS)
        .filter(|q| q.field("encryption_marker").less_than(current))
        .obj::<UserDoc>()
        .stream_query_with_errors()
        .await?
        .err_into::<SweepError>();
    let report: SweepReport = sweep::reencrypt_records_batched(
        keyring.id(),
        candidates,
        BatchedSweepOptions::new()
            .with_batch_size(BATCH_SIZE)
            .with_concurrency(CONCURRENCY),
        |docs: Vec<UserDoc>| reencrypt_batch(keyring, writer, docs),
    )
    .await;
    Ok(report)
}

/// Re-encrypts a batch of documents through one encryptor and writes them back in one `BatchWrite`,
/// each write stamped with the encryptor's marker and conditional on the update time the query read.
/// A batch is not atomic: a document changed since the query fails its own write only, and the
/// sweep counts it as skipped.
async fn reencrypt_batch(
    keyring: &UsersKeyring,
    writer: &FirestoreSimpleBatchWriter,
    docs: Vec<UserDoc>,
) -> Result<Vec<(UserDoc, Result<ReencryptOutcome, SweepError>)>, SweepError> {
    let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await;
    let encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker = encryptor.record().encryption_marker();
    let mut batch = writer.new_batch();
    let mut done: Vec<(UserDoc, Result<ReencryptOutcome, SweepError>)> =
        Vec::with_capacity(docs.len());
    let mut queued: Vec<(UserDoc, ReencryptOutcome)> = Vec::with_capacity(docs.len());
    for doc in docs {
        let queue = async {
            let aad = EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from(doc.id.as_str())?);
            let reencrypted = encryptor.reencrypt(&aad, &doc.email).await?;
            let outcome = ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted);
            // A current value still gets the encryptor's marker, so that it leaves the query.
            let email = reencrypted.map_or_else(|| doc.email.clone(), |reencrypted| reencrypted.value);
            writer
                .db
                .fluent()
                .update()
                .fields(paths!(UserFields::{email, encryption_marker}))
                .in_col(USERS)
                .precondition(FirestoreWritePrecondition::UpdateTime(doc.updated.into()))
                .document_id(&doc.id)
                .object(&UserFields { email, encryption_marker })
                .add_to_batch(&mut batch)?;
            Ok::<ReencryptOutcome, SweepError>(outcome)
        };
        match queue.await {
            Ok(outcome) => queued.push((doc, outcome)),
            Err(error) => done.push((doc, Err(error))),
        }
    }
    if queued.is_empty() {
        return Ok(done);
    }
    let response: FirestoreBatchWriteResponse = batch.write().await?;
    done.extend(queued.into_iter().enumerate().map(|(index, (doc, outcome))| {
        let code: Option<Code> = response
            .statuses
            .get(index)
            .and_then(|status| Code::try_from(status.code).ok());
        let outcome = match code {
            Some(Code::Ok) => Ok(outcome),
            // Written since the query: the writer encrypted the document under its own record.
            Some(Code::FailedPrecondition) => Ok(ReencryptOutcome::Skipped),
            _ => Err(SweepError::Write(code)),
        };
        (doc, outcome)
    }));
    Ok(done)
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);
let db: FirestoreDb = FirestoreDb::with_options(
    FirestoreDbOptions::new("my-project".to_string()).with_request_timeout(REQUEST_TIMEOUT),
)
.await?;
let writer: FirestoreSimpleBatchWriter = db
    .create_simple_batch_writer_with_options(
        FirestoreSimpleBatchWriteOptions::new()
            .with_retry_max_elapsed_time(BATCH_WRITE_RETRY_LIMIT),
    )
    .await?;
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<FirestoreKeyringStore, Ring>::new(
    FirestoreKeyringStore::new(db.clone()),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let keyring: UsersKeyring = registry
    .open(GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref).await?, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"))
    .await?;
let report: SweepReport = sweep_users(&keyring, &writer).await?;
println!("{report:?}");
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

The retry limit ends retries, and the request timeout ends a request that never gets an answer, the candidate query included.

CONCURRENCY batches of 500 writes in flight are a high write rate for a collection that has seen little traffic. Firestore advises starting a new collection at 500 writes a second and raising the rate by 50% every 5 minutes, its 500/50/5 rule, so raise CONCURRENCY step by step on one.

A range filter never matches a document without the field, so a document written before the marker needs the marker written first, see Move envelope values to a keyring.

A partitioned query does not fit the sweep: PartitionQuery takes no filter, so its workers would read every document, the current ones included.

The marker takes 10 bytes of a document: the field name as a 5-byte string and the 5 bytes of the value. Its entry in the ascending index takes the size of the document’s name, of its parent’s name, of the field name and of the value, plus 32 bytes, by Firestore’s storage size rules: about 70 bytes for a short document id in a top-level collection. 10 million documents hold about 0.1 GB of markers and 0.7 GB of index, about $0.13 a month at about $0.18 per GiB.

The sweep_firestore example runs this sweep end to end, from the writes to the report, on the Firestore emulator. Its memory holds the batches in flight only, so it runs the same over millions of documents. A stream that ends early, on a deadline or an error, leaves the documents it did not reach below the current marker, and the next run of the sweep finds them.

Sweeps on PostgreSQL

A sweep of a PostgreSQL table: the encryption marker as a column, the query for the rows behind, and the conditional write back. The mechanism is in Sweeps, and the example has the five properties of Sweeps by store.

The marker is a BYTEA column with a btree index:

CREATE TABLE users (
    id BIGINT PRIMARY KEY,
    email BYTEA NOT NULL,
    encryption_marker BYTEA NOT NULL
);
CREATE INDEX users_encryption_marker ON users (encryption_marker);

The candidates are one query, encryption_marker < $1 with no ORDER BY, so the planner is free to read them from the index on encryption_marker, and query_raw streams its rows: memory holds the batches in flight only. The query holds its connection for as long as it streams, so the UPDATEs go through a second one. sweep::reencrypt_records_batched cuts the candidates into batches of 1000 rows, each re-encrypted through one encryptor and written back in one UPDATE over arrays of the ids, the new values and the values it read. The UPDATE is conditional on the bytes it read, which a write of any other process changes, since every randomised value has its own nonce. A process that has not seen the promotion yet still encrypts under the old version and stamps the old epoch, which the row holds already, so the marker alone does not detect its write:

use std::collections::HashSet;
use std::fmt::{self, Display, Formatter};
use std::num::NonZeroUsize;

use futures_util::{future, StreamExt};
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::GcpKmsProvider;
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::PostgresKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::sweep::{self, BatchedSweepOptions, ReencryptOutcome, EncryptionMarker, SweepReport};
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Keyring, KeyringError, Plaintext,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
use tokio_postgres::{Client, NoTls, Row};
use kms_aead::providers::GcpKmsKeyRef;
use kms_aead_keyring::{KeyringId, KeyringRegistry, KeyringRegistryOptions};
use tokio_postgres::Config;

type UsersKeyring = Keyring<PostgresKeyringStore<NoTls>, GcpKmsProvider, Ring>;

const EMAIL: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("email"),
);
const BATCH_SIZE: NonZeroUsize = NonZeroUsize::new(1000).unwrap();
const CONCURRENCY: NonZeroUsize = NonZeroUsize::new(16).unwrap();

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Email(String);

impl Plaintext for Email {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Email)
    }
}

/// A row the query returned.
struct UserRow {
    id: i64,
    email: Encrypted<Email, Randomized>,
}

/// The row id, and nothing of its columns.
impl Display for UserRow {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        write!(f, "users/{}", self.id)
    }
}

/// Why the sweep failed on a row or a batch: an error of the keyring or of the users table.
enum SweepError {
    Keyring(KeyringError),
    Users(tokio_postgres::Error),
}

impl From<KeyringError> for SweepError {
    fn from(error: KeyringError) -> Self {
        SweepError::Keyring(error)
    }
}

impl From<tokio_postgres::Error> for SweepError {
    fn from(error: tokio_postgres::Error) -> Self {
        SweepError::Users(error)
    }
}

/// The text the sweep logs: codes, never the server's message, whose detail can quote a row's
/// values.
impl Display for SweepError {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        match self {
            SweepError::Keyring(error) => write!(f, "the keyring failed with {}", error.code()),
            SweepError::Users(error) => match error.code() {
                Some(state) => write!(f, "the users table failed with SQLSTATE {}", state.code()),
                None => f.write_str("the users table failed"),
            },
        }
    }
}

/// Re-encrypts every user whose marker is below the current one, read through `reader` and written
/// back through `writer` in batches of [`BATCH_SIZE`] with [`CONCURRENCY`] in flight, and returns
/// the sweep's report.
async fn sweep_users(
    keyring: &UsersKeyring,
    reader: &Client,
    writer: &Client,
) -> Result<SweepReport, tokio_postgres::Error> {
    let current: EncryptionMarker = keyring.record().encryption_marker();
    // A row that does not read is a failed candidate. An error of the stream itself is one too, and
    // ends it: once its connection drops, the stream returns that error on every poll and never
    // its end.
    let mut failed = false;
    let candidates = reader
        .query_raw("SELECT id, email FROM users WHERE encryption_marker < $1", [current])
        .await?
        .take_while(move |row: &Result<Row, tokio_postgres::Error>| {
            let more = !failed;
            failed = row.is_err();
            future::ready(more)
        })
        .map(|row: Result<Row, tokio_postgres::Error>| {
            let row: Row = row?;
            Ok::<UserRow, SweepError>(UserRow {
                id: row.try_get("id")?,
                email: row.try_get("email")?,
            })
        });
    let report: SweepReport = sweep::reencrypt_records_batched(
        keyring.id(),
        candidates,
        BatchedSweepOptions::new()
            .with_batch_size(BATCH_SIZE)
            .with_concurrency(CONCURRENCY),
        |batch: Vec<UserRow>| reencrypt_batch(keyring, writer, batch),
    )
    .await;
    Ok(report)
}

/// Re-encrypts a batch of rows through one encryptor and writes it back in one `UPDATE` that stamps
/// the encryptor's marker, row by row only where the row still holds the bytes the query read. A
/// row changed since the query is not returned by the `UPDATE`, and the sweep counts it as skipped.
async fn reencrypt_batch(
    keyring: &UsersKeyring,
    client: &Client,
    batch: Vec<UserRow>,
) -> Result<Vec<(UserRow, Result<ReencryptOutcome, SweepError>)>, SweepError> {
    let encryptor = keyring.encryptor().await;
    let encryption_marker: EncryptionMarker = encryptor.record().encryption_marker();
    let mut done: Vec<(UserRow, Result<ReencryptOutcome, SweepError>)> =
        Vec::with_capacity(batch.len());
    let mut queued: Vec<(UserRow, ReencryptOutcome)> = Vec::with_capacity(batch.len());
    let mut emails: Vec<Encrypted<Email, Randomized>> = Vec::with_capacity(batch.len());
    for user in batch {
        let reencrypt = async {
            let aad = EMAIL.aad(&EntityId::try_from(user.id.to_string())?);
            let reencrypted = encryptor.reencrypt(&aad, &user.email).await?;
            let outcome = ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted);
            // A current value still gets the encryptor's marker, so that it leaves the query.
            let email = reencrypted.map_or_else(|| user.email.clone(), |reencrypted| reencrypted.value);
            Ok::<_, SweepError>((email, outcome))
        };
        match reencrypt.await {
            Ok((email, outcome)) => {
                emails.push(email);
                queued.push((user, outcome));
            }
            Err(error) => done.push((user, Err(error))),
        }
    }
    let ids: Vec<i64> = queued.iter().map(|(user, _)| user.id).collect();
    let read_emails: Vec<&Encrypted<Email, Randomized>> =
        queued.iter().map(|(user, _)| &user.email).collect();
    let written: HashSet<i64> = client
        .query(
            "UPDATE users SET email = batch.email, encryption_marker = $4
             FROM unnest($1::BIGINT[], $2::BYTEA[], $3::BYTEA[]) AS batch(id, email, read_email)
             WHERE users.id = batch.id AND users.email = batch.read_email
             RETURNING users.id",
            &[&ids, &emails, &read_emails, &encryption_marker],
        )
        .await?
        .iter()
        .map(|row| row.try_get("id"))
        .collect::<Result<HashSet<i64>, tokio_postgres::Error>>()?;
    done.extend(queued.into_iter().map(|(user, outcome)| {
        // Written since the query: the writer encrypted the row under its own record.
        let outcome = if written.contains(&user.id) {
            outcome
        } else {
            ReencryptOutcome::Skipped
        };
        (user, Ok(outcome))
    }));
    Ok(done)
}

#[tokio::main]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error>> {
let key_ref = GcpKmsKeyRef::new(
    "my-project".to_string(),
    "europe-north1".to_string(),
    "my-key-ring".to_string(),
    "my-key".to_string(),
);
let config: Config = "postgres://app@localhost/app".parse()?;
let (client, connection) = config.connect(NoTls).await?;
tokio::spawn(connection);
let (reader, connection) = config.connect(NoTls).await?;
tokio::spawn(connection);
let registry = KeyringRegistry::<PostgresKeyringStore<NoTls>, Ring>::new(
    PostgresKeyringStore::new(config, NoTls),
    KeyringRegistryOptions::new(),
)?;
let keyring: UsersKeyring = registry
    .open(GcpKmsProvider::new(&key_ref).await?, KeyringId::from_static("tenant-42"))
    .await?;
let report: SweepReport = sweep_users(&keyring, &reader, &client).await?;
println!("{report:?}");
registry.shutdown(None).await;
Ok(())
}

CONCURRENCY bounds the batches in flight, but one writing connection runs their UPDATEs one after another; a pool of connections runs them in parallel.

EncryptionMarker is a BYTEA parameter and column through tokio-postgres with the postgres feature, and through sqlx with the sqlx feature. The query holds one snapshot for as long as it streams, and while it is open VACUUM cannot reclaim the old versions of the rows the sweep updates; a scheduled job that ends and runs again keeps each snapshot short, since the query of the markers below the current one returns exactly the rows the last run did not write.

The marker takes 6 bytes of a row: its 5 bytes and a 1-byte length header. A btree on a column of a few distinct epochs stores each one once per page with the list of its rows: measured on PostgreSQL 18 over 1 million rows of 7 epochs, the column took 6 bytes per row and its index about 7. 10 million rows hold about 130 MB of markers and index.

The sweep_postgres example runs this sweep end to end, from the writes to the report, on a local PostgreSQL.

Sweeps on BigQuery

A sweep of a BigQuery table, in two paths: one UPDATE in SQL for the fields BigQuery reads, and a Rust path for the rest. The mechanism is in Sweeps. The Rust path has the five properties of Sweeps by store, and the SQL path states its own below.

The marker is a BYTES column that every write of the encrypted fields sets:

CREATE TABLE users (
  id STRING NOT NULL,
  phone BYTES NOT NULL,
  encryption_marker BYTES NOT NULL
);

A field BigQuery reads, declared with Interop::BigQuery, is re-encrypted by one UPDATE in SQL, with no Rust loop and no value leaving BigQuery. Its parameters come from a Readable(Purpose::Randomized) export, see Exporting a keyset to BigQuery: @kms_key is the export’s key_uri(), @keyset its value(), and @encryption_marker the bytes of its marker, keyset.encryption_marker().value():

UPDATE users
SET
  email = AEAD.ENCRYPT(
    KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset),
    AEAD.DECRYPT_BYTES(
      KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN(@kms_key, @keyset), email, CAST(CONCAT('user/', id, '/email') AS BYTES)),
    CAST(CONCAT('user/', id, '/email') AS BYTES)),
  encryption_marker = @encryption_marker
WHERE encryption_marker < @encryption_marker;

AEAD.DECRYPT_BYTES, and AEAD.ENCRYPT of bytes, take the AAD as BYTES, the UTF-8 of the same text. AEAD.ENCRYPT encrypts under the keyset’s primary, which a Readable export sets to the active version of the record it reads from the store, and EncryptedKeyset::encryption_marker() is that record’s marker. So every row the statement re-encrypts lies in the range of the primary, as a row written through an Encryptor does. A keyset exported before a promotion has the previous version as its primary and the previous epoch’s marker, and the rows it re-encrypts stay in the next sweep’s query, so the sweep exports its keyset after the promotion. The export is a KeyringAdmin call of the administrative job.

The statement re-encrypts every encrypted field of the row, since the marker it writes covers all of them. Fields of several purposes are encrypted under one Readable keyset each, and the statement stamps the marker only when their encryption_marker() is the same; otherwise it exports them again. A row with a beacon, which SQL does not compute, goes through the Rust path below.

This sweep:

  • resumes without a checkpoint: the statement is atomic, a failed one changed nothing, and the next run’s WHERE finds every row it did not write;
  • loses no concurrent write: BigQuery runs DML under snapshot isolation, and of two conflicting statements on the same rows one fails and runs again, and its WHERE then sees the other’s marker;
  • makes no KMS call per record: BigQuery decrypts the keyset through Cloud KMS for the statement;
  • runs on a schedule until it re-encrypts no row. The administrative job retires the old version once its range query, SELECT COUNT(*) FROM users WHERE encryption_marker >= @start AND (@end IS NULL OR encryption_marker < @end), returns 0.

A Readable keyset leaves a compromised version out, so the values under it fail AEAD.DECRYPT_BYTES and the whole statement with them. After a compromise those rows go through the Rust path below, where the encryptor’s reencrypt reports their trust.

Other fields, such as ChaCha20-Poly1305 values or values under a custom AAD, are re-encrypted in Rust. BigQuery runs only a few DML statements on one table at a time, so the write is one MERGE per batch of rows rather than one UPDATE per row, conditional on the bytes each row held:

MERGE users u
USING UNNEST(@rows) p
ON u.id = p.id AND u.phone = p.old_phone
WHEN MATCHED THEN UPDATE SET phone = p.phone, encryption_marker = @encryption_marker;

The rows stream from the application’s BigQuery client, one query of SELECT id, phone, encryption_marker FROM users WHERE encryption_marker < @current with the current marker, and sweep::reencrypt_records_batched cuts them into batches. Each batch is re-encrypted through one encryptor, and the client runs the MERGE with @encryption_marker bound to the marker of the encryptor’s record. Every row of a batch goes into @rows, the current ones with their bytes as they are, so that each gets the marker:

use std::fmt::{self, Display, Formatter};
use std::future::Future;
use std::num::NonZeroUsize;

use futures_util::Stream;
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::GcpKmsProvider;
use kms_aead::KmsAeadResult;
use kms_aead_keyring::formats::Randomized;
use kms_aead_keyring::store::FirestoreKeyringStore;
use kms_aead_keyring::sweep::{self, BatchedSweepOptions, ReencryptOutcome, EncryptionMarker, SweepReport};
use kms_aead_keyring::{
    Encrypted, EntityId, EntityKind, FieldName, FieldSpec, Keyring, KeyringError, KeyringResult,
    Plaintext, Encryptor,
};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

type UsersKeyring = Keyring<FirestoreKeyringStore, GcpKmsProvider, Ring>;
type UsersEncryptor<'k> = Encryptor<'k, FirestoreKeyringStore, GcpKmsProvider, Ring>;

const PHONE: FieldSpec = FieldSpec::new(
    EntityKind::from_static("user"),
    FieldName::from_static("phone"),
);
const BATCH_SIZE: NonZeroUsize = NonZeroUsize::new(1000).unwrap();
/// BigQuery runs 2 mutating DML statements on a table at once and queues the rest.
const CONCURRENCY: NonZeroUsize = NonZeroUsize::new(2).unwrap();

#[derive(Clone)]
struct Phone(String);

impl Plaintext for Phone {
    fn to_plaintext(&self) -> KmsAeadResult<SecretValue> {
        self.0.to_plaintext()
    }

    fn from_plaintext(plaintext: SecretValue) -> KmsAeadResult<Self> {
        String::from_plaintext(plaintext).map(Phone)
    }
}

/// A row of `SELECT id, phone FROM users WHERE encryption_marker < @current`.
struct UserRow {
    id: String,
    phone: Encrypted<Phone, Randomized>,
}

/// The row id, and nothing of its columns.
impl Display for UserRow {
    fn fmt(&self, f: &mut Formatter<'_>) -> fmt::Result {
        write!(f, "users/{}", self.id)
    }
}

/// One element of `@rows`: a row with the bytes it held and the bytes to write.
struct PhoneUpdate {
    id: String,
    old_phone: Vec<u8>,
    phone: Encrypted<Phone, Randomized>,
}

/// `phone` of user `id` under the active version of `encryptor`, and the outcome of its
/// re-encryption. A value under that version comes back as it is, with `ReencryptOutcome::Skipped`.
async fn reencrypt_phone(
    encryptor: &UsersEncryptor<'_>,
    id: &str,
    phone: &Encrypted<Phone, Randomized>,
) -> KeyringResult<(Encrypted<Phone, Randomized>, ReencryptOutcome)> {
    let aad = PHONE.aad(&EntityId::try_from(id)?);
    let reencrypted = encryptor.reencrypt(&aad, phone).await?;
    let outcome = ReencryptOutcome::from(&reencrypted);
    // A current value still gets the encryptor's marker, so that it leaves the query.
    let phone = reencrypted.map_or_else(|| phone.clone(), |reencrypted| reencrypted.value);
    Ok((phone, outcome))
}

/// Re-encrypts the `rows` of the query in batches, one encryptor per batch, and writes each batch
/// through `merge`, which runs the `MERGE` with `@encryption_marker` bound to the marker it is given, the
/// marker of the batch's encryptor, and returns its number of affected rows. `ClientError` is the
/// error of the application's BigQuery client, which a row that did not read returns and a
/// `KeyringError` converts into, and its `Display` is the text the sweep logs for a failure.
async fn sweep_phones<Rows, Merge, Merged, ClientError>(
    keyring: &UsersKeyring,
    rows: Rows,
    merge: Merge,
) -> SweepReport
where
    Rows: Stream<Item = Result<UserRow, ClientError>>,
    Merge: Fn(Vec<PhoneUpdate>, EncryptionMarker) -> Merged,
    Merged: Future<Output = Result<u64, ClientError>>,
    ClientError: From<KeyringError> + Display,
{
    let options: BatchedSweepOptions = BatchedSweepOptions::new()
        .with_batch_size(BATCH_SIZE)
        .with_concurrency(CONCURRENCY);
    sweep::reencrypt_records_batched(keyring.id(), rows, options, |batch: Vec<UserRow>| {
        let merge = &merge;
        async move {
            let encryptor: UsersEncryptor<'_> = keyring.encryptor().await;
            let mut done: Vec<(UserRow, Result<ReencryptOutcome, ClientError>)> =
                Vec::with_capacity(batch.len());
            let mut queued: Vec<(UserRow, ReencryptOutcome)> = Vec::with_capacity(batch.len());
            let mut updates: Vec<PhoneUpdate> = Vec::with_capacity(batch.len());
            for row in batch {
                match reencrypt_phone(&encryptor, &row.id, &row.phone).await {
                    Ok((phone, outcome)) => {
                        updates.push(PhoneUpdate {
                            id: row.id.clone(),
                            old_phone: row.phone.value().to_vec(),
                            phone,
                        });
                        queued.push((row, outcome));
                    }
                    Err(error) => done.push((row, Err(error.into()))),
                }
            }
            let merged: u64 = merge(updates, encryptor.record().encryption_marker()).await?;
            // A `MERGE` returns a count, not the rows: a batch with one row a concurrent writer
            // changed counts every row as skipped
            let all_written = u64::try_from(queued.len()) == Ok(merged);
            done.extend(queued.into_iter().map(|(row, outcome)| {
                (row, Ok(if all_written { outcome } else { ReencryptOutcome::Skipped }))
            }));
            Ok(done)
        }
    })
    .await
}

fn main() {}

A batch is re-encrypted sequentially inside its call, and concurrency bounds the batches in flight, which BigQuery’s limit on concurrent DML per table sets low. The five properties hold per batch: a row that a concurrent writer changed fails the ON condition and stays in the query for the next run.

A MERGE returns a count, not the rows it changed, so a batch with one such row counts all its rows as Skipped, the ones it wrote included. Its re-encryptions from a compromised version then drop out of SweepReport::reencrypted_from_compromised, although they went through the trust decision below, so on this path that count is a lower bound.

The marker takes 7 bytes of a row: its 5 bytes and the 2 BigQuery counts for every BYTES value. Measured on BigQuery, 100,000 markers held 700,000 bytes, and the sweep’s query processed 700,000 bytes, the encryption_marker column alone. BigQuery does not cluster a BYTES column, so the query reads the whole column: about 70 MB at 10 million rows, about $0.0004 per query at $6.25 per TiB on demand, and its storage about $0.0013 a month at $0.02 per GiB.

The sweep_bigquery example runs the UPDATE above end to end, from the writes to the count of re-encrypted rows, against Cloud KMS and BigQuery. It writes the rows through the Storage Write API, and the UPDATE re-encrypts them straight after: DML can modify rows recently written through its gRPC interface.

Emergency runbook

Rehearse each on a staging keyring, on a schedule, and record how long propagation took.

A plaintext DEK leaked

Or is suspected to have.

  1. mark_compromised(key_id, note): the version is flagged and, if active, replaced in one write by a version that is active at once. The replacement skips the staged wait on purpose: encrypting under the leaked key stops at each process’s next refresh, and a process that has not seen the replacement yet reloads its record once, rate-limited, when it meets a value under it. When the KMS cannot encrypt a new DEK, the flag lands alone and encrypt fails with NoActiveVersion; once the KMS answers, rotate and promote_now at once, since nothing encrypts under the purpose meanwhile, or promote_now the pending version if the purpose has one. promote would refuse with PromotionTooEarly for up to record_ttl from the staging. A pending version itself is never compromised: destroy it instead;
  2. every process stops using the key within the bound of the record cache, and its DEK is evicted from the DEK cache. With the default Block policy values under the version stop decrypting through decrypt. Set Allow for the time of the sweep if availability matters more, and be aware the leaked DEK can forge values under its id meanwhile;
  3. sweep the records in the compromised version’s range, see Records under a version, reading them with decrypt_with_trust, and decide per record on the values read through the compromised version, see The trust of re-encrypted values. Run the range query again once every process has had time to refresh its record, for the writes of processes that had not seen the flag yet;
  4. export the BigQuery keysets again: a Readable keyset leaves the compromised version out. Delete the Single keyset of the compromised version once the sweep is done;
  5. retire and later destroy the version as in Retiring the old version.

Rotation protects nothing already copied: backups, point-in-time recovery and data exports still hold values the leaked DEK decrypts. Treat them as exposed and revoke the secrets inside them.

A KMS IAM over-grant

  1. Fix the IAM binding, back to the split in KMS rights;
  2. rotate the KEK in the KMS and reencrypt_deks();
  3. with KMS data-access logs enabled in advance, list the Decrypt calls the wrong principal made: every encrypted DEK they decrypted is a leaked DEK, see above. Without those logs, every version is a leaked DEK.

An encrypted DEK or the keyring record leaked

Harmless by itself, since decrypting needs the KMS. If the attacker may also gain Decrypt on the KEK, follow the IAM over-grant steps. If the attacker may also write the keyring store, see the next case and the store-writer threat in Keyring deployment.

A version the administrative job did not create

This case starts from the ERROR event of a refused record that moves backwards, or from a version in the record that the administrative job’s log does not show. The event’s rule names the version that moved backwards, except the re-encryption epoch moved back, which names none: compare the versions of the record in the store with the job’s log to find the one it did not create. Either means that something other than the job wrote the record: a restore, which the next case covers, or a writer of the store.

  1. If the version is active, mark_compromised it, so that nothing new is encrypted under it from each process’s next refresh. If it is pending, destroy it;
  2. retire and destroy the version as in Retiring the old version, after a sweep if values were encrypted under it;
  3. check the KMS audit log for the Encrypt call that encrypted its DEK. None means its encrypted DEK was copied or relabelled from another version, of this keyring or of another under the same KEK, which the KMS decrypts as it would the original; one from a principal other than the administrative job is an IAM over-grant, see above;
  4. audit who can write the keyring store: the application has no write, and only the administrative job should.

The keyring record was deleted or corrupted

A running process takes a record gone from the store as a shredded keyring, see Record cache. At its next revalidation, forced reload or administrative call it destroys every version of its cached record in memory, evicts the keyring’s keys from the DEK cache and logs a WARN, “The keyring store no longer holds the keyring record; every version is destroyed in this process”. A shred writes the record with every version destroyed before it deletes it: a process that read that record logs an INFO for the deletion instead, and the process that ran the shred logs nothing. A process that first reads the store after the delete, such as one without push, logs the WARN for a shred too, so the WARN does not tell an accidental deletion from an erasure by itself. Its handles then fail: encrypt with NoActiveVersion, decrypt with KeyVersionDestroyed, and reload with RecordMissing. Within the bound of how late a change is seen, an accidental deletion stops every process that holds the keyring. New processes fail at open with RecordMissing. A record that fails to parse or validate is a failed read instead: a running process keeps its cached record and logs the WARN of a failed revalidation, and new processes fail at open with RecordInvalid.

  1. Restore the record from the store’s backup: Firestore point-in-time recovery, a PostgreSQL backup, or a noncurrent version of the Cloud Storage or S3 object. The encrypted DEKs inside are still valid under the KEK;
  2. repeat on the restored record every administrative call made after the copy was taken, from the administrative job’s log, before any process opens the keyring again;
  3. restart every process that took the deletion. It keeps the deleted record as the baseline of the id, and refuses the restored copy with RecordRegressed and an ERROR, because the copy revives versions the process holds destroyed. A process that starts after the restore has no baseline and takes the record.

A process that never saw the deletion, such as one whose revalidations were failing meanwhile, refuses an older copy as well, and logs an ERROR. Values are never lost with the record as long as a copy of it exists. Without any copy they are gone, so keep versioning on the bucket and back up the table.

Crypto-shredding

Crypto-shredding makes values unrecoverable by destroying the keys that decrypt them, so no copy of the values has to be found. To make a scope’s values unrecoverable:

  • with a KEK per scope: destroy the KEK’s versions in the KMS. Every encrypted DEK of the scope is then unrecoverable, backups of the record and keysets exported from it included, since the KEK encrypts both;
  • with a shared KEK: KeyringAdmin::dangerously_shred(), see Shredding a keyring.

A deployment that needs shredding guarantees uses a KEK per scope.

Shredding a keyring

dangerously_shred() destroys every version of the keyring in one write, as dangerously_destroy_all_versions does, then deletes the record from the store with every earlier version of it the store keeps. The keyring encrypts and decrypts nothing from then on, except through a stream encryptor or decryptor already made, see below, and the id is free for a new keyring.

  • the write is signed as every lifecycle write is: an administration without a record signer is refused with RecordSignerMissing over a signed record, and a signing one with RecordUnsigned over an unsigned record, both before any store write;
  • a conflict of the write or of the delete starts again from a new read, up to cas_retries, then RecordConflict;
  • a shred that fails is run again. A record whose every version is destroyed already, as a failure between the write and the delete leaves it, is deleted without a write, and a record already gone returns Ok. The record left between the two steps holds no encrypted DEK, and every process that reads it destroys its versions;
  • KeyringRegistry::admin on a keyring already gone fails with RecordMissing, which an erasure treats as done.

Every process that holds the keyring takes the shred as a deleted record, see above. How late:

  • with push, within reload_min_interval of the store’s signal;
  • without push, on Cloud Storage and S3 or with watch off, once the cached record is older than record_ttl, at the next call.

The plaintext DEK leaves the process’s memory at that revalidation. A process that makes no call keeps it until the DEK cache drops it: its TTL, with purge_expired on a timer, see DEK eviction. A stream decryptor or encryptor already made keeps the key derived for its value, and goes on decrypting or encrypting with it, until it is dropped. Build a decryptor per request and do not cache it across requests, so that a shred takes effect for new requests, see Streaming encryption.

dangerously_shred() never calls the KMS and leaves every KMS key version as it was. Values encrypted under the keyring stay where they are and decrypt under nothing.

What survives a shred
