Every copy of the record outside the store’s API still holds the encrypted DEKs, which the KMS decrypts as long as a KEK version that encrypted them exists. The operator deletes them or ends the KEK versions:

  • Firestore: serves a deleted document to reads at an earlier time for one hour, and for seven days with point-in-time recovery on, which keeps the versions of every document for that window. Backups and exports hold the record until they are deleted;
  • PostgreSQL: the row’s earlier versions until a vacuum reclaims them, and backups, WAL archives and replicas. The WAL is the write-ahead log, which holds every committed change;
  • Cloud Storage: dangerously_shred() deletes every generation of the object, noncurrent ones included. Soft delete keeps every deleted generation restorable for the bucket’s retention, 7 days by default, so a bucket of keyring records turns soft delete off, or counts the retention into the time a shred takes;
  • S3: dangerously_shred() deletes every version and delete marker of the object. Replication to another bucket and backups hold copies it does not reach;
  • exported keysets: every BigQuery and Tink keyset exported from the keyring holds the keys of the versions it was exported with, under the same KEK, see Keyring limits.

Per store, what dangerously_shred() deletes is in Keyring stores.

So after a shred, either delete the record’s backups and every copy of the keysets exported from the keyring, or destroy every KEK version that encrypted them. On GCP, Vault Transit and the local KMS, rotate the KEK, re-encrypt every other keyring under it as in Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation, then destroy every previous KEK version, the ones an earlier rotation left disabled included. On AWS rotated key material is never destroyed, so only the deletion of the KMS key itself shreds. Azure Key Vault deletes a key with all its versions and never one version alone, so there too only the deletion of the key shreds. A vault with soft delete keeps a deleted key recoverable until it is purged, and with purge protection on, no purge is possible until the vault’s retention period of 7 to 90 days has passed, see Azure Key Vault soft-delete.

A KMS outage

DEKs in the cache serve reads and writes until their TTL. A longer DEK cache TTL buys time at the cost of a longer life of plaintext keys in memory. No decryption, encryption or rotation succeeds until the KMS returns, and errors carry the provider’s message. mark_compromised still lands the flag.

Cargo features

kms-aead

The crate has no default features.

FeatureWhat it adds
gcp-kms-encryptionGoogle Cloud KMS, with the native TLS roots of the system, and the crc32c crate for the checksums of its requests. Be aware it also turns on native-tls in reqwest, so in the same build the Azure provider uses native-tls whatever its TLS feature
gcp-kms-encryption-webpkiGoogle Cloud KMS with the bundled WebPKI roots, and the crc32c crate
gcp-kms-encryption-baseGoogle Cloud KMS without a TLS root store or crypto provider, and the crc32c crate. The application enables them with its own gcloud-sdk features, such as tls-roots or tls-webpki-roots, and the crate does not build without one
aws-kms-encryptionAWS KMS, with the credentials of an aws login session. The aws features read aws login sessions, see Migrating from 0.30

azure-kms-encryption-native-tlsAzure with the platform TLS library
hashicorp-vault-transit-kms-encryptionHashiCorp Vault Transit, with the system root certificates and the aws-lc-rs crypto provider

ringthe ring backend, see Crypto backends
aws-lcthe aws-lc-rs backend
aws-lc-fipsthe aws-lc-rs backend on the AWS-LC FIPS module. Needs CMake and Go to build
rustcryptothe backend on the pure Rust RustCrypto crates, no C compiler needed. Adds p256 and ed25519-dalek for signatures, and rsa for RSA-OAEP, see Asymmetric KEKs
deterministicdeterministic encryption with AES-SIV, see Deterministic encryption
streamstreaming encryption of values of any length: StreamEncryptor, StreamDecryptor, the tokio adapters EncryptingWriter and DecryptingReader, the Stream adapters EncryptingStream and DecryptingStream, the stream_encryptor and stream_decryptor calls of AeadCipher and KmsAeadEnvelope, with their bare forms, and KmsAeadEnvelope::decrypting_stream and decrypting_reader, which read the header from the whole stored value. Turns on tokio’s io-util and brings in bytes and futures-core
legacy-formatsreads the layouts written by 0.29 and earlier. Deprecated, see Formats
postgresToSql and FromSql of postgres-types for Encrypted, as BYTEA, for tokio-postgres
sqlxsqlx’s PostgreSQL traits for Encrypted, as BYTEA, without a sqlx runtime or TLS stack
opentelemetryOpenTelemetry metrics on the API crate only (no SDK): the plaintext size of each value encrypted and decrypted, and the time each call, KMS request and keyring store read takes, see Metrics

The local KMS provider, LocalKmsProvider, is in every build and needs no feature.

Every build adds serde, serde_bytes, serde_json, secret-vault-value’s serde feature, sha2, base64ct and the RustCrypto crates der, spki, pkcs8, sec1 and pem-rfc7468. sha2 computes the thumbprint of a public key and, in kms-aead-keyring, the canonical digest of a record, where no backend is named. The RustCrypto crates parse and write the framing of keys and signatures: PEM, the text armour around base64 bytes; SubjectPublicKeyInfo, the DER structure of a public key, DER being a binary encoding; PKCS#8, the DER structure of a private key; and the DER encoding of an ECDSA signature. serde_json reads and writes JSON Web Keys (JWK), the JSON form of a key, so PublicKeyFormat::Jwk, encode(Jwk) and the JWK Set functions of VerifyingKeySet are in every build, as are JsonEncoded<T> and the serde implementations of the library’s types.

Feature rules

The crate refuses some feature combinations at compile time with compile_error!:

  • a provider feature without a backend feature fails with “kms-aead needs a crypto backend for its KMS providers: enable “ring”, “aws-lc”, “aws-lc-fips” or “rustcrypto”“;
  • HashiCorp Vault Transit needs exactly one of -tls-roots and -tls-webpki, and exactly one of -tls-aws-lc-rs and -tls-ring. hashicorp-vault-transit-kms-encryption already enables -tls-roots and -tls-aws-lc-rs, so combine the other variants with hashicorp-vault-transit-kms-encryption-base instead;
  • Azure needs exactly one of azure-kms-encryption-tls-aws-lc-rs (or azure-kms-encryption), azure-kms-encryption-tls-no-provider and azure-kms-encryption-native-tls.

The aws-lc-rs feature that aws-lc and aws-lc-fips enable is not a backend choice: enabled alone, the aws-lc-rs build fails. Use aws-lc or aws-lc-fips.

A build without any backend feature compiles, and LocalKmsProvider is in it, but nothing that encrypts can be constructed, since no type implements AeadBackend.

kms-aead-keyring

The crate has no default features.

FeatureWhat it adds
ring, aws-lc, aws-lc-fips, rustcryptothe crypto backends of kms-aead, see Crypto backends. A keyring names its backend as a type parameter, such as kms_aead::backends::Ring
adminKeyringAdmin, the registry’s create, open_or_create and admin, and the types of the administrative calls, Rotation, EncryptedKeyset, KeysetSelection and the encodings module, see Applications and administration

Beacons need no feature, and export_tink_keyset needs admin only. The KMS providers come from kms-aead and its provider features, and secret-vault-value’s SecretValue is what an application type’s Plaintext encodes to.

Formats

Every byte layout the library reads or writes is on this page.

The library writes one envelope format for every provider, Tink’s KMS envelope. A value encrypted by KmsAeadEnvelope::encrypt decrypts with Tink’s KmsEnvelopeAead on the same KMS key and algorithm, and a value Tink encrypts decrypts with KmsAeadEnvelope::decrypt.

LayoutTypeBytesRead byWritten by
Tink KMS envelopeEncrypted<T, Envelope>[w: u32 BE][encrypted DEK: w][nonce: 12][ciphertext][tag: 16]kms-aead, Tink0.30.0 and later
Bare valueEncrypted<T, Bare>[nonce: 12][ciphertext][tag: 16]kms-aead, Tinkevery release
Encrypted DEKEncryptedDataEncryptionKeythe provider’s bytes, see Encrypted DEKskms-aead, Tink for GCP and AWS keys0.30.0 and later
Serialised DEKthe plaintext of an encrypted DEK, but Azure’sTink’s key message, see Serialised DEKskms-aead, Tink0.30.0 and later
TINK-prefixed valueEncrypted<T, Randomized>, Encrypted<T, Deterministic>, named by a KeyId[0x01][key id: u32 BE][value]kms-aead, Tink, BigQuery0.30.0 and later, and kms-aead-keyring through it
Streamed valueStreamEncryptor and StreamDecryptor of AeadCipher::stream_encryptor and stream_decryptor, and of the keyring’s Encryptor::stream_encryptor and Keyring::stream_decryptorsee Streamed valueskms-aead with stream; a C2SP chunked-encryption reader from byte 50.31.0 and later, and kms-aead-keyring through it
Envelope streamed valueStreamEncryptor and StreamDecryptor of KmsAeadEnvelope::stream_encryptor and stream_decryptorsee Streamed valueskms-aead with stream; a C2SP chunked-encryption reader from byte 4 + w0.31.0 and later
Bare streamed valueStreamEncryptor and StreamDecryptor of the stream_encryptor_bare and stream_decryptor_bare calls of AeadCipher and KmsAeadEnvelopesee Streamed valueskms-aead with stream, any C2SP chunked-encryption reader0.31.0 and later

Attached signatureSigned<T>see Signatures and MACskms-aead0.31.0 and later

Envelope layout v1Encrypted<T, Envelope> with legacy-formats[0x01][k: u8][key version: k][w: u16 BE][encrypted DEK: w][cipher text]kms-aead with legacy-formats0.29

Raw encrypted DEKEncryptedDataEncryptionKeythe KMS ciphertext of the DEK’s 16 or 32 raw byteskms-aead with legacy-formatsup to 0.29

k = 0 records no key version. A key version is 1 to 255 ASCII alphanumeric characters. The Azure provider encrypts the raw DEK in every release, see Encrypted DEKs.

Tink’s KMS envelope

KmsAeadEnvelope::encrypt writes Tink’s KMS envelope, and Encrypted::join writes it from a bare value and an encrypted DEK. separate reads it back:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;
let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> =
    envelope.encrypt(b"aad", &SecretValue::from("secret")).await?;

let (bare, encrypted_dek) = encrypted.separate()?;
let joined = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::join(&bare, &encrypted_dek)?;
assert_eq!(joined.value(), encrypted.value());
Ok(())
}
  • w is 1 to 4,096 bytes, the limit of every Tink implementation, on write and on read;
  • the encrypted DEK is the provider’s EncryptedDataEncryptionKey, the KMS ciphertext of the serialised DEK, or of the raw DEK for Azure;
  • the AAD goes to the DEK’s AEAD only. The KMS encryption of the DEK has no AAD, as in Tink, so Tink’s AWS client sends no encryption context and its GCP client no additional authenticated data;
  • the rest is a Bare value.

The envelope records neither the algorithm nor the KEK version, so the writer and the reader, kms-aead or Tink, are configured with the same algorithm. Tink calls it the DEK template:

AlgorithmTink key typeTink for Java and GoTink for C++ and Python
ChaCha20-Poly1305ChaCha20Poly1305Keyreads and writesno
AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCMAesGcmKeyreads and writesreads and writes
AES-256-GCM-SIVAesGcmSivKeyreads and writesreads and writes

AES-256-GCM is the default of KmsAeadEnvelope::new, so every Tink implementation reads the values of an envelope that names no algorithm. The default of 0.29 and earlier was ChaCha20-Poly1305, see Migrating from 0.29.

Tink for Python reads a kms-aead value with the key URI of the provider’s key:

from tink import aead
from tink.integration import gcpkms

aead.register()
key_uri = 'gcp-kms://projects/p/locations/l/keyRings/r/cryptoKeys/k'
remote = gcpkms.GcpKmsClient(key_uri).get_aead(key_uri)
envelope = aead.KmsEnvelopeAead(aead.aead_key_templates.AES256_GCM, remote)
plain_text = envelope.decrypt(value, aad)

Checked on a Cloud KMS key and an AWS KMS key, both ways: AES-128-GCM, AES-256-GCM and AES-256-GCM-SIV with Tink 1.16.1 for Python, and ChaCha20-Poly1305 with Tink 1.24.0 for Java on the Cloud KMS key. tests/tink_interop.py and examples/tink_interop.rs repeat the Python check.

Encrypted DEKs

EncryptedDataEncryptionKey is the bytes the provider returned from an encryption, which are also the bytes to store and the bytes it decrypts. Each provider’s bytes name the KEK version that encrypted the DEK where the KMS needs one to decrypt:

ProviderBytesKey version
GCPthe Cloud KMS ciphertext, starting with 0x0ainside the ciphertext
AWSthe AWS KMS ciphertext blob, starting with 0x01not exposed
Azure[0x01][k: u8][key version: k][Key Vault encrypted DEK]in the layout
Vault Transitthe Transit ciphertext, vault:vN:…the N
local[0x01][i: u8][KEK id: i][KEK version: u32 BE][nonce: 12][encrypted DEK][tag: 16]in the layout
any, encrypted with a public key[0x02][a: u8][i: u8][KEK id: i][k: u8][KEK version: k][RSA block], see DEKs encrypted with a public keyin the layout
an application’s ownwhatever it returns

The Azure layout is the library’s own: Key Vault decrypts only with the key version that encrypted, and no Tink client exists for Azure. For the same reason, the Key Vault encrypted DEK is the KMS ciphertext of the raw DEK of 16, 32 or 64 bytes, as in 0.29, not of the serialised DEK: AES key wrap, A128KW to A256KW, takes only multiples of 8 bytes, which no serialised DEK is. The local KMS encrypts the DEK with AES-256-GCM under the KEK version, with its header up to the version as the AAD, so neither the id nor the version can be changed without the decryption failing.

The 0x02 layout is the same for every KMS: a producer holding the public half of an RSA key encrypts the DEK in its own process, and the provider of the KMS that holds the private half decrypts it. The providers of 0.31 read it beside their own bytes, and no earlier release reads it, see DEKs encrypted with a public key and Asymmetric KEKs.

Store value() or to_hex_string(), and read them back with EncryptedDataEncryptionKey::from or try_from_hex_string. Neither holds the KEK version of kek_version(), see Storing encrypted DEKs.

Serialised DEKs

The plaintext a provider encrypts is the DEK serialised as Tink serialises a key, the message of its Tink key type with version 0, which protobuf leaves out. The Azure provider encrypts the raw DEK instead, see Encrypted DEKs:

AlgorithmMessageBytes
ChaCha20-Poly1305ChaCha20Poly1305Key[0x12][0x20][key: 32]
AES-128-GCMAesGcmKey[0x1a][0x10][key: 16]
AES-256-GCMAesGcmKey[0x1a][0x20][key: 32]
AES-256-GCM-SIVAesGcmSivKey[0x1a][0x20][key: 32]
AES-256-SIV, deterministicAesSivKey[0x12][0x40][key: 64]
HMAC-SHA256, MAC keyHmacKey[0x1a][0x20][key: 32]
HMAC-SHA512, MAC keyHmacKey[0x1a][0x40][key: 64]

A beacon key of HMAC-SHA256 is serialised as [0x1a][0x20][key: 32], its 32 bytes in key_value, field 3, as in Tink’s HmacPrfKey without its parameters: the bytes of a 32-byte AesGcmKey. It is encrypted as a DEK is, raw under an Azure key. A MAC key of a keyring is serialised the same way, in field 3 and never in field 2, since [0x12][0x40][key: 64] is byte for byte an AES-SIV key, see MACs in a keyring.

KmsAeadEnvelope::generate_encrypted_dek, generate_encrypted_deterministic_dek and the generate_encrypted_dek of both ciphers and of BeaconMac encrypt their keys the same way. The message does not name its type, and AES-256-GCM and AES-256-GCM-SIV keys are written alike, so the algorithm of the reader decides how the key is used, as in Tink.

A decrypted DEK that is no serialised Tink key, or under an Azure key no raw DEK, fails with INVALID_KEY_FORMAT. An encrypted Tink keyset decrypts under the same KEK too, and this is what keeps it out of the DEK cache.

Tink keysets and the TINK prefix

Two items describe the TINK-prefixed values:

  • kms_aead::KeyId: the key id of Tink’s output prefix of type TINK, [0x01][key id: u32 BE], the start of every Randomized and Deterministic value and of every beacon. It picks the key that decrypts the value, and Tink and BigQuery pick the key of a keyset by it. It is not authenticated;
  • kms_aead::formats::TinkKeyset: a serialised google.crypto.tink.Keyset of enabled, symmetric, TINK-prefixed keys, the bytes tinkey writes for the same keys. Encrypted by a Cloud KMS key, it is the keyset of BigQuery’s KEYS.KEYSET_CHAIN: an application writes it for its one key, see One fixed key, and a keyring exports it for its versions.

TinkKeyset::into_encrypted_bytes writes the keyset encrypted by a KMS key, as Tink writes an encrypted keyset: a google.crypto.tink.EncryptedKeyset whose encrypted_keyset, field 2, is the KMS ciphertext of the serialised keyset, encrypted with no associated data. Tink’s binary keyset writers leave out keyset_info, field 3, and so does the library. Tink’s KeysetHandle.read reads it with the KMS client of KekRef::tink_key_uri, which Tink has for GCP and AWS keys, and Tink for Go for Vault Transit keys too. A keyring exports it for its versions, see Exporting a keyset to Tink.

Formats of Encrypted

Every value the library encrypts is an Encrypted<T, F>: the bytes of one layout, named by its format F from kms_aead::formats. Encrypted::try_from and Encrypted::try_from_hex_string check stored bytes against it:

FormatLayoutShortest valuekey_id()Written by
EnvelopeTink’s KMS envelopean encrypted DEK of 1 to 4,096 bytes, then 28 bytesnoneKmsAeadEnvelope::encrypt, Encrypted::join
Bare[nonce: 12][ciphertext][tag: 16]28 bytesnoneAeadCipher::encrypt_bare, KmsAeadEnvelope::encrypt_with_dek and encrypt_with_encrypted_dek, Encrypted::separate
Randomized[0x01][key id: u32 BE][nonce: 12][ciphertext][tag: 16]33 bytesKeyIdAeadCipher::encrypt, and kms-aead-keyring through it
Deterministic[0x01][key id: u32 BE][synthetic IV: 16][ciphertext]21 bytesKeyIdDeterministicCipher::encrypt, and kms-aead-keyring through it

A Bare value is Tink’s AEAD output under a key with the output prefix RAW, and the part of every Envelope value after the encrypted DEK. It names no key: the application keeps track of the key that decrypts it.

The check reads the framing only, and whether the bytes are authentic is known when they are decrypted. With legacy-formats, an Envelope value also holds the envelope layout v1 of 0.29 and the length-prefixed envelope, checked by their lengths, so a column decodes its old rows into the same type until they are upgraded, see Upgrading stored values. Without the feature, Encrypted::try_from refuses them with LEGACY_FORMAT. separate refuses them in every build. The layout of 32-bit targets before 0.24.0 has Tink’s framing, so it reads as an Envelope value in every build, and KmsAeadEnvelope::decrypt decrypts it with legacy-formats.

The plaintext inside is the encoding of the value’s Plaintext type: UTF-8 for String, the bytes themselves for Vec<u8> and SecretValue, true or false for bool, decimal ASCII for the integers, and compact JSON for JsonEncoded<T>. See Stored values.

Beacons

A Beacon<T> is a prefix and a truncated HMAC-SHA256:

[0x01][key id: u32 BE][HMAC-SHA256: the first ceil(bits / 8) bytes]
  • 0x01 is the version of the beacon layout. A later layout takes another first byte, so that beacons of both layouts can share a column during a re-index;
  • the key id names the beacon key that calculated the beacon;
  • the HMAC keeps its first bits bits, and the unused low bits of the last byte are zero. A beacon is 6 to 37 bytes.

The HMAC covers the bits, the context and the plaintext, as the Plaintext type encodes it:

HMAC-SHA256(key, [bits: u16 BE][context length: u32 BE][context][plaintext])

so that beacons of two fields, or of two bit counts, never match. Beacon::try_from checks the prefix and that 1 to 32 bytes of HMAC follow, and fails with INVALID_BEACON_FORMAT otherwise.

Keyring key ids

kms-aead-keyring writes the key id of a version into the TINK prefix of every value and beacon under it. The two high bits of the id are the purpose, and the rest is the ordinal:

BitsMeaning
31set for deterministic versions, alone
30set for beacon versions, alone
31 and 30set for MAC versions
0 to 29the ordinal, from 1, counted per purpose

So the ids of each purpose are:

  • randomised: 1, 2, 3, …;
  • deterministic: 2147483649, 2147483650, …;
  • beacon: 1073741825, 1073741826, …;
  • MAC: 3221225473, 3221225474, ….

An id whose ordinal is 0 is reserved: 0, 2^30, 2^31 and 2^31 + 2^30. A value that carries one fails with ReservedKeyId. A value whose id is of another purpose than the call fails with KeyPurposeMismatch. Both fail before any store or KMS call. Ids are never reused: a destroyed version keeps its id in the record. A key id goes up to 2^32 - 1, so a SQL column holding one is a BIGINT.

Keyring records

kms-aead-keyring keeps a keyring record as one JSON document, the same in every keyring store. A record with deterministic and beacon versions, after one randomised rotation and with another one staged, with the encrypted DEKs shortened:

      "created_at": "2026-10-18T12:18:31.054603074Z",
      "state_changed_at": "2026-10-18T12:18:31.054603074Z",
      "encrypted_dek": "AQp0ZW5hbnQta2VrAAAAAWVYn2R7AWay...",
      "kek_version": "1"
    }
  ],
  "created_at": "2026-10-18T12:18:31.053483320Z",
  "updated_at": "2026-10-18T12:18:31.054603074Z"
}
  • schema is 1;
  • kek names the provider and the KEK the keyring was created with;
  • algorithms holds the algorithm new versions of each purpose use. deterministic, beacon and mac are null for a keyring without versions of that purpose. mac is mac-hmac-sha256 or mac-hmac-sha512, distinct from the beacon’s hmac-sha256, since the algorithm name fixes a version’s purpose;
  • signature is an optional top-level member, absent from the record above: "signature": "<standard base64 of the detached Signature bytes>", written where the administration signs its records, see The signed keyring record. A reader of 0.30 ignores it;
  • seal_epoch is the record’s re-encryption epoch: it counts the writes that made a version active, from 1 at the creation, see Encryption markers;
  • versions holds every version ever created, in creation order, each under its key id;
  • state is pending, active, decrypt_only, retired or destroyed. For a pending version, state_changed_at is the time it was staged;
  • compromised is null, or {"at": "<time>", "note": "<the operator's note, or null>"};
  • seal_epochs is the version’s re-encryption range as epochs, {"start": <epoch>, "end": <epoch or null>}: the epoch of the write that made it active, and the epoch of the first write after it stopped being active that moved the epoch on, null until then. It is null for a version that was never active, see Encryption markers;
  • encrypted_dek is the standard base64 of the encrypted DEK, the bytes the provider returned, here the local KMS layout. It is null for a destroyed version;
  • kek_version is the version of the KEK that encrypted encrypted_dek, as the KMS reported it, here the local KEK version. It is null for a destroyed version, and under a KMS that reports none, such as AWS KMS;
  • the times are RFC 3339, in UTC.

A reader parses at most 1 MiB of JSON. The size of a version is mostly its encrypted DEK, so the number of versions that fit depends on the KMS:

KEKBytes per versionVersions in 1 MiB
local KMS359about 2,900
Vault Transit380about 2,750
GCP Cloud KMS412about 2,550
AWS KMS504about 2,050
Azure, RSA 2048-bit key674about 1,550
Azure, RSA 4096-bit key1,014about 1,050
Cloud KMS, RSA 2048-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)608about 1,700
Cloud KMS, RSA 3072-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)776about 1,350
Cloud KMS, RSA 4096-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)948about 1,100
AWS KMS, RSA 2048-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)602about 1,750
AWS KMS, RSA 4096-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)942about 1,100
Azure, RSA 2048-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)678about 1,550
Azure, RSA 4096-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)1,018about 1,000
Vault Transit, RSA 2048-bit key, public-key encryption (computed)608about 1,700
local KMS, RSA 2048-bit key, 10-byte KEK id, public-key encryption (computed)619about 1,700

The local KMS row was measured on a record the crate wrote after 500 randomised rotations, each version AES-256-GCM, decrypt-only with a re-encryption range. The other rows are the same record with each encrypted DEK replaced by one of that KMS’s length, and a KEK version of that KMS’s form: none for AWS, two digits for GCP and Vault, 32 hex digits for Azure. The rows marked computed were not measured: they are the same record with the encrypted DEK of the 0x02 layout, the header and one RSA block, see DEKs encrypted with a public key.

A reader fails with RecordInvalid on a document that breaks any of:

  • at most one active and one pending version per purpose, neither of them compromised;
  • an encrypted DEK on every version but the destroyed ones, and a kek_version, when there is one, of 1 to 255 ASCII alphanumerics beside it;
  • unique key ids of the purpose of their algorithm, increasing within a purpose in creation order;
  • no two versions with the same encrypted_dek bytes (DuplicateEncryptedDek), so that a key filed under a second id is refused where the record is read;
  • a seal_epoch of 1 or more;
  • seal_epochs on every active, decrypt-only and retired version and on no pending one, open on the active version, starting at 1 or more and below its end, and within the seal_epoch.

Encryption markers

An encryption marker is the re-encryption epoch of the keyring record a write encrypted a record’s fields under, which the application stores beside them:

[0x01][re-encryption epoch: u32 BE]

5 bytes in all, so that markers compare as their epochs do, and so do their bytes as Firestore, PostgreSQL and BigQuery compare them. EncryptionMarker::ENVELOPE, 01 00 00 00 00, is epoch 0, the marker of a record of envelope values. EncryptionMarker::try_from fails with InvalidEncryptionMarker on bytes of any other length or first byte. Serde stores a marker as bytes, an array of numbers in serde_json, and the postgres and sqlx features as BYTEA. See Sweeps for the query it serves.

The record’s JSON members for the epoch and the ranges keep the names seal_epoch and seal_epochs. KeyVersion::reencrypt_range() returns the markers of a version’s seal_epochs as a ReencryptRange: start(), and end(), None while the range is open. A record whose fields were encrypted through one Encryptor, and which stores the marker of the encryptor’s record, lies in the range of every version its fields are under. See Records under a version for the query it serves.

DEKs encrypted with a public key

An encrypted DEK that a public key encrypted has one layout for every KMS:

[0x02][a: u8][i: u8][KEK id: i bytes][k: u8][KEK version: k ASCII alphanumerics][RSA block]
  • 0x02 is the layout. No encrypted DEK of a built-in provider starts with it before 0.31, see Telling layouts apart;
  • a is the algorithm: 0x01 for RSA-OAEP with SHA-256 as the OAEP hash and the MGF1 hash, and an empty label. A later algorithm is another value;
  • i and the KEK id are the local KEK’s LocalKekId. i is 0 for a cloud KEK;
  • k and the KEK version are the version the public key carried, as the reader names it to its KMS: 1 to 255 ASCII alphanumerics. k is 0 under AWS KMS, which has no versions, and the version is the decimal LocalKekVersion under the local KMS;
  • the RSA block is as long as the modulus: 256, 384 or 512 bytes for a key of 2048, 3072 or 4096 bits. The encrypted DEK is the header and the block, 260 bytes under AWS KMS with a 2048-bit key.

The block is the OAEP encryption of the DEK in the form its KEK’s provider encrypts it: the serialised DEK, or the raw DEK under Key Vault. The seed of OAEP is random, so two encryptions of one DEK differ.

A reader checks the header against its own shape before any KMS call, and refuses the encrypted DEK with ASYMMETRIC_ENCRYPTED_DEK_FORMAT otherwise: a known algorithm byte; a header within the bytes; i of 0 for a cloud KEK; k of 0 under AWS KMS and 1 or more elsewhere; a block of 256, 384 or 512 bytes. Nothing in the header is authenticated: a changed version or KEK id names a key that fails to decrypt the block, and no change decrypts a value.

How each KMS decrypts the block:

KMSRequest
Google Cloud KMSAsymmetricDecrypt on <key>/cryptoKeyVersions/<version>, ciphertext the block
AWS KMSDecrypt with KeyId the key, EncryptionAlgorithm RSAES_OAEP_SHA_256 and CiphertextBlob the block
Azure Key VaultunwrapKey on the key and the version with alg RSA-OAEP-256
Vault Transitdecrypt on the key with ciphertext vault:v<version>: and the standard base64 of the block, padding_scheme oaep and hash_algorithm sha2-256
Local KMSRSA-OAEP decryption with the version’s private key

None of the four KMS decryptions takes a label or an AAD. What binds a value to its place is its own AAD.

Streamed values

A streamed value is a header and chunks. Every length is in bytes and integers are big-endian. The prefixed form, the one of AeadCipher::stream_encryptor and of the keyring:

offset          len     field
     0            1     0x01, Tink's output prefix of type TINK
     1            4     key id, u32: the keyring version that encrypted the value, or the caller's KeyId
     5           24     salt, random per value
    29           32     key commitment
    61       16,400     chunk 0: 16,384 bytes of ciphertext and a 16-byte tag
    61 + k × 16,400     chunk k

The bare form is a Cobblestone-256 message as C2SP chunked encryption writes it, with the caller’s AAD as its context and the DEK as its input key:

offset          len     field
     0           24     salt
    24           32     key commitment
    56       16,400     chunk 0
    56 + k × 16,400     chunk k

The envelope form, the one KmsAeadEnvelope::stream_encryptor writes, puts the encrypted DEK in front of the bare form, as Tink’s KMS envelope puts it in front of a bare value:

offset          len     field
     0            4     w, u32: the length of the encrypted DEK, 1 to 4,096
     4            w     encrypted DEK, the provider's EncryptedDataEncryptionKey
 4 + w           24     salt
28 + w           32     key commitment
60 + w       16,400     chunk 0
60 + w + k × 16,400     chunk k
  • the header H is 61 bytes in the prefixed form, 56 in the bare form and 60 + w in the envelope form. From the Cobblestone header on, the forms are the same;
  • every chunk but the last is 16,400 bytes as stored. The last chunk is 16 to 16,399 bytes, the tag and 0 to 16,383 bytes of ciphertext, so a plaintext of a multiple of 16,384 bytes ends with a tag alone. An empty plaintext is the header and one tag, 77 bytes prefixed, 72 bare and 76 + w in the envelope form. There is one chunking for each length;
  • only the first byte of a prefixed header is checked when it is read. The key id is not authenticated: it names the key that decrypts the value;
  • a reader of the envelope form reads w from the first 4 bytes, refuses a w outside 1 to 4,096 before it fetches or allocates anything, then reads the 60 + w bytes of the header. The encrypted DEK is not authenticated either: another value’s encrypted DEK decrypts to another DEK, which fails the key commitment;
  • no byte tells the bare form from the others, since a bare value starts with its random salt. The call names the form.

The keys come from the DEK K of 32 bytes, the salt and the AAD A:

key || base nonce || commitment = HKDF-Expand(SHA-512, PRK = K,
    info = "c2sp.org/chunked-encryption@v1+" || "AEAD_AES_256_GCM" || 0x00 || salt || A,
    length = 32 + 12 + 32)

With a 32-byte PRK, HKDF-Expand is two HMAC-SHA-512 calls: T1 = HMAC(K, info || 0x01) gives the key (T1[0..32]), the base nonce (T1[32..44]) and commitment[0..20] (T1[44..64]), and T2 = HMAC(K, T1 || info || 0x02) gives commitment[20..32] (T2[0..12]). A reader derives all three and compares the commitment in constant time before it decrypts a chunk. The AAD is bound here and nowhere else: each chunk’s GCM associated data is empty.

Chunk k holds plaintext bytes [k × 16,384, (k + 1) × 16,384) and is AES-256-GCM(key, N_k, aad = "", M_k), with the nonce N_k = base nonce XOR k, k as a 96-bit big-endian integer. There is no final flag: the final chunk is the one that is shorter than a full one.

For a plaintext of L bytes the stored length is Z = H + L + 16 × n with n = L div 16,384 + 1 chunks. From a stored length Z, with body = Z - H:

Z < H + 16          refused: shorter than the header and one tag
n = body div 16,400 + 1
r = body - (n - 1) × 16,400     the final chunk as stored
r < 16              refused: r = 0 is a value cut at a chunk boundary, 1 to 15 no encryptor writes
n > 2^38            refused
L = body - 16 × n

A value holds at most 2^38 chunks, 4 PiB less one byte of plaintext. The refusals are STREAM_LENGTH. Z is what the store reports: until the final chunk has been decrypted, L is the store’s word. A reader that reads the value to its end needs no Z: the bytes after the last full chunk are the final chunk, and the rules above hold for the length it read.

For a plaintext range [a, b) with a <= b <= L, the chunks to decrypt and the bytes to fetch are below. A range open at the start has a = 0 and one open at the end has b = L, so .. is [0, L):

first = a div 16,384
end   = n                      if b == L: the final chunk, even when it holds no plaintext
        first                  if a == b < L
        (b - 1) div 16,384 + 1 otherwise
ciphertext = [H + first × 16,400, min(Z, H + end × 16,400))

The reader cuts the decrypted plaintext to [a - first × 16,384, b - first × 16,384). For a = 1,000,000 and b = 1,064,512 the chunks are 61..65 and the bytes [1,000,461, 1,066,061) prefixed or [1,000,456, 1,066,056) bare, and 4 + w bytes further in the envelope form.

The two shortest vectors of the C2SP test file as prefixed values under key id 7, with the key 59454c4c4f57205355424d4152494e452059454c4c4f57205355424d4152494e and an empty AAD:

vector 1, an empty plaintext, 77 bytes:
0100000007 43a2e39431cbae0389d3fc86adf46f7c3a24de88d9311617
e53f3164cc1d5dc738a66b97f0bf786565c31799c2f463d50a7e10046011dc6f
c3c939e3a5db7affb6191551669554fb

vector 2, a plaintext of 7 bytes, 84 bytes:
0100000007 a1a1b56f6db58cd1ede0c5c9db62aef20d3f96a6c594bb92
52bb38122de91236502b42a1d7fcab820ffa292940d2f86add212cfa42c38df1
9d3cc9139ee61a5990d3db57433514229beea49a8a72bd

The lines are the prefix and the salt, the commitment, and the final chunk. In the bare form the vectors’ ct are the stored values as they are.

Vector 2 in the envelope form, with the 7 bytes wrapped as the encrypted DEK, 90 bytes:

0000000777726170706564 a1a1b56f6db58cd1ede0c5c9db62aef20d3f96a6c594bb92
52bb38122de91236502b42a1d7fcab820ffa292940d2f86add212cfa42c38df1
9d3cc9139ee61a5990d3db57433514229beea49a8a72bd

The first line is w, the encrypted DEK and the salt. A real encrypted DEK is the provider’s, see Encrypted DEKs. Its bytes play no part in the key derivation, which takes the DEK.

Signatures and MACs

Detached and attached signatures and MACs are Tink-prefixed values:

LayoutTypeBytesLength
Detached signatureSignature<T>see Signatures and MACskms-aead
Attached signatureSigned<T>see Signatures and MACskms-aead
Detached MACMac<T>see Signatures and MACskms-aead, and kms-aead-keyring for its MAC versions
Attached MACWithMac<T>see Signatures and MACskms-aead, and kms-aead-keyring for its MAC versions
  • 0x01 is the version of the layout. A later layout takes another first byte;
  • the key id names the key that signed or computed the tag. It is not authenticated and is a hint only: a value whose id is changed verifies under no key;
  • the signature is the raw signature of the key’s algorithm: 64 bytes for Ed25519 (RFC 8032) and r || s with each scalar left-padded to 32 bytes for P-256. The algorithm is not in the value: the verifying key fixes it;
  • the tag is the full HMAC output, never truncated: 32 bytes under HMAC-SHA256, 64 under HMAC-SHA512. The key version’s algorithm fixes the length;
  • w is the signature or tag length, 1 to 65,535, so a reader finds the payload at offset 7 + w without knowing the key.

TryFrom<Vec<u8>> checks a value against its layout and fails with INVALID_SIGNATURE_FORMAT or INVALID_MAC_FORMAT.

Every signature and every MAC is computed over the pre-image, never over the payload itself. It is the Pre-Authentication Encoding (PAE) of DSSE, the Dead Simple Signing Envelope, with the SHA-256 of the payload as its body:

pre-image = "DSSEv1" SP DEC(len(type)) SP type SP "32" SP SHA-256(payload)
  • SP is one byte 0x20;
  • DEC(n) is n in ASCII decimal with no leading zeros;
  • type is the payload type: 1 to 255 bytes of visible ASCII, 0x21 to 0x7E, such as application/vnd.example.invoice+json;
  • SHA-256(payload) is the 32-byte digest of the payload bytes.

The pre-image is 46 to 302 bytes long. Ed25519 signs it as it is, and ECDSA P-256 signs its SHA-256. A MAC is the HMAC of the pre-image. The payload is the bytes Signable::to_payload returns, which the library neither parses nor re-serialises.

The key id of a signing key is derived from its public key:

thumbprint input = {"crv":"Ed25519","kty":"OKP","x":"<base64url of the 32 bytes>"}
                 | {"crv":"P-256","kty":"EC","x":"<base64url of x>","y":"<base64url of y>"}
digest           = SHA-256(thumbprint input)
kid              = base64url(digest), without padding, 43 characters
key id           = the first four bytes of digest, as a big-endian u32

The thumbprint input is the JWK thumbprint of RFC 7638: the required members of the key in lexicographic order, with no whitespace. A publisher with a numbering of its own sets the id instead, and the published key carries it as the integer member kms_aead_key_id. The key id of a MAC key is the keyring’s id of the version, see Keyring key ids.

A set of public keys is a JWK Set (RFC 7517). Each key has kty, crv, x (and y), kid the full thumbprint, use sig and alg EdDSA or ES256, and kms_aead_key_id where the publisher set the id:

{"keys":[
  {"kty":"OKP","crv":"Ed25519","x":"11qYAYKxCrfVS_7TyWQHOg7hcvPapiMlrwIaaPcHURo",
   "kid":"kPrK_qmxVWaYVA9wwBF6Iuo3vVzz7TxHCTwXBygrS4k","use":"sig","alg":"EdDSA"},
  {"kty":"EC","crv":"P-256","x":"YP7UuiVanTHJYet0xjVtaMBJuJI7Yfps5mliLmDyn7Y",
   "y":"eQP-EAi4vJmkGunpVii8ZPLxsgwtfp9Rd6PClNRGIpk",
   "kid":"DOvxvJiAdIqVWIkFt5hDtCunXLF0BV4-JGv4f-ALSm0","use":"sig","alg":"ES256"}
]}

The signed keyring record

A keyring record carries its signature as the top-level member signature, the standard base64 of a detached Signature over the record’s canonical document:

canonical = JCS(record document without its "signature" member)
pre-image = PAE("application/vnd.kms-aead.keyring-record+json", SHA-256(canonical))

JCS is the JSON Canonicalization Scheme of RFC 8785: members sorted by name, no whitespace, standard string escaping with non-ASCII kept as UTF-8 and control characters as \u00XX. It is computed over the parsed JSON document with every member kept, including members this release does not know, so a later release may add members and sign records that carry them. A record holds strings, integers, booleans, null, arrays and objects; a fraction, an exponent or an integer beyond 2^53 anywhere in it is RECORD_INVALID. The canonical document of a record with a null, a nested compromised, a note holding é and the control character 0x07, and a member future this release does not define, on one line of 579 bytes without the line breaks shown here:

{"algorithms":{"beacon":null,"deterministic":null,"mac":null,"randomized":"aes-256-gcm"},
"created_at":"2026-10-25T09:00:00Z","future":{"a":[true,null],"b":1},"id":"tenant-42",
"kek":{"key":"tenant-kek","provider":"local"},"schema":1,"seal_epoch":1,
"updated_at":"2026-10-25T09:00:00Z","versions":[{"algorithm":"aes-256-gcm",
"compromised":{"at":"2026-10-25T09:00:00Z","note":"clé \u0007"},
"created_at":"2026-10-25T09:00:00Z","encrypted_dek":"AQ==","kek_version":"1","key_id":1,
"seal_epochs":{"end":null,"start":1},"state":"decrypt_only",
"state_changed_at":"2026-10-25T09:00:00Z"}]}

Its SHA-256 is 195ad529d5ec1beec7f6d806169d598723bf067c6abd6b522ab330310f2d79d2, with é as the two bytes c3 a9. The pre-image is DSSEv1 44 application/vnd.kms-aead.keyring-record+json 32 and that digest, 90 bytes. Under the Ed25519 key of the vectors below the stored member is "signature": "AZD6yv6RAlxJsp1uzsdIQxlvyawlD282Dfrtg3QthYeSPnQ02B6jbpOBFA65Y2Zq5lILiXyyTCxj9bv36JGSWC2H2UAG".

Telling layouts apart

The first bytes of a stored value:

First bytesLayout
00 00 00 00the length-prefixed envelope of 0.28.1 and earlier
00, then a length of 1 to 4,096Tink’s KMS envelope, an envelope streamed value, or the 4-byte length prefix of 32-bit targets before 0.24.0
01envelope layout v1 of 0.29, or a TINK-prefixed value
02a DEK encrypted with the public half of an asymmetric KEK
FF 01encrypted DEK layout v1 of 0.29

Only the decrypted DEK tells a Tink envelope from the 32-bit layout before 0.24.0: a raw key of 16 or 32 bytes against a serialised Tink key, whose lengths differ. An encrypted DEK of AWS, Azure or the local KMS also starts with 0x01, and Tink’s LEGACY and CRUNCHY output prefixes with 0x00.

A column that holds two layouts:

  • Tink envelopes and TINK-prefixed values: the first byte, 0x00 or 0x01;
  • Tink envelopes and 0.29 envelopes: the first byte, 0x00 or 0x01;
  • Tink envelopes and 0.28.1 envelopes: the first four bytes;
  • 0.29 envelopes and TINK-prefixed values: no byte tells them apart, so the layout is recorded outside the value, such as in another column;
  • bare values and any other layout: a bare value has no framing, and its random nonce can start with any byte, so a column of bare values holds no other layout;
  • streamed values and bare streamed values: no byte tells them apart, since a bare one starts with its random salt, so the call names the form, as decrypt_bare does for a bare value, see Streamed values;
  • envelope streamed values and Tink envelopes: both start with [w][encrypted DEK], so the layout is recorded outside the value, as for bare values;
  • encrypted DEKs of 0.29 and of 0.30: 0xFF 0x01 starts no encrypted DEK of a built-in provider, so the library tells them apart for those. It never inspects the bytes of an application’s own provider: strip the header [0xFF][0x01][k][key version] of its 0.29 keys before reading them.

Values written by 0.29 and earlier

The layouts of 0.29 and earlier are read only with the deprecated legacy-formats feature:

  • KmsAeadEnvelope::decrypt reads the three envelope layouts, which Encrypted<T, Envelope> holds with the feature;
  • the *_with_encrypted_dek calls and the DEK cache read the encrypted DEK layout v1 of 0.29 and raw encrypted DEKs.

Encrypted::separate never reads the older envelope layouts: for Azure, the encrypted DEK they hold needs the provider to become its bytes.

Without the feature, reading them fails with KmsAeadError::LegacyFormat, the code LEGACY_FORMAT, and one of:

  • KmsAeadLegacyFormat::EnvelopeV1;
  • KmsAeadLegacyFormat::LengthPrefixedEnvelope;
  • KmsAeadLegacyFormat::EncryptedDekV1;
  • KmsAeadLegacyFormat::RawEncryptedDek, for an encrypted DEK that decrypts to the raw key. The values of 32-bit targets before 0.24.0 fail with it, since their framing is Tink’s.

The error never echoes the bytes read. With the feature, the first read of each layout in a process logs one WARN event that names the upgrade methods. Without it, the KMS AEAD decrypt spans of such values record error_code=LEGACY_FORMAT, see Observability.

Upgrading stored values

With legacy-formats, two methods of KmsAeadEnvelope rewrite a stored value in Tink’s format, and return None when it already is in it, so a sweep writes only the rows that change:

  • upgrade_legacy_value, for envelope values;
  • upgrade_legacy_encrypted_dek, for encrypted DEKs stored apart, such as the bytes of 0.29’s to_bytes.

Each decrypts the DEK with the provider, without the DEK cache, and encrypts it again as a serialised Tink key when it was in an older layout: one KMS call for a current value, two for an old one. A value in Tink’s framing needs the decryption to be told from the 32-bit layout before 0.24.0. The AEAD bytes stay as they are.

An upgraded DEK is encrypted under the KEK’s current version, so the upgrade also does what reencrypt_dek and reencrypt_encrypted_dek do for a value or a DEK already in Tink’s format, see Re-encrypting DEKs after a KEK rotation. Those two refuse a layout of 0.29 or earlier with LEGACY_FORMAT, so a sweep after a KEK rotation upgrades a value in an old layout and re-encrypts the DEK of a value in Tink’s format.

The envelope’s algorithm is the one the values were encrypted with: ChaCha20-Poly1305 for values that 0.29 and earlier encrypted under their default. A raw DEK does not record its algorithm, and the encrypted DEK of the upgrade records it as a Tink key type, so upgrade_legacy_value takes the value’s AAD and decrypts the value before it writes anything. Under another algorithm or another AAD it fails with DECRYPT. upgrade_legacy_encrypted_dek has no value to decrypt, so it records the envelope’s algorithm for any key of its length. Be aware not to upgrade a DEK stored apart under another algorithm than its values were encrypted with: use upgrade_legacy_value wherever the values are at hand.

A sweep over stored rows:

use kms_aead::backends::AeadBackend;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, KmsAeadDecryptionProvider, KmsAeadEnvelope, KmsAeadResult};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

/// A stored envelope value and the AAD it was encrypted under.
struct Row {
    aad: Vec<u8>,
    value: Vec<u8>,
}

/// Rewrites the envelope values of `rows` in Tink's format, and returns how many changed.
#[allow(deprecated)]
async fn upgrade_values<P, B>(
    envelope: &KmsAeadEnvelope<P, B>,
    rows: &mut [Row],
) -> KmsAeadResult<usize>
where
    P: KmsAeadDecryptionProvider,
    B: AeadBackend,
{
    let mut upgraded = 0;
    for row in rows.iter_mut() {
        let stored = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::try_from(row.value.clone())?;
        if let Some(value) = envelope.upgrade_legacy_value(&row.aad, &stored).await? {
            row.value = value.into_value();
            upgraded += 1;
        }
    }
    Ok(upgraded)
}
use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher, KmsAeadEncryptionProvider, KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions};
#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> KmsAeadResult<()> {
let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
// A value as 0.28.1 wrote it: [w: u64 BE][encrypted raw key][cipher text]
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::ChaCha20Poly1305)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;
let bare = cipher.encrypt_bare(b"aad", &SecretValue::from("secret"), &dek)?;
let encrypted_dek = provider.encrypt_data_encryption_key(&dek).await?;
let mut legacy = u64::try_from(encrypted_dek.value().len()).unwrap().to_be_bytes().to_vec();
legacy.extend_from_slice(encrypted_dek.value());
legacy.extend_from_slice(bare.value());
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::with_options(
    provider,
    KmsAeadEnvelopeOptions::new().with_algorithm(AeadAlgorithm::ChaCha20Poly1305),
)?;
let mut rows = vec![Row { aad: b"aad".to_vec(), value: legacy }];
assert_eq!(upgrade_values(&envelope, &mut rows).await?, 1);
assert_eq!(upgrade_values(&envelope, &mut rows).await?, 0);
let upgraded = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Envelope>::try_from(rows[0].value.clone())?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt(b"aad", &upgraded).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("secret"));
Ok(())
}

Upgrade every reader to 0.30 before the sweep writes: 0.29 and earlier cannot read Tink’s format.

Deprecation

Reading the layouts of 0.29 and earlier is deprecated since 0.30.0 and will be removed in a later breaking release, together with the legacy-formats feature and the upgrade methods, which carry #[deprecated] from their first release, so every call site warns. Without the feature, the older layouts keep failing with LEGACY_FORMAT.

Error handling

Every fallible call returns KmsAeadResult<T>, whose error is kms_aead::errors::KmsAeadError:

  • SystemError: the configuration or the environment, such as invalid provider options or a KMS client that cannot be built, and the caller’s own source or sink of a stream failing (STREAM_IO);
  • NetworkError: a KMS that could not be reached, did not answer in time, failed on its side or limited the rate of requests. The same call may succeed later;
  • EncryptionError: encrypting or decrypting a value or a DEK failed, a stored value could not be read, or a KMS refused the request, such as a denied permission or a disabled key. The same call fails the same way again;
  • UnsupportedAlgorithm: the backend does not implement the algorithm, from constructors only;
  • SignatureError: a signature could not be made, read or verified, or a KMS refused a sign or public-key request; never retried;
  • LegacyFormat: a stored value in a layout of 0.29 or earlier, which only the legacy-formats feature reads, see Formats.

The enum is #[non_exhaustive], so a match on it needs a _ arm.

Codes

code() returns a code to match on: one of the library’s own codes, or the code the KMS returned, such as AWS’s IncorrectKeyException. It is also the error_code field of the spans.

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::errors::KmsAeadError;
use kms_aead::formats::Bare;
use kms_aead::{AeadAlgorithm, AeadCipher, Encrypted};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;

fn main() -> kms_aead::KmsAeadResult<()> {
let cipher = AeadCipher::<Ring>::new(AeadAlgorithm::Aes256Gcm)?;
let dek = cipher.generate_dek()?;
let cipher_text: Encrypted<SecretValue, Bare> =
    cipher.encrypt_bare(b"orders/1042", &SecretValue::from("secret"), &dek)?;

match cipher.decrypt_bare(b"orders/1043", &cipher_text, &dek) {
    Err(KmsAeadError::EncryptionError(error)) if error.public.code == "DECRYPT" => {
        // Not authentic under this key and AAD
    }
    other => panic!("{other:?}"),
}

let too_short = Encrypted::<SecretValue, Bare>::try_from(vec![0; 8]);
assert!(matches!(&too_short, Err(e) if e.code() == "INVALID_CIPHER_TEXT_FORMAT"));
Ok(())
}

The KMS codes are the gRPC status codes of Google Cloud KMS, such as PermissionDenied, the AWS error codes, such as NotFoundException, and Key Vault’s error codes, such as KeyNotFound. A sign or public-key request that fails with a network code is a NetworkError, and with any other KMS code a SignatureError that keeps the KMS’s code.

Retries

The library retries nothing itself. The AWS and Azure SDKs apply their own retry policies, which the Azure provider takes in client_options.

A NetworkError may be retried, with a backoff. An EncryptionError or a SignatureError should not be: a revoked permission, a missing key or an encrypted DEK of another key fails the same way on every retry. The variant per provider:

ProviderNetworkErrorEncryptionError
Google Cloud KMSgRPC Unavailable, DeadlineExceeded, ResourceExhausted, Aborted, Internal, Unknown, Cancelledany other gRPC code, such as PermissionDenied, Unauthenticated, NotFound, InvalidArgument, FailedPrecondition
AWS KMSThrottlingException, KMSInternalException, DependencyTimeoutException, KeyUnavailableException, a 5xx or 429 status, a timeout, a connection failure, a response the SDK could not readany other AWS error code, such as AccessDeniedException, NotFoundException, IncorrectKeyException, InvalidCiphertextException, DisabledException, KMSInvalidStateException, a request that could not be built or signed, and AWS_ERROR
Azure Key Vaulta 5xx or 429 status, a connection or I/O failureany other status, such as Forbidden or KeyNotFound, and a response that does not parse
Vault TransitVAULT_STATUS_5xx, VAULT_STATUS_429, VAULT_HTTP, VAULT_TIMEOUTany other VAULT_STATUS_<status>, VAULT_RESPONSE, VAULT_RESPONSE_TOO_LARGE, VAULT_CIPHERTEXT

Google’s client libraries retry the Cloud KMS Encrypt and Decrypt calls on Unavailable and DeadlineExceeded only. Keep a retry on the other Google Cloud KMS network codes to a few attempts.

STREAM_IO is the caller’s own source or sink failing, such as a reset download or a full disk, and the library cannot tell whether it may be retried. The root cause is the source’s or the sink’s error, and it says whether a retry can help. The retry is the whole stream again, from the start.

Keyring errors

Every fallible call of kms-aead-keyring returns KeyringResult<T>, whose error is KeyringError, also #[non_exhaustive]. code() returns kms-aead’s code for KmsAead, such as DECRYPT or a KMS’s code, and the variant’s name in upper snake case otherwise, such as KEY_VERSION_COMPROMISED. It is the error_code field of the keyring’s spans and the error.type of its metrics. Match on the variant and its fields, or on the code: the Display text may change in any release.

VariantCodeMeaning
KmsAeadkms-aead’sa KMS call, a decryption or an encoding failed, see Codes
StoreSTOREthe keyring store failed: store, operation, and the client’s error as root_cause and source()
InvalidIdentifierINVALID_IDENTIFIERa keyring id, an AAD part or a store name that breaks the rule of its type
RecordMissingRECORD_MISSINGno record for the keyring: at open and admin, before an administrative write, and from reload once the process took the record as deleted, see A record gone from the store. A dangerously_shred() of a keyring already gone returns Ok
RecordExistsRECORD_EXISTScreate found a record
RecordInvalidRECORD_INVALIDa record that breaks a size cap or an invariant, such as two versions holding the same encrypted DEK (DuplicateEncryptedDek) or a signature member that is not a detached signature (MalformedSignature), see Keyring records
RecordUnsignedRECORD_UNSIGNEDan unsigned record where record verification is Required, or where a signing admin writes over it, see Signing the keyring record
RecordSignatureInvalidRECORD_SIGNATURE_INVALIDa record whose signature fails under the verifying keys, or that is signed where the registry has no key; the cause carries the signature error’s code
RecordSignerMissingRECORD_SIGNER_MISSINGa write by an administration without a record signer over a signed record, before any store write
RecordSignatureRemovedRECORD_SIGNATURE_REMOVEDan unsigned record where the process holds a signed one, under VerifyIfSigned

RecordConflictRECORD_CONFLICTan administrative write lost every compare-and-swap, attempts of them

KeyVersionUnknownKEY_VERSION_UNKNOWNno version with the value’s key id, also after a reload
KeyVersionRetiredKEY_VERSION_RETIREDthe value’s version is retired; reinstate brings it back
KeyVersionDestroyedKEY_VERSION_DESTROYEDthe value’s version is destroyed

ReservedKeyIdRESERVED_KEY_IDa key id no version has: ordinal zero
DeterministicDisabledDETERMINISTIC_DISABLEDa deterministic version created without the deterministic feature
InteropINTEROPan encryption or a keyset export that a field’s Interop reader would not read
CustomAadUnsupportedCUSTOM_AAD_UNSUPPORTEDcustom_aad on a field BigQuery decrypts
ExportAlgorithmUnsupportedEXPORT_ALGORITHM_UNSUPPORTEDa version in an export whose algorithm the keyset’s reader does not read
UnknownKeyVersionStateUNKNOWN_KEY_VERSION_STATEFromStr of a name that is no KeyVersionState
InvalidEncryptionMarkerINVALID_ENCRYPTION_MARKERstored bytes that are not an EncryptionMarker

Besides the retry policies of the store clients, such as aws-sdk-s3’s, the keyring retries only an administrative write’s compare-and-swap, up to cas_retries times. What a caller may run again:

  • KmsAead: as in Retries, a NetworkError with a backoff;
  • Store: the library does not classify a store’s errors, so the decision follows the client’s error in root_cause;
  • RecordConflict: the administrative call, which reads the record again;
  • EncryptorOutdated: the write, with a new encryptor from Keyring::encryptor, see Re-encrypting a value;
  • PromotionTooEarly: the promotion, once its remaining has passed.

The other variants fail the same way until the record, the configuration or the call changes.

What errors never carry

No error carries plaintext, key material in any form, plaintext or encrypted, AAD content or cipher text bytes, in Display or in Debug. An application can log errors with {} and {:?}. A JsonEncoded value that does not decode also logs a redacted outline of its JSON, see Observability.

message is meant for a person reading logs. public holds the code, the part safe to return to the application’s own callers, and it is Serialize and Deserialize. std::error::Error::source() returns the root cause, the error of the SDK or library that failed, when there is one.

Observability

The library uses tracing. Every envelope call and every KMS call opens one span at the DEBUG level, and the spans carry what a call used besides how long it took: the mode, the algorithm and backend, whether the DEK came from the cache, the KMS key and its version, and the error code.

The spans are:

  • KMS AEAD encrypt: the envelope’s encrypt, encrypt_with_dek, encrypt_with_encrypted_dek, stream_encryptor and stream_encryptor_bare;
  • KMS AEAD decrypt: the envelope’s decrypt, decrypt_with_dek, decrypt_with_encrypted_dek, stream_decryptor and stream_decryptor_bare;
  • KMS AEAD generate DEK: generate_encrypted_dek and generate_encrypted_deterministic_dek;
  • KMS AEAD decrypt DEK: decrypt_deterministic_dek;
  • KMS AEAD re-encrypt DEK: reencrypt_dek and reencrypt_encrypted_dek;
  • KMS AEAD beacon: the envelope’s beacon;
  • KMS encrypt DEK: each provider’s encryption of a DEK, or its encryption with a public key;
  • KMS decrypt DEK: each provider’s decryption of a DEK;
  • KMS random: a call to the GCP or AWS KMS generator, when the provider uses it;
  • Signature sign: a Signer’s sign, sign_attached and sign_digest;
  • Signature verify: a Verifier’s verify, verify_signed and verify_digest;
  • MAC compute: an Authenticator’s mac and mac_attached;
  • MAC verify: an Authenticator’s verify and verify_with_mac;
  • KMS sign: each signing provider’s sign request;
  • KMS public key: each signing provider’s request for the public key of its key, and each KMS provider’s public_key of an asymmetric KEK.

The KMS spans nest inside the envelope span, so a DEK the envelope had to decrypt shows its KMS call as a child, and a DEK served by the cache shows none. AeadCipher and DeterministicCipher open no spans: they run once per value inside the caller’s span.

Showing the spans

Any tracing subscriber works. With tracing-subscriber, an EnvFilter that enables DEBUG for the library and span close events, every span is printed with its fields when it ends:

use kms_aead::backends::Ring;
use kms_aead::formats::Envelope;
use kms_aead::providers::{LocalKekId, LocalKmsProvider};
use kms_aead::{Encrypted, KmsAeadEnvelope};
use secret_vault_value::SecretValue;
use tracing_subscriber::fmt::format::FmtSpan;

#[tokio::main(flavor = "current_thread")]
async fn main() -> Result<(), Box<dyn std::error::Error + Send + Sync>> {
let subscriber = tracing_subscriber::fmt()
    .with_env_filter("kms_aead=debug")
    .with_span_events(FmtSpan::CLOSE)
    .finish();
tracing::subscriber::set_global_default(subscriber)?;

let provider = LocalKmsProvider::<Ring>::ephemeral(LocalKekId::try_from("example".to_string())?)?;
let envelope = KmsAeadEnvelope::<LocalKmsProvider<Ring>, Ring>::new(provider)?;

let aad = b"orders/1042";
let encrypted: Encrypted<SecretValue, Envelope> =
    envelope.encrypt(aad, &SecretValue::from("secret")).await?;
let decrypted: SecretValue = envelope.decrypt(aad, &encrypted).await?;
assert_eq!(decrypted, SecretValue::from("secret"));
Ok(())
}

The decrypt prints, without timestamps:

DEBUG KMS AEAD decrypt{/kms_aead/mode="per_call" /kms_aead/algorithm=aes-256-gcm /kms_aead/backend=ring /kms_aead/dek_cache=disabled}:KMS decrypt DEK{/kms_aead/provider=local /kms_aead/key="example" /kms_aead/kek_version="1" /kms_aead/response_time=0}: kms_aead::spans::kms: close time.busy=16.4µs time.idle=6.95µs
DEBUG KMS AEAD decrypt{/kms_aead/mode="per_call" /kms_aead/algorithm=aes-256-gcm /kms_aead/backend=ring /kms_aead/dek_cache=disabled}: kms_aead::spans: close time.busy=62.0µs time.idle=12.3µs

Full example available here, run it with cargo run --example tracing --features ring.

OpenTelemetry

With tracing-opentelemetry every span field becomes a span attribute with the same name, such as /kms_aead/kek_version, so the KMS calls and cache hits show up next to the latency of a request in Cloud Trace, Jaeger, etc.

The fields are declared empty when the span opens and recorded once their value is known, which tracing-opentelemetry exports the same way as fields given at the start. Add its layer to the subscriber as usual:

use opentelemetry::trace::TracerProvider;
use tracing_subscriber::layer::SubscriberExt;
use tracing_subscriber::util::SubscriberInitExt;

let provider = opentelemetry_sdk::trace::SdkTracerProvider::builder()
    .with_batch_exporter(exporter) // any exporter, e.g. opentelemetry-otlp
    .build();

tracing_subscriber::registry()
    .with(tracing_subscriber::EnvFilter::new("info,kms_aead=debug"))
    .with(tracing_opentelemetry::layer().with_tracer(provider.tracer("my-service")))
    .init();

Be aware the library spans are DEBUG, so the filter has to enable that level for kms_aead.

Fields

Every field name has the /kms_aead/ prefix, such as /kms_aead/mode. A value nobody reported is left unrecorded, never recorded as 0 or an empty string.

KMS AEAD encrypt and KMS AEAD decrypt

FieldValue
modeper_call for encrypt, decrypt and the stream calls, store_less for them on a store-less envelope, caller_dek for the *_with_dek and *_with_encrypted_dek calls
algorithmchacha20-poly1305, aes-128-gcm, aes-256-gcm or aes-256-gcm-siv
backendring, aws-lc or rustcrypto
dek_cachedisabled for an envelope without a cache; hit or miss for a call that decrypts through an enabled cache, a failed decryption recording miss; unrecorded for a call that looks nothing up
error_codethe code of the error the call returned

KMS AEAD generate DEK and KMS AEAD decrypt DEK

FieldValue

algorithmthe algorithm of the key, aes-256-siv for deterministic keys
backendgenerate DEK only: the envelope’s backend, ring, aws-lc or rustcrypto
dek_cachedecrypt DEK only: bypassed beside an enabled cache, since the call never goes through it, disabled otherwise
error_codethe code of the error the call returned

KMS AEAD re-encrypt DEK

FieldValue
dek_cachebypassed beside an enabled cache, since the call never goes through it, disabled otherwise
error_codethe code of the error the call returned

Its children are a KMS decrypt DEK under the version that encrypted the DEK and a KMS encrypt DEK under the current one.

KMS AEAD beacon

FieldValue

A beacon key the cache did not hold shows its KMS decrypt DEK as a child.

The key of each provider:

  • GCP: the CryptoKey name, projects/<project>/locations/<location>/keyRings/<ring>/cryptoKeys/<key>;
  • AWS: the key or alias ARN, or the configured key id when no region is known;
  • Azure: <vault URL>/keys/<name>;
  • Vault Transit: <vault address>/<namespace>/<mount>/keys/<name>, without the namespace segment when none is set;
  • local: the KEK id.

With the opentelemetry feature the signing and MAC calls record their duration as the envelope calls do. The spans carry no payload, signature, tag or key bytes.

Events

Besides the spans, the library logs:

  • WARN, inside the KMS span: a GCP or Vault Transit response without a key version;
  • WARN, once per process and layout: the first read of a layout of 0.29 or earlier with legacy-formats;
  • WARN, when a Vault Transit provider is created: an http address to another host, or a system CA certificate that cannot be loaded;
  • WARN, on the kms_aead::json target: a JsonEncoded value that does not decode, see JSON that does not decode;
  • INFO, when a provider that holds the public key of an asymmetric KEK is created (PublicKeyKekProvider, AsymmetricKekProvider and LocalAsymmetricKmsProvider): the KEK, its version and the public key’s fingerprint, the SHA-256 of its SubjectPublicKeyInfo in hex, so that an operator compares the producer’s and the administration’s;
  • INFO, when a local KMS provider is created: its KEK id and versions, each with where it came from, in the key and kek_versions fields;
  • INFO, when a local signing provider is created: its key id and versions, each with its algorithm, key id and where it came from, in the key and key_versions fields.

The library retries nothing itself, so there is no retry field.

JSON that does not decode

A JsonEncoded value whose bytes are not the JSON of its type, a decrypted plaintext or the payload of an attached signature or MAC after it verified, fails with INVALID_PLAINTEXT or INVALID_PAYLOAD. The error never quotes the bytes, and names serde_json’s category of failure (Syntax, Data or Eof) without the line and column, which are byte offsets that give away the length of the values before them. The library logs one WARN event on the kms_aead::json target beside it, with an outline of the JSON.

FieldValue
error_codeINVALID_PLAINTEXT or INVALID_PAYLOAD
decoded_typethe Rust type the JSON does not decode to
payload_typethe PAYLOAD_TYPE of a signed type; unrecorded for a plaintext
json_errorserde_json’s error: the message of a syntax error, such as EOF while parsing a string, or Data error for JSON of another shape than the type, with the line and column under SIZES
outlinethe outline of the JSON

By default the outline shows the shape of the JSON: the type of every value, <key> for every key, the nesting, and where serde_json stopped reading it, with serde_json’s message for a syntax error. That message is fixed text, and it can state a property of a value the outline hides, such as number out of range, invalid unicode code point or unexpected end of hex escape. The default shows no length and no position, since a length gives away a hidden boolean.

A type opts into more with a policy, the second type parameter of JsonEncoded. A signed payload is often public, so its policy may show more than an encrypted plaintext’s. See JSON that does not decode for a policy and the outlines it produces. kms_aead::json=off in an EnvFilter turns the event off.

An application outlines JSON of its own with RedactedJson, with no encryption involved, see Log JSON payloads without leaking data.

Keyring spans

The kms-aead-keyring crate follows the same rules: one DEBUG span per call, field names with the /kms_aead_keyring/ prefix, and nothing recorded for a value nobody reported. kms-aead’s spans nest inside them, so a KMS decrypt DEK under a Keyring decrypt is a DEK cache miss, and a KMS encrypt DEK under a Keyring rotate is the new version’s DEK. Enable kms_aead_keyring=debug in the filter beside kms_aead=debug. The spans from Keyring rotate to Keyring export are those of KeyringAdmin calls, under the admin feature.

SpanOpened byFields

backendring, aws-lc or rustcrypto; unrecorded for deterministic calls, since AES-SIV runs on RustCrypto whatever the keyring’s backend
