Keyring events

  • WARN: a read of a compromised version under the Allow policy, with the key id;
  • WARN: a read refused because its version is retired or destroyed, for an operator watching a sweep;
  • WARN: a record that holds a DEK encrypted with a public key, loaded under any record verification mode but Required, with the keyring id, once per record loaded. Under an asymmetric KEK anyone holding the public key can plant a version, see Keyrings under an asymmetric KEK;
  • WARN: a KeyringAdmin without a record signer built over a provider whose encrypts_with_public_key() is true;
  • WARN: a failed revalidation, the cached record staying in use;
  • WARN: “The keyring store no longer holds the keyring record; every version is destroyed in this process”, when a read finds the record gone: the process then holds the record with every version destroyed and stops revalidating the keyring, see A record gone from the store. It is an INFO, “The keyring store no longer holds the keyring record of a shredded keyring; every version is destroyed in this process”, when the record the process held had every version destroyed already, which a shred leaves before it deletes the record. The process that ran the shred logs nothing for it;
  • ERROR: a refreshed record refused because it moves a version backwards, with the first rule it breaks. Alert on it: it is the detection of a rewritten or restored record;
  • ERROR: a refreshed record refused by record verification, with its code, RECORD_UNSIGNED, RECORD_SIGNATURE_INVALID or RECORD_SIGNATURE_REMOVED; the process keeps the record it holds, and the Keyring load span records the code. Alert on it as on the previous one;
  • WARN: a compromised flag written without a rotation because the new DEK could not be encrypted;
  • INFO: the active version of a purpose changed, with the old and new key ids;
  • WARN: the store’s listener disconnected, reconnected, or did not start, and a keyring opened with no tokio runtime to follow the store’s signals;
  • WARN: a KeyringRegistry dropped while its background tasks run, which aborts them, with the count of open keyrings, and a shutdown whose timeout aborted the tasks still running, with their count;
  • WARN: a record a sweep could not re-encrypt, with the Display of the application’s candidate in record and of its error in error. Both are the application’s own text, so it keeps values, secrets and personal data out of them. A record id is often an entity id, so the sweep’s log is an administrative log;
  • WARN: a candidate the sweep’s stream returned as an error, with the Display of the error in error;
  • WARN: a batch of sweep::reencrypt_records_batched whose re-encryption failed as a whole, with its number of candidates in candidates and the Display of the error in error, and a batch that returned a different number of outcomes from it was given candidates, with both counts in candidates and outcomes;
  • DEBUG: a rate-limited reload for an unknown key id, and a compare-and-swap conflict that is retried.

The keyring’s spans, events and errors leave out everything kms-aead’s do, listed below. The AAD rule matters most there, since the default AAD holds the entity id: neither the AAD nor an EntityId appears anywhere. Key ids, keyring ids and store names are identifiers and appear.

Metrics

With the opentelemetry feature, kms-aead records OpenTelemetry metrics for every envelope call and KMS request. kms-aead-keyring’s opentelemetry feature turns it on, and the keyring records into the same instruments, with its own attributes. Both depend on the opentelemetry API crate only (no SDK): the application installs the SDK and an exporter, such as OTLP with opentelemetry-otlp or Prometheus with opentelemetry-prometheus. Without the feature neither crate depends on OpenTelemetry.

The instruments come from the global meter provider, with an instrumentation scope named after kms-aead and its version, the first time either crate records, and stay bound to it for the life of the process. Be aware to install the provider before the first KMS, envelope or keyring call, otherwise the instruments record nothing:

use opentelemetry::Key;
use opentelemetry_sdk::metrics::exporter::PushMetricExporter;
use opentelemetry_sdk::metrics::{Aggregation, Instrument, SdkMeterProvider, Stream};

fn install_meter_provider(exporter: impl PushMetricExporter) -> SdkMeterProvider {
    let provider = SdkMeterProvider::builder()
        .with_periodic_exporter(exporter)
        // The plaintext size without its smallest and largest value, see below.
        .with_view(|instrument: &Instrument| {
            if instrument.name() != "kms_aead.plaintext.size" {
                return None;
            }
            Stream::builder()
                .with_aggregation(Aggregation::ExplicitBucketHistogram {
                    boundaries: Vec::new(),
                    record_min_max: false,
                })
                .build()
                .ok()
        })
        // Optional: the durations without `kms_aead.keyring`, see below.
        .with_view(|instrument: &Instrument| {
            let kept: &[&'static str] = match instrument.name() {
                "kms_aead.operation.duration" => &[
                    "kms_aead.operation",
                    "kms_aead.mode",
                    "kms_aead.algorithm",
                    "kms_aead.backend",
                    "kms_aead.purpose",
                    "error.type",
                ],
                "kms_aead.store.duration" => &["kms_aead.store", "kms_aead.reason", "error.type"],
                _ => return None,
            };
            Stream::builder()
                .with_allowed_attribute_keys(kept.iter().map(|key| Key::from_static_str(key)))
                .build()
                .ok()
        })
        .build();
    opentelemetry::global::set_meter_provider(provider.clone());
    provider
}
let provider = install_meter_provider(opentelemetry_sdk::metrics::InMemoryMetricExporter::default());
provider.shutdown().unwrap();

The names follow OpenTelemetry’s semantic conventions: the library’s own attributes have the kms_aead. prefix, and a failed call carries error.type, the code of its error. A successful call has no error.type.

InstrumentTypeUnitAttributesKeyring calls add
kms_aead.plaintext.sizehistogram, no bucketsBykms_aead.operation, kms_aead.mode, kms_aead.algorithm, kms_aead.backendkms_aead.keyring, kms_aead.purpose, kms_aead.key_id
kms_aead.operation.durationhistogramskms_aead.operation, kms_aead.mode, kms_aead.algorithm, kms_aead.backend, error.typekms_aead.keyring, kms_aead.purpose
kms_aead.kms.durationhistogramskms_aead.provider, kms_aead.operation, error.type
kms_aead.store.durationhistogramskms_aead.keyring, kms_aead.store, kms_aead.reason, error.type
kms_aead.record.refusalscounter{record}kms_aead.keyring, error.type
  • kms_aead.plaintext.size records the plaintext length of each value encrypted or decrypted: its count is the values, and its sum their bytes. It has no buckets. The SDK also keeps the smallest and the largest value of each series and OTLP exports them, unless a view turns them off as above. A failed call records no value;
  • kms_aead.operation of a value is encrypt or decrypt. The envelope also times beacon, generate_dek for generate_encrypted_dek and generate_encrypted_deterministic_dek, decrypt_dek for decrypt_deterministic_dek, and reencrypt_dek for reencrypt_dek, reencrypt_encrypted_dek and the deprecated upgrade_legacy_value and upgrade_legacy_encrypted_dek;
  • kms_aead.mode is the envelope’s per_call or store_less for encrypt, decrypt, the stream calls, reencrypt_dek and upgrade_legacy_value, caller_dek for the *_with_dek and *_with_encrypted_dek calls, beacon, the calls that generate or decrypt a DEK, reencrypt_encrypted_dek and upgrade_legacy_encrypted_dek, and keyring for a keyring’s calls. A Signer and a Verifier record sign and verify with the mode signature, and an Authenticator records mac and verify_mac with the mode caller_dek;
  • kms_aead.algorithm and kms_aead.backend are those of the span. kms_aead.algorithm is absent where it is unknown: on a re-encryption, in which the algorithm plays no part, on a probe, which runs under every version, and on a keyring call that fails before it finds its version. A keyring’s deterministic call has kms_aead.backend rustcrypto, since AES-SIV runs on RustCrypto whatever the keyring’s backend;
  • a keyring call is recorded once, with kms_aead.mode keyring: it runs kms-aead’s ciphers, never its envelope. encrypt is an encryptor’s encrypt and encrypt_deterministic, decrypt is decrypt, decrypt_with_trust and their deterministic pair, reencrypt is reencrypt and reencrypt_deterministic, which also record the decrypt and encrypt they make, beacon is an encryptor’s beacon, and probe is beacon_probes and deterministic_probes. decrypt_envelope_for_migration is a decrypt with no kms_aead.purpose. kms_aead.key_id is the version’s key id, as a number;
  • probes and beacons record no value. A probe is never stored, and a beacon is an HMAC-SHA256 output: HMAC takes no nonce, and its bound as a PRF is far beyond the values one key can encrypt. Signatures and MACs record no value either. A keyring’s MAC calls add no keyring call of their own: the Authenticator they run records each one;
  • kms_aead.kms.duration covers the request to the KMS only, as the span’s response_time does. kms_aead.operation is encrypt_dek, decrypt_dek, random, sign or public_key, or encrypt_dek_with_public_key for an encryption with the public key of an asymmetric KEK, which is a local operation and no KMS request, so that the duration tells the two apart. kms_aead.provider is the span’s provider. The KEK name is left out;
  • kms_aead.store.duration covers every read of a keyring record, with the store and reason of the Keyring load span;
  • the stream calls of an envelope, stream_encryptor, stream_decryptor and their bare forms, and a keyring’s stream_encryptor and stream_decryptor are recorded in kms_aead.operation.duration with kms_aead.operation stream_encrypt and stream_decrypt. The plaintext size instrument records nothing for them, since the size is not known at the call. AeadCipher’s stream calls open no span;
  • kms_aead.record.refusals counts each keyring record a process refused because of its signature, by code in error.type: RECORD_UNSIGNED, RECORD_SIGNATURE_INVALID or RECORD_SIGNATURE_REMOVED. It is the metric of the ERROR event above.

No attribute names a DEK, so the envelope’s values are recorded per algorithm, not per key. A store-less envelope needs no alert on them: it renews its DEK at max_uses and max_bytes, see Volume limit.

A Prometheus exporter adds the unit to the name and replaces the dots with underscores: kms_aead_plaintext_size_bytes_count and kms_aead_plaintext_size_bytes_sum, kms_aead_operation_duration_seconds, and attributes such as kms_aead_key_id. For an alert on the values and bytes under one keyring version, see Alerting on volume.

The keyring’s plaintext size has one time series per keyring, purpose, key version and operation. With a keyring per tenant, that is a series per tenant and version. Under cumulative temporality, the default, the SDK keeps a series until the process restarts, also once its version is no longer used. The SDK keeps at most 2000 attribute sets per instrument and records every further one into one series with the attribute otel.metric.overflow, with no error: an alert per version stops counting the versions past the limit. Raise the limit with Stream::builder().with_cardinality_limit in a view where the keyrings and their versions come near it. Key ids are numbered per keyring, so the plaintext size keeps kms_aead.keyring: without it, version 1 of every keyring would add up to one series. The duration histograms do not need it, and with their buckets they cost the most series, so the view above drops it from them. The view’s with_allowed_attribute_keys needs the SDK’s spec_unstable_metrics_views feature.

What is never logged

No span field or event carries:

  • plaintext, DEKs in any form, plaintext or encrypted, or KEK material;
  • AAD content, which can hold entity ids and so personal data;
  • cipher text bytes;
  • the length of a value or of its AAD. For AES-256-GCM the plaintext length is the value’s length minus a fixed overhead, and a length tells short values such as yes, no or a country code apart.

The kms_aead.plaintext.size histogram is the exception: it adds up plaintext lengths. A keyring or an envelope that encrypts one value per export interval gives that value’s length to whoever reads the metrics. The SDK also keeps the smallest and the largest value of each series, under cumulative temporality since the process started, and OTLP exports them: the exact length of the shortest and the longest value per keyring and version, at any traffic. A Prometheus exporter exports neither. The OpenTelemetry API gives kms-aead’s instrument no setting to leave them out, so the view in Metrics turns them off with record_min_max: false. Treat the histogram like the values themselves where traffic is low, and everywhere without that view.

The kms_aead::json event is the other exception, under a policy that opts in: its outline then carries the length of the values and keys of the JSON that did not decode, which gives away a hidden boolean, and the values the policy reveals. The default policy carries neither, but every policy shows serde_json’s message for a syntax error, which is fixed text and can state a property of a hidden value, such as number out of range.

Key identifiers are recorded: KEK names, key versions and local KEK ids. A KEK name identifies the project, the location and the key, and a per-tenant KEK name carries the tenant id, so treat the key field accordingly in a trace pipeline.

Errors follow the same rules, see Error handling.

Security considerations

Threat model

Envelope encryption protects stored values from whoever can read the store but cannot use the KEK: a database dump, a backup, a replica, a data export or an operator with read access to the tables. Decrypting a value needs a KMS decryption, and the KMS checks the caller’s permission and, for the cloud KMSs, writes an audit log entry.

It does not protect values from:

  • a process, or whoever controls it, that may decrypt with the KEK: it can read every value it can fetch;
  • whoever can read the memory of a process while it holds plaintext DEKs, in the DEK cache or as a store-less writer’s DEK;
  • a reader of the store learning which values are equal, for values encrypted with deterministic encryption.

The AAD protects a value from being moved: a value copied into another row or column, with an AAD that names its place, fails to decrypt there. The AAD itself is not encrypted, and neither is the length of the encrypted DEK at the start of Tink’s KMS envelope, which is not authenticated either.

An encrypted DEK carries no context that the KMS authenticates. Whoever can write where encrypted DEKs are stored, such as a database table or a keyring store, can copy an encrypted DEK to another record or relabel it, and the KMS decrypts it for any principal allowed to decrypt with that KEK. The controls for this are in the deployment:

  • write access to the stored keys is kept apart from the right to decrypt with the KEK;
  • each isolation boundary, such as a tenant or an environment, has a KEK of its own, so a DEK copied across it fails to decrypt;
  • the KMS audit log of decrypt calls, turned on where the KMS leaves it off by default, shows which principal decrypted which key.

A DEK encrypted with the public half of an asymmetric KEK leaves no KMS audit log entry; the decrypt log and the record’s signature are the controls, see Asymmetric KEKs.

The local KMS has no IAM, no audit log and no HSM, see its limits.

Nonces

Every AEAD algorithm takes a random 96-bit nonce from the backend’s CSPRNG for each value, and writes it in front of the cipher text. NIST SP 800-38D allows 2^32 encryptions under one key with random 96-bit nonces, and the library applies the same bound to every algorithm:

  • the per-call envelope encrypts one value per DEK, so the bound does not apply to it;
  • a store-less envelope renews its DEK at max_uses, 2^24 by default and at most 2^32;
  • with encrypt_with_dek and AeadCipher, the application counts the values it encrypts under one key, and moves to a new key before 2^32;
  • the local KMS encrypts each DEK it encrypts with AES-256-GCM, so a KEK version is good for about 2^32 encryptions. Add a new version before that.

AES-256-GCM-SIV (RFC 8452) resists nonce misuse: a repeated nonce reveals only whether the two values encrypted with it are equal, and does not break integrity.

Deterministic encryption with AES-SIV takes no random nonce.

Data volume

AES-GCM also has a bound on the data under one key, whatever the nonces. The bounds per algorithm:

  • AES-GCM: RFC 8446, section 5.5, allows “up to 2^24.5 full-size records (about 24 million)” of 2^14 bytes, 2^38.5 bytes, “while keeping a safety margin of approximately 2^-57 for Authenticated Encryption (AE) security”. Luykx and Paterson, “Limits on Authenticated Encryption Use in TLS”, table 1, give the same 2^24.5 records for a confidentiality advantage of 2^-60. RFC 8452, section 9, allows 2^48 blocks, 2^52 bytes, under one AES-GCM key for an advantage of 2^-32;
  • AES-GCM-SIV: RFC 8452, section 9, allows 2^32 values of up to 8 GiB each under one key with random nonces for an advantage of 2^-32, so the bound on the number of values bounds it already;
  • ChaCha20-Poly1305: draft-irtf-cfrg-aead-limits, section 6.3, gives it no confidentiality limit beyond the security of ChaCha20 itself, and its integrity bound depends on the number of forgery attempts, which no writer can count.

The bound applies to AES-GCM keys this way:

  • a store-less envelope renews its DEK at max_bytes, 2^38 bytes by default under AES-GCM, below the 2^38.5 of RFC 8446, and at most 2^52, see Volume limit. AES-256-GCM-SIV and ChaCha20-Poly1305 have no default volume limit;
  • with encrypt_with_dek and AeadCipher, the application counts the bytes it encrypts under one AES-GCM key too, and moves to a new key before 2^38 bytes.

Key lifetime and zeroisation

DataEncryptionKey, LocalKek and the Vault token are zeroised on drop and shown as *** by Debug. The DEK cache zeroises a key when its entry leaves the cache and no with_dek closure holds it.

Some copies are out of the library’s reach:

  • the KMS SDKs and HTTP clients the providers use keep copies of each plaintext DEK in request and response buffers, which kms-aead cannot zeroise. The same holds for the Vault token in the HTTP stack;
  • the copies of a key inside a crypto backend: what each backend clears is in its section of Crypto backends;
  • an expired DEK stays in the cache’s memory until the next call on the cache or purge_expired;
  • decrypt returns the application’s own T: Plaintext. Only SecretValue as T is zeroised on drop; String and Vec<u8> take over the decrypted buffer and leave it in freed memory, and what the application does with the value afterwards is its own.

What rotation does not protect

Rotating the KEK in the KMS makes the KMS encrypt new DEKs under the new key version. It does not:

  • re-encrypt the DEKs already stored: they still decrypt with the old key version, which the KMS must keep enabled for as long as those values are read;
  • re-encrypt any value: a DEK that leaked keeps decrypting every value encrypted under it, whatever the KEK version that encrypts it. To retire a leaked DEK, decrypt and encrypt the values again under new DEKs;
  • reach DEKs already in a DEK cache: they keep serving until their entries expire, or until invalidate or clear. The same holds for disabling a KMS key or revoking an IAM grant;
  • renew a store-less writer’s DEK: it is renewed by its policy, or once it leaves the cache.

With the local KMS, rotation is a new KEK version. The old version has to stay in the provider for as long as DEKs encrypted under it are read, and crypto-shredding means destroying every copy of it, backups included.

Keyring deployment

A keyring record decides which versions are read and which one encrypts new values, and the administration signs it where record signing is on, see Signing the keyring record; without it, its integrity rests on the keyring store’s access control. The two principals of a deployment and their rights are in Applications and administration.

A writer of the keyring store can copy or relabel an encrypted DEK, between versions of one keyring or between keyrings under the same KEK: the KMS decrypts an encrypted DEK for any principal allowed to decrypt with the KEK, whatever version or keyring it is filed under. Such a writer can bring a compromised or destroyed version’s DEK back into use under a new key id, or point one tenant’s version at another tenant’s DEK. Under a symmetric KEK, a writer without KMS rights cannot add a DEK of its own, since every decryption names the configured KEK and the KMS refuses a DEK encrypted under another key; an asymmetric KEK encrypts for anyone holding its public key, see Keyring limits. The controls for this are the deployment’s, as in the threat model:

  • write access to the keyring store for the administration only, and read-only access for the applications;
  • one KEK per isolation boundary, such as a tenant or an environment, so a DEK copied across it fails to decrypt;
  • backups of the record, or versioning in the store, to compare with and restore from;
  • the KMS audit log of encrypt calls, which shows whether the administrative job encrypted a version’s DEK. An asymmetric KEK’s encryption is local and leaves none: the signature on the record and the KMS log of the signing call take its place.

A running process also refuses every record that moves a version backwards, with an ERROR, see Record cache. That catches a rewritten state, such as a cleared compromised flag, and not a copied DEK filed under a new version.

Keyring limits

  • The administration is trusted. KMS encrypt plus store write re-activates any version, and so does an application that provisions keyrings lazily with the admin feature and those rights. With record signing on, the right to sign records plus store write does. The KMS audit log is the detection;
  • a new process has no baseline. A store writer can clear a compromised flag, re-activate a version or restore a destroyed one for the processes that start after the write. Running processes refuse such a record and log an ERROR, also a process that took the record’s deletion, which keeps the deleted record as the baseline. A signed record read under Required refuses such a write; under VerifyIfSigned a running process refuses it and a new process has no baseline. A signature proves the record was written by a signer, not that it is the latest: a store writer can restore an older signed record;
  • an asymmetric KEK encrypts for anyone holding its public key, an RSA key on Azure Key Vault included: whoever holds it encrypts a DEK of their own into the record. A signed record under RecordSignaturePolicy::Required is the control, see Keyrings under an asymmetric KEK;
  • decrypt_envelope_for_migration knows no key version. It decrypts the encrypted DEK an envelope value carries, so an encrypted DEK kept from a version, compromised or destroyed since, still decrypts values through it. Keep it in the read path for the migration only;
  • a destroyed version’s DEK lives as long as the KEK version that encrypted it. A backup of the record holds encrypted DEKs the KMS still decrypts, and a keyset exported before the destroy holds the version’s key under the same KEK, see Crypto-shredding;
  • dangerously_shred() reaches the store’s API only. It deletes the record with the earlier versions the store keeps, and never calls the KMS. What the store keeps beyond its API stays until the operator deletes it: Firestore serves a deleted document to reads at an earlier time for one hour, and for seven days with point-in-time recovery on; Cloud Storage’s soft delete keeps every deleted generation restorable for the bucket’s retention, 7 days by default; backups, exports, WAL archives, replicas and replication hold the record until they are deleted. Every such copy, and every BigQuery and Tink keyset exported from the keyring, still decrypts while a KEK version that encrypted its DEKs exists: delete the copies, or rotate the KEK, re-encrypt every other keyring under it and destroy the previous KEK versions. Values encrypted under the keyring stay where they are and decrypt under nothing, and the keyring id is free for a new keyring;
  • a running process keeps the deleted record as the baseline of its id. A store writer who deletes the record and writes another under the id gains nothing against it: a signed keyring stays signed, so under VerifyIfSigned an unsigned record fails with RecordSignatureRemoved, and a copy of the record from before the deletion, signed or not, fails with RecordRegressed and an ERROR, because it revives a version the process holds destroyed. A keyring created again under the id has other versions, by key id or creation time, and decrypts. The baseline lasts until the process restarts, so an accidentally deleted record that is restored is taken by a new process only. A new process has no baseline, and Required is the control for it;
  • an exported BigQuery keyset holds the versions’ keys, encrypted with no associated data, since BigQuery decrypts it with none. Treat it as a copy of the record’s readable versions, and export again after every rotation and every mark_compromised;
  • a sweep re-encrypts forgeries as trusted values. Values read through a compromised version are decided per record before a sweep re-encrypts them, see The trust of re-encrypted values;
  • deterministic fields leak equality and frequency, and an equality query across a rotation links a value’s history for whoever reads the query, see Queries across rotations;
  • beacons leak likely equality and frequency, as far as their bits tell values apart, see What a beacon leaks;
  • a promotion’s guard covers healthy processes only. promote refuses until record_ttl has passed since the rotation, and promote_now skips that. A process whose revalidations fail keeps an older record for longer: when it meets a value under a version it has not seen, it reloads its record, and fails the read when it cannot reach the store. So the wait beyond the record TTL is the operator’s, see Staged rotation.

OSS

Open source code is created through voluntary collaboration of software developers. The original authors license the code so that anyone can see it, modify it, and distribute new versions of it. You should manage all OSS using the same procedures and tools that you use for commercial products. As always, train your employees on cyber security best practices that can help them securely use and manage software products. You should not solely rely on individuals, especially on the projects like this reading sensitive information.

Versioning

Please don’t use broad version dependency management not to include a new version of dependency automatically without auditing the changes.

Reporting a vulnerability

See SECURITY.md.
